## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/software-engineering-craft-en

The case for engineering craft used to rest on the cost of typing code. In 2026 that case collapses: an AI assistant can produce a 500-line working module in fifteen minutes. So the loud voices say craft now matters less. They are wrong, and this book is the careful argument for why. The bottleneck did not disappear — it rotated. A 2016 engineer's day was 60% writing / 20% reviewing / 20% maintaining; a 2026 engineer's day is 20% / 40% / 40%. The disciplines that pay rent in the new split — tests as spec, refactoring under test, naming as design, ADRs, code review, version-control narrative, and AI as collaborator — are what this book actually teaches.

Three working engineers carry the chapters. Asher Goldman at Trellis is the staff engineer at an eight-year CRM codebase, the one who runs every surprising line through what was the author worried about? before changing it. Aki Tanaka at Helix is the senior engineer at an AI-native startup who refuses to open Claude Code until her failing tests are red on screen — a habit that formed the autumn an agent produced six hundred plausible-looking lines that corrupted webhook state in staging and ate two days plus a Sunday. Carmen Ortiz manages engineering at Currents and writes every review comment label-first ( Blocking: , Suggestion: , Decision needed: ) so the reader knows what is required of them before reading any prose. They appear and reappear, anchored to distinct stacks (TypeScript/Postgres, Rust/Cloudflare Workers, Python/FastAPI) and distinct organizational realities, so the disciplines land on real working code rather than thought experiments.

The book is structured around discipline-over-dogma: every rule comes with its reasoning and its when-to-break-it clause. The chapter on TDD is honest about when test-after is the rational choice. The chapter on patterns names which ones aged badly (Singleton, Visitor) and why. The chapter on big rewrites surveys both Joel Spolsky's classic warning and the cases where rewrites shipped. A failure case study sits next to every win — Northwind's ledger migration that stalled at 25% traffic and was abandoned, alongside the parallel-paths refactor that worked. Thread C runs through Parts III-VIII as a recurring callback: the seven code lies LLMs tell in 2026 (happy-path bias, over-abstraction, inconsistent style, missing error handling, off-by-one boundary errors, hallucinated APIs, plausible-looking insecure code), each chapter naming which lie its discipline catches, and Appendix D consolidating all seven into a two-minute printable AI-code review checklist. By the last chapter, when Asher, Aki, and Carmen converge on a five-month cross-organization customer-consent migration, the disciplines stop being abstractions: you have watched them practiced for 23 chapters across three real engineering contexts.

**Target audience**：Working software engineers (2–10 years experience); engineering managers seeking vocabulary for what they sense in code reviews; senior engineers on promotion track; juniors aiming to skip five years of pattern-discovery; tech leads onboarding new teams; consultants advising on code health.

**Prerequisites**：One year shipping production code in any language. Comfort with the command line, a basic understanding of version control, and willingness to read code rather than skim it.

**Study hours**：100–120 hours across 23 chapters (4–5 hours per chapter, 4–5 Socratopia sessions per chapter).

## Introduction

This is a book about software engineering craft in 2026, when AI assistants generate a large share of working code. Its central observation: AI changed the equation. The cost of producing a working first draft of code has collapsed; the cost of reviewing, integrating, and maintaining that code has not. The bottleneck moved from typing to reviewing, integrating, and maintaining. Craft, in 2026, is the set of disciplines that minimize the cost of the work that did not collapse.

The book is organized around the disciplines that _compound_ over a five-to-ten-year career: tests as spec, refactoring under test, naming as design, ADRs, code review, version-control craft, and AI as collaborator. Each is taught with reasoning, mechanics, and a 2026-specific angle. Each chapter ends with the three-year question: _in three years, when someone debugs this code at 2am, will they thank you or curse you?_ The disciplines that produce the "thank you" answer are the ones the book is about.

Three protagonists recur through the chapters as case studies. Asher Goldman is a Staff Engineer at Trellis, a CRM SaaS with an 8-year codebase and 200 engineers; his daily reality is legacy refactoring, technical debt management, and the archaeology of decisions made before he arrived. Aki Tanaka is a Senior Engineer at Helix, an AI-native devtools startup with 25 engineers and a 2-year-old Rust-and-TypeScript codebase; her daily reality is the AI-collaborative workflow as production discipline. Carmen Ortiz is an Engineering Manager at Currents, a 50-person data platform organization; her daily reality is code review culture, async engineering across three time zones, and the team-level disciplines that scale beyond any individual. They appear across the chapters where their contexts are most illustrative; in the final chapter, they converge on a joint multi-team migration project.

The book also tracks two recurring threads. Thread B follows a single function (`calculate_refund`) across five maturity stages — first draft (Chapter 2), after first review (Chapter 15), after first bug (Chapter 19), after first refactor (Chapter 10), and five years later (Chapter 23) — as a sidebar example of how the disciplines compound on the same lump of code. Thread C catalogs the seven characteristic patterns of bugs that LLMs introduce in 2026-generated code: plausible-but-untested edge cases, over-abstraction (the pattern tax), inconsistent style with surrounding code, missing or wrong-granularity error handling, subtle off-by-one and boundary errors, hallucinated library APIs, and plausible-looking insecure code. Every chapter in Parts III through VIII names which of these lies the chapter's discipline catches and shows the catch in concrete code; Appendix D consolidates all seven into a printable review checklist.

The book's structure follows the dependency order of the disciplines. Part I (Chapters 1-2) establishes the 2026 frame and the foundation skill of reading old code. Part II (Chapters 3-4) covers Git craft, local and team. Part III (Chapters 5-8) builds the testing foundation: pyramid, property-based and mutation testing, test doubles, TDD versus test-after. Part IV (Chapters 9-11) builds refactoring on top of the test foundation: the twelve refactorings that pay for themselves, refactoring under test, the multi-month parallel-paths refactor. Part V (Chapters 12-14) builds the design vocabulary: patterns that stayed useful, patterns that aged badly, hexagonal architecture and functional core / imperative shell. Part VI (Chapters 15-16) covers code review and team practices. Part VII (Chapters 17-18) covers documentation and naming. Part VIII (Chapters 19-21) covers technical debt and codebase sustainability: the four-quadrants model, the Boy Scout Rule and Strangler Fig pattern, and the rare cases when a big rewrite is justified. Part IX (Chapters 22-23) closes with the AI-collaborative workflow itself and the synthesis across all prior chapters.

This is not a TDD evangelism book. It is not a GoF design-patterns catalog. It is not a "clean code" prescriptive book. It is not an architecture book; architecture belongs to a different volume in this series. It is not an interview prep book and not an observability or SRE book; those have their own volumes. Every chapter follows the discipline-over-dogma principle: every rule comes with its reasoning and its when-to-break-it clause. The engineer who knows when to apply each discipline and when to skip it is the engineer this book aims to produce.

The intended reader is the working software engineer with two to ten years of experience, the engineering manager who wants vocabulary for what they sense in code reviews, the senior engineer on the promotion track, the junior engineer trying to skip five years of pattern discovery, and the tech lead onboarding a new team. The prerequisite is one year of shipping production code in any language. Comfort with the command line, basic version-control familiarity, and willingness to read code rather than skim it are assumed.

To get the most out of the book: read sequentially. Each chapter builds on the previous; skipping ahead means missing the foundation that makes the later chapters land. After the first read-through, return to the disciplines you found most compelling and apply them deliberately for a month each. The book is the map; the practice is the journey. Over a year of deliberate practice on five or six disciplines, the engineer's craft compounds visibly; over a five-year career, the difference between the engineer who practiced and the engineer who did not is enormous.

The reader who finishes the book should think: _craft in 2026 is not what it was in 2016; the bottleneck shifted from typing to reviewing and integrating; the disciplines that compound are tests as spec, naming, ADRs, refactoring under test, version-control craft, code review as discipline, and AI as collaborator; I can ship code that lasts five years._ That feeling, the long-term-thinking engineer, is what this volume exists to produce.

Software Engineering Craft is Volume 1 of Series III in the Modern Practitioner's Software Library. The library covers foundational craft (Series III), system architecture (Series IV), and production operations (Series IV continued). This volume is the foundation; the later volumes build on it.

Good luck with the practice. The craft is the work; the work is the craft.

— SocraLab

## Chapter 1: Why Craft Matters More in 2026

A senior engineer at a large fintech opened a pull request from her junior teammate on a Monday morning in March 2026. The PR was 1,200 lines, touched eleven files, added a new payment-reconciliation module, and had 94% test coverage. The branch was three days old. The junior had typed perhaps 200 of those lines; the rest came from a Claude Code agent running in the background while he reviewed.

The senior engineer pulled the branch, ran the suite, watched it pass, and then sat back. The code worked. The tests passed. The coverage was higher than her own typical PRs. And yet she spent the next four hours reading every line, asking the junior six probing questions, and ultimately requesting three structural changes that the agent would never have proposed on its own. The merge happened on Wednesday.

Three days for 1,200 lines of working code: in 2016 that would have been a normal pace for one engineer. In 2026 it is a normal pace for one engineer reviewing an agent's work. Something has shifted, and the shift is the subject of this book.

### 1.1 The equation that broke

The traditional case for engineering craft assumed a scarcity that no longer holds. The case went roughly like this. Writing code is slow, expensive, and error-prone. Therefore engineers should write less code, write it carefully, name things well, refactor when patterns emerge, and review each other's work to catch mistakes before they ship. Craft was a response to the bottleneck of human typing speed and human attention. Every line cost something, so every line should pull its weight.

In 2026 that scarcity assumption is gone for the writing step. An engineer with an AI assistant can produce a working 500-line module in fifteen minutes. The first draft is plausible, often syntactically clean, often coverage-adequate. The cost per line of _generated_ code has fallen by an order of magnitude or more, depending on task.

If the case for craft rested only on the cost of writing, you might conclude that craft now matters less. Some loud voices on the internet say exactly that. They are wrong, and the rest of this chapter explains why. The short version: the equation rebalanced rather than disappeared. The bottleneck moved.

#### Definition: The 2026 Craft Equation

In 2026, the cost of producing a working first draft of code has collapsed, while the cost of reviewing, integrating, and maintaining that code has not. **Craft is the set of disciplines that minimize the cost of the second category, the one that did not collapse.**

In plain language, this means: AI is cheap at the part where you generate plausible code; humans are expensive at the part where you ensure that the plausible code is actually correct, fits the system around it, and survives the next five years of change. Craft is what makes the human side of that equation cheaper.

The senior engineer in the opening vignette spent four hours reviewing three days of agent output. That ratio is the new craft economy. If you can review well, your codebase compounds. If you cannot, your codebase rots in fast-forward because plausible-looking code accumulates faster than ever before.

### 1.2 Three jobs the AI cannot do

Strip away the noise from any 2026 engineering team and you see three jobs that AI assistants do not do well, and that consume most of a working engineer's day:

  1. **Reviewing for fit.** AI generates code that is locally correct and globally inconsistent. It does not know that your team voted against the Result type in March, or that the `OrderService` is being deprecated in favor of `OrderAggregate`, or that the test fixture that the AI just invented contradicts a real fixture twenty files away. Catching these requires holding the codebase in your head, and the codebase in your head is not in any prompt.
  2. **Integrating across boundaries.** A module that works in isolation is one-tenth of the work. The other nine-tenths is making it cooperate with the seventeen other modules that already exist. AI is good at writing isolated modules. AI is poor at deciding which of the seventeen other modules the new module should talk to, which interfaces should change to accommodate it, and which existing patterns it should preserve.
  3. **Maintaining over years.** Three years from now, the original author of this code may be gone, the original requirements may have shifted, the dependencies may have evolved through three major versions, and the original AI model that wrote the code is retired. The code that ages well is the code that is readable without the original author present. AI assistants produce plausible-now code, not necessarily readable-in-three-years code.

Each of these is a craft skill. None of them are about typing speed. All of them compound over a career.

### 1.3 What compounds and what depreciates

The most useful exercise for a working engineer in 2026 is to sort their daily skills into two piles. The left pile is **skills that compound** : every year you practice them, you become more valuable, because the skill is rare and the world needs more of it. The right pile is **skills that depreciate** : every year, the skill becomes less valuable, because automation or AI is closing in on it.

Here is a rough sort, current as of 2026.

Compounding | Depreciating  
---|---  
Reading old code with intent | Typing boilerplate  
Writing tests that test (not pass) | Writing happy-path tests  
Refactoring under a safety net | Memorizing language syntax  
Naming as design | Recalling stdlib function signatures  
Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) | Filling in CRUD templates  
Reviewing AI-generated code | Stylistic enforcement (linters do it)  
Version-control narrative | Local commit hygiene (tools enforce it)  
Directing AI workflows | Manual git operations  
Domain modeling | Single-purpose script writing  
Cross-system integration thinking | Pure within-module algorithms  
  
You will notice a pattern. The left column is dominated by skills that require holding context outside the current 500-line window. The right column is dominated by skills that operate inside a 500-line window. AI assistants in 2026 operate inside a window. They do not yet hold the kind of distributed context that the left column requires. This will not be true forever, but it is true for at least the planning horizon of this book.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is that "AI will do all of these eventually, so craft is doomed." The mistake is the _eventually_. Eventually, ocean rises submerge coastal cities, and yet people still buy houses near the coast based on the next thirty years rather than the next thousand. The right horizon for craft skill investment is the next five to ten years of your career, not the asymptotic limit of AI capability. Within that horizon, the compounding skills compound and the depreciating skills depreciate.

A second common misconception is that "junior engineers can skip the depreciating skills and go straight to the compounding ones." The mistake is that the compounding skills are _built on_ the depreciating ones. You cannot review AI-generated code well without first writing some code yourself. You cannot name things well without first naming many things poorly. The depreciating skills remain necessary as a foundation; they are simply not, by themselves, sufficient for a career.

### 1.4 The honest case for craft

The honest case for craft in 2026 has three parts, and each part is verifiable in your own work this week.

#### Part 1: Code is a liability, not an asset

Every line of code in production is something you must maintain. Maintenance includes: fixing bugs, updating dependencies, complying with new regulations, removing the line when it is no longer needed, explaining the line to a new hire, and reviewing every change to the line for the rest of its life. The asset is what the code _does_ for users. The lines of code are the cost.

In 2016, this framing was already true, but the cost of producing lines was high enough that engineers naturally produced few of them. In 2026, the cost of producing lines is near zero, and the cost of maintaining them is unchanged. So **producing more lines per hour without producing more user value per hour is a net loss**. AI makes it easy to produce more lines. Craft is what keeps the asset-to-liability ratio sane.

#### Part 2: The bottleneck moved, but did not disappear

A pre-2020 engineer's day might have been: 60% writing, 20% reviewing, 20% maintaining. A 2026 engineer's day looks more like: 20% writing (often by directing an agent), 40% reviewing (the agent's output and others'), 40% maintaining (debugging, refactoring, migrating, explaining). The total time is the same. The split has rotated.

If you optimize only for the 20% writing slice, you are optimizing the part that AI already handles. If you optimize for the 80% review-and-maintain slice, you are optimizing the parts that AI does not handle and that compound over your career.

#### Part 3: The codebase outlives the model

The AI model that wrote a file in 2024 has been deprecated by 2026. The AI model writing files in 2026 will be deprecated by 2028. But the file remains. Whoever maintains the file in 2028 will not have the prompt that produced it, will not have the conversation that shaped it, and may not even know which model generated it. They will have only the file and the surrounding codebase.

This is the most underappreciated fact in 2026 engineering. **The code persists; the model that wrote it does not.** A codebase that depends on continuous AI co-piloting to be maintainable is a codebase that fails when the model changes. A codebase that is maintainable by humans reading code is a codebase that survives model transitions.

Craft, in this frame, is the set of disciplines that make code maintainable by humans reading it, not by AI continuing to assist them.

### 1.5 What this book covers and what it does not

This book covers what we call the _compounding disciplines_ : testing as discipline, refactoring under test, patterns sensibly applied, code review as discipline, documentation that helps, naming as design, technical debt management, and the AI-collaborative workflow. It treats each as a craft skill with mechanics worth learning, not as a slogan worth reciting.

This book does _not_ cover system architecture (the kind of decisions that separate microservices from monoliths, that choose between event sourcing and CRUD, that design APIs for ten years of use). Architecture has its own book in this series. The line is roughly: architecture decides the shape of the system; craft decides the shape of the lines inside the system. Both matter. They are different skills.

This book does _not_ cover SRE, observability, or production operations. Those also have their own book.

This book's examples and three protagonists work primarily in _server-side web_ , _B2B SaaS_ contexts: a CRM (Trellis), an AI-native devtools startup (Helix), a data platform (Currents). The disciplines transfer broadly — testing, refactoring, naming, ADRs, code review, version-control craft, AI direction — but their _concrete shape_ varies by domain. Mobile engineers will find that trunk-based development and feature flags need adjustment for App Store review cycles. Embedded engineers will find that hexagonal architecture is harder when the hardware is part of the system. ML platform engineers will find that the test pyramid bends differently when test data is the spec. Games developers will find that big rewrites _do_ sometimes ship (an engine rewrite between titles). The disciplines this book teaches still hold; they just look different in your context. Read with the disciplines in mind, not the specific SaaS stack — the SaaS stack is a vehicle, not the destination.

This book does _not_ prescribe one true methodology. Every chapter follows the **discipline-over-dogma** principle: the rule comes with its reasoning and its when-to-break-it clause. The senior engineer who knows when to skip TDD is more valuable than the engineer who applies it religiously, and the engineer who knows when to apply TDD is more valuable than the engineer who refuses it on principle. Both extremes are easy; the middle is craft.

### 1.6 A working engineer's three-year question

The single most useful framing in this book, applied to any technical decision you face, is the **three-year question** :

> What happens to this code in three years?

Three years is long enough that the original author may be gone, the original AI model is retired, the dependencies have shifted, and at least one major refactor has happened nearby. Three years is short enough that the code is probably still in production rather than retired.

Every chapter in this book applies the three-year question to its discipline. Chapter 5 asks: _what happens to this test suite in three years?_ Chapter 9 asks: _what happens to this refactored function in three years?_ Chapter 17 asks: _what happens to this README in three years?_ The discipline lives or dies by the answer.

Try it now. Open the last PR you merged. Ask: in three years, when someone debugs this code at 2am, will they thank you or curse you? The honest answer is the beginning of craft.

### 1.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A team lead at a startup says: "We don't have time for craft. We're pre-revenue, we're moving fast, we'll clean up later." The codebase is six months old, 80,000 lines, with 40% AI-generated code and 8% test coverage. Drawing on this chapter's argument, give the honest response. Why is "we'll clean up later" specifically dangerous in the 2026 context, more so than in 2016?

Hint

Consider what changed between 2016 and 2026 about the cost-of-writing curve, and how that interacts with the cost-of-maintenance curve.

**Answer:** "Clean up later" was always a dangerous claim, but in 2026 it is dangerous in a specific new way: the codebase has grown faster than human-led codebases used to grow. At 80,000 lines in six months, the team has effectively produced what a 2016 team might produce in 18-24 months, but they have not had 18-24 months to absorb the code into anyone's head. So the "later" cleanup is harder, not easier, because: (1) the codebase is large enough that no one holds it in their head; (2) much of it was AI-generated, so the local author may not remember why each part exists; (3) at 8% coverage, the safety net for refactoring is missing, so cleanup is dangerous. The 2016 version of "clean up later" might have worked because the codebase was small enough to triage. The 2026 version usually does not, because the codebase outran the team's ability to remember it.

The principled response is: the team should slow line production now, raise test coverage to at least 50% (Chapter 5), institute the code review discipline (Chapter 15), and apply the three-year question to every PR. They will produce fewer lines per week, but the lines will be lines they can still maintain in 2028.

**Q2.** Sort the following 2026 engineering activities into "compounds over a career" and "depreciates over a career." For each, give your reasoning.

(a) Memorizing the React 18 hooks API. (b) Writing characterization tests for a legacy module before refactoring it. (c) Naming a domain concept after a workshop with the product team. (d) Filling in a CRUD endpoint from a Linear ticket via Claude Code. (e) Writing an ADR for the choice of database driver. (f) Setting up a new repository's CI pipeline by copying from a template.

Hint

Apply the rule from §1.3: does the activity require context outside the local 500-line window?

**Answer:** (a) **Depreciates.** API memorization was always low-leverage; in 2026 the AI knows the API and you do not need to. Knowing that hooks _exist_ and what kinds of problems they solve is compounding; memorizing the exact signatures is not. (b) **Compounds.** Characterization testing requires holding the legacy system's behavior in your head, deciding which behaviors to lock in, and judging which are accidental versus essential. AI cannot do this without you. The skill is reusable across every legacy codebase you will ever touch. (c) **Compounds.** Domain naming requires understanding the business, the product, and the codebase simultaneously. It is the act that crystallizes design (Chapter 18). AI cannot do this from inside the codebase alone. (d) **Depreciates.** The CRUD endpoint is precisely the kind of work that AI does well in 2026. Doing it by hand is fine when you start, but spending career time getting fast at it is wasted time; AI will be faster. (e) **Compounds.** ADRs encode design judgment for future maintainers (Chapter 17). The skill of writing an ADR that ages well requires knowing what will and will not matter in three years; that judgment compounds. (f) **Depreciates.** Template-based pipeline setup is automatable and increasingly automated. Knowing what a good pipeline contains is compounding; knowing the exact YAML incantation is not.

**Q3.** The chapter claims that "the code persists; the model that wrote it does not." What is the operational implication for a team that uses AI heavily today? Name three concrete practices the team should adopt now, before model transitions force them to.

Hint

Think about what a human-maintainable codebase looks like in §1.4, Part 3, and reason backward to the practices that produce it.

**Answer:** Three concrete practices:

  1. **Treat AI-generated code as a draft, not a deliverable.** Every AI-generated PR should go through human review with the explicit question: "If the model that wrote this is gone in two years, can a human maintain this from the code alone?" If the answer is no, the code must be revised before merge.
  2. **Write ADRs for non-obvious decisions made during AI sessions.** When an AI conversation produces a decision (which library to use, which pattern to apply, why this abstraction), capture the _reasoning_ in an ADR (Chapter 17). The conversation history will be lost; the ADR persists.
  3. **Maintain a CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md file at the project root** (Chapter 22) that captures the codebase's conventions, anti-patterns to avoid, and design principles. This file serves both future AI sessions and future humans; it is the codebase's institutional memory.

**Q4.** A junior engineer asks: "If AI is so good at writing code, why should I bother learning to code well? Shouldn't I just learn to prompt well?" Give a response that is honest about both halves of the question.

Hint

Refer to the second common misconception in §1.3.

**Answer:** Prompting well is necessary but not sufficient. Prompting produces a first draft; the rest of the engineer's job is reviewing, integrating, and maintaining that draft. You cannot review code you cannot read. You cannot integrate code into a system you do not understand. You cannot maintain code whose patterns are invisible to you. So learning to code well is the foundation on which prompting well is built, not the alternative to it.

The honest version of the junior's question is: "How should I split my learning time between coding skills and AI-collaboration skills?" The answer, in 2026, is something like 60% coding skills and 40% AI-collaboration skills for the first two years of a career, then gradually rotating toward 40/60 as the coding skills become automatic and the AI-collaboration skills become the differentiator.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Take a PR you have merged in the last two weeks. Apply the three-year question (§1.6) to each substantive change. For each change, predict whether a maintainer in 2029 will thank you or curse you, and explain why. If you do not have a recent PR, use the following synthetic one: _a 300-line PR that adds a new internal API endpoint, includes a single happy-path integration test, and uses a new utility library`nano-validator` introduced in this PR for the first time_.

Hint

For each change, list what the 2029 maintainer will know and not know. The gap is the curse.

**Answer:** For the synthetic PR, the 2029 maintainer will not know: (a) why `nano-validator` was chosen over the validator already in the codebase (if there was one), (b) whether the happy-path test was the only path tested because the others were impossible or because we did not bother, (c) whether the new endpoint duplicates existing functionality. They will curse you for these gaps.

What would earn thanks: (a) an ADR (Chapter 17) for the `nano-validator` choice explaining the alternatives considered, (b) explicit tests for the unhappy paths or an explicit comment that no unhappy paths exist for this endpoint, (c) a one-line PR description noting which existing endpoint (if any) this resembles and why a new one was needed. None of this takes more than fifteen minutes today; all of it saves hours in 2029.

The application of the three-year question to your own recent PR follows the same pattern: identify the gaps that the 2029 maintainer will have, then ask which of those gaps a fifteen-minute investment now can close.

**Q6.** Reframe your team's last sprint retrospective in 2026-equation language. List the sprint's activities and estimate the percentage of time spent on (a) writing new code, (b) reviewing code (yours and others'), (c) maintaining existing code (debugging, refactoring, dependency updates, on-call). If the split differs significantly from the 20/40/40 ratio sketched in §1.4, what does that tell you?

Hint

A 60% writing split in 2026 is a signal worth investigating; a 20% reviewing split is a different signal.

**Answer:** This is a self-reflection exercise; the answer depends on the team. A high writing percentage (above 40%) in a 2026 team suggests one of three things: (a) the team is in a greenfield phase where most of the codebase does not yet exist, (b) the team is not yet using AI effectively (so writing is still slow), or (c) the team is producing code faster than it can review or maintain, which is the dangerous pattern from Q1. A high reviewing percentage (above 50%) suggests heavy AI use without yet having calibrated review depth; this is the _learning_ phase and is healthy if temporary. A high maintenance percentage (above 60%) suggests technical debt has accumulated and is now consuming team velocity (Chapter 19); the team needs a debt-reduction sprint.

The exercise is not to hit 20/40/40 exactly. It is to know which split you are running, and to know whether that split is appropriate to your codebase's stage.

**Q7.** Choose a function in your codebase that was written with significant AI assistance. Read it line by line and answer: which of the three jobs from §1.2 (reviewing for fit, integrating across boundaries, maintaining over years) would benefit most from a human-only rewrite of this function? Where did the AI do well, and where did the AI miss?

Hint

Look for inconsistencies with surrounding code style, hallucinated or misused library APIs, missing edge case handling, or over-abstraction. These are the canonical AI-generated-code defects (Chapter 15, Thread C).

**Answer:** This is an open exercise; the answer depends on the function. A common finding: the AI did well at the local logic and the happy-path tests, did poorly at consistency with surrounding style (used different naming conventions, imported a different validator than the rest of the codebase uses, structured error handling differently from neighboring modules). The fix is usually a small refactor for consistency rather than a rewrite. The lesson, however, is that the inconsistency cost real review time and real cognitive load for the next maintainer; the AI's "win" of writing fast was partially eaten by the review.

The Thread C lies from the syllabus (lies #1, #3, #4, #5) are the usual suspects: untested edge cases, inconsistent style, missing error handling, off-by-one near boundaries. Note which lies your specific function exhibits; you will see the same lies in Chapter 15 with concrete review guidance.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute the following position: _AI is a craft amplifier. Engineers with strong craft skills get dramatically better with AI; engineers with weak craft skills get faster at producing weak code. The result is widening rather than narrowing skill gaps in the profession._

**Discussion:** A defense of the position argues that craft skill (reviewing, naming, refactoring, architectural taste) is precisely the kind of skill AI does not yet replicate. An engineer who reviews well multiplies their effective output via AI; an engineer who reviews poorly multiplies their _bad_ output via AI. So the gap between the top quartile and the bottom quartile widens, not narrows. Evidence supporting the position: 2025-2026 reports from large engineering organizations showing top performers' productivity increasing 2-3× with AI, while bottom performers' productivity stays flat or declines.

A refutation argues that AI is a leveler: junior engineers now have access to senior-quality first drafts, instant explanations of unfamiliar code, and an always-available pair-programming partner. This narrows rather than widens the gap, because the floor has risen faster than the ceiling. Evidence supporting the refutation: rapid skill acquisition by junior engineers in 2024-2025 cohorts, and the rise of "AI-native" engineers who reach senior productivity in two years rather than five.

The synthesis (and the more useful answer) is that both effects are happening simultaneously. AI raises the floor (junior engineers get better fast) _and_ raises the ceiling (senior engineers with good craft compound faster). The gap may not widen or narrow uniformly; it may bifurcate. The middle of the distribution thins as engineers either invest in compounding skills or fall behind. This is consistent with the _barbell_ hypothesis in §1.4 Part 2.

The right follow-up question for any working engineer: which side of the bifurcation are you investing in?

**Q9.** Consider the analogy: in 1880, learning to read a map and navigate by stars was a craft skill; in 2026, almost no one can navigate by stars, and GPS handles the work. Was this a craft loss or a craft transition? Apply the analogy to programming in 2026 and 2030. Where does it hold and where does it break?

**Discussion:** The analogy holds in one important way: the _bottleneck_ moved. Navigation by stars was the bottleneck of nautical travel in 1880; by 2026 the bottleneck is fuel logistics, route planning, weather modeling, and traffic management. Star-navigation skills were not destroyed; they became niche. So a working "navigator" in 2026 still exists, but the job is different.

The analogy breaks in one important way: GPS is a stable, well-understood tool with a predictable failure mode (loss of signal). AI in 2026 is a fast-evolving tool with unpredictable failure modes (hallucination, over-abstraction, plausibility without correctness). A navigator who trusts GPS blindly fails only when the signal drops; an engineer who trusts AI blindly fails whenever the AI is confidently wrong, which is far more often. So the analogy under-predicts the value of craft in 2026, because AI failures are more frequent and more subtle than GPS failures.

The forward-looking question: as AI becomes more reliable (as GPS did), will craft become less necessary? The honest answer is _partially yes, partially no_. The "reviewing for fit" and "integrating across boundaries" jobs are likely to remain human for the rest of this decade, because they require system-wide context that no current AI can hold. The "writing first drafts" job is increasingly AI's. The barbell deepens.

### Chapter Summary

The 2026 craft equation: AI has collapsed the cost of writing code, but the cost of reviewing, integrating, and maintaining code is unchanged. Craft, in 2026, is the set of disciplines that minimize the cost of the second category. The bottleneck moved from typing to reviewing; the disciplines that compound moved with it. **Code is a liability; the asset is what it does for users.**

Three jobs the AI cannot do well (and which therefore define craft work in 2026): reviewing for fit, integrating across boundaries, maintaining over years. Each requires context outside the local 500-line window. Each compounds over a career. Chapter 2 takes the maintenance claim seriously and operationalizes it as a reading discipline: 90% of an engineer's career is reading code, not writing it, and reading is the skill the next 22 chapters depend on. Later, Chapter 19 will quantify the cost of failing this discipline through the lens of technical debt, and Chapter 23 will synthesize the seven compounding disciplines this book covers into a working engineer's mental model.

The three-year question is the chapter's portable tool: _in three years, when someone debugs this code at 2am, will they thank you or curse you?_ Every later chapter applies it to its discipline. If you remember nothing else from this chapter, remember the three-year question and the equation that produced it. The rest of the book teaches the disciplines that make the answer "thank you."

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Apply the three-year question to your last merged PR.** Open it now. For each substantive change, predict whether the 2029 maintainer will thank you or curse you. The gaps are the disciplines worth investing in next.
  2. **Sort one of your weekly activities into the compounding column or the depreciating column.** If it lives on the right side (typing boilerplate, memorizing syntax, filling CRUD templates), let AI take it and invest the freed time in the left side (reading old code, naming, ADRs, code review).
  3. **Treat every line of code as a liability, not an asset.** When the agent suggests adding 200 lines and the function only needs 20, the right answer is the smaller code, not the more impressive diff. The asset is what users get; the lines are what you maintain.

---

## Chapter 2: The Code You'll Maintain — Reading Old Code as the First Skill

In the autumn of 2025, Asher Goldman joined Trellis as a Staff Engineer. Trellis is a CRM SaaS company eight years into its codebase, two hundred engineers, four million lines of TypeScript across one Node monolith and seventeen satellite services. On day three of his onboarding, his manager asked him to investigate a recurring bug in the lead-scoring service. The bug surfaced once a week, always on Tuesday mornings, always for accounts created before 2021, always producing a score one decimal point off from the expected value.

Asher spent his first four days at Trellis reading code, not writing any. At every surprising line, he asked the same question, the one his old mentor had drilled into him until it was reflex: _what was the author worried about?_ He read the failing test (there was one, written in 2022, marked `.skip` since 2023 with the comment `// flaky, revisit when we migrate to v2`). He read the lead-scoring service's main flow. He read three years of `git log --follow` history on the offending file. He read the `git blame` for the magic constant `0.7` that appeared in line 142, and asked the question again. He read the migration record for the 2021 schema change that the bug correlated with. He never opened an AI assistant during these four days.

On day eight, he wrote a six-line fix and a five-line test. The PR was titled "Fix lead-scoring rounding for pre-2021 accounts." It was merged within two hours and the Tuesday-morning alert stopped. His manager asked him afterward: "How did you find that so fast?" Asher's answer, which became part of the team's onboarding lore, was: "I didn't write any code for a week. I just read."

Chapter 1 argued that maintenance dominates a working engineer's career in 2026. This chapter takes that claim seriously and asks: what is the _skill_ of reading code, and how do you get good at it?

### 2.1 The reading-to-writing ratio

A common claim in software engineering, often attributed to Robert C. Martin and earlier to Joshua Bloch, is that engineers spend ten times as long reading code as writing it. The ratio is hard to pin down precisely; Peter Hallam at Microsoft Research measured developer activities in the early 2000s and found reading-to-writing ratios in the range of 4:1 to 10:1 depending on the task; later Stack Overflow Developer Surveys and GitHub Octoverse reports have produced similar numbers under different methodologies, and the GitHub Copilot 2024 productivity study indirectly confirmed the pattern (developers were faster _after_ AI raised the writing-speed ceiling, suggesting writing was not the bottleneck). The point is not the exact number. The point is that the ratio is heavily weighted toward reading, and that almost no formal training treats reading as a skill in its own right.

You learned to write code in school, in bootcamp, in tutorials, in side projects. You learned the syntax of three languages, the structure of a function, the rules of inheritance and composition, the patterns of asynchronous code. You probably did not learn how to read 50,000 lines of unfamiliar code and have a useful mental model after one afternoon. That skill is acquired by practice and by accident, rarely by deliberate study.

The 2026 frame makes this gap urgent. AI assistants are now very fast at writing first drafts. They are slow and unreliable at reading large codebases with intent. The reading skill is what humans uniquely contribute when an AI presents a 1,200-line PR for review (Chapter 1, opening vignette). So reading is no longer a passive skill that you acquire incidentally; it is the central skill that justifies your salary in 2026.

#### Definition: Reading with intent

Reading with intent is the practice of approaching a piece of unfamiliar code with a specific question in mind, and adjusting your reading strategy to answer that question efficiently. **Reading with intent has a defined exit condition; reading without intent does not.**

In plain language, this means: you do not read a codebase like you read a novel. You read it like a detective reads a case file. You know what you are looking for, you know what counts as an answer, and you stop when you have the answer.

Asher's four-day investigation at Trellis was reading with intent. His question was: _what causes the score to be off by exactly 0.1 for pre-2021 accounts on Tuesdays?_ Every file he opened, every line of `git log` he read, every test he traced through was in service of that question. When he had the answer (a rounding step in a Tuesday-batch reconciliation job that used a constant calibrated for post-2021 schema), he stopped reading.

### 2.2 The reading order: tests, entry points, core models, details

When you arrive at an unfamiliar codebase with a question, what do you read first? The convention in this book, refined over many years of practice and supported by the structure of well-organized codebases, is the following order:

  1. **Tests first.** The tests tell you what the codebase claims to do. They are often more honest than the documentation. They reveal the boundaries the authors considered, the failure modes they care about, and (by omission) the scenarios they did not test.
  2. **Entry points next.** The entry points (CLI commands, HTTP handlers, message consumers, scheduled jobs) tell you how the codebase is used. They are the boundary between the system and the outside world. Reading them tells you what the system _does_.
  3. **Core models third.** The core models (domain entities, primary types, foundational interfaces) tell you how the codebase _thinks_. They are the vocabulary the rest of the code uses. Without them, every later file is a foreign language.
  4. **Details last.** The details (utility functions, parsers, formatters, helpers) are read only when a specific question routes you to them.

This order is the inverse of the order most engineers default to, which is to open the file the manager pointed at and start scrolling. Defaulting to scrolling produces shallow understanding; the test-entry-model-detail order produces deep understanding.

#### Why tests first?

A test is a self-contained unit of intent. It says: "Given this input and this state, when we run this code, we expect this output." Tests do three things that prose documentation rarely does:

* **They state what matters.** If a behavior is not tested, the team did not commit to it. Whether that is good or bad, it tells you something.
* **They state the inputs and outputs explicitly.** No ambiguity, no implicit context.
* **They are executable.** If you doubt the description, you can run it. Documentation does not run.

Reading tests first is also the fastest way to learn the vocabulary of the codebase. The test setup imports the core types. The test setup constructs the core objects. By the time you have read fifty test files, you have absorbed the codebase's domain language without trying. By the time you start reading production code, you already speak the language it speaks.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "tests are boilerplate, the real code is in the implementation." The mistake is the assumption that tests are _generated from_ the implementation, when in well-crafted codebases the relationship runs the other way: the tests are the specification and the implementation satisfies them. In a codebase where tests are boilerplate generated to inflate coverage (Chapter 5), this misconception is partially correct: those tests really are useless. In a codebase where tests are the specification (the codebase you want to be in), the misconception is harmful. Read the tests first and you will know which kind of codebase you are in by the end of your first afternoon.

### 2.3 `git blame` as historical document

Every line of code has a history. In a Git-managed codebase, that history is one command away. `git blame` is not a tool for finding someone to blame; it is a tool for reading the _intent_ behind a line, which is often invisible from the line itself.

Consider this real-shape example from a fictional Trellis file (Asher's day-five reading):
    
    
    // src/scoring/legacy-rounding.ts
    export function legacyRoundScore(score: number, accountCreatedAt: Date): number {
      if (accountCreatedAt < new Date('2021-03-15')) {
        return Math.round(score * 10) / 10 - 0.05;
      }
      return Math.round(score * 100) / 100;
    }
    

The function does two different things depending on a date. Why? The line itself does not say. The PR that introduced it might. The commit message that introduced it almost certainly does. The author who wrote it (now retired) certainly knew. `git blame` is the door to that knowledge:
    
    
    $ git blame -L 3,5 src/scoring/legacy-rounding.ts
    ^abc1234 (Priya Mehta 2021-03-22 14:02:11) if (accountCreatedAt < new Date('2021-03-15')) {
    ^abc1234 (Priya Mehta 2021-03-22 14:02:11)   return Math.round(score * 10) / 10 - 0.05;
    ^abc1234 (Priya Mehta 2021-03-22 14:02:11) }
    

The commit `abc1234`, from March 2021, by Priya Mehta. `git show abc1234` reveals the commit message:
    
    
    fix(scoring): preserve pre-migration rounding for legacy accounts
    
    Before the March 2021 schema migration, scores were stored as
    single-decimal floats with a 0.05 rounding bias from the v0
    spec. Post-migration scores are stored as double-decimal floats
    without the bias. Customer dashboards display historical scores,
    so the rounding must match the storage epoch.
    
    Refs: TRELLIS-1842
    

Suddenly the line makes sense. The bias was not a bug; it was the v0 spec. The 2021 migration changed the storage format but not the displayed history. The 0.05 subtraction reproduces the legacy display so that customer dashboards remain consistent over time.

Without `git blame`, the function looks like a bug. With `git blame`, it is a deliberate piece of backward compatibility that should not be touched without understanding the consequence.

#### The `git blame` discipline

The discipline is: **whenever a line of code surprises you, run`git blame` before forming a judgment.** "Surprise" includes:

* A magic constant with no comment.
* A conditional whose predicate is opaque.
* A function whose name does not match what it appears to do.
* A workaround that looks unnecessary.
* An apparent inconsistency with surrounding code.

In each case, the surprise is data. The data is asking you a question: _why is this here?_ `git blame` plus the commit message is often the answer. Sometimes it is not (the commit message is empty or unhelpful); then you escalate to `git log --all --full-history` on the file, or `git log -S "specific string"` to find when the string was introduced.

### 2.4 Code archaeology: the patterns

When you read old code with sufficient practice, certain patterns recur. Recognizing them shortens your reading time because you know what kind of artifact you are looking at.

#### The dead conditional

A conditional whose predicate can no longer be true. Often introduced for a feature that was rolled back, a migration that completed, or a vendor that no longer exists. The branch is still there, the test (if any) is still there, and removing it is a yak-shave that no one has gotten around to.

Diagnostic: instrument the predicate for a week and observe; if the branch is never taken, it is dead. Remove with confidence after one production cycle.

#### The load-bearing comment

A comment that warns against doing something obvious, with a reason that is easy to forget. For example:
    
    
    # DO NOT use a HashMap here — order matters for downstream consumers
    # (see incident-2023-09-14)
    ordered_events = []
    

The comment is load-bearing because removing the comment without removing the constraint leads to a recurrence of incident-2023-09-14. Some teams add an automated test for the constraint; many do not. The comment is the only protection. Treat it as such.

#### The migration scar

A piece of code that was once two pieces of code, merged during a migration, and now bears the marks of both ancestors. Symptoms: a function that takes two arguments of the same type with different validation logic on each, a class with two `__init__` paths, a Bash script with a `case` statement covering "old format" and "new format" three years after everyone supposedly moved off the old format.

Migration scars often look like over-engineering to a new reader. They are usually not over-engineering; they are the surface of a completed migration whose old format still lives in someone's backup, or whose old API is still called by an undocumented downstream consumer. Touch with care.

#### The author's worry

A piece of code that does more than it strictly needs to, with elaborate guards, defensive nulls, redundant validation. Diagnostic question: _what was the author worried about?_ The answer is usually a real production incident or a class of bug they had been bitten by. `git log` will usually confirm or deny within five minutes.

If the author's worry is still valid, leave the code in place. If the worry is no longer valid (the upstream service no longer returns null, the validation is now done at the boundary, the bug class has been eliminated by a type system upgrade), the defensive code is now dead weight and can be removed. The point is to _check_ rather than to assume.

### 2.5 Building a mental model

The goal of reading is not to remember the code; it is to build a mental model that lets you reason about the code without having it open. A working engineer at Trellis with three years of tenure can answer questions like "If we change the lead-scoring threshold from 0.7 to 0.8, what breaks?" without opening a file, because the model lives in their head.

How do you build the mental model deliberately rather than by accident?

#### The three-pass technique

For a new codebase, three reading passes produce a usable mental model:

  1. **The breadth pass.** Read every directory name and every top-level file name. Goal: know what _exists_. Time: 30 minutes to 2 hours depending on size. Output: a one-page sketch of the codebase's territory.
  2. **The depth pass.** Pick three or four core modules (chosen by reading the top-level entry points) and read each in full. Goal: know how the _core_ works. Time: 1-3 days. Output: a few diagrams in your notebook.
  3. **The query-driven pass.** For each subsequent question you face, read whatever the question routes you to. Goal: extend the model as needed. Time: ongoing. Output: increasingly broad coverage.

Most engineers skip directly to pass 3 because the manager hands them a ticket and asks them to fix the bug. The result is a mental model with deep coverage of a few unrelated files and no skeleton connecting them. With passes 1 and 2 first, every later query slots into a frame.

#### The notebook discipline

Keep a notebook (paper, Markdown file, Obsidian vault, whatever fits your habits) for each codebase you maintain. Record:

* **The vocabulary.** What does the codebase call its core concepts? Are "Lead" and "Account" different types? Is there a "Customer" anywhere? Vocabulary inconsistencies are _bugs_ in the team's shared mental model; surface them in code review (Chapter 15).
* **The puzzles.** Lines or patterns you do not yet understand. Revisit them when you learn more. After three months, an empty puzzle list means you have a complete model (or you have stopped paying attention; verify which).
* **The land mines.** Code that looks innocuous but that you now know has hidden consequences. The `legacyRoundScore` function from §2.3 is a land mine. Document them so that the next engineer (and your future self) avoids stepping on them.

The notebook is the externalized half of your mental model. The internal half is in your head; the external half compounds across colleagues, across hires, across job changes.

### 2.6 Reading AI-generated code

In 2026, a significant fraction of the code you read was generated by AI. Reading AI-generated code is a different skill from reading human-written code, and the differences are worth naming.

#### What is the same

The reading order is the same. Tests first, entry points next, core models third, details last. Even if every line was generated by an agent, the structural reading order still applies.

`git blame` still helps, with one caveat. If the commit was created by an agent in agent-PR mode (Chapter 22), the _author_ in `git blame` may be the agent's service account. The commit message may be terse or templated. Some teams configure their agents to include a "Co-authored-by: human reviewer" trailer, which restores partial intent. Others do not, and the blame is hollow.

#### What is different

AI-generated code has a characteristic _texture_ that human code rarely has:

* **Local correctness, global inconsistency.** The function does what the docstring says, but it imports a different utility than the rest of the codebase uses for the same purpose.
* **Plausible names.** The names sound right (`processOrder`, `validateInput`, `computeMetrics`) but they are sometimes too generic, hiding the actual domain concept the codebase uses elsewhere.
* **Over-explanatory comments.** Comments that restate what the code says, rather than explaining why.
* **Defensive code for impossible cases.** Null checks for inputs that the type system guarantees are non-null, retry logic for synchronous local calls.
* **Confident-looking error handling that swallows errors.** `try { ... } catch (e) { console.error(e); return null; }` is a Thread C (Chapter 15) red flag.

Reading AI-generated code requires _more_ skepticism than reading human-generated code, not less. Human code is often wrong about the requirement; AI code is often wrong about the codebase context. Both need careful reading. The difference is the failure mode.

### 2.7 When to stop reading and start writing

A reading session that never converts to a writing session is procrastination dressed up as diligence. Reading has a job: produce a clear mental model that justifies a specific writing action.

The signal that you have read enough is one of these three:

  1. **You can predict the failure mode.** You can say "this code will fail if X happens, because of the line at file Y." If you can predict it, you have understood it.
  2. **You can describe the change in one sentence.** Not "make the lead-scoring better" but "subtract 0.05 from pre-2021 accounts in the Tuesday batch reconciliation, conditional on the migration epoch." If the description is one sentence and concrete, you have understood the problem.
  3. **You can write the test before the fix.** If you can write the test that would have failed before your fix and that will pass after your fix, you understand the change well enough to make it.

Asher's day-eight test (six lines) and fix (five lines) were the product of seven days of reading. The seven days are not lost time; they are the only reason the six-line fix was correct.

* * *

**Thread B —`calculateRefund`, stage 1: first draft**

A function we will follow through this book lives in the Helix billing service. Its first draft, written in 2025 in a hurry by a junior engineer between sprints, looked like this:
    
    
    def calculate_refund(order_id):
        order = db.query("SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = %s", (order_id,))[0]
        amount = order['total']  # full refund, the only case we have today
        db.execute("INSERT INTO refunds (order_id, amount) VALUES (%s, %s)",
                   (order_id, amount))
        return {'order_id': order_id, 'amount': amount, 'status': 'OK'}
    

Fifty-five lines if you include the surrounding helpers (omitted here). One function. No tests. No types. The status field is a string, not an enum; the amount comes from a raw row dict; the database call and the business logic and the result construction are interleaved. The engineer who wrote it knew it was rough; they shipped it because the deadline was tighter than the polish.

Reading this first draft now, the reader can apply this chapter's archaeology patterns: there is no load-bearing comment (the engineer had not yet been bitten); there is no migration scar (the function is too new to have one); there is one author's worry, namely the `[0]` index, which will throw if the order does not exist. The author worried about the right thing but did not yet have the vocabulary to make the worry explicit.

We will return to `calculate_refund` four more times: after its first review (Chapter 15) catches what the author missed; after its first bug (Chapter 19) reveals a concurrency edge case the staging environment never produced; after its first refactor (Chapter 10) splits it into testable seams; and five years later (Chapter 23) when the disciplines of this book have compounded across the function's lifetime.

* * *

### 2.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Defend or refute the claim: "Reading old code is for senior engineers; junior engineers should focus on writing." Use the §2.1 reading-to-writing ratio as evidence.

Hint

Consider what the 4:1 to 20:1 ratio implies about how a junior engineer's time actually splits.

**Answer:** The claim is wrong. The 4:1 to 20:1 reading-to-writing ratio applies to engineers at every level. A junior who focuses only on writing is optimizing for 5-20% of the actual job. The first year of a junior engineer's career is dominated by reading (onboarding, understanding the existing codebase, learning the team's conventions), and so reading skills compound earlier and faster for juniors than for seniors. The defensible version of the claim is: "Reading old code well is _harder_ to teach, so seniors learn it by accident over many years." The fix is to teach reading deliberately from day one, as this chapter argues.

**Q2.** A teammate proposes that the codebase delete all tests from before 2022 because "they don't reflect the current architecture." Apply the test-first reading discipline (§2.2) to this proposal. What is your response, and what would you ask the teammate to do before merging the deletion PR?

Hint

What information is in old tests that is not anywhere else?

**Answer:** Old tests often encode _historical contracts_ that the current architecture still satisfies, even if the test names and structures look outdated. Deleting them removes the only documentation of why certain behaviors exist. The response: do not merge yet. Ask the teammate to (a) identify which of the old tests are passing currently, (b) for the passing ones, decide whether the behavior they test is still part of the contract (if so, keep the test or rewrite it in current style; if not, document the deprecation and then delete), (c) for the failing tests, investigate why they are failing. A failing test that has been failing since 2023 is also data: either the behavior was deliberately changed (in which case the test should have been updated or deleted then) or the test has a real bug that everyone has been ignoring.

The general principle: old tests are historical documents (§2.3). Treat their deletion as you would treat the deletion of any historical document, which is to say carefully and with intent.

**Q3.** You inherit a function called `processData(data)` with no documentation, no tests, and a body of 200 lines. List the first five things you would do to understand it, in order, and explain why this order.

Hint

Apply the §2.2 reading order to a single function rather than a whole codebase.

**Answer:**

  1. **Read the function's callers.** `grep -r "processData(" .` reveals where the function is invoked. The callers are the function's "entry points" in microcosm; they reveal what the function is expected to do.
  2. **Run`git blame` on the function header.** Find the original commit and read the commit message. The message often explains the original intent better than any code.
  3. **Read the function's tests, even if they are in a different file.** Search for `describe('processData')` or equivalent. If none exist, note this as a debt to fix.
  4. **Read the input type and output type.** What does `data` look like? What is returned? Even if untyped, the function probably accesses specific fields; those are the de facto schema.
  5. **Read the body, top to bottom, with the §2.4 archaeology patterns in mind.** Look for dead conditionals, load-bearing comments, migration scars. By the time you reach the bottom, you should be able to write a one-sentence description of what the function does.

The order matters because each step constrains the next. The callers tell you the _expected_ behavior; the tests confirm or deny; the types narrow the input space; the body fills in the mechanics. Reading the body first (the default) means you do not know what to look for, so you read every line equally, which is slow and shallow.

**Q4.** Identify a load-bearing comment in a codebase you maintain (or the synthetic example below). Explain why it is load-bearing and what would happen if you ignored it.
    
    
    # Do NOT call this function in a loop — the underlying RPC has
    # unbounded retry and will tarpit the upstream service.
    def fetch_user_profile(user_id: str) -> UserProfile: ...
    

Hint

What constraint is the comment protecting, and what is the cost of violating it?

**Answer:** The comment is load-bearing because it encodes a _non-functional_ property of the function (retry behavior of an underlying RPC) that is invisible from the function's signature or body. Ignoring it: someone writes a list comprehension like `[fetch_user_profile(uid) for uid in user_ids]` for 1,000 users. Each call retries with exponential backoff on failure; failures cascade because the upstream is now overloaded by 1,000 simultaneous request-storms; the upstream goes down; an incident is declared.

The fix is to add a load test or a linter rule that catches the loop pattern, so that the constraint is enforced by code rather than by comment. But until then, the comment is the only line of defense, and removing it (because "comments rot") is dangerous. Treat the comment as code; review it as code; protect it as code.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Take a 300-1000 line file from a real codebase (or use the description below if you do not have one available). Apply the §2.5 three-pass technique, but apply it to the single file rather than the whole codebase. Write your one-page sketch, your two-or-three diagrams, and your puzzle list. How long did it take? What did you learn that you would not have learned by scrolling through?

Synthetic file: a 500-line TypeScript file `src/billing/refund-calculator.ts` containing one main exported function `calculateRefund(order, refundRequest, customer)` and twelve internal helpers, with no tests in the same directory.

Hint

For a single file, the "breadth pass" is reading every function signature and the top of every function body. The "depth pass" is reading the main exported function in full.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise; the answer depends on the file. For the synthetic file, a likely sketch would identify: the public entry (`calculateRefund`), the internal helpers grouped by purpose (validation helpers, calculation helpers, formatting helpers), the data shape implied by the function signature (`order`, `refundRequest`, `customer`). The diagram would show the call graph: `calculateRefund` calls perhaps three top-level helpers, each of which calls two or three lower-level helpers. The puzzle list would include: any helper whose purpose is not clear from name and signature, any constant that looks like a magic number, any conditional that branches on something opaque.

The learning compared to scrolling: scrolling produces familiarity with the _surface_ of the file; the three-pass technique produces understanding of the _structure_. Scrolling can be done while distracted; the three-pass technique cannot. Time investment is similar (perhaps 30-60 minutes for a 500-line file); the output is qualitatively different.

**Q6.** Choose a function in your codebase that was significantly authored by an AI assistant. Read it using the §2.6 AI-generated-code lens. Identify at least two of the following AI-texture markers if present: local correctness with global inconsistency, plausible-but-generic names, over-explanatory comments, defensive code for impossible cases, error-swallowing handlers. For each marker found, propose a fix.

Hint

Compare the AI-generated function against neighboring functions in the same file for naming and import conventions.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common finding pattern: the AI-generated function uses `formatDate(d)` while the codebase elsewhere uses `dateUtils.format(d)`; the AI-generated function has a comment `// Validates the input` above a line that is obviously a validation; the AI-generated function has a null check on a parameter that the type system declares non-null. Fixes: replace the imported helper with the codebase's existing one (consistency); delete the redundant comment (it adds noise without information); remove the null check (it is dead code).

The deeper lesson: each fix is a five-second change; together they signal that the AI was inserted into the codebase without conforming to it. This signal predicts future friction; institutionalize the lesson via a CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md file (Chapter 22) that captures the codebase's conventions for future AI sessions.

**Q7.** You join a team and need to understand a service that you have never seen before. The service is 50,000 lines, 200 tests, six developers contributed over four years. You have one week before you are expected to fix bugs in it. Plan your reading. Allocate days to passes 1, 2, and 3. State what you would skip if you only had three days.

Hint

Allocate Day 1 to breadth, Days 2-3 to depth on the core modules identified in Day 1, Days 4-7 to query-driven reading on whatever surfaces during onboarding tickets.

**Answer:** A reasonable seven-day plan:

* **Day 1** : Breadth pass. Read every directory and top-level file. Read the README. List all entry points (HTTP routes, CLI commands, scheduled jobs, message consumers). Produce a one-page sketch.
* **Day 2** : Read all 200 tests at a fast pace (probably 4-6 seconds per test on average). Note which behaviors are tested, which entry points have no tests, what the test vocabulary is. The goal is absorbing the domain language.
* **Days 3-4** : Depth pass. Pick the three core modules (chosen as the ones with the most callers, identified via grep). Read each in full. Build a call graph in your notebook.
* **Days 5-7** : Pick three of the existing bugs or tickets and trace each through. Each trace extends your model.

If only three days: keep Day 1 (breadth) and Day 2 (tests) intact, and compress depth to one core module on Day 3. Skip the bug traces. The result is a thinner model but the reading order is preserved; a thin model is still better than a deep-but-disconnected model.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue both sides: (a) "AI assistants should be allowed to read the codebase on the developer's behalf during onboarding, so that the developer does not need to spend a week reading." (b) "AI-summarized onboarding produces engineers who cannot maintain the codebase, because the mental model lives in the AI rather than in the engineer's head." Which side has more weight in 2026, and why?

**Discussion:** The pro-AI-summary argument: reading 50,000 lines is slow; an AI can produce a summary in minutes. The engineer gets faster onboarding, the team gets faster productivity, the codebase gets the bug fixes sooner. As of 2026, AI summarization quality is high enough to produce useful high-level overviews.

The con argument: a summary is not a mental model. When a 2am bug surfaces and the AI assistant is unavailable (rate limited, model deprecated, internet down), the engineer with only an AI-summarized model has no model of their own to reason from. The week of human reading produces a model that lives in the engineer's head and that the engineer can extend without further AI assistance. The summary produces a model that lives in the AI's context window and that evaporates when the session ends.

The 2026-honest position: use AI summaries as a _supplement_ to human reading, not as a replacement. Start with the AI summary as the breadth pass (Day 1); follow with human reading for the depth pass (Days 2-4). The AI accelerates pass 1; the human is irreplaceable for pass 2. Treating the AI summary as the whole onboarding is the same mistake as treating the AI's first-draft PR as the whole engineering task (Chapter 1, §1.4 Part 3): the model that wrote the summary will be gone in two years, but the engineer's mental model persists.

**Q9.** What disciplines from this chapter compound the most over a 10-year career? Rank the chapter's claims (reading order, `git blame` discipline, archaeology pattern recognition, three-pass technique, notebook discipline, AI-text recognition, stop-reading signal) by compounding rate, and defend your ranking.

**Discussion:** A defensible ranking:

  1. **Archaeology pattern recognition.** Patterns transfer across codebases; an engineer who recognizes load-bearing comments and migration scars in one codebase recognizes them in every codebase they touch. Highest transfer rate.
  2. **The notebook discipline.** Compounds because the notebook itself accumulates across codebases. An engineer with five years of notebooks has a personal reference library.
  3. **The three-pass technique.** Compounds because it makes every new codebase faster than the last; the technique itself does not change, but your speed at executing it does.
  4. **Reading order (tests first).** Compounds, but the gains plateau after a few years because the order is a small fixed habit.
  5. **`git blame` discipline.** Compounds, but the gain is per-codebase rather than per-career; in a codebase with empty commit messages, the discipline has nothing to feed on.
  6. **Stop-reading signal.** Compounds slowly; it is a calibration skill, useful but not transformative.
  7. **AI-text recognition.** Compounds, but the texture of AI-generated code will _change_ as models evolve, so the specific markers will need to be re-learned. The meta-skill (looking for inconsistency with surrounding code) compounds more than the specific markers do.

The defense of #1: the patterns this chapter names (dead conditional, load-bearing comment, migration scar, author's worry) are genuinely universal. They appear in every codebase older than three years. An engineer who has internalized them reads every old codebase faster. Compare to a language-specific skill: knowing Java idioms helps in Java codebases, not in Python codebases. The archaeology patterns help everywhere.

### Chapter Summary

Reading is the dominant activity in a 2026 engineering career, and reading well is a skill that compounds over time. The chapter's central tools: read with intent (§2.1); follow the test-entry-model-detail order (§2.2); treat `git blame` as a historical document, not a finger-pointer (§2.3); recognize the archaeology patterns of dead conditionals, load-bearing comments, migration scars, and the author's worry (§2.4); build a mental model through three passes (breadth, depth, query-driven) and externalize half of it into a notebook (§2.5); apply extra skepticism to AI-generated code because of its characteristic local-correctness-global-inconsistency texture (§2.6); and stop reading when you can predict the failure, describe the change in one sentence, or write the test before the fix (§2.7).

The four-day reading sprint that produced Asher's six-line fix at Trellis is the chapter's working example. It compounds with Chapter 3 (local Git craft, which makes `git blame` and `git log` more powerful) and with Chapter 10 (refactoring under test, where reading the existing tests is the first move). In Chapter 15 it returns as the foundation of code review: a reviewer who cannot read well cannot review well. Reading is not glamorous, and AI cannot do it for you in 2026 in any deep sense. It is the foundation skill that the rest of this book builds on.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **The next time you encounter a surprising line of code, ask Asher's question before changing it** : _what was the author worried about?_ Run `git blame`, read the commit message, look for the incident the line is defending against. Then decide.
  2. **Read tests before reading implementation when entering an unfamiliar module.** Even if the tests are thin, they tell you what the team committed to. Implementation tells you only what the team wrote.
  3. **Start a notebook for the codebase you maintain.** Three sections: vocabulary, puzzles, land mines. Add to it weekly. After three months you have an externalized half of your mental model; after a year you have a personal reference library.

---

## Chapter 3: Local Git Craft

Two engineers ship the same feature. Engineer A merges a single commit titled "added stuff" containing 1,400 lines of mixed concerns. Engineer B merges seven commits with messages like "extract Permission type from User model", "add PermissionService with read-only methods", "wire PermissionService into AccessGuard", each fewer than 200 lines and individually buildable. Six months later, a production bug surfaces. The team uses `git bisect`. Engineer A's commit, when bisected, points to "added stuff" and the bisect output is useless. Engineer B's commits, when bisected, point to the exact 80-line commit that introduced the bug. The fix is identified in twenty minutes versus four hours.

This chapter argues that Git is not just a version control tool; it is a _thinking_ tool. The atomic-commit discipline, the choice between rebase and merge, the use of bisect as systematic debugging, the use of reflog as undo, and the use of interactive rebase to shape a coherent narrative: these are all craft skills, and they all compound. The next chapter takes these local skills to the team level. This one stays at the individual workstation.

### 3.1 The atomic commit discipline

The first principle of Git craft is the _atomic commit_. An atomic commit changes one thing, leaves the codebase in a buildable state, and has a message that explains the change.

#### Definition: Atomic Commit

An atomic commit satisfies three properties: (1) it makes a single conceptual change; (2) the tree at the commit builds and passes tests; (3) the commit message states what changed and why, in the imperative mood.

In plain language: if a teammate cherry-picks your commit onto a different branch, it should apply cleanly, build, pass tests, and read as a coherent unit of work. If any of those three fails, your commit is not atomic.

The discipline produces three downstream benefits:

* **Bisectability.** A bisect that lands on an atomic commit can pinpoint the bug to a small surface area. A bisect that lands on "added stuff" is useless.
* **Reviewability.** Each commit can be reviewed independently. A PR with seven atomic commits is seven small reviews, not one large review.
* **Revertability.** When something breaks, you revert one commit, not a heterogeneous bundle.

#### How atomic commits feel in practice

In practice, the discipline is uncomfortable at first because it conflicts with how most engineers work. The typical session is: open the IDE, start changing files, follow the train of thought wherever it leads, commit at the end with a vague message.
