The atomic-commit alternative is: change files, commit early, change more files, commit again, possibly amend or split commits with interactive rebase before pushing.

The mental shift is to treat the commit as a _publishing_ unit. Before you commit, ask: _does this commit make sense to a stranger reading the history?_ If the answer is no, you have not yet earned the commit. You may need to split (with `git add -p`), squash (with `git rebase -i`), or rewrite the message.

A common Trellis-style workflow that produces atomic commits naturally:
    
    
    # Start
    $ git checkout -b extract-permission-type
    
    # Make change 1: extract the type
    $ vim src/auth/types.ts
    $ git add -p src/auth/types.ts  # interactively stage only the lines for this commit
    $ git diff --cached  # review what's staged
    $ git commit -m "Extract Permission type from User model
    
    Permission was an inline union in User; lifting it makes it
    reusable by the upcoming PermissionService and shrinks User
    to its domain-essential fields. No behavior change."
    
    # Make change 2: build the service that uses the new type
    $ vim src/auth/permission-service.ts
    $ git add src/auth/permission-service.ts
    $ git commit -m "Add PermissionService with read-only methods
    
    First slice: read methods only. Write methods land in a separate
    PR. The service is constructor-injected so AccessGuard can
    substitute a fake in tests."
    
    # Make change 3: wire it in
    $ vim src/auth/access-guard.ts src/auth/__tests__/access-guard.test.ts
    $ git add src/auth/access-guard.ts src/auth/__tests__/access-guard.test.ts
    $ git commit -m "Wire PermissionService into AccessGuard
    
    AccessGuard now takes a PermissionService in its constructor.
    Tests updated to inject a fake. No production caller changes;
    the service is provided by the existing DI container."
    

Three commits, three concepts, three reviews. If a bug surfaces tomorrow in `AccessGuard`, the bisect lands on commit 3 and the surface is 80 lines, not 800.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "atomic commits slow me down." The mistake is conflating speed-of-typing with speed-of-shipping. Atomic commits add maybe ten minutes to a typical feature session. They save hours during the next bisect, during code review, during the inevitable revert. The investment is recovered the first time a teammate has to bisect through your history. After two such recoveries, the discipline pays for the rest of the year.

A second common misconception is "I'll just clean it up before I push." This sometimes works, but it requires you to remember the conceptual splits _after_ you have lost the cognitive context that produced them. Most engineers, when they try to clean up retroactively, end up with two commits: "WIP" and "more WIP". The discipline of committing as you go is the only reliable producer of atomic commits.

### 3.2 Rebase vs merge: an honest comparison

Few topics in Git generate more tribal argument than rebase versus merge. The honest answer is that both have their place; the dogma is wrong from either side. This section gives you the criteria for choosing.

#### What rebase does

`git rebase main` takes your current branch's commits and replays them on top of the latest `main`. The result is a _linear_ history. Your commits appear after the most recent `main` commit, as if you had started your branch from there.

Rebase rewrites commit hashes. If you have already pushed and a teammate has based work on your branch, rebasing will require them to handle the divergence (usually with `git pull --rebase` or a force-with-lease push). Rebase is therefore safe on your local branch before you push; less safe (but not forbidden) after you have pushed; dangerous on shared branches.

#### What merge does

`git merge main` creates a _merge commit_ that combines your branch's history with `main`'s history. The result is a _branching_ history that visibly preserves the parallel work. Hashes do not change. Anyone tracking your branch sees the same hashes after the merge as before.

Merge is always safe in the sense that it does not rewrite history. The cost is that the history is no longer linear; you see a graph of merges rather than a list of changes.

#### When to prefer rebase

* **Cleaning up your local branch before opening a PR.** This is the cheapest, safest use of rebase, and the highest-value one. Squash WIP commits, split mixed-concern commits, polish messages.
* **Keeping a long-lived feature branch up to date with`main`.** Rebasing onto `main` every day or two keeps the branch from drifting; conflicts are resolved in small chunks.
* **Maintaining a linear history on the receiving end.** Some teams require that all PRs land as rebased-and-fast-forwarded sequences, so that `main` is a single line. This produces clean `git log --oneline` and clean bisects.

#### When to prefer merge

* **Recording a real branch event.** If two streams of work genuinely happened in parallel and you want to preserve that fact (release branches, hotfix branches, fork integrations), merge is honest about the parallelism.
* **Sharing a branch with teammates who base work on it.** If others are committing to your branch, rebasing will rewrite their context. Merge keeps everyone's hashes stable.
* **Avoiding rebase conflict re-resolution.** A long-running branch rebased onto a fast-moving `main` may require resolving the same conflict multiple times (once per commit). A merge resolves the conflict once.

#### A practical rule

For most working engineers in most situations, the rule that produces the best results is:

  1. **On your local branch before pushing** : rebase freely. Use interactive rebase to make the history atomic.
  2. **After pushing a PR branch that no one else is using** : rebase or merge, your team's preference. Force-with-lease pushes are fine.
  3. **After pushing a branch that teammates have based work on** : merge, or coordinate explicitly before rebasing.
  4. **On`main` itself**: never rebase. Never force-push. This is the team's shared history; rewriting it is an incident.

The dogma that "rebase is evil" comes from category 4 abuse; the dogma that "merge produces dirty history" comes from category 1 laziness. Neither is the rule for the rest of the cases.

### 3.3 `git bisect` as systematic debugging

The story at the chapter opening is concrete. `git bisect` is binary search over history: you tell Git that the bug exists at commit X and did not exist at commit Y, and Git checks out the midpoint, asks you whether the bug is present, narrows the range, and repeats. The result is a logarithmic search: a 1,000-commit history yields the answer in about 10 steps.

#### The basic bisect workflow
    
    
    # Start the bisect with a known-bad and known-good commit
    $ git bisect start
    $ git bisect bad HEAD               # current commit has the bug
    $ git bisect good v2.4.0            # this tag did not
    
    # Git checks out the midpoint; test it
    $ npm test -- some-failing-test
    # tests pass:
    $ git bisect good
    # tests fail:
    $ git bisect bad
    
    # After log2(N) steps, Git tells you the first bad commit:
    # "abc1234 is the first bad commit"
    
    $ git bisect reset
    

The discipline is to have a _deterministic test_ for "the bug is present." A bug that manifests only in production every Tuesday is hard to bisect; a bug that fails a specific test is trivial. So the first move when you suspect a regression is to write the test that fails (Chapter 6, mutation testing, deepens this).

#### Automating with `git bisect run`

Once you have a deterministic test, you can fully automate the bisect:
    
    
    $ git bisect start
    $ git bisect bad HEAD
    $ git bisect good v2.4.0
    $ git bisect run npm test -- some-failing-test
    # walk away; Git binary-searches autonomously
    

Twenty minutes later, Git reports the first bad commit. Asher used this technique at Trellis to find a regression in lead-scoring that had been introduced six weeks earlier; the team had been blaming the wrong subsystem for the entire six weeks. One automated bisect closed the question in twenty minutes of wall-clock time and approximately zero minutes of engineer attention.

#### When bisect fails

Bisect can fail when:

* The commits in the range are not individually buildable (this is the _atomic commit_ argument again; non-atomic commits produce broken midpoints).
* The bug is non-deterministic (flaky tests, race conditions, time-of-day dependencies). Mitigation: run the test many times at each midpoint, or fix the flake first (Chapter 5).
* The bug is caused by a change _outside_ your repository (a dependency update, a database schema change, an external API change). Mitigation: bisect dependency versions separately.

When bisect fails for the second reason, the failure is itself informative: you have learned that the bug is non-deterministic, which narrows the suspect space considerably.

### 3.4 `git reflog` as undo

The reflog records every state your `HEAD` and your branches have been in, locally. Two retention windows matter, and the second one is the one that bites: `gc.reflogExpire` defaults to **90 days** for entries still reachable from a ref, while `gc.reflogExpireUnreachable` defaults to **30 days** for entries that have become unreachable (which is exactly what happens to commits orphaned by `reset --hard` or rebase). So the safety net for the operations this chapter is teaching you to recover from (destructive operations that produce unreachable commits) is the 30-day window, not the 90-day one. After 30 days plus a `git gc`, the orphans are gone. Recover early.

The canonical save:
    
    
    # I just did something terrible, like reset --hard to the wrong commit
    $ git reflog
    # shows last N states of HEAD with their hashes
    # d1f3a91 HEAD@{0}: reset --hard 9a7c2d3  <- the mistake
    # 4e2c8b5 HEAD@{1}: commit: WIP good stuff <- where I want to be
    # ...
    
    $ git reset --hard HEAD@{1}
    # recovered
    

The reflog is local to your machine; it does not sync to remotes. So a force-pushed branch you have lost on the remote can sometimes be recovered from your reflog if you have a recent local copy.

#### The reflog discipline

The discipline is: **before any destructive operation, consult the reflog or note the current commit.** A destructive operation includes `reset --hard`, `checkout` of a different branch with uncommitted changes, `rebase` that you might want to undo, and any `--force` push. Practically:
    
    
    # Before the dangerous operation, capture a label
    $ git log --oneline -n 1
    # abc1234 my current state
    
    # Do the dangerous thing
    $ git rebase -i HEAD~10
    
    # If it went wrong:
    $ git reset --hard abc1234
    

The label `abc1234` is just one hash; it costs nothing to copy; it saves you when you need it. With practice, you do this reflexively before any operation you have not done a thousand times.

### 3.5 Interactive rebase as storytelling

A push-ready PR branch should tell a coherent story. The story is sometimes the order in which the commits actually happened; more often, it is a _cleaned-up_ version where the dead ends, the WIPs, the typo-fix commits are absent.

Interactive rebase is the tool for the cleanup. The command `git rebase -i HEAD~10` opens an editor with the last 10 commits listed and a verb in front of each:
    
    
    pick a1b2c3d Extract Permission type
    pick d4e5f6a fix typo in Permission
    pick b7c8d9e Add PermissionService
    pick c1d2e3f WIP getting tests to pass
    pick f4a5b6c Wire PermissionService into AccessGuard
    pick g7h8i9j fix the actual bug from the WIP
    pick j0k1l2m address review comments
    

You change the verbs to reshape the history:

* `pick` keeps the commit as is.
* `reword` keeps the commit, opens its message for editing.
* `squash` merges the commit into the previous one, prompts for combined message.
* `fixup` like squash but discards the commit message.
* `edit` stops at the commit so you can amend it.
* `drop` removes the commit entirely.
* Move lines around to reorder commits.

The cleaned-up version of the above might be:
    
    
    pick a1b2c3d Extract Permission type
    fixup d4e5f6a   # typo fix folded silently into Extract
    pick b7c8d9e Add PermissionService
    fixup c1d2e3f   # WIP folded into PermissionService
    pick f4a5b6c Wire PermissionService into AccessGuard
    fixup g7h8i9j   # bug fix folded into Wire
    fixup j0k1l2m   # review comments folded into Wire
    

Three commits, three concepts, clean story. The story is what reviewers and future bisects see.

#### The storytelling principle

The principle is: **your published history is a finished essay, not a working draft.** Your reflog has the working draft; that is fine. Once you push, the published history is what your team reads. Treat it accordingly.

Some teams have a stronger version of this principle: every PR must be rebased and squash-merged so that `main` has one commit per PR. This is reasonable; it is also slightly hostile to bisecting (you lose the within-PR commits and only see PR-level granularity). Other teams preserve every PR commit; this is also reasonable. The team's choice should be explicit (Chapter 4); the individual's discipline within their own branch is universal.

### 3.6 The AI-Layer: directing the agent in Git

In 2026, an AI agent operating in agent-PR mode (Chapter 22) makes its own commits. The commits are sometimes atomic, often not. The commit messages are sometimes useful, often templated. The discipline of this chapter applies to agent commits as much as to your own.

#### The four AI Git habits worth establishing

  1. **Configure the agent's commit message template.** Most agent runtimes accept a system instruction that includes a commit-message format. Specify the imperative mood, a 50-character subject line, a body that explains why, and a footer with the relevant ticket ID. Without this, the agent defaults to a generic style that does not match your team.
  2. **Have the agent commit per atomic change, not at the end of the session.** Instruct the agent (in CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md, Chapter 22) to commit after each independently-buildable change rather than producing one large commit at the end. The agent will usually comply; the discipline must be specified.
  3. **Run`git diff` between the last human commit and the agent's HEAD before merging.** This is the _review for fit_ job from Chapter 1, §1.2. Even if you trust the agent on each individual commit, the _aggregate_ may produce a shape you did not intend. Skim the cumulative diff before merging.
  4. **Treat the agent's`git log` as something to clean up before pushing.** Use interactive rebase to reword vague messages, squash WIPs, drop dead-end attempts. The agent's working history is not your published history.

#### Catching Thread C lies via Git history

The agent's commit history is a useful surface for catching Thread C lies (Chapter 15):

* A flurry of small fixup commits in the same file (`fix off-by-one`, `fix import path`, `fix typo`) is a sign of Lie #5 (subtle boundary errors) or Lie #6 (hallucinated APIs); review extra carefully.
* A commit that touches many files for "consistency" without a clear purpose is a sign of Lie #2 (over-abstraction) or Lie #3 (inconsistent style).
* A commit message that says "improved error handling" without specifying which errors and how is a sign of Lie #4 (missing or wrong-granularity error handling).

Reading the agent's commit history is itself a craft skill, and it is the first review pass you do on agent work.

### 3.7 A worked example: bisecting a regression

The following bisect is a working example from a fictional Trellis incident, illustrative of the discipline.

The bug: as of last Tuesday, the lead-scoring API returns 500 errors for about 0.3% of requests, all for accounts in the EU region.

Step 1: write a failing test that reproduces the bug.
    
    
    // src/scoring/__tests__/regression.test.ts
    test('EU account scoring should not 500', async () => {
      const account = await createAccount({ region: 'EU', plan: 'enterprise' });
      const response = await api.score(account.id);
      expect(response.status).toBe(200);
      expect(response.body.score).toBeDefined();
    });
    

The test fails on `HEAD`.

Step 2: confirm the test passes on a known-good commit. The release tag from two months ago, `v2.18.0`, is suspected as the last good version.
    
    
    $ git checkout v2.18.0
    $ npm install
    $ npm test -- regression.test.ts
    # passes
    $ git checkout main
    

Step 3: bisect.
    
    
    $ git bisect start
    $ git bisect bad HEAD
    $ git bisect good v2.18.0
    $ git bisect run npm test -- regression.test.ts
    

Git binary-searches through approximately 240 commits in about 8 steps, each step taking 30-60 seconds. After 6 minutes, the output:
    
    
    abc1234 is the first bad commit
    commit abc1234
    Author: Priya Mehta
    Date: 6 weeks ago
        feat(scoring): use cached region-tax-rate for performance
    
        Cache region tax rates for 5 minutes. Reduces tax-table
        lookups by ~80% for batch scoring jobs.
    
        Refs: TRELLIS-2018
    

Step 4: read the commit. The cache key is by region; the cache invalidation runs on a 5-minute timer. The bug, on inspection: the EU tax-rate table was updated in a recent ETL job, and the cache holds stale rates for 5 minutes. For 0.3% of requests (those hitting the cache at the wrong moment), the stale rate causes a downstream multiplication overflow and a 500. Bisect found the introducer; reading the commit found the cause; the fix is a cache invalidation on tax-rate updates.

Total time: about twenty minutes of engineer time, six of which were the automated bisect itself. Compare to the "scroll through the codebase looking for what changed" alternative, which is hours of attention and a not-guaranteed result.

### 3.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A teammate's PR contains a single commit titled "fix payment bug + small refactor + extract helper" with 380 lines across 11 files, half of which are unit tests for a new util function unrelated to the payment fix. You are reviewing it. Three weeks from now a regression test fails and someone needs to bisect to find when it started failing. What specific problems will this commit's shape cause for the future bisect, and what would you ask the author to change before merging today?

Hint

Apply §3.1's three properties (single change, buildable, descriptive message) and §3.3's failure modes.

**Answer:** Three problems the bisect will hit when it lands on this commit:

  1. **Surface area is too large.** The bisect tells you "the bug started somewhere in these 380 lines across 11 files." That is no better than not bisecting; you still have to read 380 lines to find the actual cause. An atomic commit shrinks the surface to one conceptual change (usually 30–80 lines).
  2. **Mixed concerns confuse the diagnosis.** Bisect lands on a commit that does three things; the actual bug-causing change is one of them. You cannot tell which without reading the whole diff.
  3. **Revert is at the wrong granularity.** When the bisect finishes, "revert this commit" reverts the payment fix _and_ the refactor _and_ the helper. You may need to manually un-revert the parts you want to keep, which is slow and error-prone.

Ask the author to split: one commit for the payment fix (with a test of the bug), one commit for the small refactor, one commit for the extracted helper. Each commit should leave the tree buildable so future bisects do not have to skip midpoints. The split takes 10 minutes today; the bisect saves hours later, the first time it is needed.

**Q2.** A teammate proposes that "we should always squash all PR commits into one before merging, because main should have one commit per feature." Defend or refute the proposal using the §3.5 storytelling principle.

Hint

Think about who reads `main`'s history and for what purpose.

**Answer:** The proposal has some merit and some cost. The merit: a one-commit-per-PR `main` history is easy to scan with `git log --oneline`, and reverts at the PR granularity are simple. The cost: bisect granularity drops to the PR level. A 1,500-line PR squashed into one commit gives bisect a much larger surface area than the same 1,500 lines split into seven atomic commits.

A defensible compromise is the _merge-commit-with-preserved-history_ option: the PR is merged with a merge commit, but the individual commits within the PR are preserved on `main`. `git log --first-parent` then shows the PR-level history (one entry per merge); `git log` without that flag shows full granularity for bisect. This gives both views.

The strong version of the proposal (always squash) trades bisectability for tidiness. For a team that bisects often, this is a poor trade. For a team that never bisects (because the test suite catches regressions before merge, or because the codebase is small), it may be fine. The choice should be explicit; the dogma is not.

**Q3.** When is rebase the right choice and when is merge the right choice? Give a specific scenario for each.

Hint

§3.2's practical rule lists four cases. Pick one rebase case and one merge case.

**Answer:**

Rebase scenario: You are working on a feature branch alone; you have made eight commits, four of which are WIP or fixup; `main` has moved on by twelve commits since you branched. Before opening a PR, you rebase onto `main` and use interactive rebase to clean up your eight commits into three atomic commits with polished messages. Reviewer sees a clean three-commit PR on top of current `main`; bisects work cleanly; no merge commits clutter the history.

Merge scenario: Your team operates a release branch (`release-2.7`) that has diverged from `main` for two weeks; bug fixes have landed on both branches. You merge `main` into `release-2.7` to bring the release in line with the latest fixes, producing a merge commit. The merge commit honestly records that the two streams of work were parallel and have been reconciled; rebasing `release-2.7` onto `main` would rewrite hashes that other engineers have based work on and would break their environments.

**Q4.** Three scenarios. For each, say whether `git reflog` will save you, and if so what command sequence to run:

(a) Forty minutes ago you ran `git reset --hard HEAD~5` to throw away some experimental work, then realized one of the commits you discarded had a fix you actually wanted. No `git gc` has run since.

(b) Yesterday you force-pushed your `feature/x` branch to overwrite a teammate's force-push of the same branch (you didn't realize they had work there). Their work is no longer on the remote, and your local never had it.

(c) An hour ago you saved a file with extensive edits but had not yet run `git add`. You then ran `git checkout main` and lost the edits to the working-tree restore.

Hint

The reflog is _your local_ HEAD history, not a remote backup or a working-tree journal.

**Answer:**

(a) **Yes, reflog saves you.** Run `git reflog`, find the HEAD@{N} just before the `reset --hard`, then `git reset --hard HEAD@{N}` (or cherry-pick the specific commit you want back). The orphaned commits are still in the object store; the reflog points to them. You have 30 days before `gc.reflogExpireUnreachable` cleans them up.

(b) **No, your reflog cannot help.** Your reflog records _your_ HEAD's history. The teammate's commits never touched your local repo; your reflog has no entries for them. They might recover from their own reflog if their local still has the work; otherwise the commits are gone. (This is why force-push on shared branches is the moment to ask the team, not the reflog.)

(c) **No.** Uncommitted working-tree changes never enter the Git object store. The reflog tracks HEAD movements; it does not journal file contents. The edits are lost. (Some editors keep their own undo history that may help; check VS Code's local history, JetBrains' local history. But Git itself cannot help.)

The general rule: the reflog rescues _committed_ work that has become unreachable from any branch ref. It does not rescue uncommitted work, and it does not rescue someone else's work.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Take a recent PR you have merged (or use the synthetic PR below). Examine its commit history. Could it be split into more atomic commits? Could messages be rewritten to better state the why? Produce a `git rebase -i` plan that would improve the history.

Synthetic PR: a single commit titled "Add user permissions feature" containing: a new `Permission` type, a new `PermissionService` class with read and write methods, changes to `AccessGuard` to consume the service, changes to three existing routes to enforce permissions, a typo fix in a comment in an unrelated file, and 23 new tests.

Hint

Look for independent conceptual changes that could be reviewed and reverted separately.

**Answer:** A reasonable split:
    
    
    pick a1b2c3d Extract Permission type into src/auth/types.ts
    pick d4e5f6a Add PermissionService (read methods only)
    pick b7c8d9e Add PermissionService write methods
    pick c1d2e3f Inject PermissionService into AccessGuard
    pick f4a5b6c Enforce permissions in /api/leads
    pick g7h8i9j Enforce permissions in /api/accounts
    pick j0k1l2m Enforce permissions in /api/users
    drop k0l1m2n   # the unrelated typo fix belongs in its own PR
    

Eight conceptual changes (or seven, dropping the typo). Each can be reviewed, bisected, and reverted independently. Each message states what changed and why. The typo fix is dropped because it does not belong in this PR; it should be a separate one-commit PR. The rebase produces a history that, six months from now, makes "when did we add write permissions for the users API?" answerable in one `git log --oneline | grep` instead of requiring a deep dive.

**Q6.** A production bug has appeared. The test `test_payout_rounding_eu` started failing yesterday on `main`, and the last known good version is the tag `v3.8.0` from six weeks ago. Walk through the bisect you would run. What are the inputs, what are the expected commands, and what would you do if the bisect lands on a commit that does not build?

Hint

§3.3's basic workflow plus the failure mode discussion.

**Answer:** The bisect:
    
    
    $ git bisect start
    $ git bisect bad HEAD
    $ git bisect good v3.8.0
    $ git bisect run npm test -- test_payout_rounding_eu
    

Expected: Git binary-searches the range, runs the test at each midpoint, marks each as good or bad, and after roughly 8-10 steps reports the first bad commit. Total wall-clock time depends on test speed; for a 30-second test, the bisect completes in 5-8 minutes.

If a midpoint commit does not build: bisect run will exit with a non-zero code, and Git may interpret that as "bad" or may mark the commit as `skip`. The fix is to detect the build failure explicitly in the bisect command and exit 125 (the special "skip this commit" code):
    
    
    $ git bisect run sh -c 'npm install || exit 125; npm run build || exit 125; npm test -- test_payout_rounding_eu'
    

This skips non-buildable midpoints rather than misclassifying them, and bisect continues with the buildable neighbors. If too many commits in the range are non-buildable, the bisect may return inconclusive; at that point, the deeper fix is to enforce buildability per commit going forward (§3.1's atomic-commit discipline). For the current bisect, you fall back to reading the commits in the unresolved range manually.

**Q7.** Set up your editor and shell so that the atomic-commit and notebook disciplines from this chapter and Chapter 2 are habitual rather than effortful. Describe (a) the keybinding or alias you would add to make `git add -p` one keystroke, (b) the commit-message template you would put in `~/.gitmessage`, (c) the shell alias you would add for `git log --oneline --graph --decorate`.

Hint

Aliases reduce the friction; the friction is what kills the discipline.

**Answer:** Three concrete configurations:

(a) A Git alias for partial staging:
    
    
    $ git config --global alias.ap "add --patch"
    # now `git ap file.ts` enters patch mode
    

Or a shell alias:
    
    
    alias ga="git add --patch"
    

(b) A commit-message template at `~/.gitmessage`:
    
    
    # Subject: imperative mood, <= 50 chars, no trailing period
    #
    # Why this change is needed (not what it does; the diff shows that).
    # Wrap at 72 chars.
    #
    # Refs: TICKET-123
    

And enable it:
    
    
    $ git config --global commit.template ~/.gitmessage
    

(c) A pretty log alias:
    
    
    $ git config --global alias.lg "log --oneline --graph --decorate --all"
    # now `git lg` shows the graph
    

The point is not the specific commands but the principle: reduce friction on the disciplines you want to be habits. An engineer who has to type 30 characters to do partial staging will not partial-stage; an engineer who types `git ap` will.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue for and against the position that "in 2026, with AI agents making commits, the atomic-commit discipline is more important than ever." What changes about commits when an agent makes them?

**Discussion:** For the position: agents produce code much faster than humans, so a given session produces more commits in less time; the _volume_ of history requiring review and maintenance has gone up, and the per-commit clarity has to compensate. An agent that emits 30 commits in an hour with vague messages produces an unreadable history; an agent that emits 30 atomic commits with clear messages produces a readable one. The discipline is therefore _more_ important because the alternative is worse.

Against the position: agents are inconsistent about message quality and atomicity; expecting the discipline to scale to agent output may be unrealistic; perhaps the right approach is to _squash_ agent commits into one human-reviewed commit at the end of the session, abandoning atomicity in exchange for a cleaner reviewer experience. In this view, the discipline shifts from "commits as you go" to "commits at session end, after human review."

The synthesis: both views have merit and the right answer depends on the agent's trustworthiness in a given codebase. For a well-tuned agent in a codebase with a strong CLAUDE.md (Chapter 22), atomic agent commits are achievable and the discipline scales. For an unfamiliar agent or a new codebase, squashing at session end and producing one human-curated commit may be safer. Most teams in 2026 land somewhere between: agents commit per atomic change, humans rebase-interactive before pushing, the published history is a curated story even though the working history was agent-driven.

**Q9.** Trace the chain of dependencies between Git craft (this chapter) and the other compounding disciplines named in Chapter 1 (testing, refactoring, naming, code review, ADRs). For each of those disciplines, name one specific way Git craft enables or constrains it.

**Discussion:**

* **Testing** : Bisect requires the test suite to be reliable enough to use as a binary decision oracle. So Git craft _requires_ test-suite craft; the two compound together. Chapter 6's mutation testing makes the test suite a sharper decision oracle, which makes bisects faster.
* **Refactoring** : Atomic commits are a refactoring enabler. The Fowler refactorings (Chapter 9) are most safely applied in small, reviewable, revertable steps; the atomic commit is the natural granularity for each step. Without atomic commits, refactoring is risky because the commit-level revert is at the wrong granularity.
* **Naming** : A commit message is itself an act of naming. The discipline of writing imperative, specific, atomic messages exercises the same skill as naming functions and types well. Engineers who write good commit messages also tend to write good function names; the skills correlate strongly.
* **Code review** : A PR with seven atomic commits is seven small reviews; a PR with one giant commit is one large review. The reviewing engineer's mental load (Chapter 15) is dramatically lower with atomic commits; the discipline of Git craft is therefore a gift to the team's reviewing capacity.
* **ADRs** : A well-written commit message that explains _why_ a change was made is a micro-ADR (Chapter 17). For decisions too small for a full ADR but too important for an empty commit message, the message is the only persisted explanation. Git craft is therefore the substrate on which lightweight decision documentation lives.

The compounding is multi-directional: Git craft enables the other disciplines, and the other disciplines reward Git craft with their accumulated value. An engineer who invests in all of them simultaneously sees the compound interest in the second year; an engineer who invests in only one sees flat returns.

### Chapter Summary

Local Git craft is the substrate on which most of the rest of this book stands. The atomic-commit discipline (§3.1) is the foundation: every later technique (bisect, review, revert) is more powerful with atomic commits and less powerful without. Rebase and merge are tools for different jobs (§3.2); the dogma against either is wrong. `git bisect` (§3.3) turns hours of debugging into minutes, but only when the commits are atomic and the tests are deterministic; the reflog (§3.4) is the safety net that makes destructive operations recoverable; interactive rebase (§3.5) is the storytelling tool that turns a working draft of commits into a published essay.

In 2026 with AI agents making commits (§3.6), the discipline scales by being specified to the agent in CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md (Chapter 22) and enforced via human rebase before push. Reading the agent's commit history is itself a Thread C catching tool: small flurries of fixups signal Lies #5 and #6, multi-file consistency-only commits signal Lies #2 and #3.

Chapter 4 takes these local skills to the team level: branching doctrine, Conventional Commits, semantic versioning, feature flags, the agent-PR workflow. The local skills compound when the team adopts them; the team skills compound when the locals are already in place. Reading old code (Chapter 2) becomes easier with good local Git craft because `git blame` and `git log` work; refactoring under test (Chapter 10) becomes safer because bisect can find the regression that the refactor introduced; the disciplines stack. The investment is one of the highest-return investments in this book.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Try`git add -p` as your default for the next sprint.** Stage hunk by hunk rather than `git add .`; commit each conceptual change separately. Painful for one PR, reflexive after three. If it costs more than it saves after a sprint, drop it — but most engineers who try find it sticks.
  2. **Configure your shell aliases for the disciplines you want to be habits.** `git ap` for partial staging, `git lg` for the pretty graph, `~/.gitmessage` for the commit-message template. Friction is what kills the habit; aliases remove the friction.
  3. **The next time you have a regression of unknown origin, reach for`git bisect run` before reaching for the debugger.** Write the failing test, name the last known-good tag, walk away while Git binary-searches. The answer arrives in minutes; the alternative is hours.

---

## Chapter 4: Team Branching Doctrine

Carmen Ortiz took over engineering management at Currents in early 2025. Currents is a fifty-person data platform engineering organization split across five teams, all running Python with FastAPI and Postgres. She inherited a branching model that her predecessor called "GitHub Flow but with release branches," which is to say it was undefined. Some teams branched off `main`, some off a `dev` branch, some off the last release tag. Hotfixes sometimes shipped through `main`, sometimes through the release branch, sometimes through both. The release on January 14, 2025, included a feature that had not been approved by anyone, because the feature branch had been silently merged into a release branch that no one was monitoring.

Carmen spent her first quarter rewriting the branching doctrine. She landed on trunk-based development with Conventional Commits, semantic versioning, feature flags via LaunchDarkly, and a documented PR review SLA. The transition took six weeks of work distributed across the team, and the immediate result was nothing dramatic. The interesting result emerged over the following year: ship cadence accelerated meaningfully, rollback-triggering incidents fell by a large fraction, and weekend on-call pages became rare rather than routine. (The Currents numbers are illustrative; the _shape_ of these gains — they arrive together, on a multi-quarter timescale, after the prerequisites land — is what real teams adopting trunk-based development report.)

This chapter is about how individual Git craft (Chapter 3) scales to team Git craft. The local discipline of atomic commits, bisects, and interactive rebase compounds dramatically when a team adopts shared conventions; without the shared conventions, even excellent individual practice does not compound past the individual.

### 4.1 The three branching models

Three branching models dominate working engineering teams in 2026. They are not all equally good, but they are not all equally bad either. Choosing the right one for your team requires honest assessment.

#### Trunk-based development

In trunk-based development, all engineers commit to a single long-lived branch (usually `main`), often multiple times per day, with feature work hidden behind feature flags (§4.4) until ready. Branches, if used at all, are short-lived (less than two days) and small (less than 400 lines). The model assumes a fast, reliable test suite and a culture of small PRs.

Strengths: maximum integration velocity; conflicts are tiny because branches are tiny; CI signals are clear because every commit is on `main`; bisects are clean because the history is roughly linear.

Weaknesses: requires fast CI (every commit triggers a full pipeline) and high test quality; requires feature flags for any work that cannot ship to production immediately; requires a culture of small PRs that some teams find hard to adopt.

This is Carmen's choice for Currents, and it is the choice that produces the fastest mean-time-to-ship in well-run teams.

#### GitHub Flow

A simpler model. Branches off `main`; each branch is one feature or fix; PRs are merged into `main` when ready; `main` is always deployable. No release branches, no `dev` branch. Branches can be longer-lived than in trunk-based, but the spirit is similar.

Strengths: simple, well-understood, fits the GitHub UI naturally; lower CI requirements than trunk-based.

Weaknesses: encourages longer branches than trunk-based, with larger conflicts; the model is silent on what to do when a feature is half-done at release time, which leads ad-hoc to feature flags or to long-running branches.

This is the most common model in 2026 and a reasonable default for teams under twenty engineers.

#### Git Flow

A more elaborate model. Two long-lived branches (`main` and `develop`); feature branches off `develop`; release branches off `develop` when stabilizing; hotfix branches off `main`. Each merge type has its own ceremony.

Strengths: gives structure to teams shipping versioned releases (e.g. installable software, mobile apps, libraries) where multiple versions are in production simultaneously.

Weaknesses: heavy ceremony for teams that ship continuously; long-running `develop` branch tends to drift from `main`; the model was designed for a world where releases happened every few weeks, not multiple times per day.

In 2026, Git Flow remains appropriate for shipped-versioned products (mobile apps, on-prem software, library releases) but is over-engineered for most SaaS web teams.

#### How to choose

The honest decision criteria:

Property | Trunk-based | GitHub Flow | Git Flow  
---|---|---|---  
Best ship cadence | Many times per day | Daily to weekly | Weekly to monthly  
CI requirement | Fast, reliable | Moderate | Moderate  
Feature flag use | Pervasive | As needed | Rare  
Branch lifetime | < 2 days | < 2 weeks | Weeks to months  
Team size sweet spot | 5-500 | 1-50 | 5-200 (versioned products)  
Mental overhead | Low after adoption | Low | High  
  
If your team ships multiple times per day and has fast CI, prefer trunk-based. If your team ships daily-to-weekly and has moderate CI, prefer GitHub Flow. If your team ships versioned releases of installed software, prefer Git Flow. The pure dogma of any one model applied to all teams is the failure mode; the matching to context is the discipline.

### 4.2 Conventional Commits

Conventional Commits is a specification for commit message format. The format:
    
    
    <type>(<scope>): <description>
    
    [optional body]
    
    [optional footer]
    

Where `<type>` is one of a fixed vocabulary (`feat`, `fix`, `docs`, `style`, `refactor`, `test`, `chore`, `perf`, `ci`, `build`, `revert`) and `<scope>` is an optional area of the codebase. Examples:
    
    
    feat(auth): add MFA enforcement for admin role
    fix(scoring): correct rounding for pre-2021 accounts
    refactor(billing): extract RefundCalculator from BillingService
    chore(deps): bump tokio to 1.41
    

A breaking change is marked with `!` after the type or with a `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer:
    
    
    feat(api)!: rename /users/me to /users/current
    
    BREAKING CHANGE: clients calling /users/me must update to /users/current
    

#### Why it works

Three downstream benefits:

* **Automatic changelog generation.** Tools like `conventional-changelog` produce a release-readable changelog by reading commit messages. The changelog is then accurate by construction; no human writes the wrong thing.
* **Automatic version bumping.** Combined with semantic versioning (§4.3), the commit types determine the next version automatically: a `feat` bumps minor, a `fix` bumps patch, a `BREAKING CHANGE` bumps major.
* **Triage support.** A reviewer or maintenance engineer can `git log --grep=^feat` to find all features, or `git log --grep=^fix` to find all bug fixes. The history becomes queryable.

#### What goes wrong

Two failure modes worth naming.

The _tagging dance_ : engineers mark trivial changes as `feat` because the tooling rewards features more than fixes, leading to inflated minor-version bumps. Mitigation: agree on what counts as a feature in a team contract; review the type in PR review.

The _scope drift_ : scopes start specific (`auth`, `billing`, `scoring`) and end vague (`misc`, `cleanup`, `various`). Mitigation: commit a `commitlint` rule with an enumerated list of allowed scopes, updated when new modules appear.

### 4.3 Semantic versioning

Semantic versioning (semver) is a contract about what version number changes mean:

* **MAJOR** version bumps for incompatible API changes.
* **MINOR** version bumps for backward-compatible new features.
* **PATCH** version bumps for backward-compatible bug fixes.

Combined with Conventional Commits (§4.2), the version bump is mechanical: any commit with `BREAKING CHANGE` bumps major; any `feat` bumps minor; any `fix` bumps patch. Tools like `semantic-release` automate the bump and tag from commit history.

#### Where semver is the right contract

Semver is the right contract for: libraries, SDKs, public APIs, CLI tools shipped to users who pin versions. The version number is part of the contract these users rely on.

#### Where semver is the wrong contract

Semver is the wrong contract for: continuously deployed SaaS web applications. The current version of the SaaS _is_ the version; users do not pin versions; the version number is internal bookkeeping. For these systems, a simpler scheme (date-based, build-number-based, git-sha-based) is usually clearer. Forcing semver on a CD SaaS produces version numbers that bear no relationship to reality and confuses everyone.

The discipline is to know which contract your software is making. For Currents (data platform SaaS), Carmen chose date-based versions for the internal product and semver for the open-source CLI Currents ships. Two contracts, two purposes.

### 4.4 Feature flags as the ship-incomplete-safely doctrine

A feature flag (also called a feature toggle) is a runtime conditional that decides whether a piece of code is active for a given request, user, or environment. Example:
    
    
    if (await flags.isOn('new_scoring_algorithm', { userId })) {
      return newScoringAlgorithm(input);
    }
    return legacyScoringAlgorithm(input);
    

Both code paths are deployed; only one runs for a given user. Flags are toggled via a service (LaunchDarkly, Statsig, Unleash, Flipt) without redeploying code.

#### Definition: Feature Flag

A feature flag is a runtime conditional under external control that allows a code path to be shipped to production in a deactivated state, and activated gradually or selectively after deployment.

In plain language: feature flags decouple _deploying code_ from _releasing functionality_. You can deploy half-finished work safely because the half-finished path is off; you can release the finished version to 1% of users, then 10%, then 100%, watching metrics at each step.

#### Why this enables trunk-based development

The reason trunk-based development works at high cadence is feature flags. A feature flag lets a long feature (six weeks of work) be merged in many small PRs, each of which lands on `main` deactivated. The feature is _deployed_ continuously and _released_ exactly once, when the flag is flipped to 100%. The team gets the integration benefits of small PRs (small conflicts, fast review, fast CI) without the release-coordination cost of long branches.

#### Choosing a feature flag system

Four serious systems in 2026:

* **LaunchDarkly** : market leader; mature; expensive at scale; rich targeting rules.
* **Statsig** : experimentation-first; A/B testing built in; favored for product teams that run experiments.
* **Unleash** : open-source; self-hostable; favored for teams with strong infrastructure or data-residency requirements.
* **Flipt** : open-source; lightweight; favored for smaller teams or for use in test environments.

The choice depends on team size, budget, and whether experimentation features matter. Carmen chose LaunchDarkly for Currents because the team valued mature targeting rules and integration with their existing analytics pipeline.

#### The flag-rot problem

Feature flags accumulate. A flag added for the 2024 redesign is still in the code in 2026, still being evaluated on every request, with the "off" branch dead code. Flag rot is a real form of technical debt (Chapter 19); the mitigation is a documented lifecycle:

  1. Flag is added with a `kill_by_date` of, e.g., one quarter from now.
  2. When the flag reaches 100% rollout and is stable, an issue is filed to remove it.
  3. The removal PR deletes the flag from code, removes it from the flag service, and is reviewed normally.

Without this discipline, a five-year-old codebase has hundreds of dead flags and the code path that anyone reads is interleaved with conditionals that no one understands.

### 4.5 The PR as unit of work

The PR (pull request, or merge request) is the unit at which work is reviewed and merged in 2026 teams. The discipline of treating the PR as the unit shapes what good work looks like.

#### Sizing

A PR should be small enough to review carefully in one sitting. The published research converges here: Caitlin Sadowski and colleagues' 2018 paper "Modern Code Review: A Case Study at Google" (ICSE) and SmartBear's _Best Kept Secrets of Peer Code Review_ (Cohen, 2006 onward) both put the effectiveness sweet spot in the 200–400 line range of substantive change, excluding tests and generated code. Above 600–800 lines, review quality drops measurably; reviewers approve faster, miss more bugs, and the post-merge defect rate rises. Currents tracked the same pattern internally after Carmen's transition, which is the usual finding when teams measure their own data.

A 1,500-line PR is not one PR; it is three PRs in a trench coat. Split it.

#### PR templates

A PR template guides the author to write what reviewers need. A useful template includes:
    
    
    ## What
    One sentence describing the change.
    
    ## Why
    What problem does this solve? Link to ticket, ADR, or incident.
    
    ## How
    Brief overview of the approach. Anything non-obvious about the implementation.
    
    ## Testing
    What was tested? What was not?
    
    ## Risk
    What could go wrong? What rollback plan?
    
    ## Flag
    Behind a feature flag? Which one?
    

A template is not bureaucracy; it is a forcing function for the author to think about reviewer needs before writing the description. Skipping a section is a signal; an empty "Risk" section in a 1,000-line database migration is a red flag.

#### Review SLAs

A team that cares about ship velocity needs explicit review SLAs. Carmen's Currents SLA, after her transition:

* **First review response** : 4 business hours during local working hours.
* **Author response to review comments** : 1 business day.
* **Re-review after author response** : 4 business hours.
* **Hard limit** : a PR open for more than 5 business days is escalated to the team lead.

The SLAs are not punitive; they prevent the dead-PR-syndrome where work sits unreviewed for two weeks, the author loses context, and the merge happens carelessly. Currents publishes the SLAs in the team's onboarding doc and tracks aggregate compliance monthly. Carmen's own review comments under this SLA always open with the same shape — a bolded label (`**Blocking**`, `**Suggestion**`, `**Nit**`, `**Praise**`) before any prose, so the author knows the required action from the first character. It is the habit she most asks new hires to adopt; she finds it disciplines her own thinking as much as the author's response.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "review SLAs slow me down because they interrupt my own work." The mistake assumes that reviewing is overhead. In a team that ships features through PRs, reviewing _is_ the work; an engineer who never reviews is not pulling their share, and an engineer who reviews promptly is enabling the team's velocity. The slowdown perception is real (interrupting deep work to review) but the alternative (PRs piling up) slows the team more in aggregate than the reviewer is slowed individually. The discipline is to batch reviews twice per day rather than reactively; this keeps the SLA without thrashing the reviewer's day.

### 4.6 The agent-commits-PR workflow

In 2026, agent mode (Cursor agent, Claude Code, Devin, v0) is increasingly the implementer; the human is increasingly the reviewer and director. The workflow that emerged in 2025-2026:

  1. A human writes a spec, a failing test, or a clear ticket description.
  2. The agent works on a branch, makes commits, writes tests, runs the test suite locally.
  3. The agent opens a PR with a description matching the team's template.
  4. The human reviews the PR, requests changes, or merges.
  5. The agent responds to review comments by pushing more commits.
  6. The PR merges when the human is satisfied.

This workflow inverts the traditional flow: the human is no longer the author of the code, they are the reviewer and final-decision-maker. The implications are deep:

* **Review skill becomes the bottleneck.** A team that ships ten agent-PRs per engineer per day needs reviewers who can review ten PRs per day without quality degradation. This is harder than it sounds (Chapter 15).
* **Agent identity matters.** The Git author of the agent's commits should be distinct (e.g. a service account named `claude-agent[bot]`) so that `git blame` reveals whether a line was human-authored or agent-authored. This information is valuable during maintenance.
* **The CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md file becomes load-bearing** (Chapter 22). The agent learns the codebase's conventions from this file; without it, the agent's PRs are full of style inconsistencies (Thread C Lie #3).
* **The agent's commit messages need explicit specification.** Without instruction, the agent defaults to a generic style; with instruction (in the CLAUDE.md), the agent matches Conventional Commits and the team's scope vocabulary.

#### Configuring the agent for Currents

Carmen's CLAUDE.md at Currents includes:
    
    
    # Commit conventions
    Use Conventional Commits format.
    Allowed types: feat, fix, refactor, test, docs, chore, perf, ci.
    Allowed scopes: auth, billing, scoring, ingest, api, db, infra.
    
    # PR conventions
    Open one PR per atomic change.
    Target trunk; do not branch off feature branches.
    Use the PR template at .github/pull_request_template.md.
    Behind a feature flag if user-visible.
    
    # Testing
    Write tests in pytest. Use the existing fixtures in `tests/conftest.py`.
    Do not introduce new test frameworks.
    
    # Style
    Match the surrounding file. Do not reformat existing code.
    

This file is read at the start of every agent session. The agent's PRs at Currents look like the team's other PRs; without the file, they would not.

### 4.7 A working example: Trellis adopts trunk-based

Trellis adopted trunk-based development in early 2025, after years of GitHub Flow. The transition was the kind of multi-team change that takes a quarter and several incidents to land. The illustrative timeline:

* **Week 1** : Asher and two other staff engineers wrote a proposal. The proposal was a single ADR (Chapter 17) explaining the current pain (long-running branches drifting from `main`, large merge conflicts, slow CI on PR branches) and the proposed change.
* **Week 2-3** : Discussion. The most contentious point: trunk-based requires feature flags, and Trellis had only ad-hoc feature flags. The team agreed to adopt LaunchDarkly as a prerequisite.
* **Week 4-6** : LaunchDarkly integration. A small team built the SDK wrapper and the flag-lifecycle process.
* **Week 7-10** : Migration. Teams converted long-running branches into trunk-based work behind flags. CI was tightened to run on every `main` commit, with a one-hour SLA on green builds. PR sizes shrank intentionally; the team published a target of < 400 lines.
* **Week 11-12** : The first incident. A flag misconfiguration deployed a half-finished feature to 100% of users. Post-mortem produced a new rule: flags default to off in production, and rollout requires a two-step approval (Carmen-style review SLA applied to flag changes too).

By Week 16, the model was stable. PR sizes were averaging 250 lines; review turnaround was averaging 6 hours; CI was green on `main` 95% of the time; the on-call burden dropped because incident-causing PRs were smaller and revertable.

The lesson Asher took: the model did not work because it was the right model; it worked because the team committed to the prerequisites (feature flags, fast CI, small PRs) and adjusted when the model surfaced new failure modes. The right model imposed without the prerequisites is worse than the wrong model with the prerequisites.

### 4.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Your team currently runs Git Flow with weekly releases. You believe trunk-based would be a better fit. Apply §4.1's decision criteria honestly: when would you recommend the change, and when would you recommend staying?

Hint

The decision table in §4.1 lists six properties. Score your team on each.

**Answer:** Recommend trunk-based if: the team's CI is fast (< 15 minutes for the full suite) and reliable (green > 95% on `main`), the team is willing to adopt feature flags pervasively (and has a flag service), the team can shrink typical PRs to under 400 lines, and the product is continuously deployed (not versioned-installable).

Recommend staying on Git Flow if: the product is versioned and installable (mobile app, on-prem software, library) where multiple versions are in production simultaneously, the team has weak CI that cannot support trunk's per-commit pipeline pressure, or the team is in a regulatory environment that requires explicit release branches (some financial-services teams).

The pure middle case (a SaaS team shipping weekly with moderate CI and resistance to feature flags) is the best candidate for GitHub Flow rather than either of the extremes. Choosing trunk-based for this team without first investing in CI and flags will produce friction that the team blames on trunk-based when the cause is the missing prerequisites.

**Q2.** Compare Conventional Commits with the alternative of free-form commit messages. What does the team gain and lose?

Hint

§4.2's three downstream benefits and two failure modes.

**Answer:** Gain: queryable history (`git log --grep=^feat`), automatic changelog generation, automatic version bumps via semantic-release, lower cognitive load on what to write (the format prompts the author). Lose: some expressiveness (the format constrains what you write), some flexibility (a commit that is part feat and part fix has to be split or the type chosen approximately), some adoption friction (the team has to learn the vocabulary).

The trade-off is favorable for most teams shipping libraries or products with changelogs. For internal-only services where the changelog is irrelevant, the gain is smaller; the format then is mostly a style choice rather than a functional one. Most 2026 teams adopt the format anyway because it integrates with toolchains and because consistency across teams has value when engineers move between codebases.

**Q3.** A teammate adds a feature flag for a small change and rolls it out to 100% on the same day, then leaves the flag in place "for future use." Why is this a problem? What should the team's flag lifecycle have prevented?

Hint

§4.4's flag-rot problem.

**Answer:** The flag is now dead code: a runtime conditional that always takes the same branch. It adds cost (the flag is evaluated on every request, paying a network or cache lookup) and confusion (the "off" branch is unreachable but still present in the code, where it will mislead future readers). Multiplied across hundreds of such flags over years, the codebase becomes unreadable.

The team's lifecycle should have required: (a) a `kill_by_date` on the flag at creation, (b) an automatic issue filed when the flag reaches stable 100%, (c) a removal PR scheduled within one or two sprints of 100% rollout. The teammate's "for future use" reasoning is exactly the rationalization the lifecycle is designed to prevent: future use is hypothetical, current cost is real, and the right pattern is to remove now and re-add if the future use materializes.

**Q4.** Why is review SLA discipline (§4.5) considered part of the engineering culture rather than a project-management concern?

Hint

Who is responsible for ship velocity in a 2026 engineering team?

**Answer:** In a team where the PR is the unit of work, ship velocity is determined by the rate at which PRs flow through review and merge. If reviews stall, ship velocity drops, regardless of how fast individual engineers write code. Reviewing is therefore part of the production capacity of the team, not a manager-imposed overhead. An engineer who reviews promptly is enabling the team's output; an engineer who reviews never is throttling it.

This is an _engineering_ concern because the engineers are the only ones who can review well: they have the domain knowledge, the codebase context, and the technical judgment. A manager can set an SLA but cannot do the reviews. So the discipline must be internalized by engineers as part of their professional craft, not externalized as a metric they comply with. Carmen's SLA at Currents worked because the engineers adopted it as their own; an SLA imposed without that adoption usually does not work.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Your team is migrating from GitHub Flow to trunk-based development. Write a one-page migration plan listing: (a) prerequisites the team must establish first, (b) the migration steps in order, (c) the metrics you would track to know whether the migration is succeeding.

Hint

§4.7 describes Trellis's actual timeline. Adapt the structure.

**Answer:** A reasonable plan:

**(a) Prerequisites**

  1. CI must run in under 15 minutes for the full suite, green on `main` ≥ 95% of the time.
  2. A feature flag service must be in place (LaunchDarkly, Statsig, Unleash, or Flipt) with the team trained on its use.
  3. A documented flag lifecycle (creation, monitoring, removal) must be agreed.
  4. A target PR size must be agreed (e.g. ≤ 400 lines of substantive change).
  5. Review SLAs must be documented and accepted.

**(b) Migration steps**

  1. Write an ADR explaining the change, the prerequisites met, the expected outcomes.
  2. Land the prerequisites: integrate the flag service, tighten CI, train the team.
  3. Convert in-progress long branches into trunk-based work behind flags; close the long branches.
  4. Set the new norm: all new work is trunk-based from this point.
  5. Watch for incidents in the first month; expect at least one flag-related misconfiguration and respond with a process update rather than a model retreat.
  6. Hold a retrospective at the three-month mark.

**(c) Metrics**

* PR size distribution (target: median < 300 lines, P90 < 600 lines).
* PR review turnaround (target: median < 4 hours during working hours).
* CI green-on-`main` percentage (target: ≥ 95%).
* Mean time to ship a feature (compare before/after).
* Incident rate (compare before/after; expect a transient bump during migration).
* Flag count and average flag age (watch for rot).

If the metrics are not moving in the right direction by month three, investigate before declaring success.

**Q6.** Write a feature flag lifecycle policy for your team. Specify: how flags are created, who can flip them, how rollout is staged, when flags are cleaned up.

Hint

A policy should make the easy thing the safe thing.

**Answer:** A reasonable policy:

**Creation** : any engineer can create a flag. At creation, the flag must have: a name following the convention `<scope>_<feature>_<verb>` (e.g. `scoring_new_algorithm_enabled`), a description, an owner (engineer or team), a `kill_by_date` no more than two quarters out, and a default value of `false` in all environments except local dev.

**Flipping** : in development environments, any engineer. In staging, any engineer with a notification to the team channel. In production, two-person approval (the owner plus one other engineer) and a notification to the team channel; for flags affecting more than 10% of users, an additional approval from the on-call engineer.

**Staged rollout** : 0% → 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%. At each stage, the owner watches the relevant dashboards for at least one business day before advancing. A failed stage triggers an immediate revert to the previous percentage and a post-mortem before the next attempt.

**Cleanup** : when a flag has been at 100% for two weeks in production, an issue is automatically filed against the owner. The owner has one sprint to land a removal PR. If the deadline passes, the issue escalates to the team lead. A flag that has been at 0% for one month (effectively dead) is also subject to removal.

**Exceptions** : long-lived operational flags (kill switches, region toggles, throttles) are exempt from the cleanup rule but must be tagged as `operational` and reviewed annually.

This policy makes flags useful without letting them rot, and it forces the team to be deliberate about which flags persist.

**Q7.** Configure a CLAUDE.md (or AGENTS.md) file for your codebase that specifies: commit message conventions, PR conventions, testing conventions, and codebase style guidance. Use Carmen's example from §4.6 as the starting structure; adapt to your codebase.

Hint

The file should be specific to your codebase, not generic.

**Answer:** A reasonable structure (adapt as needed):
    
    
    # Project conventions for AI agents
    
    ## Commit conventions
    Use Conventional Commits.
    Allowed types: feat, fix, refactor, test, docs, chore, perf.
    Allowed scopes: <list from your codebase>.
    Subject line ≤ 50 chars. Body wrapped at 72.
    
    ## PR conventions
    One atomic change per PR. Target main; do not branch off feature branches.
    Use the template at .github/pull_request_template.md.
    Behind a feature flag if user-visible. Flag name follows `<scope>_<feature>_<verb>`.
    
    ## Testing
    Framework: <pytest / vitest / jest / etc>.
    Use the existing fixtures in <fixtures path>; do not introduce new test frameworks.
    Cover happy path, at least one error path, and at least one boundary case.
    
    ## Style
    Match the surrounding file. Do not reformat existing code.
    Linter: <eslint / ruff / etc>. Run before committing.
    Imports: <grouped by stdlib / local / third-party in that order>.
    
    ## Anti-patterns to avoid
    - Do not introduce a new HTTP client; the codebase uses <existing client>.
    - Do not introduce a Singleton; use the existing DI container.
    - Do not catch the base Exception type; catch specific exceptions only.
    
    ## Where to look first
    - Tests are in <tests path>.
    - Domain models are in <models path>.
    - Entry points are in <entry points path>.
    

The file should be checked in and reviewed when conventions change. The agent reads it at session start; the team reads it in onboarding. It serves both audiences.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue for and against: "In 2026, all branching models eventually converge to trunk-based development because AI agents make small, fast PRs the dominant workflow." What evidence would settle the debate?

**Discussion:** For: agent-PR mode (§4.6) produces many small PRs at high cadence; trunk-based is the model best suited to this pattern; the other models impose friction (long branches, ceremony) that agents do not respect well; therefore the equilibrium is trunk-based. Evidence: teams adopting agent-PR mode are increasingly converging on trunk-based and feature flags.

Against: branching model choice depends on product type (versioned vs SaaS), regulatory context, and team skill, none of which change because the implementer is an agent. A regulated financial team still needs release branches; a mobile app team still needs version branches. Agents adapt to the team's model; the team's model adapts to the product, not to the implementer. Evidence: many 2026 teams using agents heavily still use Git Flow or GitHub Flow because their product demands it.

The synthesis: agent-PR mode raises the _value_ of trunk-based for teams that _can_ adopt it, by amplifying the cadence advantage. But it does not eliminate the _constraints_ on the teams that cannot. The convergence is real but partial; the dogmatic version of either side is wrong. The settling evidence would be a multi-year longitudinal study of agent-using teams, segmented by product type; in 2026 we have only fragments of such data, and the answer is "trunk-based is gaining share, but not universally."

**Q9.** Trace how the disciplines of Chapter 3 (local Git craft) compose with the disciplines of Chapter 4 (team Git doctrine). Name three specific compositions and one tension.

**Discussion:**

Compositions:

  1. **Atomic commits + Conventional Commits** : an atomic commit becomes more useful when the format is conventional, because the type+scope makes the atomic unit queryable. The compounding is multiplicative: atomic-alone is useful, conventional-alone is partially useful, both together are dramatically useful.
  2. **Interactive rebase + PR-as-unit-of-work** : interactive rebase before pushing produces a clean PR; the clean PR is reviewable as a unit; the unit reviewable PR enables the SLA discipline. Each step depends on the prior step.
  3. **Bisect + trunk-based development** : trunk-based produces a roughly linear history, which makes bisect's binary search trivial. Bisect's value compounds with the history quality; the history quality compounds with the team model. The two together turn debugging from hours to minutes.

Tension:

  1. **Interactive rebase + team-shared branches** : the local discipline of rebasing produces a clean history, but if the branch is shared (others have based work on it), rebasing rewrites their context. The tension resolves by convention: in trunk-based with short branches, the rebase happens before push and the conflict does not arise; in longer-branch models, the team needs an explicit policy on when rebase is allowed.

The general lesson: local craft does not automatically scale to team craft; the team's conventions must allow the local craft to apply. A team without explicit branching doctrine produces engineers who individually practice good Git craft but collectively produce a tangled history. Doctrine is the multiplier.

### Chapter Summary

Team Git craft is the multiplier on individual Git craft (Chapter 3). The right branching model (§4.1) depends on product type and team context; trunk-based with feature flags is increasingly the default for SaaS teams shipping continuously, but Git Flow remains right for versioned products and GitHub Flow remains a reasonable default for smaller teams. Conventional Commits (§4.2) makes commit history queryable and enables automatic changelogs and version bumps; semantic versioning (§4.3) is the right contract for libraries and CLIs but not always for SaaS. Feature flags (§4.4) decouple deploying code from releasing functionality, enabling trunk-based to work at high cadence; the lifecycle discipline prevents flag rot. The PR as unit of work (§4.5) is the granularity at which review and merge happen; SLA discipline is part of the team's culture, not a project-management imposition. The agent-PR workflow (§4.6) inverts the traditional flow: humans review, agents implement; the CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md file is the load-bearing artifact that aligns agent output with team conventions.

The Trellis transition (§4.7) illustrates the multi-quarter shape of a model change; prerequisites first, conversion second, retrospection third. The right model imposed without the prerequisites is worse than the wrong model with the prerequisites.

Part III (Chapters 5-8) takes the next step: testing as discipline. Trunk-based development assumes fast, reliable tests; feature flags assume that both code paths are tested; agent-PR mode assumes that the reviewer can trust the agent's tests. Without test discipline, the team Git doctrine collapses. Chapter 5 opens with the test pyramid honestly assessed: coverage as a metric, snapshot testing's narrow use, and the 2026 reality of AI-generated tests that pass without testing.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Adopt a feature flag lifecycle before adopting trunk-based development.** Flags without a `kill_by_date` and a removal process rot the codebase faster than they speed delivery. Lifecycle first; trunk-based second.
  2. **Set a PR-size target for your team and put a soft warning at 600 lines.** Not a hard block. Reviewers and authors both adjust their behavior when the warning is automatic; the post-merge defect rate drops within a quarter.
  3. **Treat review SLA compliance as an engineering metric, not a manager metric.** An engineer who reviews promptly is enabling team velocity; one who reviews never is throttling it. Make it part of how the team understands its own performance.

---

## Chapter 5: The Test Pyramid Honestly + Coverage + Snapshot Testing

A junior engineer at Helix opens Cursor agent and types: "Add comprehensive tests for the new pricing module." Forty-five seconds later, the agent has written 217 new test cases across three files. The test runner reports 98% line coverage on the pricing module. The PR is opened for review.

Aki Tanaka, the senior reviewer on that PR, reads it for forty minutes. She finds: 89 of the 217 tests are functionally identical to other tests with minor input variations; 41 tests assert the wrong thing (they check that a function was _called_ rather than that it produced the _right answer_); 23 tests use mocks for a class that has a real in-memory implementation already available in the codebase; the remaining 64 are roughly correct, of which perhaps 30 add meaningful coverage that was not already provided by the existing 80 tests.

Aki's review comment: "Coverage is now 98%. The number of tests doing real work has roughly doubled, not 3×. Please delete the duplicates, fix the mocks, and keep the 30 that actually test something."

This chapter is about the difference between coverage and confidence. The test pyramid, snapshot testing, and the AI-generated-test problem are all aspects of the same question: which tests genuinely test, and which tests merely pass?

### 5.1 The test pyramid

The test pyramid is a heuristic shape for a project's test suite. It puts many fast tests at the bottom (unit tests), fewer slower tests in the middle (integration tests), and very few slow tests at the top (end-to-end tests). The shape is a pyramid because both speed and brittleness compound across the layers; you want most of your testing where it is fastest and most robust.

#### Definition: Test Pyramid

The test pyramid is a heuristic distribution of tests in a project, with roughly 60-70% unit tests, 20-30% integration tests, and 5-10% end-to-end tests. The shape reflects the trade-off between test speed and test coverage of integrated behavior: unit tests are fast but miss integration bugs; end-to-end tests catch integration bugs but are slow and brittle.

In plain language: write many small fast tests for individual functions and classes; write a moderate number of medium-fast tests for important integration paths; write a small number of slow end-to-end tests for the most critical user-facing flows. Do not invert the pyramid.

#### Why the shape works

Three operational reasons:

* **CI feedback loop.** A unit test suite that runs in 30 seconds gets run constantly by developers and CI; a unit test suite that runs in 30 minutes gets run rarely. The shape preserves the feedback loop.
* **Failure localization.** When a unit test fails, the bug is in or near the unit. When an end-to-end test fails, the bug could be anywhere in the system. The shape pushes most testing to where failure localization is cheap.
* **Brittleness gradient.** End-to-end tests are brittle to UI changes, timing changes, environment changes; integration tests are brittle to interface changes; unit tests are brittle only to implementation changes. The shape minimizes overall brittleness.

#### The inverted pyramid antipattern

A common failure mode is the inverted pyramid: many end-to-end tests, few unit tests. Teams arrive there for understandable reasons (e.g. the product is UI-heavy and feels under-tested without UI tests; the team has Selenium expertise; the unit-testing infrastructure was never built). The result is a slow, flaky CI pipeline; bugs that surface late and far from the cause; and a team that gradually distrusts the test suite. Most teams that consciously assess their pyramid shape and discover an inversion subsequently invest in pushing tests down into the pyramid.

#### The hourglass and the diamond

Two variant shapes you may encounter. The _hourglass_ has many unit tests, few integration tests, many end-to-end tests. This is what happens when teams skip integration testing because "we have units and e2e, that's enough." The integration layer is often where the real bugs are; the hourglass tends to miss them.

The _diamond_ has few unit tests, many integration tests, few end-to-end tests. This is sometimes appropriate for systems whose units are too simple to need testing in isolation (thin wrappers over a database, for example) and whose integration with the database is the interesting behavior. The diamond is a legitimate variant for some systems, but it is not a default.

### 5.2 Coverage as a metric: uses and abuses

Code coverage measures the fraction of code lines (or branches, or paths) executed by the test suite. It is the easiest test metric to compute and the easiest test metric to misuse.

#### What coverage tells you

A line uncovered by tests is a line with no protection against regression. If you change that line, no test will catch the resulting bug; you will catch it in production. So uncovered code is a _risk indicator_.

Coverage is informative when it is _low_. A module at 5% coverage almost certainly has untested behavior; investigating which behaviors will likely surface bugs that have been waiting. Coverage works in the negative: it tells you where the gaps are.

#### What coverage does not tell you

Coverage does not tell you whether the tests are _good_. A test that executes 100 lines of code but asserts nothing about the result still produces 100 lines of coverage. A test that asserts the wrong thing also produces coverage. A test that calls the function with one input that happens to traverse most of the code also produces coverage, even though the function may fail on every other input.

The classic illustration:
    
    
    def divide(a, b):
        return a / b
    
    def test_divide():
        divide(10, 2)  # no assertion at all
    

This test produces 100% coverage of `divide` and would not catch any bug whatsoever. Coverage tools do not know that the assertion is missing; they only know that the line was executed.

#### The 80% trap

A common organizational goal is "80% coverage." The goal sounds reasonable; it produces predictable bad incentives. Engineers who are blocked at 80% coverage will write tests until the metric hits 80%, regardless of the value of those tests. The tests they write at the margin are usually low-value: tests for simple getters, tests with weak assertions, tests for code paths that are already well-covered. Coverage rises; confidence does not.

A more useful framing is _coverage thresholds as ratchets_ : a module currently at 60% may not regress below 60% in any PR; PRs that lower coverage must explicitly justify it. The threshold is per-module rather than global, and the goal is monotonic improvement rather than a fixed target. This avoids the 80%-trap incentive while preserving the negative signal.

#### Branch coverage versus line coverage

Line coverage counts lines executed. Branch coverage counts decision branches taken. The two diverge whenever a conditional exists:
    
    
    def f(x):
        if x > 0:
            return 'positive'
        return 'non-positive'
    

A test with input `x=5` executes the `if x > 0` line and the `return 'positive'` line; the `return 'non-positive'` line is never executed. Line coverage on this function is 2/3 ≈ 67% (some tools report it as "two executable lines covered"; the exact percentage depends on how the tool counts the `def` line, but the principle is the same: only the lines actually run get credit). Branch coverage is 50% — only the true branch of the conditional was taken; the false branch was not.

Add a second test with input `x=-3` and the false branch runs; line coverage rises to 100% and branch coverage rises to 100%. Notice how line coverage hit 100% while still missing the _boundary_ case at `x=0`; this is the gap mutation testing exposes (Chapter 6).
