This is one of the rare cases where mocks are the right tool; the call structure _is_ the spec.

The general rule (§7.2): stubs for simple return values, fakes for stateful read-write, spies for side-effect verification, mocks for protocol-specific tests. Dummies satisfy signatures when the dependency is never exercised in the test path.

**Q2.** Explain why a test full of `mock.assert_called_with(...)` assertions is brittle. Give a concrete refactor that would break such tests unnecessarily.

Hint

What does the assertion encode about the production code?

**Answer:** `mock.assert_called_with(...)` encodes the exact sequence and arguments of internal calls into the test. Any change to the internal call structure (caching, batching, reordering) breaks the test even if the resulting behavior is identical.

Concrete refactor: a function `process_order(order)` calls `customer_repo.find(order.customer_id)` then later calls `customer_repo.find(order.customer_id)` again (two fetches). A reviewer says "fetch once, reuse" and refactors to a single fetch. Behavior unchanged. But tests that asserted `customer_repo.find.assert_called_with(order.customer_id)` (with `_called_once_with` variants) now fail because the call count changed. Hours of test rework for a behavior-neutral refactor.

The fix: use a fake or spy that asserts on the resulting state or observable behavior, not on internal call structure. Then the refactor causes zero test failures.

**Q3.** When is a mock appropriate? Give an example where you would intentionally choose a mock over a fake.

Hint

The §7.2 list mentions three categories.

**Answer:** Mocks are appropriate when:

(a) The dependency cannot be faked easily. Example: a Stripe SDK whose internal behavior is complex and not worth re-implementing in tests. Mock the specific method (`stripe.Charge.create`) the function calls.

(b) The test is specifically about the interaction protocol. Example: a circuit-breaker test that verifies the breaker opens after three consecutive failures. The test mocks the downstream service, configures it to fail three times, then asserts that the breaker's state changes. The interaction (the number of retries before the breaker opens) is the behavior being tested.

(c) The behavior is the protocol itself, not the result. Example: a retrying HTTP client that should retry on 5xx but not on 4xx. The test mocks the HTTP transport, configures it to return specific status codes, and verifies the retry behavior.

In each case, the call structure is the _subject_ of the test, not the implementation detail. So encoding it in the assertion is appropriate.

**Q4.** A staff engineer pushes back on your proposal to add a Repository layer between the business logic and the ORM (SQLAlchemy in this codebase). Their argument: "SQLAlchemy already gives you a typed object-oriented API to the database. Adding a Repository on top is just an extra layer of indirection; exactly the pattern-tax Chapter 13 warns against. We are a 3-engineer team on a 6-month-old codebase; we do not need this." Construct your response. When are they right and when are they wrong, specifically for this team?

Hint

The Repository's value depends on (a) testing pain, (b) codebase lifetime, (c) likelihood of storage migration, (d) domain-language clarity.

**Answer:** They are partly right and partly wrong; the answer is "it depends on the dimensions they did not weigh."

**Where they are right (don't add it yet):**

* 3-engineer team, 6-month codebase, no storage-migration on the horizon, business logic is thin "store this, retrieve that." Adding a Repository now is pattern-tax in §13.3's exact sense; speculative flexibility that will never be exercised. The ORM-as-Repository shortcut is fine here. Save the work.

**Where they are wrong (might want it soon):**

* If the test suite is already painful (every test of `OrderService` requires Postgres-in-Docker because the test cannot easily substitute the database), that pain compounds linearly with the number of tests. A Repository plus in-memory fake makes 100 unit tests run in milliseconds instead of seconds; the test investment pays back within a quarter even on a small team.
* If business logic is starting to read like ORM code (`session.query(Subscriber).filter(...).filter(...).options(joinedload(...))` inside what should be domain logic), the codebase is leaking persistence into business logic. The Repository's _domain-language_ benefit applies regardless of team size.

**Concrete test:** Look at one randomly-chosen service-layer method. Can you read its purpose in domain terms (`active subscribers in region X`), or does it require translating ORM idioms? If translation is needed, the Repository pays off now, not later.

**My recommendation to the staff engineer:** "You're right that we don't need it everywhere. Let's add it for the one entity where testing is most painful (probably `Customer`) and see if the testing speedup is real. If yes, expand. If no, don't expand."

The general rule (§7.3): Repository pays off when (a) testing is painful enough that fakes matter, or (b) the codebase will outlive a single ORM choice, or (c) business logic should read as business logic. Below those thresholds, the ORM is the Repository; the staff engineer is right.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Identify a mock-heavy test file in your codebase. Pick one mock and rewrite the test using a fake instead. Compare the resulting test for readability and brittleness.

Hint

The fake usually requires writing or finding an in-memory implementation; the upfront cost is balanced by the reduced brittleness.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common finding: the mock-based test is shorter (5-10 lines of mock setup), but each assertion encodes call structure. The fake-based test is longer (10-20 lines for the fake plus 5 for the test), but each assertion is about outcome. After the rewrite, the test reads more naturally as a description of behavior, and a behavior-neutral refactor of the production code does not break it.

The investment in the fake is amortized: the same fake serves many tests. After three or four tests use the fake, the per-test cost is lower than the mock-based equivalent.

**Q6.** Implement an in-memory Repository for one entity in your codebase. Use it in at least three tests. Report the test code before and after.

Hint

Start with the simplest interface (find, save) and add methods as tests need them.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A reasonable in-memory Repository for a Customer entity:
    
    
    class InMemoryCustomerRepository:
        def __init__(self):
            self._customers: dict[str, Customer] = {}
        def find(self, cid: str) -> Optional[Customer]:
            return self._customers.get(cid)
        def save(self, customer: Customer) -> None:
            self._customers[customer.id] = customer
        def find_by_region(self, region: str) -> list[Customer]:
            return [c for c in self._customers.values() if c.region == region]
        def delete(self, cid: str) -> None:
            self._customers.pop(cid, None)
    

Before: three tests with three separate Mock setups. After: three tests that share one fake. The before is longer per test and brittle; the after is shorter per test and robust.

The fake itself is about 15 lines. It is also itself testable: write a few tests that verify the fake's behavior matches the contract (find returns None for missing IDs, save-then-find returns the saved customer, etc.). This is a small investment that pays off across the test suite.

**Q7.** Take an AI-generated test that uses mocks. Apply the §7.6 redirection: rewrite the test using a fake or spy where appropriate. Update the CLAUDE.md with the test-double preference. Run the AI again on a similar test; verify it now uses the fake.

Hint

The CLAUDE.md update is the durable fix; the rewrite is the immediate one.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. The CLAUDE.md update looks like the §7.6 example. After the update, prompt the AI with the same test request; the test should now construct fakes from `tests/fakes/` rather than `Mock()` objects. If the AI does not comply, strengthen the CLAUDE.md with more specific instructions (e.g. "use InMemoryCustomerRepository from tests/fakes/customer_repo.py for all tests of OrderService").

The durable lesson: AI behaviors that violate a team convention are a CLAUDE.md gap. The fix is the CLAUDE.md update, not endless review-and-rewrite of individual PRs.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute the position: "All tests should use real or in-memory implementations; mocks should be banned from the codebase." What is the strongest counter-example?

**Discussion:** For the ban: mocks produce brittleness; fakes produce robustness; if every test uses fakes, the refactoring discipline of Chapter 10 is dramatically smoother; the prohibition is simple and enforceable.

Against the ban: some dependencies cannot reasonably be faked. Vendor SDKs (Stripe, Twilio, large cloud SDKs) are too complex to re-implement; the test must use a mock or a thin wrapper. Some tests are _about_ the interaction protocol (retry behavior, circuit-breaker behavior); the call structure is the subject of the test, and mocking is the natural expression.

The strongest counter-example: testing a circuit-breaker's behavior across many failure scenarios. The breaker's job is to count failures and open after a threshold. Without a mock that can be configured to fail N times then succeed, the test must construct a real failing service, which is impractical and slow. Mocks are essential here.

The synthesis: the right rule is "fakes by default, mocks when justified, justification documented." Banning mocks outright is too strong; making them the default is too weak. Carmen's Currents adopted exactly this rule: PRs with new `Mock()` constructions require a one-line justification in the test docstring.

**Q9.** Trace the relationship between Chapter 5 (test pyramid), Chapter 6 (mutation testing), and this chapter (test doubles). How do the three disciplines compose? Where do they conflict?

**Discussion:**

Compositions:

* **Test pyramid + test doubles** : the pyramid's pressure is for fast unit tests at the bottom. Fast unit tests require lightweight isolation, which is what test doubles provide. Without doubles, unit tests are slow (they hit real dependencies); without the pyramid, the team does not invest in doubles. The two compound.
* **Mutation testing + test doubles** : mutation testing reveals when tests with weak doubles miss bugs. A mock that returns `None` for all calls produces tests that catch nothing; mutation testing surfaces this as low mutation scores even at high coverage. Mutation testing thus disciplines the doubles choice: weak doubles produce weak mutation scores.
* **Pyramid + mutation testing** : the pyramid's shape is the input; the mutation score is the output of each layer. A good pyramid with good doubles produces strong mutation scores at the unit layer; a bad pyramid or bad doubles produces weak scores even at high coverage.

Tensions:

* **Fake fidelity versus speed** : high-fidelity fakes (sqlite, redis-memory-server) catch more bugs but slow down the unit tests, fighting the pyramid's speed pressure. The resolution is to use lower-fidelity fakes for unit tests and higher-fidelity for integration tests; the pyramid distributes the fidelity-cost trade-off across layers.
* **Mock-based test brittleness versus completeness** : mocks let you verify protocols (good for mutation testing on interaction-heavy code) but produce brittle tests (bad for refactoring under test, Chapter 10). The two pressures pull in opposite directions; the resolution is to localize mocks to the small number of protocol-specific tests where they belong and use fakes everywhere else.

The general lesson: the disciplines are not independent. Adopting one without the others produces partial benefits and unexpected costs; adopting all three together produces the test suite that the rest of this book assumes you have.

### Chapter Summary

The five test doubles (§7.1) are a spectrum, and choosing the right level is part of the craft. Dummies satisfy signatures; stubs return canned values; fakes have working logic with shortcuts; spies record interactions; mocks add expectations. Stubs and spies are simple and safe; fakes are the workhorses for stateful dependencies; mocks are the right tool for a narrow class of protocol-specific tests but are over-used in practice.

The Repository pattern (§7.3) makes fakes ergonomic by giving each entity a small interface that test implementations can satisfy. Even with an ORM, an explicit Repository pays off in test substitutability, interface stability, and domain-language clarity. In-memory implementations (§7.4) range from hand-rolled fakes to sqlite and redis-memory-server; the right fidelity depends on the layer (unit tests want speed, integration tests want fidelity).

Mocking hurts (§7.5) when it over-specifies call structure, drifts from reality, or lies through silent defaults. The fixes are using fakes for stateful behavior, contract-testing mocks against real interfaces periodically, and preferring strict mocks that fail on unexpected calls.

The AI-Layer (§7.6) is that LLMs over-mock by default; the CLAUDE.md instruction is the durable fix; the gradual migration to fakes is the codebase-level fix. The Helix and Trellis examples illustrate both the cost of mocking (the 47-test refactor) and the payoff of fakes (the comparable refactor that broke zero tests).

Chapter 8 takes the test discipline to its strategic question: when to TDD and when to test-after. With Chapters 5, 6, and 7 in place, the team has the tools to choose; without them, the choice is academic because the tests would not be good enough either way.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Write one in-memory Repository this week.** Pick the most-mocked entity in your test suite. Build the in-memory version (15–30 lines). Use it in three tests. Compare to the mock version for readability and brittleness. Then expand to more tests as you touch them.
  2. **Make new`Mock()` constructions require a one-line justification in the test.** Not "ban mocks"; too strong. But making them require explicit reasoning shifts the default toward fakes without imposing a rule no one will follow.
  3. **Add a "Test doubles" section to your CLAUDE.md.** Specify: fakes by default for stateful dependencies, stubs for canned values, spies for side effects, mocks only when justified. The agent's defaults shift toward your defaults; review noise drops within a sprint.

---

## Chapter 8: TDD vs Test-After — The Honest Debate

A junior engineer pushed a PR at Helix containing a 400-line feature with no tests. The review comment from Aki: "Where are the tests?" The junior's response: "I'll add them after." Three days later, the engineer added 30 tests, all of which passed on the first run. The PR merged.

A senior engineer at Trellis pushed a PR containing a 380-line feature with 25 tests interleaved with the implementation. The tests were written before the code that satisfied them; the implementation evolved through several rewrites as the tests forced clarification. The PR merged.

Both engineers shipped working code. Both PRs had test coverage. Both engineers learned something from the experience. The interesting question is not "which engineer was right?" but "for what kind of work is each pattern appropriate?" The honest answer is that both patterns are defensible, and the dogmatic version of either is wrong. This chapter is about how to decide.

### 8.1 What TDD actually is

Kent Beck's _Test-Driven Development by Example_ (2002) introduced the discipline that most people now call TDD. The core loop:

  1. **Red** : write a test for behavior that does not yet exist. Run it; it fails.
  2. **Green** : write the minimum code to make the test pass. Do not generalize; do not refactor. Run the test; it passes.
  3. **Refactor** : with the test passing, restructure the code (extract functions, rename, deduplicate) without changing behavior. Run the tests after each refactor; they continue to pass.

The loop repeats: red, green, refactor, red, green, refactor. Each cycle is small (a few minutes), and the test suite grows as the implementation grows.

#### Definition: Test-Driven Development

TDD is a discipline in which tests are written before the code that satisfies them, and the implementation is built incrementally through the red-green-refactor loop. The tests are not primarily a verification tool; they are a _thinking_ tool that forces the engineer to clarify the desired behavior before writing the code that produces it.

In plain language: TDD is a way of thinking about a problem in small, testable pieces. The test is the question; the code is the answer. The discipline is the cycle that prevents either side from getting too far ahead of the other.

#### What TDD is not

Several misconceptions are common enough to be worth naming.

* TDD is **not** "write tests at any point and call yourself TDD." The discipline is specifically test-first; test-after produces tests (good) but not TDD (different).
* TDD is **not** "write all the tests, then all the code." That is _test-first_ but not _TDD_ ; the incremental cycle is part of the discipline.
* TDD is **not** a guarantee of better code. Engineers who do TDD badly produce bad code in test-driven fashion. The discipline supports good thinking; it does not replace it.
* TDD is **not** a religion. Beck himself has said that TDD is a tool with appropriate contexts. The religious version (TDD always, no exceptions) is a strawman that critics rightly attack and that practitioners rarely defend.

#### Why the discipline works when it works

When TDD works well, three mechanisms compound:

* **The test forces clarification of intent.** Writing the test before the code requires the engineer to state precisely what the code should do. Ambiguities surface in test-writing rather than in implementation; the conversation is cheaper.
* **The incremental cycle keeps the cognitive load small.** Each cycle is a tiny piece (one behavior, a few minutes). The engineer never holds more than a few minutes of work in their head. Mistakes are cheap; backtracking is one cycle.
* **The tests are usable.** Tests written first tend to be focused, decoupled, and at the right granularity. Tests written after the fact often replicate the implementation's structure and end up brittle.

When TDD does not work well, the most common cause is that the discipline was applied to a problem where the _test_ is not the right unit of thinking. We explore this in §8.3.

### 8.2 What test-after actually is

The alternative pattern: write the code first, then write the tests. The engineer explores the problem space by writing code, possibly prototyping, possibly throwing away early attempts. When the code seems right, tests are added that exercise the resulting interface.

#### Definition: Test-After

Test-after development writes the implementation first, then writes tests that verify the implementation's behavior. The implementation is shaped by the engineer's understanding of the problem rather than by the tests; the tests document and protect the resulting code.

In plain language: explore first, lock in afterward. The tests are the lock; the exploration is the work.

#### When test-after works well

* **Exploratory work.** When the problem space is unfamiliar, writing code is part of the learning. Tests written too early would lock in a wrong shape.
* **Spike-then-stabilize.** A _spike_ is a deliberate exploratory implementation, often thrown away. Tests are added after the spike has revealed the right shape.
* **Refactoring of legacy code.** Adding tests _after_ the code already exists is the natural pattern; characterization tests (Chapter 10) lock in current behavior before refactoring.
* **Pair work with AI in 2026.** When an AI generates a draft implementation in seconds, writing tests after the AI's draft is the natural workflow; writing tests before the AI would slow the feedback loop.

#### When test-after fails

* **The "I'll write tests later" trap.** Tests written later are often written after the engineer has moved on, the context is fading, and the tests end up shallow. Many "test-after" projects are actually "no-test" projects with good intentions.
* **Test-shaped-by-implementation.** Tests written after the code tend to mirror the implementation's structure, sometimes asserting on internal details that should be encapsulated. The tests become coupled to implementation; refactoring breaks tests unnecessarily.
* **The over-confident reviewer** : the PR author says "tests are coming" and the reviewer accepts. Tests do not come, or come weakly. The discipline of test-before forces the test conversation upfront.

### 8.3 The honest case for TDD

The honest case for TDD has three parts.

#### Part 1: TDD is a thinking tool, not a verification tool

Beck's framing in _TDD by Example_ is the framing that survives 2026 scrutiny: TDD is for working out what you want the code to do. The tests are the question; the code is the answer; the discipline forces the question to be asked clearly before the answer is written.

This is most valuable when the problem is _underspecified_. If you do not know exactly what the function should do, writing a test forces you to commit. The commitment is cheap (a failing test) and revisable (write a different test). The alternative is writing code that does something _like_ what you want, and discovering during integration that the something-like is wrong.

#### Part 2: TDD produces testable design

A function that is hard to test usually has design problems: too many dependencies, too much hidden state, too much I/O entangled with logic. Writing the test first surfaces these problems immediately; you cannot easily write a test for a poorly designed function, so the discipline pushes you toward a better design.

This is the deepest argument for TDD. The tests are _forcing functions_ for design quality. Engineers who do TDD for years internalize the design pressure; their code becomes naturally testable, naturally decoupled, naturally focused. The skill compounds beyond the discipline itself.

#### Part 3: TDD's incremental cycle keeps you out of debugger hell

A failing test surfaces a specific behavioral gap. Fixing the gap is a known, bounded problem. The alternative (writing 500 lines, then noticing five things are wrong) requires diagnosing which of the five interacts with which, and the debugging cost is non-linear in the number of issues. TDD's small cycle keeps each issue isolated.

This is the productivity argument. A working engineer doing TDD does not spend hours in the debugger; they spend minutes in the test-and-fix loop, repeatedly. The cycle time is faster _in steady state_ even though the per-feature time can look slower at the start.

### 8.4 The honest case for test-after

The honest case for test-after also has three parts.

#### Part 1: Test-after is the natural pattern for exploratory work

When you do not yet understand the problem, writing tests first forces premature commitment. The exploration is the work; the tests come once the exploration produces a stable shape.

Aki at Helix worked on a new pricing rule engine in 2024. The first version was a 200-line spike with no tests, written in a day to confirm that the design was viable. The second version, two weeks later, was the production-quality version with tests. Writing tests during the spike would have been wasted: the spike's shape was abandoned and rewritten. Tests on the production version were valuable; tests on the spike would have been deletions.

#### Part 2: Test-after pairs naturally with AI in 2026

When an AI agent generates an implementation in seconds, the human's role is review, integration, and _test_ (Chapter 1). The natural workflow is: the AI drafts the implementation; the human reviews and asks for tests; the AI generates tests; the human strengthens the tests with mutation testing and property-based tests (Chapter 6).

Writing tests first in this workflow would require the human to write tests for code that does not exist yet, then ask the AI to satisfy them. This works for some tasks (tests-as-spec, see §8.5) but is awkward for others. For routine work, test-after is faster and the quality with mutation discipline is comparable.

#### Part 3: Test-after fits some problem shapes better

Not all code is amenable to test-first. Examples:

* **UI work** : the visual result is the spec, and writing visual tests before the UI is harder than writing the UI first and adding visual regression tests after.
* **Data exploration** : a script that produces a one-time report is exploratory; tests for it would be over-engineering.
* **Performance optimization** : the optimization is the work; the test is "the function still produces the same output" (covered by existing tests) or "the function is faster" (a benchmark, not a test).
* **Migrations** : writing a migration script for a one-time data move is test-after by nature; the verification is post-hoc.

Trying to TDD these shapes produces friction. The dogmatic response (TDD anyway) is the wrong response; the discipline is to recognize when test-after fits and to choose it.

### 8.5 Tests-as-spec: the 2026 dominant workflow

A specific workflow has emerged in 2025-2026 that combines the best of test-first and AI assistance: tests-as-spec.

#### The workflow

  1. The human writes failing tests that describe the desired behavior. The tests are the spec.
  2. The human asks the AI to write the implementation that makes the tests pass.
  3. The AI generates the implementation; the human runs the tests.
  4. Iterate: if the tests fail or the implementation is poor, refine the tests or ask the AI to revise.
  5. When the tests pass and the implementation looks reasonable, merge.

This is TDD with the AI as the implementer. The human's job becomes test-writing and review; the AI's job is implementation. The discipline of test-first transfers directly to AI work.

#### Why it works

* **The test as spec is a precise instruction to the AI.** "Make this test pass" is less ambiguous than "write a function that handles refunds." The test is executable specification; the AI's compliance is verifiable.
* **The cycle is fast.** The AI's draft is seconds; the test run is seconds; the cycle is a minute. The human iterates many times per hour, refining the spec as understanding deepens.
* **The resulting tests are real tests.** Unlike test-after AI generations (which can be coverage filler, Chapter 5), the tests in tests-as-spec are written for behavior and not for coverage; they encode intent.

#### A position worth taking: tests-as-spec is TDD, just with a different driver

It is fashionable in 2026 to describe tests-as-spec as a "new" workflow distinct from TDD. This book disagrees. The discipline at the core of TDD is _write the test first, then write the minimum code to pass it, then refactor_. Tests-as-spec is the red-green-refactor loop with the green step delegated to an agent. The human still writes the test (red), the implementation is produced to make it pass (green), and the human refactors what came back (refactor; though "refactor" in this context often means "ask the agent to revise"). The structure is preserved; the labor moves.

Saying "tests-as-spec is TDD's heir" is more useful than saying "tests-as-spec is something new" because it locates the workflow in a thirty-year tradition with known failure modes and known compounding effects. Engineers who treat tests-as-spec as a 2026 invention are likely to re-derive Beck's lessons from scratch. Engineers who treat it as TDD applied to a new driver inherit the lessons.

#### The catch

Tests-as-spec works for problems where the _test_ is the right specification: pure functions, well-defined transformations, API contracts. It works less well for problems where the spec is visual, exploratory, or emergent. UI work, performance optimization, and data exploration usually do not fit.

Aki uses tests-as-spec at Helix for most backend work. For UI work, the team uses a different workflow: AI generates a UI draft from a description; the human iterates visually; tests come after. Both workflows are appropriate to their context.

### 8.6 Choosing the pattern for the task

The honest position is that TDD, test-after, and tests-as-spec are three patterns; the engineer chooses among them based on the task. A rough decision matrix:

Task type | Recommended pattern | Reason  
---|---|---  
Pure function with known spec | TDD or tests-as-spec | Test is precise; cycle is fast  
Algorithm with subtle edge cases | TDD or tests-as-spec | Test-first surfaces the edge cases  
API contract implementation | tests-as-spec | The contract test is the spec  
Exploratory data work | Test-after | Tests would lock in throwaway shape  
UI work | Test-after | Visual spec is harder to express in tests  
Performance optimization | Test-after | Optimization is the work; benchmarks come after  
Legacy code refactoring | Test-after (characterization) | Tests lock in current behavior before refactoring  
Vendor SDK wrapping | Test-after | Spec emerges from understanding the SDK  
Bug fix with clear repro | TDD | Test reproduces the bug; fix makes it pass  
Standard CRUD endpoint | tests-as-spec or test-after | Either works  
  
The matrix is a heuristic; the principle is _fit-for-purpose_. The dogmatic positions ("always TDD" or "TDD is dead") miss this; the discipline is to know the patterns and pick wisely.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "TDD is faster overall because it catches bugs early." The mistake is overgeneralizing from contexts where TDD does work well. For pure functions with clear specs, TDD is often faster; for exploratory work where you do not yet know what the spec is, TDD is slower because you must repeatedly throw away tests as the spec changes.

A second common misconception is "test-after is just an excuse not to test." The mistake is conflating _discipline_ with _pattern_. A disciplined test-after engineer writes the same tests they would have written under TDD; an undisciplined TDD engineer writes shallow tests that satisfy the cycle but not the intent. The pattern is not what makes the tests good; the discipline is. The pattern is a tool for the discipline.

### 8.7 The mechanics of TDD: a worked example

Consider a real-shape problem at Trellis: implementing a `calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date)` function. The spec, as the engineer understands it: 5% of the unpaid balance, charged once per 30 days late, capped at 25% of the invoice total.

TDD cycle 1:
    
    
    # Red
    def test_no_fee_when_not_late():
        invoice = Invoice(total=100, due_date=date(2026, 5, 1), paid=0)
        fee = calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date=date(2026, 5, 1))
        assert fee == 0
    # Test fails: function doesn't exist.
    
    # Green
    def calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date):
        return 0
    # Test passes. The implementation is trivially correct for this case.
    
    # Refactor: nothing to refactor yet.
    

TDD cycle 2:
    
    
    # Red
    def test_fee_when_30_days_late():
        invoice = Invoice(total=100, due_date=date(2026, 5, 1), paid=0)
        fee = calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date=date(2026, 5, 31))
        assert fee == 5  # 5% of 100
    # Test fails: function returns 0.
    
    # Green
    def calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date):
        days_late = (current_date - invoice.due_date).days
        if days_late < 30:
            return 0
        return invoice.total * 0.05
    # Test passes.
    
    # Refactor: extract a constant for clarity.
    LATE_FEE_RATE = 0.05
    def calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date):
        days_late = (current_date - invoice.due_date).days
        if days_late < 30:
            return 0
        return invoice.total * LATE_FEE_RATE
    # Tests still pass.
    

TDD cycle 3:
    
    
    # Red
    def test_fee_compounds_monthly():
        invoice = Invoice(total=100, due_date=date(2026, 5, 1), paid=0)
        fee = calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date=date(2026, 7, 30))
        assert fee == 15  # 5% × 3 (May, June, July periods)
    # Test fails: function returns 5.
    
    # Green
    def calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date):
        days_late = (current_date - invoice.due_date).days
        if days_late < 30:
            return 0
        months_late = days_late // 30
        return invoice.total * LATE_FEE_RATE * months_late
    # Test passes.
    
    # Refactor: nothing yet.
    

TDD cycle 4:
    
    
    # Red
    def test_fee_capped_at_25_percent():
        invoice = Invoice(total=100, due_date=date(2026, 1, 1), paid=0)
        fee = calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date=date(2026, 12, 31))
        assert fee == 25  # 364 days late → 12 periods × 5% = 60, capped at 25% of 100 = 25
    # Test fails: function returns 55.
    
    # Green
    def calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date):
        days_late = (current_date - invoice.due_date).days
        if days_late < 30:
            return 0
        months_late = days_late // 30
        fee = invoice.total * LATE_FEE_RATE * months_late
        return min(fee, invoice.total * 0.25)
    # Test passes.
    
    # Refactor: extract the cap constant; consider extracting fee calculation.
    LATE_FEE_RATE = 0.05
    LATE_FEE_CAP_RATE = 0.25
    
    def calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date):
        days_late = (current_date - invoice.due_date).days
        if days_late < 30:
            return 0
        months_late = days_late // 30
        fee = invoice.total * LATE_FEE_RATE * months_late
        cap = invoice.total * LATE_FEE_CAP_RATE
        return min(fee, cap)
    

After four cycles, the function handles all four cases that the engineer thought of. A property-based test (Chapter 6) might surface additional cases (negative paid amounts, zero invoice totals, very-far-future dates); a mutation test would reveal whether the assertions are strong enough. But the core implementation is built incrementally, with the tests as the spec, and the engineer never holds more than a few minutes of work in their head at once.

### 8.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A teammate says: "I do TDD! I write all the test cases for a feature first, then I write the implementation, then I run the tests." Is this TDD? Why or why not? If you wanted to coach them toward the canonical practice, what specific change in their workflow would you suggest, and why does that change matter?

Hint

TDD has three parts: test-first, _plus_ one-at-a-time, _plus_ minimum-implementation. Which is the teammate missing?

**Answer:** Not quite TDD; it is _test-first_ without the cycle. The teammate is missing the _one-at-a-time_ and _minimum-implementation_ parts. Writing all tests then all code is the "draw the whole owl, then write tests" pattern; it loses TDD's main benefits.

The specific change: ask them to write _one_ failing test, then write _the minimum code to make it pass_ (even if obviously incomplete), then write the _next_ failing test, and so on. The "minimum code to pass" rule is the crucial one; it prevents over-engineering. When the test asks for "return 0 for zero days late", the implementation returns `0`. The test gets greener; you write the next test. The implementation grows incrementally, driven by the tests, not by anticipation.

Why does this matter? Three reasons:

* **Cognitive load stays small.** One test = one decision. Writing 20 tests at once requires holding 20 specs in your head simultaneously.
* **The implementation evolves to match the tests, not the engineer's prediction.** Over-engineering is the natural failure mode of test-first-without-cycle.
* **The refactor step has a job.** With one test passing and the minimum code in place, the refactor step has a small concrete improvement to make. With all 20 tests already written, the refactor step turns into "rewrite the whole thing."

The cycle is the discipline, not the order.

**Q2.** Give a task type for which TDD is well-suited and a task type for which it is poorly suited. Justify each.

Hint

The §8.6 matrix categorizes tasks.

**Answer:** Well-suited: a pure function with a known spec, like the `calculate_late_fee` example. The test is precise (one input, one expected output); the cycle is fast (write test, write code, refactor, repeat); the test-first discipline forces clarification of the spec (what happens at exactly 30 days? at 25.5%?). The friction is low; the value is high.

Poorly-suited: visual UI work for a new product feature where the spec is "make it look right." Writing tests for "looks right" is harder than writing the UI and iterating visually. The test-first cycle would consume effort on test mechanics that does not advance the visual goal. Test-after with visual regression tooling is the natural fit.

The principle: TDD fits where the test is a precise spec; it does not fit where the spec is emergent, visual, or exploratory.

**Q3.** Aki and a junior engineer are pairing on a new pricing rule. Aki writes 8 failing tests for the rule's behavior, then opens Claude Code and asks the agent to make them pass. The junior asks: "Wait, is this TDD or is this just prompting?" Aki says it is TDD. The junior is unconvinced because Aki did not write the implementation. Who is right, and what is the deeper point the junior may be missing about what "TDD" actually is?

Hint

Recall §8.5 and the position taken in §8.5 about tests-as-spec being TDD-with-a-different-driver.

**Answer:** Aki is right; the junior is confusing _labor distribution_ with _discipline_. TDD's core is red-green-refactor: write a failing test, write the minimum to make it pass, then refactor. The discipline says nothing about _who_ writes the "green" step. In classical TDD, the same engineer who wrote the test also writes the implementation. In tests-as-spec, the human writes the test (red), the agent produces the implementation (green), and the human reviews/iterates (refactor). The cycle is preserved; only the labor moves.

The deeper point the junior may be missing: TDD's value was never that the engineer's fingers typed both the test and the implementation. Its value was the _thinking discipline_ of stating the spec precisely before producing the code, plus the _iteration cycle_ of small failing-then-passing steps. Both are preserved in tests-as-spec; arguably _more_ preserved, because the agent's implementation is fast enough that the cycle can run many times in an hour.

What is _not_ preserved: the engineer's hands-on familiarity with the implementation. The agent wrote the code; the human read and approved it. This is real cost; it shifts where the engineer's mental model lives. For routine work this is fine. For genuinely novel logic where the engineer needs to deeply understand the implementation, classical TDD (where the human writes the code) may still be the right choice.

The junior's instinct — that tests-as-spec is "different" from TDD — is half right (different _who_) and half wrong (same _what_). Help them see that the discipline is the cycle, not the typing.

**Q4.** Defend or refute: "TDD makes refactoring safe." When is the claim true, and when is it false?

Hint

Refactoring safety depends on test quality, not on when the tests were written.

**Answer:** The claim is partially true. TDD tends to produce tests at the right granularity and with the right decoupling (because the tests force testable design, §8.3 Part 2). Such tests do make refactoring safer; the test suite catches behavior changes without false alarms for structural changes.

But the safety comes from the _test quality_ , not from the _order_ of test-writing. Test-after engineers who write equally good tests achieve equal refactoring safety. TDD's correlation with safe refactoring is mediated by test quality; the discipline is one path to good tests, not the only path. The claim "TDD makes refactoring safe" is misleading if it suggests that the safety is automatic; the safety is earned by the tests, not by the order.

The claim is false in cases where TDD produces low-quality tests anyway. An engineer who does TDD but writes tests that over-couple to implementation produces fragile tests; refactoring under those tests is unsafe regardless of when they were written.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Pick a small task you would normally do without TDD. Re-do it with TDD. Compare the result, the time taken, and the final test suite quality.

Hint

A pure-function task is a fair comparison; UI work is not.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common finding for a pure-function task (e.g. a string parser, a price calculator): TDD takes 10-20% more time on the first pass but produces tests that are visibly stronger (more focused, more decoupled) than the test-after version. The first time you do this, the TDD result feels slow; after several iterations, the speed difference shrinks as the cycle becomes habitual.

A second-order finding: the TDD tests _survive_ the next refactor. The test-after tests sometimes break on refactors and need updating. The total cost across the lifecycle is often lower for TDD.

The exercise is most informative when repeated on three or four small tasks; the pattern emerges across the sample rather than from any single instance.

**Q6.** Use the tests-as-spec workflow with an AI agent for a small feature. Write the tests yourself; ask the agent to implement. Record what worked, what required iteration, and what the agent missed.

Hint

Start with a function whose contract you can fully specify with 3-5 tests.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common pattern: the agent's first implementation passes most tests, fails one or two. The failure is often a missed edge case that the test did surface; the agent revises and passes on the second try. After the implementation passes, ask the agent to add edge-case tests (negative inputs, empty inputs, boundary values); the agent often adds useful ones that you had not thought to specify.

The lesson: tests-as-spec is highly effective when the spec is _clear enough_. The 2026 reality is that humans often write specs that are clear in their head but ambiguous on paper; the test discipline forces the clarification, and the AI's failure to comply with an ambiguous test surfaces the ambiguity.

**Q7.** Identify a task in your work history where TDD would have been the wrong choice. Explain why, and describe what you would do instead.

Hint

Tasks with emergent specs, visual specs, or exploratory specs are candidates.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common candidate: writing a dashboard visualization where the spec was "show the user useful metrics, the team will iterate." The actual right metrics emerged after three weeks of stakeholder feedback. TDD would have produced tests for an early metric set that was abandoned within a week; the tests would have been deletions.

The right pattern was: build the dashboard quickly with no tests; iterate visually with the product team; lock in the final metric set with tests once the iteration stabilized. The test investment was justified after, not during, the exploratory phase. The discipline was _not_ "no tests"; the discipline was "no tests yet, then comprehensive tests at the right moment."

The reasoning that justifies test-after here: the cost of writing tests for the early dashboards was high (visual tests are expensive) and the lifetime of those tests was short (the dashboards were rewritten weekly). The cost-lifetime balance flipped against test-first.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue both sides: (a) "In 2026 with AI agents implementing code, TDD becomes more valuable because the tests are the human's distinctive contribution." (b) "In 2026 with AI agents implementing code, TDD becomes less valuable because the cycle's speed advantage disappears when the AI generates fast."

**Discussion:** For (a): the human's role in 2026 is review and direction (Chapter 1); writing the test is a precise act of direction; tests-as-spec is the workflow that brings this together. The discipline of writing tests first is therefore the _core_ human skill in AI-augmented engineering. TDD's value compounds because each test is a piece of the codebase's intent that survives model transitions.

For (b): TDD's speed advantage came from the small cycle keeping the engineer's cognitive load low. The AI now generates many lines of code in seconds; the cognitive bottleneck moves to _review_ , not to _writing_. The TDD cycle's small-step advantage matters less when the writing step is no longer the bottleneck.

The synthesis: TDD's _thinking-tool_ value (Part 1 of §8.3) is robust to the AI shift; the discipline of writing the test first still clarifies the spec, which is needed whether the human or the AI writes the implementation. TDD's _productivity_ value is partially eaten by AI's speed; the cycle is faster but the relative gain over test-after is smaller. The net is that TDD remains valuable but for slightly different reasons in 2026: as a spec discipline rather than as a productivity discipline.

**Q9.** Consider the claim: "Test-after is acceptable only if the engineer would have written the same tests under TDD." Is this a useful standard? What does it imply about how to review test-after PRs?

**Discussion:** The claim is a useful standard for one specific purpose: it sets the _quality bar_ for test-after tests at the level that TDD discipline would have produced. An engineer who shipped weak test-after tests by claiming "I did write tests" cannot defend the claim if the tests do not match what TDD would have surfaced.

But the standard has limits: TDD is itself not a fixed quality bar (different engineers produce different tests). The standard "would you have written the same tests under TDD" assumes the engineer can answer honestly, which not all can. And some tests are uniquely test-after (characterization tests for legacy code, visual regression tests for UI) where the TDD comparison is not meaningful.

The operational version: in PR review, the reviewer asks "do these tests cover the behavior the code is supposed to have, with the assertion strength they would have under good TDD?" If yes, the test-after is acceptable. If no, ask for the missing tests. The standard is internal to the team's discipline, not to the order of writing.

Apply this in Currents-style code review (Chapter 15): the reviewer's question is about test quality, not about test order. The team that internalizes this saves itself the religious wars and focuses on the actual outcome.

### Chapter Summary

TDD (§8.1) is the red-green-refactor discipline: write a failing test, write minimum code to pass it, refactor with the tests as the safety net. Test-after (§8.2) is the alternative: write code first, write tests after. Both are defensible; both have failure modes.

The honest case for TDD (§8.3) is that the test is a _thinking tool_ that clarifies intent, that the discipline produces testable design as a side effect, and that the small cycle keeps cognitive load low. The honest case for test-after (§8.4) is that exploratory work, UI work, performance work, and AI-assisted drafting all fit test-after more naturally than test-first.

Tests-as-spec (§8.5) is the 2026 workflow that combines TDD's discipline with AI's speed: humans write failing tests as a precise spec; AI implements; humans review and iterate. This is increasingly the dominant workflow for backend tasks with clear specs; it is awkward for visual or exploratory tasks.

The matrix (§8.6) gives task-by-task guidance; the dogmatic positions ("always TDD" or "TDD is dead") miss this nuance. The discipline is to know the patterns and choose; the religion is to apply one pattern everywhere.

Chapter 9 opens Part IV (Refactoring) with the twelve refactorings that pay for themselves. The test discipline from Chapters 5-8 is the prerequisite: refactoring without tests is rewriting with risk; refactoring under test (Chapter 10) is the safe-net version of the same work. The AI-Layer of Part IV is that AI is excellent at mechanical refactoring at speed; the human's job is to know which refactorings are appropriate and to trust the AI's mechanical execution against the tests.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **For your next pure function with a known spec, do one full red-green-refactor cycle by hand.** Even if you normally test-after. The exercise rebuilds the muscle memory; tests-as-spec with AI becomes a small extension of TDD rather than a new workflow you have to learn.
  2. **For your next AI-assisted feature, write the failing tests yourself before opening the agent.** This is Aki's habit. The cycle is shorter and the spec is sharper than describing the work in prose.
  3. **Stop using TDD vs test-after as a moral stance.** Both are tools. Pick by task type per §8.6's matrix. The dogmatic positions are losing time arguments; the calibrated engineer ships.

---

## Chapter 9: The 12 Refactorings That Pay for Themselves

Martin Fowler's _Refactoring_ (1999, 2nd ed. 2018) catalogues over sixty refactorings. The book is excellent reference material; it is poor pedagogy for the working engineer in 2026 because the sixty are not equally useful. About a dozen pay for themselves so often that they should be reflexive; the other fifty are situational; many are niche.

This chapter teaches the twelve refactorings that, in our experience across many codebases, account for the majority of valuable real-world refactoring work. Each is taught with a real-shape before/after, the signal that should trigger it, and the AI-Layer note about what the agent can and cannot do.

Asher Goldman at Trellis applied all twelve in the first quarter of 2026. The cumulative effect on a particular subsystem: 3,200 lines of legacy code became 2,400 lines with the same behavior; the test suite became more robust (mutation score rose from 51% to 73%); the time to add a new feature in that subsystem dropped from days to hours. The investment was about three weeks of dedicated refactoring effort across the quarter; the payoff continued accruing for the rest of the year.

### 9.1 Refactoring 1: Extract Function

**Signal** : a function is more than 30-50 lines, or contains multiple comments separating logical sections, or has comments like `# now compute the discount` that read like function names.

**Before** :
    
    
    def process_order(order, customer):
        # Validate the order
        if not order.items:
            raise InvalidOrderError("no items")
        if order.total < 0:
            raise InvalidOrderError("negative total")
        for item in order.items:
            if item.quantity <= 0:
                raise InvalidOrderError(f"invalid quantity for {item.sku}")
    
        # Compute discount
        discount = 0
        if customer.tier == 'enterprise':
            discount += 0.10
        if order.total > 1000:
            discount += 0.05
        if customer.is_grandfathered:
            discount += 0.03
    
        # Apply discount
        order.total = order.total * (1 - discount)
    
        # Save and notify
        save_order(order)
        notify_customer(customer, order)
    

**After** :
    
    
    def process_order(order, customer):
        validate_order(order)
        discount = compute_discount(order, customer)
        order.total = order.total * (1 - discount)
        save_order(order)
        notify_customer(customer, order)
    
    def validate_order(order):
        if not order.items:
            raise InvalidOrderError("no items")
        if order.total < 0:
            raise InvalidOrderError("negative total")
        for item in order.items:
            if item.quantity <= 0:
                raise InvalidOrderError(f"invalid quantity for {item.sku}")
    
    def compute_discount(order, customer):
        discount = 0
        if customer.tier == 'enterprise':
            discount += 0.10
        if order.total > 1000:
            discount += 0.05
        if customer.is_grandfathered:
            discount += 0.03
        return discount
    

The benefit: `process_order` now reads as a list of steps, each named in domain language; the validation and discount logic are independently testable.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents perform Extract Function reliably. The risk is that the agent extracts too eagerly, producing many tiny functions that fragment readability. The human's judgment is needed for the _granularity_ : when does extracting reduce clarity rather than improve it?

### 9.2 Refactoring 2: Inline Function

**Signal** : a function adds no semantic value beyond what its body would have. The function name and the body are basically the same, or the function is called only once and the call site is no clearer than the inlined body.

**Before** :
    
    
    def is_premium_customer(customer):
        return customer.tier == 'enterprise'
    
    def get_pricing(customer, product):
        if is_premium_customer(customer):
            return product.enterprise_price
        return product.standard_price
    

If `is_premium_customer` is called only from `get_pricing` and the function name does not clarify the call site:

**After** :
    
    
    def get_pricing(customer, product):
        if customer.tier == 'enterprise':
            return product.enterprise_price
        return product.standard_price
    

The benefit: one less function to read; the condition is now visible inline.

This refactoring is the inverse of Extract Function. The point is _direction_ : Extract Function consolidates repeated logic into a named unit; Inline Function un-wraps a unit that did not earn its name.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents perform Inline Function safely when the function has one caller. They are less reliable when the function has multiple callers; inlining changes the codebase shape in ways the agent may not fully consider.

### 9.3 Refactoring 3: Rename

**Signal** : a name is misleading, generic, or out of date. Common offenders: `data`, `info`, `process`, `handle`, `manager`, `helper`. Less common but more dangerous: a name that was accurate when written and is now wrong because the surrounding code evolved.

**Before** :
    
    
    def process(data):
        """Apply tax to the order total."""
        return data * 0.08
    

**After** :
    
    
    def apply_tax(amount):
        return amount * 0.08
    

The benefit: the function's purpose is now visible at the call site; the parameter name matches what is actually passed.

Rename is the most underrated refactoring. Chapter 18 (Naming as Design) is dedicated to it. In this chapter, the point is mechanical: in 2026 with rename tools in every IDE and most agents, the operation is cheap and the value is high.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents perform Rename safely when the rename is purely mechanical (a function or variable in a single language). They fail when the name is also encoded outside the codebase (in serialized formats, in external configuration, in the database schema). The human's job is to scope the rename: code-only is safe; cross-system requires more care.

### 9.4 Refactoring 4: Move

**Signal** : a function is in the wrong file or module. It uses more of another module's data than of its current module's. It belongs conceptually to a different domain.

**Before** (in `src/orders.py`):
    
    
    def is_customer_eligible_for_discount(customer):
        if customer.tier == 'enterprise':
            return True
        if customer.is_grandfathered:
            return True
        return False
    

The function takes only a `customer`; it uses nothing from `orders`. It belongs in `customers.py`.

**After** (in `src/customers.py`):
    
    
    def is_eligible_for_discount(customer):
        if customer.tier == 'enterprise':
            return True
        if customer.is_grandfathered:
            return True
        return False
    

The benefit: the function is now near related code; future readers find it where they look for customer logic.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents perform Move reliably for function-level moves within a language. Cross-module moves with import updates are usually handled correctly; cross-language moves (e.g. Python helper to a Rust crate) require human direction.

### 9.5 Refactoring 5: Replace Conditional with Polymorphism

**Signal** : a long chain of conditionals (`if/elif/else` or `switch`) on a type tag, with the same chain repeated in multiple places.

**Before** :
    
    
    def compute_shipping_cost(order):
        if order.shipping_type == 'standard':
            return 5.99
        elif order.shipping_type == 'express':
            return 14.99
        elif order.shipping_type == 'overnight':
            return 29.99
        elif order.shipping_type == 'free':
            return 0
        raise ValueError(f"Unknown shipping type: {order.shipping_type}")
    
    def get_shipping_label(order):
        if order.shipping_type == 'standard':
            return "Standard (5-7 business days)"
        elif order.shipping_type == 'express':
            return "Express (2-3 business days)"
        # ... and so on
    

**After** :
    
    
    class ShippingType(Protocol):
        def cost(self) -> float: ...
        def label(self) -> str: ...
    
    class StandardShipping:
        def cost(self): return 5.99
        def label(self): return "Standard (5-7 business days)"
    
    class ExpressShipping:
        def cost(self): return 14.99
        def label(self): return "Express (2-3 business days)"
    
    # ... and so on
    

The benefit: the conditional logic is replaced by polymorphism; adding a new shipping type means adding one class, not modifying every conditional.

This refactoring is often over-applied (Chapter 13). The signal is _the same chain repeated in many places_ ; one isolated conditional may not need polymorphism. The trade-off is added abstraction for added flexibility; weigh by how often the chain repeats.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents perform this refactoring competently but tend to over-eagerly apply it. The human's judgment is needed: is the chain repeated enough to justify the abstraction?

### 9.6 Refactoring 6: Replace Magic Number with Constant

**Signal** : a literal value appears in code with no obvious meaning. The reader has to guess what `0.08` or `1024` represents.

**Before** :
    
    
    def calculate_total(subtotal):
        return subtotal * 1.08
    
    def is_file_too_large(path):
        return os.path.getsize(path) > 1024 * 1024 * 10
    

**After** :
    
    
    SALES_TAX_RATE = 0.08
    MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE_BYTES = 10 * 1024 * 1024
    
    def calculate_total(subtotal):
        return subtotal * (1 + SALES_TAX_RATE)
    
    def is_file_too_large(path):
        return os.path.getsize(path) > MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE_BYTES
    

The benefit: the meaning is named at the place where it lives; future maintainers can find and update the constant in one place.

A subtlety: not every literal needs a constant. `0` and `1` rarely need names; `len(items) > 0` does not need `MIN_ITEMS_FOR_PROCESSING = 1`. The signal is _unexplained_ ; if the meaning is obvious, leave the literal.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents are excellent at this refactoring and tend to apply it too eagerly. The human's judgment is needed for the _naming_ : a poorly-named constant is worse than a magic number, because it suggests a meaning that may be wrong.

### 9.7 Refactoring 7: Extract Class

**Signal** : a class has too many responsibilities. Its methods divide into clear sub-groups, with each sub-group using a different subset of the class's fields.

**Before** :
    
    
    class Customer:
        def __init__(self, id, name, email, address_line1, address_line2,
                     city, state, postal_code, country):
            self.id = id
            self.name = name
            self.email = email
            self.address_line1 = address_line1
            self.address_line2 = address_line2
            self.city = city
            self.state = state
            self.postal_code = postal_code
            self.country = country
    
        def get_full_address(self):
            return f"{self.address_line1}\n{self.address_line2}\n{self.city}, {self.state} {self.postal_code}\n{self.country}"
    
        def is_us_address(self):
            return self.country == 'US'
    
        def get_display_name(self):
            return self.name
    
        def is_email_verified(self):
            return self.email_verified  # assume this field exists
    

**After** :
    
    
    class Address:
        def __init__(self, line1, line2, city, state, postal_code, country):
            self.line1 = line1
            self.line2 = line2
            self.city = city
            self.state = state
            self.postal_code = postal_code
            self.country = country
        def get_full(self):
            return f"{self.line1}\n{self.line2}\n{self.city}, {self.state} {self.postal_code}\n{self.country}"
        def is_us(self):
            return self.country == 'US'
    
    class Customer:
        def __init__(self, id, name, email, address):
            self.id = id
            self.name = name
            self.email = email
            self.address = address
        def get_display_name(self):
            return self.name
        def is_email_verified(self):
            return self.email_verified
    

The benefit: each class has focused responsibilities; addresses can be tested and changed independently of customer identity.

**AI-Layer** : AI agents extract classes when prompted but rarely identify the opportunity unprompted. The human spots the cohesion problem; the agent executes the mechanical split.

#### A note on the remaining five

You have now seen seven refactorings in detail. The remaining five (§§9.8-9.12) follow the same Signal / Before / After / AI-Layer structure but are presented more tightly: the discipline of the format is the same; the prose around each does not need to be. Read them at speed; the patterns are the point, not the example chrome around them.

### 9.8 Refactoring 8: Replace Inheritance with Composition

**Signal** : a class inherits to share implementation, not because it _is-a_ the parent. Symptoms: methods inherited but unused, methods overridden to do nothing, multiple inheritance with no clear hierarchy.

**Before / After** :
    
    
    # Before: Robot is-not-an Animal but inherits to share eat/sleep stubs
    class Animal:
        def eat(self): pass
        def sleep(self): pass
    class Dog(Animal):
        def bark(self): pass
    class Robot(Animal):
        def beep(self): pass
        def eat(self): pass     # overridden to do nothing
        def sleep(self): pass   # overridden to do nothing
    
    # After: composition via has-a (no inheritance)
    @dataclass
    class Mortal:
        def eat(self): pass
        def sleep(self): pass
    @dataclass
    class Dog:
        mortal: Mortal
        def bark(self): pass
    @dataclass
    class Robot:
        def beep(self): pass    # Robot doesn't compose Mortal; it isn't one
    

Has-a beats is-a when the relationship is implementation-sharing. Inheritance for shared implementation is a Chapter 13 anti-pattern; this refactoring is the fix.

**AI-Layer** : agents reach for inheritance because tutorial code teaches it; CLAUDE.md should prefer composition explicitly.

### 9.9 Refactoring 9: Introduce Parameter Object

**Signal** : 5+ parameters with conceptual groupings inside them.

**Before / After** :
    
    
    # Before: wall of parameters
    def create_order(customer_id, item_skus, item_quantities, shipping_line1,
                     shipping_line2, shipping_city, shipping_state, shipping_postal,
                     shipping_country, payment_method, payment_token, billing_address):
        ...
    
    # After: parameter object groups by concept
    @dataclass
    class Address:
        line1: str; line2: str; city: str; state: str; postal_code: str; country: str
    @dataclass
    class Payment:
        method: str; token: str
    @dataclass
    class OrderRequest:
        customer_id: str
        items: list[OrderItem]
        shipping_address: Address
        payment: Payment
        billing_address: Address
    
    def create_order(request: OrderRequest): ...
    

The signature stops growing; each grouping becomes a named domain concept reusable elsewhere.

**AI-Layer** : agents over-apply this; a 3-parameter function does not need a wrapper. Threshold is roughly 5–7 parameters or 2–3 grouped concepts.

### 9.10 Refactoring 10: Replace Loop with Pipeline

**Signal** : a loop builds a list by filtering and transforming, with intermediate accumulators.

**Before / After** :
    
    
    # Before
    def get_active_enterprise_emails(customers):
        result = []
        for c in customers:
            if c.tier == 'enterprise' and c.is_active and c.email:
                result.append(c.email.lower())
        return result
    
    # After (Python comprehension)
    def get_active_enterprise_emails(customers):
        return [c.email.lower() for c in customers
                if c.tier == 'enterprise' and c.is_active and c.email]
    
    
    
    // After (TypeScript)
    const getActiveEnterpriseEmails = (customers) =>
      customers
        .filter(c => c.tier === 'enterprise' && c.isActive && c.email)
        .map(c => c.email.toLowerCase());
    

The declarative pipeline reads as the data flow itself. Language-dependent: pipelines fit Python/JS/Rust/Scala; older C and embedded environments may stay with the loop form.

**AI-Layer** : agents produce the pipeline form by default; no special prompting needed.

### 9.11 Refactoring 11: Combine Functions into Class

**Signal** : multiple functions take the same data and operate on it in related ways. The data threads through a chain of functions; a class would co-locate it.

**Before / After** :
    
    
    # Before: data passed through standalone functions
    def parse_csv(text): ...
    def filter_csv(rows, column, value): ...
    def csv_to_json(rows): ...
    
    rows = parse_csv(text)
    rows = filter_csv(rows, 'region', 'EU')
    result = csv_to_json(rows)
    
    # After: methods on a class, chainable
    class CsvProcessor:
        def __init__(self, text):
            self._rows = [r.split(',') for r in text.split('\n')]
        def filter(self, column, value):
            header, *data = self._rows
            idx = header.index(column)
            self._rows = [header] + [r for r in data if r[idx] == value]
            return self
        def to_json(self):
            header, *data = self._rows
            return [dict(zip(header, r)) for r in data]
    
    result = CsvProcessor(text).filter('region', 'EU').to_json()
    

Data and operations co-locate; intermediate variables disappear. **Watch out** : if the operations are _unrelated_ , the class is artificial. The signal is _related operations on shared data_.

**AI-Layer** : agents apply this when prompted but rarely identify the opportunity. Human spots the cohesion; agent does the split.

### 9.12 Refactoring 12: Decompose Conditional

**Signal** : the _what_ (condition) and the _how_ (branch action) are tangled together; the reader must understand both at once.

**Before / After** :
    
    
    # Before: condition logic + branch logic mixed
    def schedule_refund(order, current_date):
        if (order.status == 'completed' and
            (current_date - order.completed_at).days < 30 and
            not order.has_been_refunded and
            order.payment_method != 'gift_card'):
            refund = order.total - (order.partial_refund_amount or 0)
            if order.customer.tier != 'enterprise':
                refund = refund * 0.95
            return process_refund(order, refund)
        return None
    
    # After: named predicate, named action
    def schedule_refund(order, current_date):
        if not is_eligible_for_refund(order, current_date):
            return None
        return process_refund(order, compute_refund_amount(order))
    
    def is_eligible_for_refund(order, current_date):
        return (order.status == 'completed'
                and (current_date - order.completed_at).days < 30
                and not order.has_been_refunded
                and order.payment_method != 'gift_card')
    
    def compute_refund_amount(order):
        base = order.total - (order.partial_refund_amount or 0)
        return base * 0.95 if order.customer.tier != 'enterprise' else base
    

Condition and action each get a name; both are testable independently; `schedule_refund` becomes a clean three-step process.

**AI-Layer** : agents apply this well. Risk: over-decomposition; a simple `if x > 0` does not need a named predicate.

### 9.13 Applying the twelve as a discipline

The twelve refactorings form a working engineer's reflex set. After a few months of deliberate practice, you apply them without thinking: you see the signal, you apply the refactoring, you run the tests, you commit.

#### The deliberate-practice phase

For the first two-three months of practicing the twelve:

  1. **Keep a paper or note of the twelve.** Refer to it during refactoring sessions.
  2. **For each PR you open** , ask: which of the twelve could apply to the code I changed? Apply at least one if applicable; commit it as a separate refactoring commit.
  3. **In code review** , suggest one of the twelve to authors. Be specific: "Consider Extract Function on the validation block."
  4. **In your notebook (Chapter 2)** , record interesting applications: the signal, the refactoring, the result.

After two-three months, the list moves from your paper into your head, and the practice becomes reflexive.

#### The AI-Layer pattern

For each refactoring, the AI-Layer note specified what AI does well and what humans must direct. The general pattern:

* **AI excels at mechanical execution.** Once the refactoring is identified, the AI applies it across many sites quickly. Renames, extracts, inlines, moves at the file level: all fast.
* **AI is unreliable at identifying when the refactoring applies.** The signal recognition is human work; the agent rarely says "this code has the X smell, you should apply Y."
* **AI is unreliable at granularity.** The agent tends to over-apply Extract Function (producing many tiny functions) and Replace Conditional with Polymorphism (producing many small classes). The human's judgment is needed for the granularity.

The 2026 workflow: human identifies, AI executes, human reviews. This is faster than humans doing both ends and safer than letting AI do both.

### 9.14 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Reading a 180-line file `csv_utils.py`, you find:

* one function `process_csv(text)` that internally has three labeled sections (`# parse`, `# validate`, `# transform`) totalling 95 lines;
* five top-level functions `parse_csv_rows`, `find_csv_column`, `filter_csv_by_value`, `csv_to_json`, `validate_csv_schema` that each take a `rows: list[list[str]]` argument and operate on it differently.

Which of the twelve refactorings would you apply to each, and in what order? Why?

Hint

One pattern is _cohesion within a single function_ (extraction); the other is _cohesion across functions that share data_ (combination).

**Answer:**

* For `process_csv(text)`: apply **Extract Function** (Refactoring 1). The three labeled sections are candidates that already name themselves. Pull each out as `_parse_csv`, `_validate_csv`, `_transform_csv`. After extraction, `process_csv` reads as a three-step pipeline; each helper is independently testable.
* For the five top-level functions all taking `rows`: apply **Combine Functions into Class** (Refactoring 11). The shared `rows` data threading through five separate functions is the signal. Wrap in a `CsvProcessor` class that owns the rows; methods become `parse`, `find_column`, `filter_by`, `to_json`, `validate_schema`. Calls become chainable: `CsvProcessor(text).filter_by('region', 'EU').to_json()`.

Order matters: do **Combine first** , then **Extract within each method as needed**. The Combine surfaces the shared concept (`CsvProcessor`); methods then become natural homes for extracted helpers. Doing Extract first on the five functions before Combining produces a mess of orphan helpers; Combining first organizes them.

The deeper point: the two refactorings address _different cohesion problems_. Extract solves cohesion _within_ a single function (sections that should be named). Combine solves cohesion _across_ functions (shared data that wants a home). When a file has both problems; as this one does; they compose, not compete.

**Q2.** Replace Conditional with Polymorphism is often over-applied. When is it the wrong choice?

Hint

§9.5's "added abstraction for added flexibility" trade-off.

**Answer:** It is the wrong choice when: (a) the conditional appears in only one place (no repetition to consolidate); (b) the cases are stable and unlikely to grow (no need to enable extension); (c) the case logic is small and the polymorphism adds more boilerplate than it removes; (d) the language has good alternative constructs (sum types, pattern matching, lookup tables) that fit the problem better.

A common 2026 alternative is a lookup table: `SHIPPING_COSTS = {'standard': 5.99, 'express': 14.99, ...}` is shorter than a polymorphic class hierarchy and clearer for purely data-driven cases. Polymorphism is appropriate when each case has _behavior_ that varies, not just data. Conflating the two is the over-application failure mode.

**Q3.** Inline Function is the inverse of Extract Function. When would you inline a function that someone else extracted?

Hint

Extraction is justified when the name adds clarity; inlining is justified when the name does not.

**Answer:** Inline when: (a) the extracted function is called only once and the call site is no clearer than the inlined body; (b) the function's name is misleading (the body does more or less than the name suggests); (c) the function's body is shorter than its declaration overhead (a one-line function named with three words may not earn its weight); (d) the function's existence implies a stable abstraction that has not materialized (e.g. a function named `applyDiscount` that does one thing and is only called once).

The discipline is to ask whether the function-as-a-name _adds information_. If the name is a synonym for the body's mechanics, the function is fog; inline it. If the name introduces a domain concept that the call site cannot express without it, keep the function.

**Q4.** Your team has a 4-year-old codebase. You are reviewing a PR that fixes a bug in a function called `process(data)` and includes 25 changed lines plus 14 new test cases. The reviewer comments: "Nice fix. While you're here, would you also rename `process` to `compute_eligibility_score`? You'll need IDE help to update the 87 call sites, but it's two minutes of mechanical work." The PR author replies: "That's scope creep, and renames are cosmetic; let's do that in a separate PR if we ever need to." Who is right? What is the _quantitative_ case for or against the rename being part of this PR?

Hint

A name is read many more times than it is written. Multiply across the codebase's lifetime.

**Answer:** The reviewer is right; the author is undervaluing the rename in a specific way that is easy to fall into.

The quantitative case: the function is called 87 times. Every reader of every one of those 87 sites pays a small cognitive tax to translate "`process(data)`" into "what does this actually compute?" Suppose the tax is 3 seconds per read for someone familiar with the codebase, and 15 seconds for a new engineer who has to follow the function definition to confirm what it does. Suppose the codebase will exist for 3 more years with 6 engineers averaging 2 reads of any given call site per year. That is `87 × 6 × 2 × 3 = 3,132 seconds` ≈ **52 minutes of cumulative reader tax** in the optimistic case (familiar readers only). Add new-engineer reads and the number climbs to several hours.

The cost of the rename today: ~2 minutes of mechanical work plus 5 minutes of review.

The author's "scope creep" framing misses that this _is_ a Boy Scout Rule case (Chapter 20): a small improvement on code you are already touching, aligned with the substantive change. It would be scope creep only if it were a _large_ refactor or _unrelated_ to the function the PR is fixing. Renaming a function whose bug you are fixing, while you have the IDE tools open and the test suite green, is the canonical Boy Scout improvement.

The author's "renames are cosmetic" framing is wrong in the deeper sense: names are read more than they are written. The asymmetry between writer-cost and reader-cost is what makes naming the most underrated refactoring. Chapter 18 develops this in detail; the reviewer in this scenario is applying it correctly.

A defensible compromise: the rename can be a separate commit _within the same PR_ ; atomic, separately revertable, but landing together with the fix. Best of both arguments.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Identify three places in your codebase where Extract Function would improve clarity. For each, propose the function name and the boundaries of the extraction.

Hint

Look for functions with comment-separated sections or functions over 50 lines.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* A function with comments like `# Step 1: validate`, `# Step 2: process`, `# Step 3: save`. Each comment is a candidate function name. Extract three functions.
* A function with a 20-line nested loop that computes a derived value. The loop is a candidate for extraction into a function whose name describes the computation.
* A long error-handling block at the end of a function. The error logic can often be extracted into a `_handle_X_error` helper, leaving the happy path clean.

For each, the proposed boundary is the smallest enclosing block that has a coherent purpose; the name is the purpose stated as an imperative verb phrase.

**Q6.** Take a class in your codebase that takes more than 5 parameters in its constructor. Apply Introduce Parameter Object. Show the before and after, and discuss whether the change improves readability.

Hint

The parameter object should group logically; arbitrary grouping is worse than no grouping.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common pattern: a class `Order` with constructor parameters `customer_id, item_skus, item_quantities, shipping_..., billing_..., payment_..., ...` (many parameters). The natural groupings are: items (a list of `OrderItem` with sku+quantity), shipping address, billing address, payment. After applying Introduce Parameter Object, the constructor takes four parameters: `customer_id, items, shipping_address, billing_address, payment` (five). The reader sees five concepts instead of a wall of parameters.

The readability improvement is real when the groupings are _natural_ (the parameters truly belong together in a domain sense). It is artificial when the groupings are arbitrary (random pairs of parameters wrapped in a struct named `Bundle1`). The discipline is to find the natural groupings; if they are not natural, do not force the refactor.
