**Q7.** Use an AI agent to apply Extract Function to a long function in your codebase. Review the result. Did the agent choose good function names? Good boundaries? Did it over-extract?

Hint

The most common failure mode is over-extraction: many three-line functions that fragment readability.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings: the agent extracts boundaries that mostly match what a human would extract, with one or two over-extractions (a single comparison turned into a two-line function); the agent's chosen names are sometimes generic (`processStep1`, `processStep2`) when the human would name in domain terms (`validateInputs`, `applyDiscount`).

The fix is interactive: ask the agent to rename the generic functions in domain terms; ask the agent to inline the smallest extractions. The total cycle is fast; the result is a cleaner refactor than either human-alone or agent-alone would produce.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute: "In 2026, AI agents make refactoring so cheap that engineers should apply the twelve refactorings as aggressively as possible." What is the cost of aggressive refactoring?

**Discussion:** For: AI makes the mechanical part of refactoring nearly free; the bottleneck moves to identification and judgment, both of which improve with practice; aggressive refactoring produces a cleaner codebase that compounds in maintainability.

Against: aggressive refactoring without judgment produces _change for change's sake_ ; each refactor has a review cost, a merge-conflict cost for adjacent in-flight branches, and a risk cost (even with tests). The total cost of refactoring is not zero; it includes the human review and the system-wide ripple. AI-driven aggressive refactoring can flood the team with PRs that overwhelm review capacity.

The synthesis: refactor as the codebase guides you, not as a deliberate program separate from the work. Apply the twelve when you touch code anyway; do not schedule "refactoring sprints" of pure refactoring. The Boy Scout Rule (Chapter 20) is the principled version: leave the code a little better than you found it, on each PR, without making refactoring the whole point.

**Q9.** The chapter argues for twelve refactorings as the working engineer's reflex set. Are there refactorings outside the twelve that you would add for your specific codebase? Are there twelve that you would demote?

**Discussion:** Codebase-specific candidates that might be added in 2026:

* **Replace Class with Module Functions** (especially in Rust, Go, Python contexts where idiomatic code is module-level functions, not classes).
* **Introduce Result Type** (in TypeScript or Python, replacing `throw`/`raise` with explicit `Result<T, E>` types).
* **Extract Type Alias** (in TypeScript, where complex types repeat).
* **Replace Mock with Fake** (per Chapter 7, in codebases migrating away from mock-heavy tests).

Codebase-specific candidates that might be demoted:

* **Combine Functions into Class** (if the codebase is functional-first; the natural unit is functions, not classes).
* **Replace Conditional with Polymorphism** (if the codebase uses sum types or pattern matching idiomatically; polymorphism is the wrong tool).

The general lesson: the twelve is a starting set, not a fixed set. Adopt them, practice them, then adjust for your codebase's idiom. The discipline of _knowing your reflex set_ is the durable skill; the specific list is the codebase-specific instance.

### Chapter Summary

The twelve refactorings (§§9.1-9.12) are the working engineer's reflex set: Extract Function, Inline Function, Rename, Move, Replace Conditional with Polymorphism, Replace Magic Number with Constant, Extract Class, Replace Inheritance with Composition, Introduce Parameter Object, Replace Loop with Pipeline, Combine Functions into Class, Decompose Conditional. Each has a signal that triggers it, a mechanical execution, and an AI-Layer note about what AI does well and what humans must direct.

The deliberate-practice phase (§9.13) of three months turns the list from paper into reflex. After the phase, the engineer applies the refactorings as they touch the code; the codebase compounds in maintainability without scheduled refactoring sprints.

The AI-Layer pattern across the twelve: AI excels at mechanical execution but is unreliable at identifying when refactoring applies and at granularity. The 2026 workflow is human identifies, AI executes, human reviews. This is the pattern that scales the discipline without losing judgment.

Chapter 10 takes the next step: refactoring under test. The twelve refactorings are safe to apply only when the test suite catches regressions; without tests, refactoring is rewriting with risk. The chapter develops the characterization-test pattern for legacy code (where tests are absent) and the safety-net story that makes the twelve usable at scale.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Keep Appendix B's pocket reference visible for three months.** Tape it next to your monitor. Look for the signals during every PR; apply the matching move; commit it separately. The list moves from paper into your reflexes by month three.
  2. **On every PR you open, apply at least one of the twelve to adjacent code.** Boy-Scout-Rule scale (Chapter 20), not a separate refactor PR. Three extracted helpers and a magic-number-to-constant compounds across a year of PRs.
  3. **When you ask an agent to refactor, name the specific move and the lines.** "Apply Extract Function on lines 45–72; name the helper `compute_proration_for_partial_refund`. Do not modify other files." Vague prompts produce vague refactors; named moves produce reliable ones.

---

## Chapter 10: Refactoring Under Test — The Safety Net Story

A 2,400-line file in the Trellis monolith contained the `OrderProcessor` class. It had been written in 2018, modified by approximately fifteen engineers over six years, and was the source of about a third of the team's bug reports. Asher's manager asked him in early 2026: "Can you refactor it?" Asher's answer: "Yes, but the file has 8% test coverage. I need to characterize it first."

Asher spent the next two weeks not refactoring. He wrote 47 characterization tests that locked in the current behavior of `OrderProcessor`. Some of those tests revealed bugs (behaviors that the team had assumed were intentional but were actually accidental); those bugs were filed and prioritized separately. The remaining 41 tests passed against the existing code. Then Asher refactored. The refactor took three weeks. Throughout, the 41 tests stayed green; whenever they went red, the refactor was rolled back to the last green state. At the end of five weeks, `OrderProcessor` was 1,400 lines distributed across four focused classes. The bug rate from that module dropped by roughly two-thirds in the following quarter.

This chapter is about that pattern: refactoring under test, characterization tests, and the safety net that makes the twelve refactorings of Chapter 9 usable at scale. Without a safety net, refactoring is rewriting with risk. With one, it is a near-mechanical discipline.

### 10.1 Tests enable refactoring, not the reverse

A common misconception is that good tests are a _result_ of good refactoring. The truth is the reverse: good tests are a _prerequisite_ for good refactoring. Without tests, every refactor is a leap of faith; with tests, every refactor is a controlled experiment with a clear pass/fail signal.

#### Definition: Refactoring Under Test

Refactoring under test is the discipline of restructuring code while a test suite continuously verifies that behavior is preserved. The test suite is the _oracle_ against which each refactoring step is checked; a refactor that breaks tests is by definition not behavior-preserving and must be revisited.

In plain language: tests tell you when you broke something. Without that signal, you cannot tell whether a refactor preserved behavior; you can only hope. Hope is not a safety net.

#### The compound effect

Tests and refactoring compound. As refactorings extract functions, the tests can become more focused (testing the extracted function in isolation). As tests become more focused, refactorings can be smaller (because the test surface is smaller). The two disciplines pull each other along.

The opposite is also true: poor tests make refactoring fearful; fearful refactoring leaves the code messy; messy code is harder to test. The downward spiral is real, and the way out is to invest in tests _before_ refactoring, not concurrently.

### 10.2 The characterization test pattern

When inheriting legacy code with low test coverage, you cannot refactor safely until you have tests. The natural impulse is to write tests for "what the code _should_ do." This is wrong; you do not yet know what the code should do, because the code itself is the documentation.

The correct first step is _characterization_ : writing tests for what the code _currently does_ , with no judgment about whether that is correct. The tests lock in the current behavior so that refactoring preserves it. After refactoring, separate work can distinguish "behaviors we want to keep" from "bugs we should fix."

#### Definition: Characterization Test

A characterization test is a test written _after_ the code, with no opinion about correctness, whose purpose is to lock in the current behavior. The test acts as a regression detector: changes that alter behavior fail the test, even if those changes might be improvements.

In plain language: run the code, observe what it does, write a test that asserts what it does. Do not assume the behavior is correct. Do not assume the behavior is incorrect. Just lock it in.

#### The mechanics

Suppose you have a function `compute_late_fee(invoice, current_date)` with no tests. Step-by-step:

  1. **Invoke the function with realistic inputs.** Pick inputs that exercise different code paths: an invoice not yet due, an invoice 30 days late, an invoice 100 days late, an invoice with a partial payment.
  2. **Observe the outputs.** Print or log them. Do not yet assert anything.
  3. **For each observed output, write a test that asserts that output.**

    
    
    def test_characterize_late_fee_30_days():
        invoice = Invoice(total=100, due_date=date(2026, 5, 1), paid=0)
        fee = compute_late_fee(invoice, current_date=date(2026, 5, 31))
        assert fee == 5  # observed, locked in
    

  4. **Repeat for many input combinations** until you have confidence the major code paths are exercised. Use coverage tools to verify; aim for 80%+ line coverage on the function.

  5. **For surprising outputs, file a separate ticket** marking the behavior as "characterized, suspected bug." Do not fix it during characterization; the goal is locking in, not improving.

After characterization, the function's behavior is encoded in the test suite. Refactoring becomes safe: any change that alters behavior fails a test.

#### The "is this a bug?" question

During characterization, you will find outputs that surprise you. Two classes:

* **Genuine bugs** : behaviors that no one wanted and that are causing visible problems (incorrect amounts, missed cases). File and fix separately.
* **Vestigial behaviors** : behaviors that no one _currently_ wants but that downstream systems may depend on (a specific error message, a specific ordering, a quirky timezone behavior). Lock in for now; investigate before fixing.

The discipline is to distinguish these without yet fixing either. The temptation to fix as you go is real; resist it. A characterization phase that mixes in fixes is no longer a characterization phase; it is a rewrite. Rewrites carry the risks the safety net was supposed to prevent.

### 10.3 The refactor-then-test versus test-then-refactor loop

In Chapter 8, we distinguished test-first from test-after. For refactoring of legacy code, the loop is _test-then-refactor_ , repeated:

  1. **Test** : write characterization tests for the function or class you intend to refactor. Confirm they pass against the current code.
  2. **Refactor** : apply one of the twelve refactorings (Chapter 9). Run the tests.
  3. **Green** : tests pass. Commit. Move on.
  4. **Red** : tests fail. The refactor changed behavior. Either fix the refactor (if the change was unintended) or update the test (only if the change was intended and approved). Default to fixing the refactor.

The loop is faster than it sounds. Each cycle is small (one refactor, one test run). With good tests, the run is seconds; with slow tests, the run is minutes. Either way, the loop is much faster than the alternative (refactor blindly, find the bug in production weeks later).

#### The "refactor first to test, then test, then refactor" pattern

A more elaborate pattern, useful for code that is currently un-testable:

  1. **Refactor to enable testing.** The code currently has hidden dependencies, hard-coded I/O, or other obstacles to testing. Apply small, _safe_ refactorings (mostly Rename and Extract Function from Chapter 9) to expose the code's surface. These refactorings should be near-mechanical; the risk of breaking behavior is low.
  2. **Test.** Now that the surface is exposed, write characterization tests.
  3. **Refactor.** With tests in place, apply the more substantive refactorings.

This pattern is the _bootstrap_ for refactoring untestable code. The trick is that step 1 must be safe enough that you do not need tests to do it; the chosen refactorings are the mechanical ones that any competent IDE or AI agent can apply reliably.

Asher used this pattern on the `OrderProcessor`. Step 1: rename a few methods to expose their purpose; extract a few obvious helper functions to make the call boundaries clearer. Step 2: characterization tests on the now-clearer interface. Step 3: the substantive refactors (Extract Class, Replace Inheritance with Composition, Decompose Conditional).

### 10.4 The AI-Layer: AI as refactor multiplier, human as trust source

In 2026, AI agents can apply the twelve refactorings at speed. A human-driven refactor that took Asher three weeks in 2024 might take three days in 2026 with AI assistance: the agent does the mechanical execution, the human directs and reviews.

The new bottleneck is _trust_. When the agent says "I refactored this class; the tests pass," you must verify:

* Did the tests actually pass? (Often yes, but sometimes the agent ran them locally and the result has not been verified by you.)
* Were the tests strong enough? (The agent's "tests pass" guarantees nothing if the tests are weak. Mutation testing, Chapter 6, becomes important here.)
* Were any tests modified during the refactor? (A refactor that modifies tests to make them pass is not behavior-preserving; the tests were the oracle and the agent moved the oracle.)
* Did the agent change behavior the tests did not cover? (The tests have coverage gaps; the agent may have changed an untested behavior. Coverage tools and human review compensate.)

#### The agent-refactor workflow

Asher's workflow at Trellis in 2026:

  1. **Characterize** : write characterization tests yourself, with AI assistance only for boilerplate. The tests must be the human's understanding of the current behavior, not the agent's interpretation.
  2. **Run mutation testing** : confirm the tests have decent kill rates. Strengthen weak tests before continuing.
  3. **Refactor with the agent** : ask the agent to apply specific refactorings (Extract Function from these lines, Replace Conditional with Polymorphism for this chain). Be specific about what; let the agent do the how.
  4. **Verify** : run the tests, check the diff, confirm no test files were modified during the refactor (use `git diff -- tests/` to inspect).
  5. **Review the structural diff** : even if the tests pass, look at the refactor at a structural level. Are the new functions named well? Are the boundaries sensible? The agent's mechanical work needs human review for _taste_.
  6. **Commit per refactor** : each application of one refactoring is one commit (Chapter 3, atomic commits). Do not bundle.

This workflow is faster than the human-only version (because the agent does the typing) and safer than the agent-only version (because the human owns the tests and the review).

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "if the tests pass, the refactor is safe." The mistake is treating tests as ground truth. Tests are _one_ signal; coverage is another; mutation score is another; human review is the fourth. The combined picture is what makes refactoring safe; any one signal alone can be fooled.

A specific way this misconception bites: an agent applies a "refactor" that introduces a subtle behavior change. The tests do not cover that behavior. The tests pass; the agent reports success; the change ships; production breaks weeks later. The fix is to read the diff, not just the test result.

### 10.5 The safety-net economics

The investment in characterization and tests is upfront; the payoff is distributed across every later refactor. The economics:

* **Without safety net** : each refactor takes time T; each refactor has a probability p of introducing a bug; the cost of a bug is C. Expected cost per refactor: T + p × C. For p = 0.1 and C = 10×T, the expected cost is 2T.
* **With safety net** : initial test investment is N × T (where N is the number of characterization tests, often 10-50); each refactor takes T (or less, with AI); each refactor has probability p' << p of introducing a bug.

The break-even point is roughly when the saved bug-cost exceeds the test investment. For a heavily-refactored module, this is usually within a few refactors. For a module that is touched twice a year, the math may not favor full characterization; pick the modules carefully.

Asher's `OrderProcessor` was a clear win. The module was being changed multiple times per sprint; the bug rate before characterization was high; the five-week investment paid back within two months in reduced bug costs alone.

The economics also favor the safety net for _AI-assisted_ refactoring. The agent is fast but not always right; the test suite is the discriminator between fast-and-right and fast-and-wrong. Without the discriminator, fast refactoring is fast _risky_ refactoring.

### 10.6 What to do when characterization is impossible

Sometimes characterization is impractical. Common reasons:

* The code has many external side effects that cannot be observed in tests (network calls, file system writes to non-deterministic paths, time-dependent behavior with no clock injection).
* The code's behavior depends on data that cannot be reproduced in tests (huge production datasets, third-party API behavior with no fake).
* The code is intrinsically non-deterministic (race conditions, randomness without seeding).

In these cases:

  1. **Refactor to enable observation.** Inject the clock, inject the filesystem, inject the random source. These are themselves small refactorings (Introduce Parameter Object on the clock, for example). After the refactor, characterization is possible.
  2. **Use shadow traffic in production.** Deploy the refactored code alongside the original; route a percentage of traffic through both; compare outputs. The comparison is the test. This is heavy infrastructure but works for systems where unit-test characterization is hopeless.
  3. **Accept a narrower characterization.** Test what you can; leave the rest as a known gap; refactor cautiously; monitor production carefully after deploy.

The discipline is not "always full characterization." It is "characterize what you can; for what you cannot, use other signals; do not refactor blindly."

### 10.7 Working example: the OrderProcessor refactor

The opening vignette is a fictional but representative example. The detailed timeline:

**Week 1: Bootstrap refactors.** Asher applies Rename (six methods renamed to expose their purpose), Extract Function (three obvious helpers extracted). No tests yet; the refactorings are mechanical and the IDE confirms them. Each is a separate commit.

**Week 2: Characterization.** Asher writes 47 characterization tests. The tests cover the main code paths: order creation, validation, discount application, payment processing, fulfillment. Coverage reaches 78%. Two tests fail on first write because the observed behavior contradicts what the team assumed; Asher files those as separate bug tickets and locks in the _current_ behavior (with comments noting the suspected bugs).

**Week 3: Mutation testing.** Asher runs mutation testing on the now-tested `OrderProcessor`. The mutation score is 52%, lower than ideal but tolerable. He strengthens five tests where the surviving mutants suggest weak assertions; the score rises to 68%.

**Weeks 4-5: Refactoring.** Asher applies the substantive refactors:

* Extract Class (split `OrderProcessor` into `OrderValidator`, `DiscountCalculator`, `PaymentProcessor`, `FulfillmentDispatcher`).
* Replace Inheritance with Composition (the original class inherited from a base class with three unused methods; replace with a composition relationship).
* Replace Conditional with Polymorphism (the shipping-cost chain becomes a small hierarchy).
* Decompose Conditional (several large conditionals in the validation logic become named functions).

Throughout, the 47 characterization tests stay green. When they go red, Asher reverts the offending refactor, investigates, and retries. The total refactor time is two weeks of focused work, distributed across the actual three weeks (the rest is review, on-call, and other commitments).

**Outcome.** The module's line count drops from 2,400 to 1,400. The test suite has 47 tests; in the next sprint, the team adds another 20 unit tests for the newly-extracted classes (each easier to test in isolation than the original monolith). The bug rate in the next quarter drops by roughly two-thirds.

The total investment: five weeks of Asher's time plus follow-on test additions by the team. The payoff: ongoing reduction in bug rate, faster feature work in the module, and the next refactor (when needed) is much cheaper because the safety net is in place.

* * *

**Thread B;`calculateRefund`, stage 4: after first refactor**

The `calculateRefund` function from Chapter 2 was a 55-line monolith: validation, computation, and result-building were interleaved with no clear seams. Before the first refactor, it had 23 characterization tests recording its actual behavior.

The refactor, applied over two days:
    
    
    def calculate_refund(order: Order, request: RefundRequest) -> RefundResult:
        validated = _validate_refund_request(order, request)
        amount = _compute_refund_amount(order, validated)
        return _build_result(order, amount)
    
    def _validate_refund_request(order: Order, request: RefundRequest) -> ValidatedRequest:
        if order.refund_status == RefundStatus.FULL:
            raise InvalidStateError("Order already fully refunded")
        if request.amount <= 0 or request.amount != request.amount:  # NaN guard
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid refund amount: {request.amount}")
        return ValidatedRequest(order=order, amount=request.amount)
    
    def _compute_refund_amount(order: Order, validated: ValidatedRequest) -> Decimal:
        remaining = order.total - order.total_refunded
        return min(Decimal(str(validated.amount)), remaining)
    
    def _build_result(order: Order, amount: Decimal) -> RefundResult:
        remaining_after = (order.total - order.total_refunded) - amount
        return RefundResult(
            order_id=order.id,
            amount=amount,
            currency=order.currency,
            status=RefundStatus.FULL if remaining_after == 0 else RefundStatus.PARTIAL,
        )
    

All 23 characterization tests pass. The main function shrank from 55 lines to 4. Each helper is independently testable. This is what refactoring under test buys: the same behavior, a better structure, and a test suite that proved nothing broke during the restructure.

* * *

### 10.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** You are about to refactor a 7-year-old function `compute_invoice_total(invoice, customer)`. Running it on a sample invoice gives `total = 1247.50`. You ask three teammates what the expected total _should_ be:

* Teammate A: "I think it should be 1247.50. That's been right for years."
* Teammate B: "Actually I think it should be 1235.00. The discount logic was changed in 2022 and I'm not sure it was right."
* Teammate C: "I have no idea what the right number is. Whatever the function returns is the contract our customers have been billed on for years."

You have one afternoon. What test do you write before touching the function; a characterization test, a regression test, or a unit test of intended behavior? Why does the _type_ of test you choose first matter so much?

Hint

A characterization test assumes nothing; a regression test assumes the prior behavior was wrong; a unit test of intended behavior assumes you already know what is right.

**Answer:** Write a **characterization test** that locks in `total == 1247.50`. Do not yet decide whether that number is "correct"; you do not know, and your teammates do not agree. The discipline is to _first_ preserve current behavior so the refactor can be safe; _then_ separately investigate whether the behavior is right.

Why the type matters: each kind of test embeds a different assumption.

* **Unit test of intended behavior** : assumes you know the right answer. If you write `assert compute_invoice_total(...) == 1235.00` (per teammate B's belief), you have just decided the refactor will change behavior; without consensus, without product input, possibly without justification. The "refactor" becomes a stealth behavior change, which is exactly what Chapter 10 warns against.
* **Regression test** : assumes the prior behavior was wrong, so it asserts the _new_ desired behavior. Same problem: you cannot regression-test what nobody has agreed is regressed.
* **Characterization test** : assumes nothing about correctness; locks in current behavior; refactor is now safe because any change to behavior fails the test. _Separately_ , you can investigate teammate B's suspicion via product, accounting, or customer-data inspection. If it turns out the 1247.50 is wrong, that is a new ticket, a new test, a new PR — not part of this refactor.

The discipline is delaying the judgment about correctness. The characterization test buys you time to refactor safely while the correctness question gets investigated separately.

**Q2.** Defend or refute: "If a refactor breaks a test, the test was wrong." When is this true, and when is it the warning sign of a bad refactor?

Hint

The default direction.

**Answer:** The default is the opposite: if a refactor breaks a test, the refactor changed behavior and is therefore not behavior-preserving. The refactor should be revisited or reverted. The test is the oracle; moving the oracle to suit the refactor is rewriting under cover of refactoring.

The exceptions: the test was over-coupled to implementation (asserted on internal call structure, asserted on private state, asserted on details that should not be part of the contract). In that case, the test is genuinely wrong: it was a brittle test that was preventing legitimate refactoring. Update the test to assert on observable behavior; the refactor stays.

Distinguishing the two cases: the broken test fails because (a) the observable result changed (refactor is wrong) versus (b) the internal mechanism changed but the observable result is identical (test is brittle). Run the production code with the refactor and the inputs from the test; if the output matches what the test expected, the test is brittle. If the output differs, the refactor is wrong.

**Q3.** Why is "refactor first to test, then test, then refactor" sometimes necessary? Give an example.

Hint

Some code is un-testable as currently written.

**Answer:** Necessary when the code is currently structured in a way that makes testing impossible or impractical, even for characterization. Common cases: a function that reads `current time` from a system call directly (cannot vary in tests), a function that makes an HTTP call to a hard-coded URL (cannot redirect in tests), a function with deeply nested logic where the interesting behavior is buried.

Example: a function `should_send_renewal_reminder(customer)` that reads `datetime.now()` directly and checks the customer's renewal date. To test it for arbitrary current dates, you must first refactor to take the current date as a parameter (Introduce Parameter Object or just an additional argument). After that small, mechanical refactor, characterization tests can vary the date and lock in the behavior. The substantive refactors come after.

The first-step refactors are chosen to be safe enough that they do not require tests: rename, extract pure helpers, inject side-effecting dependencies as parameters. These are the bootstrap operations that turn un-testable code into testable code.

**Q4.** Two engineering managers are debating the order of work for a 6-week project that involves refactoring a 2,500-line legacy payment module. Manager A says: "Spend two weeks writing tests, then four weeks refactoring." Manager B says: "Tests for code we are about to change are wasted work. Refactor first, then add tests after, only for the new shape." Which order is right, and what specific failure modes does each ordering produce or avoid? Frame your answer around a specific bug that would be caught by one approach and shipped by the other.

Hint

The tests-first / refactor-second ordering puts a safety net in place; the refactor-first ordering is a leap of faith.

**Answer:** Manager A is right; Manager B is making the most common refactoring mistake.

**Specific bug example:** suppose the legacy code rounds tax amounts using `Decimal.quantize(ROUND_HALF_EVEN)` (banker's rounding) in one place and `round()` (Python's built-in, banker's rounding at the language level but with floating-point quirks) in another. The two produce different results on edge cases like 0.125; sometimes 0.12, sometimes 0.13. The team has been billing customers correctly for years because both code paths happen to agree on the actual amounts the production data contains. A refactor "for consistency" might unify them to one rounding function, picking the wrong one, and silently re-rounding 0.1% of invoices going forward.

* **Manager A's order** catches this: the characterization tests written first will include real production-sample invoices that exercise both paths. When the refactor swaps rounding functions, one of those tests fails. The engineer pauses, investigates, discovers the two-path subtlety, makes an informed decision (probably "keep two rounding rules, add a comment explaining why").
* **Manager B's order** ships the bug: no tests exist to catch the rounding change. The refactor lands. The "tests added after" cover the refactored code's behavior; which is now subtly wrong. The bug surfaces three months later when a customer disputes a refund.

The deeper point: the engineer doing the refactor cannot know what subtleties the legacy code preserves. Characterization tests force the engineer to _observe_ the current behavior before changing it; they lock in the subtleties before the refactor erases them. Refactor-first is hopeful: it bets that the engineer correctly identifies every important behavior. Test-first removes the bet.

Hope is not engineering. Manager A's two-week investment buys four weeks of safe refactoring; Manager B's "save the two weeks" pays them back to customers in incident response.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Choose a function in your codebase with low test coverage. Write five characterization tests for it. Note any surprising behaviors you discover; do not fix them yet.

Hint

The five tests should cover different code paths; coverage tools can guide which paths are exercised.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common finding: the five tests reveal that the function handles edge cases (negative inputs, empty inputs, very large inputs) in ways the team had not realized. Some are intentional; some are bugs; some are vestigial. Lock in the current behavior; file the suspected bugs separately.

The exercise builds the muscle of _observing without judging_ during characterization. The judgment comes later; the lock-in comes first. After the exercise, the function can be refactored confidently because the safety net is in place.

**Q6.** Take a low-coverage module and apply the "refactor first to test, then test, then refactor" pattern. Do at least one small bootstrap refactor (Rename or Extract Function on something pure) before writing the characterization tests. Document the steps.

Hint

The bootstrap refactor should be one whose safety is guaranteed by the IDE or the AI agent, not by tests.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A typical sequence:

  1. **Bootstrap** : Rename a method that has a misleading name. The IDE's rename tool guarantees safety.
  2. **Bootstrap** : Extract a 30-line block into a helper function. The IDE's extract tool guarantees safety as long as no external dependencies are involved.
  3. **Characterize** : write 5-10 tests on the newly-exposed surface (the renamed method and the extracted helper).
  4. **Refactor** : apply Replace Conditional with Polymorphism (or whichever substantive refactor is appropriate) under the safety net.

The lesson: the bootstrap step is small, near-mechanical, and unblocks the test step. Without the bootstrap, the test step might require characterizing the function through a maze of intermediate state; with the bootstrap, the surface is clean.

**Q7.** Apply the AI-Layer refactor workflow from §10.4 to a real refactor. Have the agent perform the mechanical part of one of the twelve refactorings. Verify: did the agent modify any test files? Did all tests still pass? Did the agent introduce changes outside the requested scope?

Hint

Use `git diff -- tests/` and `git diff --stat` to inspect.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* The agent applied the requested refactor cleanly. Tests passed. No test file modifications. Diff was contained to the file(s) under refactor. ✅
* Or: the agent modified one test to "fix" a test that was asserting on internal call structure. ⚠️ Investigate: was the test brittle (in which case the modification is legitimate), or did the agent change the oracle to make the refactor pass (in which case the modification is wrong and must be reverted)?
* Or: the agent introduced a refactor outside the requested scope (renamed an unrelated function while applying Extract Function on the requested code). ⚠️ Discard the out-of-scope changes; the diff must match the request.

The verification step is essential. Without it, the agent's "the refactor is done" claim is unverified. With it, the agent's work is reviewable as if a junior engineer had done it.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "AI agents are the most important refactoring tool of 2026." Counter-argue: "AI agents are dangerous refactoring tools without a strong safety net." Synthesize.

**Discussion:** Pro-importance: AI makes the mechanical execution near-free; the twelve refactorings can be applied across the codebase in days where they used to take months. The codebase improves faster. Engineers who use AI for mechanical refactoring multiply their productivity on this dimension.

Pro-danger: AI agents apply refactorings without judgment about scope, granularity, or appropriateness. An agent given the prompt "refactor this class" may make ten changes, only two of which were good ideas. Without a strong test suite as a discriminator, the bad changes ship alongside the good ones. The codebase appears improved (it is shorter, has more functions, has more polymorphism) but is actually worse.

The synthesis: AI is a multiplier on what you already have. With a strong test suite (Chapters 5-7) and the human discipline of identifying which refactorings apply (Chapter 9), AI makes refactoring dramatically faster and safer. Without those prerequisites, AI accelerates the descent into a worse codebase. The discipline is to invest in tests first, in judgment second, in AI third. The order matters.

**Q9.** When is full characterization too expensive? Describe a module where the cost-benefit favors limited or no characterization, and what discipline you would use instead.

**Discussion:** Full characterization is too expensive when:

* The module is rarely touched (twice a year). The break-even point for characterization is far away; the test investment may never pay back.
* The module is scheduled for deletion within a quarter. Characterizing code that is about to disappear is wasted work.
* The module's behavior is intrinsically hard to test (heavy I/O, deep external dependencies, non-deterministic computation). The cost of building characterization infrastructure exceeds the value.
* The module's bugs are caught by other signals (production monitoring, customer reports). The marginal test value is low because the existing signals are sufficient.

Alternative disciplines:

* **Targeted testing** : characterize only the specific behavior being refactored, not the whole module. Smaller investment; smaller safety net; smaller scope of refactor.
* **Shadow traffic** : deploy the new version alongside the old; compare outputs in production. Heavy infrastructure but works for systems with hard-to-characterize behavior.
* **Cautious refactoring with monitoring** : refactor without full characterization, but watch production metrics carefully after deploy; revert on any anomaly. The discipline depends on having good monitoring (Chapter 19 deepens this).
* **Do not refactor** : accept the module as it is. Sometimes the right answer is to leave the code alone and let it eventually be replaced by something else entirely.

The discipline is _cost-benefit at the module level_ , not a universal "always characterize" rule. The universal rule is the failure mode: spending five weeks characterizing a module that gets touched twice a year is wasted work; the time was better spent on a module that gets touched daily.

### Chapter Summary

Tests enable refactoring (§10.1). Without tests, refactoring is rewriting with risk; with tests, it is a controlled discipline. The two compound: better tests enable smaller refactors; smaller refactors enable focused tests.

The characterization test pattern (§10.2) is the way into untested code. Observe, lock in, and resist the temptation to "fix" surprises during characterization; the goal is preservation, not improvement. After characterization, refactoring is safe because the test suite is the oracle.

The test-then-refactor loop (§10.3) is the basic cycle; the refactor-first-to-test variant is the bootstrap for currently-untestable code. The choice depends on whether the code's surface is observable from the outside.

The AI-Layer (§10.4) makes refactoring much faster but introduces the new bottleneck of _trust_. The verification discipline (run the tests, check the diff, confirm no test modifications, review structurally) is the safeguard. AI is a multiplier on what you already have; without tests, AI is dangerous; with tests, AI is fast.

The safety-net economics (§10.5) favor characterization for any module that is changed more than a few times per quarter; the upfront cost amortizes across many refactors. For rarely-touched modules, alternative disciplines (targeted testing, shadow traffic, cautious refactoring with monitoring) may fit better.

Chapter 11 takes refactoring to its largest scale: the six-month parallel-paths refactor with feature flags. The discipline scales but the patterns shift; the Strangler Fig pattern from Chapter 20 becomes the dominant move, and feature flags (Chapter 4) become the safety mechanism that lets the new path coexist with the old. Together, Chapters 9-11 cover the refactoring spectrum from the twelve mechanical moves to the multi-month strategic refactor.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Before your next refactor of a low-coverage module, write characterization tests first.** Observe, lock in, do not fix surprises during characterization. Refactor only when the safety net is in place; revert immediately if a test goes red.
  2. **After any agent-driven refactor, run`git diff -- tests/` to confirm no test files were modified.** A refactor that moves the oracle is a rewrite in disguise. The test files should be unchanged or only added-to, never silently altered.
  3. **Use the safety-net economics filter on every refactor proposal.** Module touched twice a year? Characterization probably does not pay back; do something targeted or leave it. Module touched twice a sprint? Characterize aggressively; the investment amortizes in weeks.

---

## Chapter 11: Big Refactors with Feature Flags

The Trellis monolith's billing subsystem was written in 2018 against a Stripe API version that has since been deprecated three times. By 2025, the billing code was a tangle of compatibility shims: an `if/else` for each of three historical API versions, three different webhook signature verifiers, four different ways to format dollar amounts. The engineers who wrote each layer were no longer at Trellis. The code worked, but every billing-related change took weeks of careful archaeology.

In early 2025, Asher proposed a six-month refactor: replace the billing subsystem with a clean implementation against the current Stripe API. The proposal was met with the standard objection: "We can't take six months without shipping anything." Asher's counter-proposal was the parallel-paths strategy: build the new subsystem alongside the old, run them simultaneously, compare outputs, and migrate traffic gradually behind a feature flag. The team ships throughout; the old subsystem is the safety net; the new subsystem grows in confidence before becoming primary.

By the end of 2025, the migration was complete. The old code path was deleted. No customer-visible incident occurred during the six months. The team's mean time to ship a billing change dropped from two weeks to two days. This chapter is about that pattern: the multi-month refactor that is too big for the test-then-refactor loop of Chapter 10, and the techniques that make it feasible.

### 11.1 When a six-month refactor pays

Not every legacy subsystem needs a six-month refactor. The work is expensive; the discipline is real; the team's bandwidth is finite. The decision criteria for committing:

#### The refactor pays when:

* **The subsystem is changed frequently.** A subsystem touched daily by multiple teams pays back the refactor quickly through reduced friction per change.
* **The accumulated debt is dragging velocity measurably.** If the team's lead time to ship in this subsystem is significantly higher than elsewhere, and the gap traces to the subsystem's structure, the refactor pays.
* **The current structure prevents an upcoming change.** A planned migration, a new feature, or a regulatory requirement cannot be implemented within the current structure.
* **The original assumptions are gone.** The world changed; the code remembers the old world. Stripe API v2 was the right choice in 2018; it is wrong in 2025.

#### The refactor does not pay when:

* **The subsystem is rarely changed.** Refactoring code that nobody touches is unmotivated effort.
* **The team lacks the bandwidth.** A six-month refactor with no slack in the team's schedule produces friction, conflict, and unfinished work.
* **The replacement architecture is unclear.** Without a clear target, the refactor is exploratory; six-month exploratory refactors usually fail.
* **The risk of mid-refactor abandonment is high.** Teams that have abandoned previous big refactors should not start another; they need a smaller pattern (Chapter 20's Boy Scout Rule or Strangler Fig).

Asher's billing refactor met all four "pays" criteria; the team allocated the bandwidth; the target architecture was clear (Stripe API current version, fewer layers of compatibility). The decision was defensible.

### 11.2 The parallel-paths strategy

The core technique for a big refactor is _parallel paths_ : build the new system alongside the old, run them simultaneously, and migrate traffic gradually.

#### Definition: Parallel Paths

The parallel-paths strategy runs two implementations of the same functionality simultaneously: the old (production) path and the new (refactored) path. Requests can be routed to either path based on a feature flag; outputs can be compared between paths for verification; traffic can be shifted gradually as confidence grows.

In plain language: instead of one path that you replace all at once, two paths that coexist while you migrate. The old path stays as the safety net; the new path grows until it is ready to become primary; the old path is deleted when the new path is proven.

#### The mechanics

The team builds an abstraction layer above the subsystem. Requests enter the abstraction; the abstraction dispatches to either the old or the new implementation based on a feature flag (Chapter 4). The output is returned to the caller; in dual-run mode, both implementations execute and outputs are compared (the _shadow read_ pattern).
    
    
    class BillingService:
        def __init__(self, old_impl, new_impl, flag_provider):
            self._old = old_impl
            self._new = new_impl
            self._flags = flag_provider
    
        def charge_customer(self, customer, amount):
            if self._flags.is_on('billing_new_impl', customer_id=customer.id):
                # New path is primary; old path runs as shadow if dual_run enabled
                new_result = self._new.charge_customer(customer, amount)
                if self._flags.is_on('billing_shadow_read', customer_id=customer.id):
                    try:
                        old_result = self._old.charge_customer(customer, amount)
                        self._compare_results(new_result, old_result)
                    except Exception:
                        pass  # shadow failures don't affect the primary path
                return new_result
            return self._old.charge_customer(customer, amount)
    

The flag (`billing_new_impl`) controls which path is primary. The second flag (`billing_shadow_read`) controls whether the other path also runs for comparison. By tuning the flags, the team can:

* Phase 1: 100% old, no shadow. Baseline.
* Phase 2: 100% old, shadow on 1% of traffic. New path runs without affecting production; outputs are compared.
* Phase 3: 100% old, shadow on 100% of traffic. Comprehensive comparison.
* Phase 4: 1% new (canary), 99% old. The new path serves real traffic for a small slice.
* Phase 5: 10% new, 90% old. Larger canary.
* Phase 6: 50/50. Half the traffic on each.
* Phase 7: 100% new. Old path still exists but unused.
* Phase 8: Old path deleted.

Each phase has metrics: error rates, latencies, output mismatches in shadow mode. A phase that introduces problems triggers a rollback to the previous phase; the team investigates before advancing.

#### The shadow-read comparison

When both paths run, the comparison logic is critical. Naive equality may fail because the two paths may differ in non-essential details (an internal ID, a timestamp formatted differently). The comparison must focus on _observable contract_ :
    
    
    def _compare_results(self, new, old):
        if new.amount != old.amount:
            self._log_mismatch('amount', new, old)
        if new.currency != old.currency:
            self._log_mismatch('currency', new, old)
        if new.status != old.status:
            self._log_mismatch('status', new, old)
        # Ignore internal IDs, timestamps that may differ harmlessly
    

The mismatches are logged with enough context to investigate (the inputs, both outputs, any environmental variables). A spike in mismatches triggers an alert; investigation reveals whether the new path has a bug or the comparison logic was wrong.

### 11.3 Feature flags as the safety mechanism

The feature flags in §11.2 are the safety mechanism. A bug in the new path can be neutralized in seconds by flipping the flag back to the old path; no deploy, no rebuild, no incident escalation.

This is the property that makes the multi-month refactor feasible. Without flags, every phase change is a deployment; the team must coordinate deploys, monitor for impact, and roll back via deploy if anything goes wrong. With flags, each phase change is a configuration change, reversible in seconds.

#### Flag-design discipline for big refactors

The flag design for parallel-paths refactors is more involved than the typical feature flag:

* **Granularity** : the flag must allow rollout by customer, by tenant, by region, or by sample percentage. The team must be able to enable for a small canary and grow.
* **Independence** : the primary-path flag and the shadow-read flag must be independent. The team may want shadow without canary, or canary without shadow.
* **Defaults** : in production, both flags default to off. The new path is dark until explicitly enabled.
* **Naming and lifecycle** : the flags are named per the team's convention (Chapter 4); they have a planned `kill_by_date` corresponding to the migration completion; they are removed in a final PR after the old path is deleted.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is that "feature flags slow things down because of the runtime overhead." The mistake assumes that the overhead is significant; for most flag services (LaunchDarkly, Statsig, Unleash, Flipt), the flag evaluation is a cached local lookup taking microseconds. The overhead is far below the variability of normal request processing.

The genuine cost of feature flags is _complexity_ : the codebase now has two paths instead of one for the duration of the migration. Engineers reading the code must understand both. This is real, but it is a _temporary_ cost; the second path disappears when the migration completes. The temporary complexity is the price of the migration's safety.

### 11.4 The Strangler Fig pattern (preview of Chapter 20)

The parallel-paths strategy is one instance of a broader pattern: the _Strangler Fig_ , named for the tropical plant that grows around a host tree and eventually replaces it. Martin Fowler popularized the term for software migrations.

#### Definition: Strangler Fig

The Strangler Fig pattern migrates a system by building the new system around the old one, gradually shifting functionality from the old to the new, and eventually removing the old. The transition is incremental; the old and new coexist during the migration; the migration completes when the old is fully replaced.

In plain language: do not replace the system in one big push. Grow the new system around it; shift functionality one piece at a time; eventually the old is hollow and can be deleted.

#### How parallel paths is a Strangler Fig variant

In the billing refactor, each function in the old `BillingService` is gradually re-implemented in the new path. Each function migrates independently: `charge_customer` first, then `refund`, then `subscription_change`, etc. Each migration follows the parallel-paths pattern. The old `BillingService` shrinks as the new one grows. After six months, the old is empty; it is deleted in a final PR.

Chapter 20 develops the Strangler Fig more broadly. In this chapter, the point is that parallel paths is the technique for _each function_ ; the Strangler Fig is the strategy for the _whole migration_.

### 11.5 The AI-Layer: agent-assisted parallel paths

In 2026, AI agents can dramatically accelerate the new-path implementation. The agent can write the new code from the old code as a specification, often catching idiomatic improvements as it goes.

#### The workflow

Asher's workflow on the Trellis billing refactor:

  1. **Specify the new path's target API** : the new path should expose the same observable behavior as the old path; the agent must match the contract.
  2. **Provide the old path as reference** : the agent reads the old implementation and re-implements it in the new path's idioms (current Stripe API, fewer compatibility shims, cleaner structure).
  3. **Provide characterization tests (Chapter 10)** : the agent verifies its implementation against the tests.
  4. **Iterate on each function** : charge_customer first, then refund, etc. The agent implements; Asher reviews; tests pass; the function is added to the new path.
  5. **Run shadow mode in staging** : the agent's implementations run alongside the old paths in a staging environment; any mismatches are investigated.
  6. **Promote each function to production canary** : 1% traffic, 10%, 50%, 100%. The flag system handles the routing.

The agent multiplied Asher's productivity on the implementation phase by perhaps 3-4×. The verification phase (shadow comparison, canary monitoring, mismatch investigation) was less amenable to acceleration; the human's judgment was needed for "is this mismatch a bug or a benign difference?" But the implementation phase was the bulk of the work, and the AI was a significant force multiplier.

#### What AI cannot do in this workflow

* **Make the migration decision.** Whether to refactor, what target architecture to choose, what timeline to commit, what risks to accept: human work.
* **Design the parallel-paths infrastructure.** The flag system, the comparison logic, the canary plan: human work (the agent can implement parts once designed).
* **Investigate mismatches.** When shadow mode reports differences, the human's job is to decide whether the difference is a bug in the new path, a quirk of the old path that was actually wrong, or a benign formatting difference.
* **Decide when to advance phases.** The metrics inform the decision; the decision is the human's.

The agent's domain is _mechanical work under specification_. The migration is full of mechanical work, so the agent is highly useful; the migration is also full of judgment calls, which remain the human's.

### 11.6 Risks and how to manage them

Big refactors carry specific risks. Naming them and planning for them is part of the discipline.

#### Risk 1: The new path drifts from the old path's contract

During the migration, the old path may continue to evolve (bug fixes, regulatory updates, new features). The new path was implemented against an earlier snapshot; if it does not absorb the ongoing changes, it falls behind.

Mitigation: the team writes a rule that all changes to the old path must be mirrored into the new path. Or: a freeze on new-feature work in the old path while the migration is underway, with only critical bug fixes mirrored. The choice depends on the team's velocity needs.

#### Risk 2: The team loses interest mid-migration

A six-month refactor competes with feature work for attention. The team's commitment can fade; the migration ends in a half-state where neither path is fully invested in.

Mitigation: explicit milestones with calendar deadlines; visible progress dashboards; periodic team retrospectives on the migration's status; leadership commitment to the timeline. Asher's billing refactor had monthly progress reports to the engineering leadership; this kept the work visible and the team's attention engaged.

#### Risk 3: The shadow comparison misses critical differences

Shadow mode catches mismatches _that the comparison logic detects_. If the comparison logic is naive, real differences go unnoticed.

Mitigation: invest in the comparison logic. Treat it as production code. Add tests for the comparison itself. Periodically audit a sample of "matched" requests by hand to verify they were actually matching.

#### Risk 4: The canary phase masks low-frequency bugs

A 1% canary catches frequent bugs; rare bugs may take many days to surface in production traffic.

Mitigation: extend the canary phase long enough to see at least 100,000 requests per percentile; advance to the next percentile only after the metrics are stable for a full week.

#### Risk 5: The old path's deletion reveals hidden coupling

After the new path is at 100%, the team deletes the old path. Sometimes the old path was imported (or partially used) by other subsystems in ways no one noticed; deletion breaks them.

Mitigation: before deletion, `grep` for all callers of old-path functions; verify each. Use a deprecation phase: the old path is marked deprecated, callers are notified, no one is allowed to add new callers, and the deletion happens when the grep is empty.

#### Case study: when parallel paths does not land

The Trellis billing refactor in §11.7 lands well. The discipline is not magic; it is a set of choices that have to be made, and a team that does not make them ends up somewhere else. The contrasting case is illustrative.

In 2024, a 60-engineer fintech we will call Northwind began a parallel-paths migration of its core ledger from a monolithic PHP service to a Go rewrite. The proposal was modeled on the same pattern as Asher's: build the new path alongside the old, route via feature flag, compare in shadow mode, migrate gradually. The ADR was sound; the leadership backing was real; the first three months looked like the Trellis timeline almost step-for-step.

By month six, the migration had stalled at 25% traffic. By month nine, it was abandoned. The team kept the Go service running for the 25% but never deleted the PHP path. Two years later, both still exist; engineers reading either codebase have to navigate the split; on-call rotations cover both stacks; new features are written twice or, more often, once in PHP and never mirrored.

Three of the §11.6 risks materialized and compounded each other:

* **Risk 1 (drift between paths)** : the PHP path kept getting new features. The team had not committed to a mirror-or-freeze policy. Product owners kept asking for changes; engineers added them only in PHP because that was faster than touching both paths. By month four, the new path was three months behind on functionality and falling further behind every sprint.
* **Risk 3 (naive comparison)** : the shadow comparison checked totals and statuses but not the per-line-item breakdown. A subtle rounding difference in the new path's tax calculation passed shadow mode for two months before a customer caught it manually. Investigation revealed dozens of similar discrepancies hiding under the comparison's coarse granularity. Confidence in the new path collapsed; the team stopped advancing canary percentages while the comparison logic was rewritten, which took another month.
* **Risk 2 (attention loss)** : the lead engineer left at month five. Their replacement did not have the same conviction; the monthly progress reports stopped; the migration drifted from "in progress" to "open question." By month eight, the team treated it as a maintenance burden, not an active project. The deletion milestone, never put on a calendar, never came.

What can be extracted from Northwind without armchair quarterbacking: the discipline articulated in §11.6 is not optional. Each risk in isolation is survivable; two compounding is hard to recover from; three in sequence is fatal. The mitigations in §11.6 are not paranoia. They are the things that have to actually happen for the strategy to work. Asher's team did them; Northwind's did not; the outcomes were proportional.

The compounding pattern matters. The drift made the new path always behind; the bad comparison hid the bugs that would have driven urgency; the attention loss removed the person who would have noticed both. Any single risk caught early is fixable; the combination is what kills migrations. Teams beginning a big refactor should reread §11.6 monthly and ask: which of these is starting to show? The migrations that fail rarely fail suddenly. They drift into failure over a quarter or two, with the warning signs visible in retrospect.

### 11.7 Working example: the billing migration timeline

The Trellis billing migration's detailed timeline, illustrative:

* **Month 1: Planning and infrastructure.** Asher writes the ADR (Chapter 17), gets team and leadership approval. The team designs the parallel-paths abstraction and integrates the flag system. The flags `billing_new_impl` and `billing_shadow_read` are added.
* **Month 2: First function migration.** `charge_customer` is the simplest function and migrates first. Asher writes characterization tests for the old path (Chapter 10). Before approving the agent's first draft, he reads each section of the old `charge_customer` and asks his standard question; _what was the author worried about?_ ; for every defensive check; two of the checks turn out to be load-bearing protections against a vendor quirk the new path would have missed. The agent implements the new path including those protections; tests pass; shadow comparison runs in staging. Two weeks of iteration; the new path lands in main, off by default.
* **Month 3: Production canary on charge_customer.** Shadow read at 100% of staging; production canary at 1%, 10%, 50%. Mismatches are investigated; two genuine bugs in the new path are fixed; one quirk in the old path is documented and reproduced. By end of month, `charge_customer` is at 100% on the new path.
* **Months 4-5: Remaining function migrations.** `refund`, `subscription_change`, `proration_charge`, `webhook_signature_verifier`. Each follows the same pattern; the agent's productivity on implementation is high; the bottleneck is verification.
* **Month 6: Cleanup.** All functions are on the new path; the old path is unused. The team verifies via grep; finds three callers of the old path in unexpected places (a metrics service, a customer-data export, a deprecated admin tool); cleans those up. The old path is deleted in a single large PR with a one-month-deprecation notice on the legacy admin tool. The flags are removed. The migration is complete.

The total team-effort: approximately 1.5 engineer-quarters distributed across six months. The payoff in this fictional case: a substantial increase in billing-change velocity, a noticeable drop in the bug rate, and a meaningfully shorter codebase. The specific multipliers will vary by team and starting condition; the _direction_ (faster ship, fewer bugs, less code) is the durable shape when a parallel-paths refactor lands well. Teams seeing the opposite direction should investigate whether the migration actually addressed the underlying debt (Chapter 19) or merely moved it.

### 11.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** When is a six-month refactor justified, and when is a smaller refactor (Chapter 9 or Chapter 20's Boy Scout Rule) preferred?

Hint

§11.1's four "pays" criteria.

**Answer:** A six-month refactor is justified when: the subsystem is changed frequently (so the payback is fast), the accumulated debt is measurably dragging velocity, the current structure prevents an upcoming required change, and the team has the bandwidth and a clear target architecture. All four should hold; if any are absent, a smaller refactor is preferred.

A smaller refactor (the twelve from Chapter 9 applied incrementally, or the Boy Scout Rule from Chapter 20) is preferred when: the subsystem is rarely changed, the debt is annoying but not blocking, the team's bandwidth is low, or the target architecture is unclear. In those cases, incremental improvement is safer and the upfront commitment is smaller.

The signal for "definitely too big to refactor" is when neither the big nor the small refactor seems right: the team should pause and reassess. Sometimes the right answer is to keep the subsystem as-is until other conditions change.

**Q2.** Explain the parallel-paths strategy in your own words. What makes it different from a "switch over" migration?

Hint

Compare the risk profiles.

**Answer:** Parallel paths runs the old and new implementations simultaneously, routing traffic via a feature flag, with the option to compare outputs in shadow mode. The migration is gradual: traffic shifts from old to new over weeks or months, with each phase verified by metrics before advancing.

A switch-over migration replaces the old with the new in one deployment. At the moment of deploy, all traffic moves; if the new path has bugs, all users are affected.

The difference is risk: parallel paths bounds the blast radius of any bug to the percentage of traffic on the new path; switch-over does not. Parallel paths costs more in complexity (two paths during the migration, the flag system, the comparison logic) but pays back in safety. For multi-month refactors of critical systems, the safety is worth the complexity.

**Q3.** Your team's parallel-paths migration of the `billing` module has been in shadow mode for two weeks. The shadow-comparison logs show:

* 99.97% of requests: outputs match exactly.
* 0.02% of requests: outputs differ in `internal_correlation_id` (random UUID); amounts and statuses match.
* 0.005% of requests: outputs differ in `status` field; old returns `"OK"`, new returns `"SUCCESS"`. Amounts match.
* 0.005% of requests: outputs differ in `amount` field; old returns `1247.50`, new returns `1247.51`. Status matches.

For each of the three classes of mismatch, decide: is this a bug in the new path, a benign difference, or something worth investigating before promoting any traffic? Justify each decision.

Hint

The observable contract is the set of values callers actually depend on. Some fields are contract; some are not.

**Answer:**

(a) **`internal_correlation_id` mismatch (0.02%)**: **benign**. By name, this is an internal identifier the caller does not depend on for behavior. Adjust the comparison logic to ignore this field. Document why in the shadow-comparison code so the next engineer does not re-add the comparison.

(b) **`status` field `"OK"` vs `"SUCCESS"` (0.005%)**: **investigate before promoting**. The status field _looks_ like contract; callers probably switch on it. Check: do any downstream consumers parse this string? If yes, even a 0.005% discrepancy means some callers may break when the new path serves them. The fix is to make the new path emit `"OK"` for compatibility, or to coordinate a migration of all consumers. Do not promote traffic until this is resolved; do not silently fix in the comparison code (that hides a real contract change).

(c) **`amount` field 1247.50 vs 1247.51 (0.005%)**: **bug in the new path**. Amount is the most contractually load-bearing field; a 1-cent discrepancy in billing is a real customer-impacting issue. The fact that it is rare suggests an edge case (rounding at specific values, NaN-to-zero coercion, currency-precision difference). Reproduce the failing case locally; trace through; fix the new path; re-run shadow until the rate goes to zero. Do not promote until clean.

The general lesson: the comparison logic is design work, not boilerplate. Decide per-field whether it is contract or implementation detail. Mismatches in contract fields are bugs (or contract changes that need coordination); mismatches in implementation fields are benign and the comparison should be tuned to ignore them. Naive equality fails on all three of the above cases for different reasons.

**Q4.** Your team's flag service has two flags that you both inherited and need to decide how to manage:

* `payments_v2_enabled`; added 14 months ago for a parallel-paths migration that completed 10 months ago. Currently set to 100%. The migration's "delete old code" PR was never opened. Both code paths still exist in the codebase.
* `new_checkout_layout_for_seniors`; added 3 weeks ago for an A/B test on customers over 65. Currently at 50/50. Product team is collecting data; will decide in two more weeks.

For each flag, what is its category, what is the appropriate lifecycle action right now, and what does each one teach about the difference between _migration flags_ and _product flags_?

Hint

Migration flags are infrastructure with a planned deletion milestone; product flags are user-visible experiments with their own lifecycle.

**Answer:**

**`payments_v2_enabled`** is a **migration flag** that has overstayed. The migration completed 10 months ago; the flag has been at 100% for nearly a year; both code paths still exist. This is the canonical migration-flag failure mode (§4.4's flag rot + §11.6 risk 5): the old path is dead code that no one is maintaining; engineers reading the codebase have to navigate two paths to understand which one matters; the flag service is evaluating a no-op on every request. Action: file the deletion PR this sprint. Grep all callers; remove the `if flag` branches; delete the old implementation; delete the flag from the flag service. One PR closes a 14-month-old loose end.

**`new_checkout_layout_for_seniors`** is a **product flag** mid-experiment. Action: leave it alone. The product team has a stated end date; the flag is doing its job (running a controlled experiment); when the decision lands, the losing variant gets deleted and the flag follows. No lifecycle issue here.

What the contrast teaches:

* Migration flags answer "which implementation runs?" User-visible behavior should be identical regardless. The "correct behavior" is "no divergence." The deletion is a planned milestone of the migration, not optional.
* Product flags answer "what experience does this user get?" Different users get different behavior on purpose. Deletion happens after the experiment concludes.

Both categories rot if neglected. The mitigation is the same; a documented lifecycle with a `kill_by_date` (Chapter 4 §4.4). What is different is the _category-specific failure mode_ : migration flags accumulate dead code in the source; product flags accumulate cognitive overhead in the team's mental model ("which experiment is this user in again?"). Both real; both preventable; both invisible until they bite.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Propose a six-month refactor plan for a subsystem in your codebase that has accumulated debt. Include: the target architecture, the parallel-paths design, the canary phases, the success metrics, and the deletion milestone.

Hint

Use §11.7 as the template.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A reasonable plan for a hypothetical "OrderProcessing" subsystem:

* **Target architecture** : split OrderProcessing into separate Validator, Calculator, and Persister classes; replace the legacy database query layer with the current ORM idioms; remove three deprecated payment-method handlers.
* **Parallel paths design** : abstract OrderProcessing behind an `IOrderProcessor` interface; old implementation in `LegacyOrderProcessor`, new in `OrderProcessorV2`. Flag `order_processing_v2_enabled` routes; flag `order_processing_v2_shadow` enables comparison.
* **Canary phases** : shadow staging 100%, then production canary 1% / 10% / 50% / 100%. Each phase one week; each transition gated on metrics.
* **Success metrics** : error rate identical to baseline (within 0.1%); latency identical (within 5%); shadow mismatch rate < 0.01% by end of phase 3.
* **Deletion milestone** : at 100% on new path for two consecutive weeks with no incidents, file deletion PR for old path; flag removal in the same PR.

Adjust to the specific subsystem; the structure transfers.

**Q6.** Implement a small parallel-paths abstraction for one function in your codebase. Use a feature flag to route. Write tests that exercise both paths. Demonstrate that flipping the flag changes the implementation.

Hint

The flag service can be a simple `os.environ` lookup for the prototype.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A minimal prototype:
    
    
    class FeatureFlag:
        @staticmethod
        def is_on(name):
            return os.environ.get(f'FLAG_{name.upper()}', '0') == '1'
    
    class Calculator:
        def __init__(self, legacy, modern):
            self._legacy = legacy
            self._modern = modern
        def compute(self, x):
            if FeatureFlag.is_on('modern_calculator'):
                return self._modern.compute(x)
            return self._legacy.compute(x)
    

Tests:
    
    
    def test_default_uses_legacy():
        os.environ.pop('FLAG_MODERN_CALCULATOR', None)
        calc = Calculator(LegacyCalc(), ModernCalc())
        assert calc.compute(5) == LegacyCalc().compute(5)
    
    def test_flag_routes_to_modern():
        os.environ['FLAG_MODERN_CALCULATOR'] = '1'
        calc = Calculator(LegacyCalc(), ModernCalc())
        assert calc.compute(5) == ModernCalc().compute(5)
    

The prototype demonstrates the pattern. A production version would use a real flag service (LaunchDarkly, etc.) and would include the shadow-read comparison; the abstraction is the same shape.

**Q7.** Identify a subsystem in your codebase that has both (a) accumulated debt and (b) low change frequency. Defend the choice _not_ to refactor it. What discipline would you apply instead?

Hint

The math of payback depends on change frequency.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A defensible non-refactor decision for a low-frequency subsystem:

The subsystem (e.g. a legacy admin tool) is changed twice a year. Refactoring it would take a quarter; the payback per change is small; the refactor never pays back. Instead:

* **Document the quirks** in the subsystem's README so that the twice-a-year visitor does not start from scratch.
* **Add tests for the few critical behaviors** so that the rare changes are safer.
* **Mark the subsystem as deprecation candidate** in the team's documentation; if it is later replaced wholesale (e.g. by a new admin tool), the refactor becomes unnecessary.
* **Apply the Boy Scout Rule** when touched: small improvements at the moment of change, no scheduled big refactor.

The discipline is to _not refactor_ with intent, rather than to default to refactoring as the answer to debt. Some debt is appropriate to live with.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute: "In 2026, AI agents make big refactors so cheap that they should be done more frequently." What changes about the cost-benefit?

**Discussion:** Pro-frequency: the agent multiplies the engineer's implementation productivity by 3-4× on the mechanical work. A six-month refactor becomes a three-month refactor; a three-month refactor becomes a six-week refactor. The economics of refactoring shift toward more frequent application.

Pro-caution: the verification phase (shadow comparison, canary monitoring, mismatch investigation) is not as accelerated. The judgment calls remain the human's. So the total elapsed time of a big refactor is less reduced than the implementation time alone. Additionally, the codebase undergoing constant refactoring is a codebase where engineers cannot maintain a stable mental model; some stability is itself valuable.

The synthesis: AI lowers the _implementation_ cost of big refactors; the _verification, judgment, and team-attention_ costs are roughly unchanged. The new equilibrium is more big refactors than 2024 but not "constantly refactoring." Teams that have a clear migration target, sufficient bandwidth, and demonstrated migration competence should do them more often; teams that lack those should not, regardless of AI speed.

**Q9.** Consider the Strangler Fig pattern (preview of Chapter 20) versus the big-rewrite alternative (Chapter 21). Why is the Strangler Fig the safer choice, and when is the rewrite ever the right answer?

**Discussion:** The Strangler Fig is safer because each step is small, verifiable, and reversible. The old system stays as the safety net throughout; if the new system has bugs, the old system catches them. The migration completes incrementally; the team learns as they go; the architecture can be revised mid-migration without restarting.

The big rewrite is the opposite: a long period of no shipping, no verifiable progress, no fallback. Joel Spolsky's "Things You Should Never Do, Part I" essay (Chapter 21) names this as the canonical failure mode. Most rewrites take much longer than estimated and end in either abandonment or a hybrid system that has the worst features of both.

When is the rewrite right? Rarely, but the cases exist:

* **Architectural impossibility** : the old system's design fundamentally cannot accommodate a required new behavior, and there is no incremental path. Example: migrating from a single-tenant to a multi-tenant database with completely different schema.
* **Domain change** : the underlying business or technical assumptions have changed so much that the old system is solving a different problem. Example: a system designed for batch processing being repurposed for real-time.
* **Strong team commitment with full leadership backing** : the team is willing and resourced for a multi-quarter rewrite, with no expectation of feature work during it. This is rare and usually preceded by an explicit business decision.

In most cases, the Strangler Fig wins. The big rewrite is the last resort, not the default. Chapter 21 develops this in depth.

### Chapter Summary

Big refactors (§11.1) are justified when the subsystem is changed frequently, the debt drags velocity measurably, the structure prevents an upcoming change, and the team has bandwidth and a clear target. All four matter; missing any one weakens the case.

The parallel-paths strategy (§11.2) is the core technique: build the new path alongside the old, route via feature flags, compare in shadow mode, migrate gradually via canary phases. The strategy bounds the blast radius of any bug to the percentage of traffic on the new path.

Feature flags (§11.3) are the safety mechanism. They make each phase change a configuration change rather than a deployment; rollbacks are seconds, not hours. The flag design for big refactors is more elaborate than typical product flags: per-percentage granularity, primary-path and shadow-read independence, planned removal lifecycle.

The Strangler Fig pattern (§11.4) is the broader strategy that contains parallel paths. Chapter 20 develops it; here, the point is that parallel paths is the technique for each function, while Strangler Fig is the strategy for the whole migration.

The AI-Layer (§11.5) is that agents are excellent at the implementation phase (writing the new path from the old as a spec) but cannot do the judgment phases (migration decision, infrastructure design, mismatch investigation, phase-advance decisions). The agent multiplies productivity on implementation by 3-4×; the human's work is concentrated in design and verification.

The risks (§11.6) are named: drift between paths, mid-migration attention loss, naive comparison, low-frequency bugs masked by short canary, hidden coupling at deletion. Each has a mitigation; the discipline is to plan for them upfront, not to discover them mid-migration.

Chapter 12 opens Part V (Patterns Sensibly Applied) with the patterns that stayed useful: Strategy, Adapter, Decorator, Observer, Iterator, Command. The transition from refactoring to patterns is natural: refactoring often produces patterns; patterns are the vocabulary of repeated refactoring moves; knowing both gives you the tools to recognize and apply structural improvements at scale.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Before approving a six-month rewrite proposal, demand the parallel-paths alternative on paper.** Most "we have to rewrite" arguments collapse when forced to articulate the Strangler Fig version of the same migration. Make the choice explicit, not default.
