  2. **Treat the shadow-comparison logic as production code.** Tests for the comparison itself; periodic audits of "matched" traffic by hand to verify the comparison is actually matching. Naive equality misses the bugs that justify shadow comparison in the first place.
  3. **Set a calendar deadline for the migration's deletion phase before the first commit lands.** Six months from now, the old code is deleted; no exceptions without an explicit team decision. Open-ended migrations become permanent dual-system tax.

---

## Chapter 12: Patterns That Stayed Useful

A junior engineer at Helix asked Aki, in early 2026, whether design patterns still mattered. "The Gang of Four book is from 1994," the junior said. "The patterns assume Java idioms that nobody writes anymore. And anyway, can I not just ask the AI for the right shape?" Aki's answer, which became part of her standard onboarding talk, was: "Some patterns aged badly. The ones we cover next chapter. But six of them stayed useful because they describe real design moves that recur across languages and decades. They are not Java idioms; they are _vocabulary_ for moves you will make repeatedly. The AI knows the patterns from its training data. If you do not, you cannot recognize when the AI applies one well and when it applies one badly. So yes, you need to learn them."

This chapter teaches the six patterns from the Gang of Four's _Design Patterns_ (1994) that have survived three decades of language and tooling evolution: Strategy, Adapter, Decorator, Observer, Iterator, Command. Each is shown in a 2026 idiom rather than the original Java; each is matched to the kind of problem it solves and the signal that should trigger it. Chapter 13 covers the patterns that did not survive.

### 12.1 Strategy

The Strategy pattern lets you swap algorithmic behavior at runtime by encapsulating each behavior in its own object behind a common interface.

#### Definition: Strategy

The Strategy pattern defines a family of interchangeable algorithms, each in its own class implementing a common interface. The client code holds a reference to the interface and can swap the concrete strategy without changing its own code.

In plain language: when you have many ways to do the same kind of thing, and you want to pick the way at runtime, Strategy is the shape.

#### A 2026 example

A pricing service computes prices via different rules for different customer tiers. A naive implementation uses a conditional:
    
    
    def price_for(customer, product):
        if customer.tier == 'free':
            return product.list_price
        elif customer.tier == 'pro':
            return product.list_price * 0.9
        elif customer.tier == 'enterprise':
            return negotiated_price(customer, product)
    

Strategy version:
    
    
    from typing import Protocol
    
    class PricingStrategy(Protocol):
        def price_for(self, customer: Customer, product: Product) -> Decimal: ...
    
    class ListPricePricing:
        def price_for(self, c, p): return p.list_price
    
    class TenPercentDiscountPricing:
        def price_for(self, c, p): return p.list_price * Decimal('0.9')
    
    class NegotiatedPricing:
        def __init__(self, negotiator): self._n = negotiator
        def price_for(self, c, p): return self._n.lookup(c, p)
    
    # A registry maps tier to strategy
    PRICING_STRATEGIES = {
        'free': ListPricePricing(),
        'pro': TenPercentDiscountPricing(),
        'enterprise': NegotiatedPricing(negotiator),
    }
    
    class PricingService:
        def __init__(self, strategies):
            self._strategies = strategies
        def price_for(self, customer, product):
            strategy = self._strategies[customer.tier]
            return strategy.price_for(customer, product)
    

The benefit: each pricing rule is testable in isolation; adding a new tier is adding a strategy, not modifying a conditional; the `PricingService` is small and stable.

#### When Strategy is the right pattern

* **The algorithm choice is data-driven** , often by a runtime value (tier, region, configuration).
* **The algorithms are independently testable** and benefit from separate test files.
* **You expect the set of algorithms to grow** , and you want adding new ones to not modify existing code (the Open-Closed Principle in action).
* **Multiple call sites need the same choice** , so encapsulating the dispatch in one place reduces duplication.

#### When Strategy is the wrong pattern

If you have two algorithms and one conditional, Strategy is over-engineering. Wait until you have three or four before extracting; the cost of the abstraction (an extra layer of types and a strategy registry) only pays back when there is enough variation to justify it. This is the discipline that distinguishes pattern-applied-well from pattern-tax (Chapter 13).

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "Strategy is appropriate any time you have a conditional." The mistake is treating Strategy as a refactoring of small conditionals; the pattern is for families of algorithms, not for binary if/else. A two-case conditional is usually clearer as a conditional than as two strategy classes. The threshold is roughly three or more algorithms, growing.

### 12.2 Adapter

The Adapter pattern lets you use an object with an interface incompatible with the one your code expects, by wrapping it in a class that translates between the two.

#### Definition: Adapter

The Adapter pattern wraps an object that has one interface to expose a different interface. The adapter's job is purely translation: it forwards calls to the wrapped object with whatever signature changes are needed.

In plain language: when you have something that almost fits but has the wrong shape, wrap it in something the right shape.

#### A 2026 example

You are integrating Stripe and PayPal into a payment system. Both vendors have SDKs, but the SDKs have different signatures:
    
    
    # Stripe SDK
    stripe.Charge.create(amount=1000, currency='usd', source='tok_visa')
    
    # PayPal SDK
    paypal_client.create_payment({'amount': '10.00', 'currency': 'USD', 'method': 'paypal'})
    

Your application code should not branch on vendor. Adapters provide a common interface:
    
    
    class PaymentProcessor(Protocol):
        def charge(self, amount_cents: int, currency: str, token: str) -> ChargeResult: ...
    
    class StripeAdapter:
        def charge(self, amount_cents, currency, token):
            result = stripe.Charge.create(
                amount=amount_cents,
                currency=currency.lower(),
                source=token,
            )
            return ChargeResult(id=result.id, status=result.status)
    
    class PayPalAdapter:
        def __init__(self, client): self._client = client
        def charge(self, amount_cents, currency, token):
            amount_str = f'{amount_cents / 100:.2f}'
            result = self._client.create_payment({
                'amount': amount_str,
                'currency': currency.upper(),
                'method': 'paypal',
                'token': token,
            })
            return ChargeResult(id=result['id'], status=result['status'])
    
    # Application code
    class CheckoutService:
        def __init__(self, processor: PaymentProcessor):
            self._processor = processor
        def checkout(self, order):
            return self._processor.charge(
                amount_cents=order.total_cents,
                currency=order.currency,
                token=order.payment_token,
            )
    

The benefit: the application code is vendor-agnostic; switching vendors is a constructor argument change; testing the application code uses a fake adapter; the vendor-specific details stay in the adapters.

#### When Adapter is the right pattern

* **You are integrating with an external system** whose interface you do not control.
* **You want the rest of the codebase to depend on a stable interface** , not on the vendor's interface.
* **You may want to switch vendors** at some point and want the swap to be local.
* **You want to test the application code** without hitting the vendor.

This is essentially the Repository pattern (Chapter 7) generalized beyond data storage: any external system gets an adapter.

#### When Adapter is the wrong pattern

If you have one vendor, one usage, and no testing pain, the adapter is over-engineering. Most teams add adapters after they discover the pain of vendor coupling rather than preemptively. The discipline is: extract an adapter the _second_ time you find yourself wanting to test around a vendor SDK or wanting to swap vendors.

### 12.3 Decorator

The Decorator pattern adds behavior to an object dynamically by wrapping it in another object that has the same interface.

#### Definition: Decorator

The Decorator pattern wraps an object in another object that implements the same interface; the outer object adds behavior before or after delegating to the inner object. Decorators stack: you can wrap a decorator in another decorator, with each layer adding its own behavior.

In plain language: when you have a thing that does X, and you want to add Y around it (logging, caching, retrying, validation), wrap it in something that does Y and then forwards to it.

#### A 2026 example

You have an HTTP client. You want to add logging, retries, and caching. Each is a cross-cutting concern; each should be independently applied:
    
    
    class HttpClient(Protocol):
        def get(self, url: str) -> Response: ...
    
    class CoreHttpClient:
        def get(self, url):
            return requests.get(url)
    
    class LoggingHttpClient:
        def __init__(self, inner): self._inner = inner
        def get(self, url):
            log.info(f'HTTP GET {url}')
            return self._inner.get(url)
    
    class RetryingHttpClient:
        def __init__(self, inner, max_attempts=3): self._inner = inner; self._max = max_attempts
        def get(self, url):
            for attempt in range(self._max):
                try:
                    return self._inner.get(url)
                except TransientError:
                    if attempt == self._max - 1:
                        raise
    
    class CachingHttpClient:
        def __init__(self, inner, cache): self._inner = inner; self._cache = cache
        def get(self, url):
            if url in self._cache:
                return self._cache[url]
            response = self._inner.get(url)
            self._cache[url] = response
            return response
    
    # Compose: cache outermost (avoid retries on cache hits), retry next, log innermost
    client = CachingHttpClient(RetryingHttpClient(LoggingHttpClient(CoreHttpClient()), max_attempts=3), cache)
    

The benefit: each behavior is in its own class; you can apply them in any combination; testing the core client does not require logging or retry mocks; the order of wrapping is the order of behavior application.

#### When Decorator is the right pattern

* **You want to add cross-cutting concerns** (logging, retry, caching, validation, authentication) to existing behavior.
* **The concerns are independent** and you may want to apply some without others.
* **You want the core object to remain focused** on its primary responsibility.

#### When Decorator is the wrong pattern

If the behavior addition is a one-off and unlikely to recombine, an inline modification or a subclass may be simpler. Decorators shine when there is a _family_ of concerns that combine; for a single cross-cutting concern with no recombination, the abstraction can be heavier than the inline alternative.

### 12.4 Observer

The Observer pattern lets one object publish events that other objects subscribe to, without the publisher knowing the subscribers.

#### Definition: Observer

The Observer pattern defines a one-to-many relationship between a _subject_ and its _observers_. When the subject changes state, it notifies all observers; the observers react. The subject does not know the observers' types; the observers register themselves.

In plain language: when you have something that emits events and other things that should react to those events, Observer is the decoupling shape.

#### A 2026 example

An order service emits events when orders change state. Various subsystems need to react: send a notification email, update the analytics dashboard, audit-log the change, trigger fulfillment:
    
    
    from typing import Callable
    
    class OrderEventBus:
        def __init__(self):
            self._subscribers: dict[str, list[Callable]] = {}
        def subscribe(self, event_type: str, handler: Callable):
            self._subscribers.setdefault(event_type, []).append(handler)
        def publish(self, event_type: str, event):
            for handler in self._subscribers.get(event_type, []):
                try:
                    handler(event)
                except Exception as e:
                    log.error(f'Handler {handler} failed for {event_type}: {e}')
    
    # Subscribers register at startup
    bus = OrderEventBus()
    bus.subscribe('order.placed', send_confirmation_email)
    bus.subscribe('order.placed', update_analytics)
    bus.subscribe('order.placed', trigger_fulfillment)
    bus.subscribe('order.cancelled', send_cancellation_email)
    bus.subscribe('order.cancelled', refund_payment)
    
    # The order service publishes
    class OrderService:
        def __init__(self, bus): self._bus = bus
        def place_order(self, order):
            # ... do the work ...
            self._bus.publish('order.placed', OrderPlacedEvent(order_id=order.id))
    

The benefit: `OrderService` does not know about email, analytics, or fulfillment; subscribers register independently; adding a new subscriber does not modify `OrderService`.

#### When Observer is the right pattern

* **You have a publisher with multiple unrelated reactors**.
* **The reactors should not be coupled to the publisher** , and the publisher should not be coupled to the reactors.
* **You want to add or remove reactors at runtime** or through configuration.
* **The reactors are genuinely independent** , so a failure in one does not prevent others from running.

#### When Observer is the wrong pattern

If you have one reactor, Observer is over-engineering; a direct call is simpler. If the reactor must run synchronously with the publisher and the publisher needs to know whether it succeeded, Observer's fire-and-forget semantics are wrong. For tight coupling with success/failure feedback, a direct call or a Command (§12.6) is better.

In 2026 distributed systems, Observer often becomes a message queue (RabbitMQ, Kafka, AWS SNS): the pattern scales beyond in-process by changing the publish mechanism while keeping the shape.

### 12.5 Iterator

The Iterator pattern lets you traverse a collection without exposing its internal structure.

#### Definition: Iterator

The Iterator pattern provides a sequential access mechanism over a collection. The iterator hides the collection's internal structure (list, tree, lazy stream); the caller sees only a `next` operation.

In plain language: when you have something with elements, expose iteration without exposing storage.

#### A 2026 example

Iterator is so deeply built into modern languages that you rarely implement it explicitly. Python has `__iter__` and `__next__`; JavaScript has generators and iterators; Rust has the `Iterator` trait; Go has range. The pattern is realized natively.
    
    
    class TreeNode:
        def __init__(self, value, children=None):
            self.value = value
            self.children = children or []
        def __iter__(self):
            # In-order traversal
            yield self.value
            for child in self.children:
                yield from child
    
    root = TreeNode(1, [TreeNode(2), TreeNode(3, [TreeNode(4)])])
    for value in root:
        print(value)  # 1, 2, 3, 4
    

The benefit: the caller does not need to know the tree's structure; the traversal can change (depth-first, breadth-first, in-order) without changing caller code.

#### When Iterator is the right pattern

* **You expose a collection** (any data structure with elements) to callers.
* **The internal structure may change** but the iteration semantics should not.
* **The collection may be infinite or lazily computed** (a stream, a paginated API result, a generated sequence) and you do not want to materialize the whole thing.
* **Multiple traversal orders are valuable** (e.g. tree traversals).

#### The lazy-iteration superpower

Iterators enable lazy evaluation:
    
    
    def primes():
        yield 2
        candidates = (i for i in itertools.count(3, 2))  # 3, 5, 7, 9, ...
        found = [2]
        for c in candidates:
            if all(c % p != 0 for p in found if p * p <= c):
                found.append(c)
                yield c
    
    # Get the first 10 primes without computing all primes
    first_ten = list(itertools.islice(primes(), 10))
    

The infinite stream of primes is iterable but never fully materialized. This is the modern shape of Iterator and is used pervasively in 2026 codebases for paginated APIs, log streams, event sources.

#### When Iterator is the wrong pattern

Iterator is rarely the wrong pattern in 2026; it is built into the language and applies almost reflexively. The wrong-pattern question is more often "should I implement an explicit Iterator class, or use the language's native iteration?" The answer is almost always native iteration; explicit Iterator classes are a Java-1990s artifact.

### 12.6 Command

The Command pattern wraps a request as an object, allowing you to parameterize, queue, log, or undo it.

#### Definition: Command

The Command pattern represents a request as an object. The object encapsulates the action, the receiver, and the parameters; the caller does not invoke the action directly but submits the command to be executed. This enables queueing, logging, retrying, and undoing.

In plain language: turn a function call into a data object; pass the data around; execute it later or differently.

#### A 2026 example

A background job system processes commands asynchronously:
    
    
    from dataclasses import dataclass
    from typing import Protocol
    
    class Command(Protocol):
        def execute(self) -> None: ...
    
    @dataclass
    class SendEmailCommand:
        to: str
        subject: str
        body: str
        def execute(self):
            email_service.send(self.to, self.subject, self.body)
    
    @dataclass
    class ProcessRefundCommand:
        order_id: str
        amount_cents: int
        def execute(self):
            refund_service.refund(self.order_id, self.amount_cents)
    
    class CommandQueue:
        def __init__(self):
            self._queue = []
        def submit(self, command: Command):
            self._queue.append(command)
        def process_all(self):
            while self._queue:
                cmd = self._queue.pop(0)
                try:
                    cmd.execute()
                except Exception:
                    log.exception(f'Command failed: {cmd}')
    
    # Usage
    queue = CommandQueue()
    queue.submit(SendEmailCommand(to='alice@example.com', subject='Refund', body='...'))
    queue.submit(ProcessRefundCommand(order_id='ord_123', amount_cents=5000))
    queue.process_all()
    

The benefit: commands are data; they can be serialized, logged, persisted to a database for retry, replayed for debugging. The execution layer can implement retry, dead-lettering, and observability uniformly across all commands.

#### When Command is the right pattern

* **You need to queue actions** for later execution (background jobs, batch processing).
* **You need to log actions** for audit or replay (event sourcing, undo-redo).
* **You need to parameterize callers** with operations they can invoke without knowing the internals.
* **You need to apply uniform cross-cutting behavior** to many different actions (retry, logging, metrics).

#### When Command is the wrong pattern

If you are calling a function and want the result back immediately, Command is unnecessary; a function call is simpler. Command shines when the call is _delayed_ or _transformed_ ; for synchronous direct execution, the pattern adds layers without value.

In 2026, Command underlies most background job systems (Celery, Sidekiq, BullMQ, RQ): the job is a command; the queue is the Command Queue; the worker is the executor. The pattern is realized in infrastructure rather than in application code, but the shape is the same.

### 12.7 The AI-Layer: when LLMs reach for patterns well and badly

LLMs in 2026 know all the Gang of Four patterns from their training data. They reach for them when prompted with problem descriptions that match the patterns' signals. The reach is sometimes right and sometimes wrong; the human's job is to recognize which.

#### When LLMs reach well

* **Strategy** for data-driven algorithm choice: the LLM correctly recognizes "different behavior per tier" and proposes Strategy. The proposal is often well-shaped.
* **Adapter** for vendor integration: the LLM proposes adapters when asked to integrate two SDKs. The shape is usually right; the names sometimes need adjustment.
* **Iterator** is automatic; LLMs default to language-native iteration without prompting.
* **Command** for background jobs: the LLM proposes Command when asked to design async job processing. The proposal usually matches the team's job framework.

#### When LLMs reach badly

* **Strategy for two-case conditionals** : the LLM applies Strategy where a simple conditional would do. The result is pattern-tax.
* **Decorator for one-off concerns** : the LLM creates a decorator hierarchy when a single inline modification was sufficient.
* **Observer when direct calls work** : the LLM introduces an event bus when the publisher and reactor are 1:1 and tightly coupled.
* **Over-application of any pattern** : the LLM applies multiple patterns to a small problem ("Let's use Strategy plus Decorator plus Observer here") when a flat function would have been clearer.

#### The discipline

The discipline is to _recognize_ when a pattern applies before letting the AI apply one. If you, the engineer, do not see the signal that justifies the pattern, do not accept the AI's pattern application. The signals from §§12.1-12.6 are the check; the AI's confidence is not.

This is the central craft argument for learning patterns. The AI can name them; the AI can apply them; only the engineer can decide whether the pattern fits. Without the vocabulary, the engineer accepts the AI's patterns blindly; with the vocabulary, the engineer reviews them. And one defense against the dismissal "patterns are for engineers who do not know modern languages well enough": the Gang of Four patterns are _design moves_ , not language idioms. The Java-1990s implementations were idioms and are dated; the moves transfer. An engineer who has internalized the moves writes Pythonic Strategy in Python and idiomatic Rust Strategy in Rust; the move is the same.

### 12.8 Patterns as vocabulary for design conversation

The deepest argument for patterns is not implementation. It is _vocabulary_. When two engineers discuss a design and one says "Strategy on the tier," the other immediately understands: an interface, multiple implementations, runtime dispatch by tier. The conversation is fast and precise.

Without the vocabulary, the same conversation would require describing the shape from scratch: "What if we extract each tier's pricing into its own class, with a common interface, and dispatch by tier at the call site?" The shape is the same; the conversation is slower and less precise.

Carmen Ortiz at Currents observed in 2025 that engineers who had learned the patterns in their first three years had measurably faster design discussions in code review. The cost was a few weeks of deliberate study early in their careers; the benefit accrued over every subsequent design conversation.

This is the case for learning patterns even in 2026 when AI can apply them on your behalf: the AI cannot have the conversation with your colleagues. You can.

### 12.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A junior engineer shows you two designs they have drafted for the codebase and asks "is this Strategy or Adapter?" — both have the same code shape (a Protocol + multiple implementations). Tell them which pattern each design is and _why_ the answer matters for how the team discusses these in code review and design conversations.

**Design A** : a `PricingPolicy` Protocol with `FreePricing`, `ProPricing`, `EnterprisePricing` implementations. The application picks one based on the customer's tier and uses it.

**Design B** : a `PaymentProcessor` Protocol with `StripeProcessor`, `PayPalProcessor`, `AdyenProcessor` implementations. The application has a configured default and never picks at runtime.

Hint

Both share the shape; the _intent_ is different.

**Answer:**

**Design A is Strategy.** The three implementations are _alternative ways of doing the same thing internally_ ; the application chooses among them per request based on data (customer tier). They are designed together as a family. The interface organizes the family.

**Design B is Adapter.** The three implementations are _translations of external systems_ into the application's expected interface. The application does not pick among them at runtime; one is configured per environment. The implementations exist because the external SDKs have different shapes; the Adapter normalizes them.

Why the distinction matters in conversation:

* "Strategy on the tier" tells your team: the choice is runtime, data-driven, varies per request. Reviewers will look for the dispatch logic and the rule for tier selection.
* "Adapter for Stripe" tells your team: there is one external SDK whose shape is being wrapped. Reviewers will look at the Adapter and ask whether the wrapping correctly hides the SDK's quirks. They will _not_ look for runtime dispatch.

A reviewer who knows the difference saves the team minutes per review by not asking the wrong questions. A team without shared vocabulary has both reviewers ask both kinds of question on both kinds of code; some of those questions waste author and reviewer time. Naming the pattern lets the conversation skip to the substance.

The same code shape, two patterns, two different reviewing concerns. The vocabulary is the multiplier.

**Q2.** When does the Decorator pattern overlap with using middleware or composition functionally?

Hint

Middleware in web frameworks is often Decorator under the hood.

**Answer:** Web framework middleware (Express middleware, FastAPI dependencies, Django middleware) is essentially the Decorator pattern. Each middleware wraps the next, adds behavior before or after delegating, and forms a chain. The interface is "handle a request and return a response"; each middleware implements it and forwards to the next.

Functional composition (`compose(f, g, h)(x) = f(g(h(x)))`) is the same shape expressed in functional rather than OO style. The behaviors stack; the order matters; the result is a single combined function.

The differences are mostly stylistic: middleware/decorator emphasizes the object-with-methods view; functional composition emphasizes the function-as-value view. In 2026, the choice is usually a question of language idiom and team preference. The pattern is the same.

**Q3.** A staff engineer reviewing AI-generated code asks for your judgment. The AI proposed this design for paginating through a database table of 50M rows:
    
    
    class UserPaginator:
        def __init__(self, db, page_size=1000):
            self._db = db
            self._page_size = page_size
            self._cursor = 0
        def has_next(self) -> bool: ...
        def next(self) -> list[User]: ...
        def reset(self) -> None: ...
    

The reviewer says: "This is the Iterator pattern. Strategy and Adapter we accept; Iterator we should reject as over-engineering. Use a generator." Is the reviewer right? When is an explicit Iterator class the correct shape in 2026 versus when is a generator strictly better?

Hint

Generators _are_ iterators; the difference is the implementation cost and the visibility of state.

**Answer:** The reviewer is partly right. The pattern (Iterator) is the right design move — separating traversal from collection storage. The 1995 Java _implementation_ of the pattern (a class with `hasNext`/`next`) is what aged badly; the 2026 Python idiom is a generator function:
    
    
    def paginate_users(db, page_size=1000):
        cursor = 0
        while True:
            page = db.query("SELECT * FROM users WHERE id > %s ORDER BY id LIMIT %s",
                            (cursor, page_size)).fetchall()
            if not page:
                return
            yield page
            cursor = page[-1].id
    

Generators _are_ iterators; the language supports the pattern natively; the explicit class adds boilerplate and exposes mutable state (`self._cursor`) that the generator hides inside the local scope.

**When the explicit class is right:**

* The iterator needs _operations beyond`next()`_: `reset()`, `seek_to(cursor)`, `current_position()`. These can be implemented on a class; they cannot on a generator function.
* The iteration is _stateful in a way callers need to inspect_ (e.g., progress reporting, observability).
* The language is old or weak on generators (older Java versions, embedded C contexts).

**When the generator is strictly better:**

* The caller just wants to iterate, possibly with `break`/early exit.
* The state is purely internal and the caller does not need to inspect it.
* The codebase is Python 3.10+, modern JS, Rust, etc. (the typical 2026 backend).

In the proposed code, the `UserPaginator` class has `next()`, `has_next()`, and `reset()`. If callers actually use `reset()`, the class is justified. If not (the class was added speculatively because the AI saw it in tutorials), reject it; use the generator. Push back with the question: "Show me one caller that uses `reset()`. If none, simplify to a generator function." The AI's defaults reflect training data; the team's _signal-driven_ discipline filters them.

**Q4.** When does Command pattern overlap with feature flags (Chapter 4) or message queues?

Hint

All three deal with delayed or conditional execution.

**Answer:** Command captures an action as data; feature flags conditionally execute different code; message queues transport commands between processes. The patterns intersect but solve different problems:

* **Command + Queue** is the canonical async-jobs pattern. The command is the work-unit; the queue is the transport; together they make the action transportable.
* **Command + Flag** is the conditional-execution pattern. The flag chooses _which_ command to execute; the command does the work.
* **Flag + Queue** alone is the gradual-rollout pattern (Chapter 11); flags control which percentage of requests go through which path.

The three combined are the 2026 background-job-with-rollout pattern: commands represent units of work; queues transport them; flags choose which version of the work to run for a given unit. Background job frameworks like Sidekiq with its versioning support implement exactly this.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Identify a place in your codebase where a Strategy or Adapter would clarify the design. Implement it. Compare the before and after for readability and testability.

Hint

Look for a 5+ branch conditional or a vendor SDK called directly from business logic.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common Strategy candidate: a discount calculation with a 5-case conditional on customer tier. Refactor to a strategy registry; each tier's discount logic is in its own class; the dispatch is a dictionary lookup. The before is one function with 30 lines of conditional; the after is one dispatch class plus five small strategy classes, each independently testable.

A common Adapter candidate: a vendor SDK call (Stripe, Twilio, SendGrid) appearing inline in business logic. Wrap the SDK in an adapter exposing the application's interface; replace the inline call with the adapter call. The business logic is now testable without the SDK; switching vendors is a constructor change.

In both cases, the upfront cost is real (more files, more classes); the payoff is in the second usage and beyond.

**Q6.** Add three Decorators to a function in your codebase: one for logging, one for retries, one for caching. Compose them in different orders and observe the behavior.

Hint

Order matters: outer decorators see fewer calls than inner ones.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. With three decorators on a function `f`:

* **Cache outermost, retry middle, log inner** : cache hits never trigger retry or log; misses do retry and log. This is the usual production composition.
* **Log outermost, retry middle, cache inner** : every call is logged once (good for visibility); retries cache on success; cache hits skip the next call.
* **Retry outermost, log middle, cache inner** : retries see multiple log entries (one per attempt) and multiple cache lookups. Useful for diagnosing the retry behavior.

The exercise teaches the order-sensitivity: decorators are not commutative. The team should agree on a standard order for production usage and document it.

**Q7.** Use an AI agent to refactor a piece of code into a pattern (Strategy, Adapter, or Observer). Review the result. Did the agent choose the right pattern? Did it apply it well? Did it over-apply?

Hint

The agent's pattern choices reflect its training data; LLMs in 2026 lean toward over-application.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* The agent applied the pattern but introduced extra layers (a factory, a registry, a builder) that the human would not have added. Strip the extras.
* The agent applied the pattern correctly but named the classes generically (`ConcreteStrategyA`, `ConcreteStrategyB`). Rename in domain terms.
* The agent applied a different pattern than requested (Observer instead of Strategy) because it interpreted the problem differently. Discuss the interpretation; choose the appropriate pattern; reapply.

The lesson: patterns are vocabulary. The agent knows the vocabulary but does not have the context that makes one pattern more appropriate than another for a specific codebase. The human's judgment is the differentiator.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "Patterns are vocabulary for design conversation; they justify themselves through faster team communication, not through code." Counter-argue: "Patterns introduce overhead in code and should be applied sparingly; their conversational value is overstated."

**Discussion:** For: §12.8 argued that the conversational value is real and measurable. A team with shared pattern vocabulary discusses designs faster and more precisely. The code overhead of applying patterns is often small (a few extra classes), and the overhead is paid once per application while the conversational benefit accrues continuously.

Against: in practice, many teams have _partial_ knowledge of patterns. Some engineers know them; others do not. The conversation that uses pattern names becomes alienating for the engineers who do not know them. The benefit is real but unevenly distributed, and the teams that _over_ -use pattern names create a dialect that excludes newer engineers.

The synthesis: patterns are valuable vocabulary _when shared by the whole team_. The investment should be in _team-wide_ pattern literacy (workshops, code reviews that name patterns, onboarding that covers the canonical set), not in a few engineers using pattern names to sound sophisticated. The vocabulary is a multiplier only when distributed; concentrated in a few mouths, it is a barrier.

**Q9.** Trace the relationship between the six patterns of this chapter and the twelve refactorings of Chapter 9. Which refactorings tend to produce which patterns?

**Discussion:**

* **Replace Conditional with Polymorphism (Chapter 9, §9.5)** is the refactoring that often produces **Strategy (§12.1)** : the conditional is replaced by a family of classes implementing a common interface.
* **Extract Class (Chapter 9, §9.7)** can produce **Adapter (§12.2)** when the extracted class wraps an external vendor SDK, or **Strategy** when the extracted class encapsulates one of a family of algorithms.
* **Replace Inheritance with Composition (Chapter 9, §9.8)** can produce **Decorator (§12.3)** when the composition is wrapping for cross-cutting concerns.
* **Combine Functions into Class (Chapter 9, §9.11)** can produce **Iterator (§12.5)** when the combined functions traverse a collection, or **Command (§12.6)** when they represent an action.
* **Decompose Conditional (Chapter 9, §9.12)** can produce **Strategy** when the decomposition reveals a family of behaviors.

The pattern is: _refactorings are the moves; patterns are the destinations_. The twelve refactorings of Chapter 9 are the mechanical tools; the patterns of this chapter and the next are the shapes that the tools produce. An engineer who knows both has the mechanics (refactorings) and the vocabulary (patterns) to design with confidence.

### Chapter Summary

Six patterns from the Gang of Four (Strategy §12.1, Adapter §12.2, Decorator §12.3, Observer §12.4, Iterator §12.5, Command §12.6) survived three decades of language evolution because each encodes a design move that recurs across languages and problem domains. The Java-1990s implementations are dated; the moves are not.

Each pattern has signals that trigger it and counter-signals that warn against application. Strategy fits families of algorithms with runtime dispatch; Adapter fits external-system integration with vendor swapping; Decorator fits cross-cutting concerns that combine; Observer fits multi-reactor publish-subscribe; Iterator fits collection traversal independent of structure; Command fits actions-as-data for queueing, logging, and undoing. The patterns can over-apply; the discipline is to recognize the signals before applying.

The AI-Layer (§12.7) is that LLMs reach for patterns from their training data; they reach well for Strategy, Adapter, Iterator, and Command; they over-apply Strategy to small conditionals and Decorator to one-off concerns. The human's job is to recognize the signal before accepting the AI's pattern; without the vocabulary, the engineer accepts blindly.

Patterns as vocabulary (§12.8) is the deepest argument: the patterns enable fast, precise design conversation among engineers who share the vocabulary. The investment in learning is recovered over every subsequent design discussion.

Chapter 13 covers the patterns that aged badly: Singleton replaced by dependency injection, Visitor replaced by sum types, Abstract Factory Factory replaced by nothing because it never worked. The chapter addresses Thread C lie #2 (over-abstraction) directly: LLMs sometimes propose these patterns; the engineer who knows why they aged badly can decline the proposal.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Refuse to apply a pattern until you have a second concrete case.** Strategy for one conditional is pattern-tax; Strategy for three is design. Wait for the second case; let the pattern emerge from demand, not anticipation.
  2. **Use pattern names in design conversation, not in code comments.** "Strategy on the tier" in a Slack thread saves five minutes per stakeholder. `# This is the Strategy pattern` above a class adds noise; the code shape already says it.
  3. **When the agent proposes a pattern, ask for the _signal_ that justifies it.** "Why Strategy here? What is the second case?" If the agent cannot answer, simplify the proposal back to the flat alternative.

---

## Chapter 13: Patterns That Aged Badly

A 2024 PR at Helix added a class called `CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider`. The class's job was to construct a `CustomerRepositoryFactory`, which in turn constructed a `CustomerRepository`. The PR author was a senior engineer who had learned design patterns in 2010 and applied them reflexively. The PR reviewer, Aki, asked one question: "What does the Provider do that a function called `get_customer_repository()` would not do?" The author thought for a few minutes and could not answer. The PR was rewritten as a single function. The original three classes disappeared.

This chapter is about patterns that, in 2026 hindsight, fail more often than they succeed. Some were always questionable; some were appropriate in their original context and have been superseded by language features. All three categories are worth knowing because LLMs sometimes propose them (Thread C lie #2: over-abstraction) and because legacy codebases still contain them. Knowing why they aged badly is the foundation for refactoring them out and for declining to add new ones.

### 13.1 Singleton: replaced by dependency injection

The Singleton pattern ensures a class has exactly one instance and provides global access to it. In 1995, this was a defensible solution to "I need one of these and I want to use it from anywhere." In 2026, it is rarely the right answer.

#### Why Singleton aged badly

The Singleton's failures, in roughly increasing order of severity:

* **Global state hides dependencies.** A function that uses `Logger.getInstance()` does not name the logger as a dependency in its signature; the dependency is implicit. Readers cannot see what the function depends on without reading the body. Multiplied across a codebase, this hidden coupling becomes a navigation problem.
* **Singletons are hard to test.** A test for a function that uses `Logger.getInstance()` cannot easily substitute a fake logger; the singleton is the singleton. Test isolation suffers; tests interfere with each other if they share singleton state.
* **Singletons enforce one-instance globally.** Real systems often want one instance _per request_ , _per tenant_ , or _per environment_. The singleton's global-uniqueness assumption is wrong for these cases.
* **Singletons compose badly.** Two singletons that depend on each other create startup-order problems that no one wants to debug.

#### What replaced it: dependency injection

Dependency injection (DI) passes dependencies explicitly to the code that uses them. The "one instance" guarantee, if it is needed, comes from constructing the instance once at startup and passing it everywhere it is needed.
    
    
    # Singleton (do not use)
    class Logger:
        _instance = None
        @classmethod
        def get_instance(cls):
            if cls._instance is None:
                cls._instance = cls()
            return cls._instance
        def log(self, msg): ...
    
    def process_order(order):
        Logger.get_instance().log(f'Processing {order.id}')
        # ...
    
    # Dependency injection
    class Logger:
        def log(self, msg): ...
    
    class OrderService:
        def __init__(self, logger: Logger):
            self._logger = logger
        def process_order(self, order):
            self._logger.log(f'Processing {order.id}')
    
    # At startup, construct once
    logger = Logger()
    order_service = OrderService(logger=logger)
    

The benefit: `OrderService` declares its dependency in its constructor; tests can pass a fake logger; the application can construct multiple OrderServices if needed; the dependency graph is explicit.

#### When (rarely) Singleton is still appropriate

Singletons are still defensible for genuinely global, truly unique resources: a process-wide thread pool, a registry of native handles, an OS-level mutex. In each case, the global uniqueness is enforced by the underlying resource, not by application convention. These are rare; for most application-level objects, DI is the better default.

#### The 2026 reality

In 2026, most major frameworks build DI in. Spring (Java) has had it for decades; FastAPI (Python) has built-in dependency injection; NestJS (TypeScript) is essentially DI-shaped; Rust uses constructor injection idiomatically. The pattern's mechanics are no longer the engineer's problem; the framework handles construction and wiring.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "Singleton is fine for things that really are global, like loggers." The mistake is conflating "is logically singular" with "should be a Singleton." A logger is logically singular per process, but DI is still the better mechanism: you construct one logger at startup, inject it everywhere, and now your tests can substitute a fake without touching global state. The "logical singularity" is preserved; the "global access" failure mode is avoided.

### 13.2 Visitor: replaced by sum types and pattern matching

The Visitor pattern lets you add new operations to a class hierarchy without modifying the classes. The pattern works by having each class accept a visitor object and call back into a visitor method specific to its type:
    
    
    # Traditional Visitor (do not use in 2026)
    class Visitor:
        def visit_circle(self, c): pass
        def visit_square(self, s): pass
    
    class Shape:
        def accept(self, visitor): pass
    
    class Circle(Shape):
        def accept(self, visitor): return visitor.visit_circle(self)
    
    class Square(Shape):
        def accept(self, visitor): return visitor.visit_square(self)
    
    class AreaCalculator(Visitor):
        def visit_circle(self, c): return 3.14159 * c.radius ** 2
        def visit_square(self, s): return s.side ** 2
    
    class PerimeterCalculator(Visitor):
        def visit_circle(self, c): return 2 * 3.14159 * c.radius
        def visit_square(self, s): return 4 * s.side
    

The original problem: in Java 1995, adding a new operation (perimeter calculation) would require modifying every shape class. Visitor avoided this; the cost was the awkward double-dispatch boilerplate.

#### Why Visitor aged badly

* **The boilerplate is significant** : every operation requires a visitor class with one method per shape, and every shape needs an accept method. The pattern is more code than the simple alternative.
* **Adding a new shape now requires modifying every visitor** , undoing the Open-Closed advantage in the other direction. The pattern privileges adding operations but penalizes adding shapes; whether this fits your codebase depends on which direction is more common.
* **The pattern is hard to read** : the double-dispatch indirection (object accepts visitor, visitor visits object) is not obvious to readers unfamiliar with the pattern.

#### What replaced it: sum types and pattern matching

In 2026 languages with sum types (Rust, OCaml, Haskell, Scala) or pattern matching (Python 3.10+, JavaScript with limited support, C# with `switch` expressions), the alternative is far simpler:
    
    
    from dataclasses import dataclass
    from typing import Union
    import math
    
    @dataclass
    class Circle:
        radius: float
    
    @dataclass
    class Square:
        side: float
    
    Shape = Union[Circle, Square]
    
    def area(shape: Shape) -> float:
        match shape:
            case Circle(radius=r): return math.pi * r ** 2
            case Square(side=s): return s ** 2
    
    def perimeter(shape: Shape) -> float:
        match shape:
            case Circle(radius=r): return 2 * math.pi * r
            case Square(side=s): return 4 * s
    

The benefit: no accept methods, no visitor classes, no double-dispatch. Adding a new operation is adding a new function; adding a new shape requires updating every function (the type system flags missing cases at compile time in statically typed languages). The trade-offs are explicit and the code is shorter.

#### When (rarely) Visitor is still appropriate

In languages without pattern matching (older Java versions, C++ before 17, JavaScript without TypeScript), Visitor remains the canonical solution. In codebases that must support polymorphic dispatch with deep class hierarchies, Visitor may still be the right shape. Both cases are diminishing; the language ecosystem has moved.

#### The 2026 reality

Python 3.10's `match` statement, TypeScript's discriminated unions, Rust's `enum`, Scala's `sealed` traits, Kotlin's `sealed` classes: pattern matching over sum types is now standard. Visitor in new code is usually a sign that the engineer or the AI agent is applying patterns from training data without recognizing that the language has a simpler tool.

### 13.3 Abstract Factory Factory: the pattern that never worked

The chapter-opening `CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider` is a specific instance of a broader anti-pattern: layers of indirection that exist only to defer construction decisions to "some future flexibility" that never materializes.

#### The shape of the anti-pattern

The Gang of Four catalog included Abstract Factory: a class that constructs related families of objects. In a few specific cases (cross-platform UI toolkits, plug-in architectures), Abstract Factory was useful. The anti-pattern is its over-application:

* **Abstract Factory** : a `WidgetFactory` that creates `Button`, `Window`, `Menu`.
* **Factory Factory** : a `WidgetFactoryFactory` that creates `WidgetFactory` based on the platform.
* **Factory Factory Factory** : a `WidgetFactoryFactoryProvider` that selects the right `WidgetFactoryFactory` based on configuration.

Each level adds a layer of indirection. Each layer was justified at the time as "future flexibility." Each layer compounds: reading the code requires understanding three layers of construction before reaching the actual `Button`.

#### Why the anti-pattern persists in legacy code

* **Each layer was added in good faith** , often by an engineer who genuinely anticipated a flexibility need. The flexibility rarely materialized; the layer remained.
* **Removing a layer requires understanding all its callers**. Layers compound on both ends; refactoring them out is real work.
* **The team that originally added the layers has moved on**. The new team inherits the layers without the original justification.

#### What replaced it: just call the constructor

In 2026, the discipline is to avoid layers of construction abstraction until the flexibility is _demonstrated_ by a real second case. Until then, call the constructor directly or use a simple factory function. If a second case appears, introduce one factory; if a third case appears with a different family, then consider an Abstract Factory. Adding the Factory Factory speculatively is the failure mode.
    
    
    # Anti-pattern
    class CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider:
        def get_factory(self, env):
            if env == 'production':
                return SqlCustomerRepositoryFactory()
            else:
                return InMemoryCustomerRepositoryFactory()
    
    class SqlCustomerRepositoryFactory:
        def create(self, db_pool):
            return SqlCustomerRepository(db_pool)
    
    class InMemoryCustomerRepositoryFactory:
        def create(self):
            return InMemoryCustomerRepository()
    
    # Application
    provider = CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider()
    factory = provider.get_factory(os.environ['ENV'])
    repo = factory.create(db_pool) if hasattr(factory, 'create') else factory.create()
    
    # 2026 alternative
    def make_customer_repository(env='production', db_pool=None):
        if env == 'production':
            return SqlCustomerRepository(db_pool)
        return InMemoryCustomerRepository()
    
    # Application
    repo = make_customer_repository(env=os.environ['ENV'], db_pool=db_pool)
    

The 2026 version is one function with one if-statement, doing exactly what the original three-class hierarchy did. Adding a third backend is adding one branch; no factory hierarchy to update.

#### The pattern-tax framing

We call this _pattern-tax_ : the overhead of patterns applied for hypothetical flexibility that never arrives. The tax accumulates as the codebase ages; readers pay it on every navigation, every onboarding, every refactor. The right discipline is to refuse to pay the tax upfront; let the patterns emerge from real second cases.

This is Thread C lie #2 (over-abstraction) made concrete. LLMs in 2026 sometimes propose pattern-tax structures because their training data includes many tutorials that demonstrate Abstract Factory at full ceremony. The discipline is to recognize the anti-pattern and decline.

### 13.4 Other patterns worth knowing as historical

A small inventory of patterns that have lost favor:

#### Template Method

The Gang of Four pattern in which a base class defines an algorithm skeleton with abstract methods that subclasses override. Modern alternative: composition with strategy functions or hooks. The inheritance-based Template Method is brittle (subclasses are coupled to the base class's algorithm); the composition alternative is more flexible.

#### Memento

The pattern for capturing and restoring object state. In 2026, this is usually handled by serialization (JSON, MessagePack, Protocol Buffers) or by event sourcing (Chapter 17 touches on this). The explicit Memento object class is rarely needed.

#### Chain of Responsibility

A pattern in which a request passes through a chain of handlers, each deciding whether to process or pass along. Modern alternative: middleware (which is essentially the Decorator pattern from Chapter 12) or simple list iteration. The explicit Chain class is rarely needed; the middleware pattern is more flexible and better understood.

#### Builder

A pattern for constructing complex objects step by step. Modern alternative: named arguments and dataclasses. In Python: `@dataclass(kw_only=True)` provides what Builder used to require dozens of lines for. In TypeScript: object literal syntax. The Builder pattern was a Java-1990s response to Java's lack of named arguments; modern languages have the feature, and the pattern is unnecessary.

Each of these patterns appears in legacy code, in AI proposals, and in old textbooks. Recognizing them as outdated is part of the 2026 craft.

### 13.5 The AI-Layer: declining pattern-tax proposals

LLMs propose patterns from training data. The training data includes both well-applied patterns and over-applied patterns. The LLM does not distinguish; the engineer must.

#### Recognizing pattern-tax proposals

Signals that an AI proposal is pattern-tax rather than legitimate pattern application:

* **Multiple levels of factories or providers** for a single use case.
* **Abstract base classes with one concrete subclass** : the base exists only because the engineer (or the AI) expected a second subclass that never appeared.
* **Interfaces (or protocols) that are implemented by exactly one class** : the interface adds no value if there is only one implementation.
* **Patterns layered when a simpler structure would work** : Strategy plus Decorator plus Observer for a problem that needs none of them.
* **Generic names that imply future flexibility** : `Handler`, `Manager`, `Processor`, `Engine` without a domain-specific noun.

When you see these in an AI proposal, push back. The pattern is to ask: "What does this add over the simpler alternative? Can you show me a second case that justifies the abstraction?" If the AI cannot justify, simplify the proposal.

#### The CLAUDE.md discipline

Specify the team's pattern bias in CLAUDE.md (Chapter 22):
    
    
    ## Pattern preferences
    
    - Prefer plain functions to classes when a class has no state and one method.
    - Prefer concrete types to interfaces when there is only one implementation.
    - Prefer constructor injection (DI) to Singletons. Do not use Singleton.
    - Prefer pattern matching to Visitor. Do not implement Visitor classes.
    - Prefer named arguments to Builder. Do not implement Builder classes.
    - Introduce abstractions when a second concrete case exists, not before.
    - Pattern tax (Factory Factory, Provider Factory, etc.) is forbidden.
    

The instruction shifts the AI's defaults toward the team's preferences. Without it, the AI defaults to patterns from training data, including the over-applied ones.

#### Common Misconception (revisited)

A common misconception is "we should apply patterns now to anticipate future flexibility." The mistake is the prediction: most predicted flexibility never arrives. The cost of pattern-tax is paid upfront and continuously; the benefit of anticipated flexibility may never come. The discipline is to apply patterns _retrospectively_ , after a second case has appeared, rather than _prospectively_ , in anticipation of a second case.

A second common misconception is "patterns make code more professional." The mistake is conflating _complexity_ with _quality_. Code with five patterns is not better than code with one; it is more complex. Quality is the right structure for the problem, which is usually simpler than the pattern-laden alternative.

### 13.6 How to refactor pattern-tax out of legacy code

Inheriting a codebase full of pattern-tax is common in 2026. The disciplined response:

#### Step 1: identify the pattern-tax structures

Walk the codebase looking for the signals from §13.5. Common findings:

* One Singleton class accessed from twenty places (DI candidate).
* One Abstract Factory hierarchy used by one consumer (collapse to a function).
* One Visitor with two implementations and four shape types (collapse to pattern matching).
* One Builder for a class with five parameters (collapse to a dataclass with named arguments).

Catalog the findings; estimate the savings (lines removed, files removed, indirection levels removed).

#### Step 2: collapse one at a time, with tests

Apply the test-then-refactor loop from Chapter 10. For each pattern-tax structure:

* Write characterization tests for the affected code paths.
* Refactor the structure to its simpler form.
* Verify tests pass.
* Commit per pattern-tax removal (atomic commits, Chapter 3).

A typical Singleton-to-DI conversion is 50-200 lines of changes touching 10-30 files. The IDE's rename and inline tools handle most of the mechanical work; the engineer's judgment is in choosing the dependency-injection structure.

#### Step 3: prevent recurrence

Update CLAUDE.md (§13.5) with the team's pattern preferences. Add code-review checklists (Chapter 15) that flag pattern-tax in new PRs. Train new engineers explicitly on what to avoid.

The discipline is to make pattern-tax a _team-recognized_ anti-pattern, not just a personal preference. If only one engineer pushes back, the next engineer reintroduces it; if the whole team agrees, the pattern-tax stays out.

### 13.7 A worked example: the Helix repository refactor

Aki at Helix inherited (in 2024) a `CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider` and its supporting hierarchy. The codebase had grown over two years; the pattern-tax had been introduced by the original engineer and replicated by an AI assistant that learned the codebase's style.

Inventory:

* `CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider` (one class, 30 lines)
* `SqlCustomerRepositoryFactory`, `InMemoryCustomerRepositoryFactory` (two classes, 20 lines each)
* `CustomerRepository` abstract base (one class, 50 lines of abstract methods)
* `SqlCustomerRepository`, `InMemoryCustomerRepository` (two classes, the real implementations)

Total pattern-tax: roughly 120 lines distributed across five classes that existed only to defer the decision of which Customer repository to construct.

The refactor:
    
    
    # Before: 5 classes, 120 lines of indirection
    class CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider: ...
    class SqlCustomerRepositoryFactory: ...
    class InMemoryCustomerRepositoryFactory: ...
    class CustomerRepository(ABC): ...  # abstract base
    class SqlCustomerRepository(CustomerRepository): ...
    class InMemoryCustomerRepository(CustomerRepository): ...
    
    # After: 1 function, 2 classes, no indirection
    class CustomerRepository(Protocol):
        def find(self, cid: str) -> Optional[Customer]: ...
        def save(self, c: Customer) -> None: ...
    
    class SqlCustomerRepository:
        def __init__(self, pool): self._pool = pool
        def find(self, cid): ...
        def save(self, c): ...
    
    class InMemoryCustomerRepository:
        def __init__(self): self._store = {}
        def find(self, cid): return self._store.get(cid)
        def save(self, c): self._store[c.id] = c
    
    def make_customer_repository(env='production', pool=None) -> CustomerRepository:
        if env == 'production':
            return SqlCustomerRepository(pool)
        return InMemoryCustomerRepository()
    

The result: 50 lines instead of 120. Two concrete classes plus a Protocol (which adds type-check value but is zero-cost at runtime). One factory function instead of a three-class hierarchy. The flexibility (swapping implementations) is fully preserved; the indirection is gone.

Aki's commit message captured the principle:
    
    
    refactor(customer-repo): collapse pattern-tax to direct construction
    
    The CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider hierarchy added three layers
    of indirection to support one runtime choice (production vs in-memory).
    Collapsed to a single factory function. Behavior unchanged; 70 lines
    removed; 4 files deleted.
    
    Removed:
    - CustomerRepositoryFactoryProvider
    - SqlCustomerRepositoryFactory
    - InMemoryCustomerRepositoryFactory
    - CustomerRepository abstract base (replaced by Protocol)
    
    The decision to defer construction to a factory is appropriate only
    when the construction itself is complex or when multiple consumers
    need the factory's flexibility. Neither applied here.
    

The PR was reviewed in twenty minutes (Chapter 15); the reduced indirection was visible to every reviewer. The team adopted the pattern of refusing new pattern-tax in subsequent reviews.

### 13.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Your codebase has a `Logger.get_instance()` Singleton used in 230 places. A new engineer asks why this is bad — after all, it works, the code compiles, the logs come out. Walk them through a _concrete_ failure scenario that would happen if you tried to write integration tests against the order-processing flow, and propose the migration to DI as a sequence of three PRs (not one giant rewrite). Why does the DI migration give back capabilities the Singleton was silently denying?

Hint

The Singleton's global state is shared _between tests_. Imagine two tests running in parallel.

**Answer:** The concrete failure: a test wants to inject a `SpyLogger` to assert that `place_order(order)` emits an audit-log entry with the order ID — but `place_order` internally calls `Logger.get_instance()`, which returns the _global_ Singleton regardless of what the test passes in. Workarounds (monkey-patching, optional-logger flags, scanning the global logger's state) all break test isolation across 230 call sites.

The DI migration in three PRs:

  1. **PR 1** : add an optional constructor parameter to one class (`OrderService.__init__(..., logger=None)`) defaulting to the Singleton. No call sites change yet. Add the first DI-based test for `OrderService`.
  2. **PR 2** : repeat the pattern across 10–15 high-value classes. Behavior unchanged; the codebase is now DI-capable.
  3. **PR 3** : construct one `Logger` at application startup, wire it through, remove `Logger.get_instance()` entirely. Tests now construct their own loggers; isolation is restored.

What DI gives back that the Singleton silently denied: test isolation (each test gets its own logger); dependencies as documentation (the constructor signature names what the class depends on); composability (a tenant-scoped logger can be wired per environment); lifecycle control (the logger gets explicit construction-time config, not lazy imports). The Singleton's "convenience" was paid for at every test, every parallel run, every environment-specific config — costs that compound across 230 call sites.

**Q2.** Two scenarios involve adding behavior to a class hierarchy. For each, pick: Visitor pattern, pattern matching over sum types, or a third option. Justify.

**Scenario A** : a Python 3.12 codebase has `Shape` with 4 concrete subclasses (`Circle`, `Square`, `Triangle`, `Pentagon`). The team wants to add `area()`, `perimeter()`, `to_svg()`, and `bounding_box()` operations. The shapes are stable (no new shapes anticipated); the operations may grow.

**Scenario B** : a Java 8 codebase (locked to Java 8 by deployment constraints) has an AST with `Expression`, `Statement`, `Declaration`, and ~30 concrete node types. The compiler-team plans to add three new analyses (`TypeCheck`, `ConstantFold`, `DeadCodeElimination`) but does not plan to add new node types.

Hint

One of these scenarios has language support for sum types / pattern matching; the other does not.

**Answer:**

**Scenario A** : use **pattern matching over a sum type**. Python 3.10+ has `match` statements; define `Shape = Union[Circle, Square, Triangle, Pentagon]`; each operation is a function with a `match shape:` body. No Visitor classes. No `accept` methods. Adding the next operation is a new function; the type checker flags missing cases at static-analysis time (with `mypy` \+ `--strict` or runtime via `case _:` fallthrough). Visitor here would be 1995 Java code translated literally into Python — boilerplate for no benefit.

**Scenario B** : use **the Visitor pattern**. Java 8 has no pattern matching for sealed hierarchies; the 30 node types make a long `if (node instanceof X)` chain unreadable. Visitor's double-dispatch is the language-appropriate idiom: define a `Visitor` interface with one method per node type; each analysis is a `Visitor` implementation. Adding an analysis is adding a new class; node types do not change. The Visitor pattern earns its keep here for the reason it earned its keep in 1995: the language does not have a better alternative.

**The general rule** : Visitor's status depends on the language. In languages with pattern matching over sum types (Python 3.10+, TypeScript with discriminated unions, Rust enums, Scala/Kotlin sealed classes, modern C# `switch` expressions), it is essentially obsolete. In languages without (Java pre-21, older C++, plain JavaScript), it remains the idiomatic choice. The pattern itself is sound; what changed is the toolchain around it. Reach for the language's modern feature first; fall back to Visitor when the language's modern feature does not exist.

**Q3.** Describe the pattern-tax anti-pattern. Why does it persist in legacy codebases?

Hint

The persistence has both technical and social causes.

**Answer:** Pattern-tax is the overhead of patterns applied speculatively for hypothetical flexibility that never materializes. Common forms: Abstract Factory Factory hierarchies, abstract base classes with one concrete subclass, interfaces with one implementation, generic-named classes (`Handler`, `Manager`, `Processor`).

Persistence causes:

* **Technical** : each pattern-tax structure is real code with real callers. Removing it requires understanding all callers and refactoring them. The mechanical cost is non-trivial.
* **Social** : the original author may still be on the team; refactoring their work can feel like criticism. The diffuse cost of pattern-tax (paid by every reader for years) does not get attributed to the original decision.
* **Recurrence** : AI agents trained on legacy patterns may re-introduce them; engineers who learned patterns from older textbooks may not see them as anti-patterns.

The fix is team-wide consensus (CLAUDE.md, code review discipline) combined with patient incremental refactoring (one pattern-tax structure at a time, under tests).

**Q4.** Why does the chapter advocate "applying patterns retrospectively, not prospectively"?

Hint

What is the failure rate of predicted flexibility?

**Answer:** Prospective pattern application predicts future flexibility; the prediction is often wrong. Many anticipated extensions never arrive; the pattern overhead is paid for no benefit. Retrospective pattern application waits for the second concrete case to demonstrate that the flexibility is real; the pattern is introduced when justified, with a real second case as evidence.

The discipline produces simpler code in the present (no pattern-tax) and appropriately patterned code in the future (when the pattern's value is proven). The alternative (apply patterns now in case we need them) accumulates pattern-tax that future engineers must navigate.

The deeper lesson: code is read more than written; the cost of pattern-tax is paid by readers; the benefit of anticipated flexibility is hypothetical. Optimize for readers; let flexibility emerge from concrete demand.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Identify a Singleton in your codebase (if any). Refactor it to dependency injection. Compare the test isolation before and after.

Hint

The refactor usually touches every call site; the IDE can help.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common pattern: a `Logger.getInstance()` or `Database.getInstance()` Singleton. Refactor by:

  1. Add a constructor parameter for the dependency to each class that uses the Singleton.
  2. At application startup, construct one instance of the dependency and inject it everywhere.
  3. Remove the Singleton's static `getInstance()` method.

Test isolation: before the refactor, tests of two classes that share the Singleton interfere (the Singleton's state persists across tests). After the refactor, each test constructs its own dependency or shares only what it explicitly wants to share; isolation is improved.

The refactor is one of the highest-leverage DI conversions; the test improvement compounds across the suite.

**Q6.** Find a Visitor in your codebase (or in a legacy codebase you have access to). Refactor it to pattern matching (in a language that supports it) or to a simple `if/elif` chain (if not). Report the line count change.

Hint

The line count usually drops by 30-60%.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A typical Visitor with 5 shapes and 3 operations: 5 accept methods + 3 visitor classes × 5 visit methods = 20 method bodies. The pattern matching alternative: 3 functions × 5 cases = 15 cases. The line count drop varies but is usually 30-50%.

The deeper savings are in _cognitive load_ : the pattern matching version reads as "for each shape, what is the area?" The Visitor version reads as "the shape accepts a visitor that visits the shape," which requires understanding the double-dispatch indirection. The reader of pattern matching learns the shapes; the reader of Visitor learns the protocol.

**Q7.** Audit one of your team's AI-generated PRs for pattern-tax. Identify any factories, abstract bases, or interfaces that exist without justification. Propose simplifications.

Hint

Look for classes with `Factory`, `Provider`, `Manager`, `Handler` in the name.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common finding: the AI generated a `XxxFactory` for a class that has one consumer and no flexibility need. Simplification: collapse the factory into a direct constructor call. Another common finding: an interface with one implementation. Simplification: remove the interface; use the concrete type directly.

For each simplification, the engineer adds a comment to the PR explaining the change. Over time, the team's CLAUDE.md absorbs the pattern preferences; subsequent AI PRs reflect them.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "AI agents make pattern-tax cheaper to identify and remove; we should be more aggressive about refactoring it out." Counter-argue: "AI agents make pattern-tax easier to introduce; the net effect on codebases is worse, not better."

**Discussion:** Pro: AI can grep for the anti-pattern signals (factories with one consumer, interfaces with one implementation) faster than humans; AI can perform the mechanical refactor (collapse the layer, update callers) under test; the human's role is decision and review, both faster than mechanical work.

Pro the counter: AI also _generates_ pattern-tax from training data, especially when prompted with generic requests like "build a flexible architecture." The AI's defaults reflect training data, which is dominated by tutorials demonstrating patterns at full ceremony. Without explicit CLAUDE.md guidance, AI-augmented codebases accumulate pattern-tax faster than they shed it.

The synthesis: AI is a multiplier in both directions. With CLAUDE.md discipline and review discipline, the multiplier helps clean up codebases. Without those disciplines, the multiplier accelerates pattern-tax accumulation. The net effect depends on team practice, not on AI alone.

**Q9.** What patterns or anti-patterns from this chapter might _not_ be anti-patterns in five years? What conditions would have to change?

**Discussion:** Singleton's status might shift if a new use case emerges where DI is impractical (a yet-to-be-invented runtime environment where injection cannot work). This is speculative; for now, DI is dominant.

Visitor's status might shift if the language ecosystem retreats from pattern matching (unlikely) or if a new dispatch problem emerges that Visitor solves better than pattern matching (also unlikely).

Pattern-tax (the broader anti-pattern) might shift if codebases become so disposable that "code we will throw away in three months" becomes the norm; in that case, the cost of pattern-tax is bounded (it dies with the codebase) and the tax may be acceptable. This is the gist of the "throw-away code" argument; it has merit for prototypes, exploratory work, and AI-generated code that will be replaced before it ages. For production code expected to live more than a year, pattern-tax remains an anti-pattern.

The general pattern: anti-patterns are _context-dependent_. The chapter's status reports are 2026-current; they should be reassessed periodically. The skill of the working engineer is to know the patterns deeply enough to recognize when their context shifts.

### Chapter Summary

Singleton (§13.1) was replaced by dependency injection because DI exposes dependencies explicitly, enables test isolation, and composes better. The replacement is now standard in most frameworks; Singleton in new code is usually a sign of training-data influence.

Visitor (§13.2) was replaced by pattern matching over sum types because pattern matching is shorter, more readable, and type-checked. The replacement requires language support, which now exists in Python, TypeScript, Rust, Scala, Kotlin, and others.

Pattern-tax (§13.3) is the broader anti-pattern of layered abstractions added speculatively. The discipline is to apply patterns retrospectively when a second case demonstrates the value, not prospectively in anticipation. The fix for inherited pattern-tax is incremental: identify, characterize, collapse, prevent recurrence.

Other dated patterns (§13.4): Template Method (replaced by composition), Memento (replaced by serialization or event sourcing), Chain of Responsibility (replaced by middleware), Builder (replaced by named arguments and dataclasses). Each appears in legacy code; each is rarely the right choice in new code.

The AI-Layer (§13.5) is that LLMs propose patterns from training data including the over-applied ones. The discipline is to recognize pattern-tax signals (multi-layer factories, single-impl interfaces, generic names) and to decline them via CLAUDE.md guidance and PR review. This is the concrete operationalization of Thread C lie #2 (over-abstraction).

Chapter 14 closes Part V with Hexagonal Architecture and Functional Core / Imperative Shell: design principles that organize the codebase to maximize testability and minimize the entanglement of business logic with I/O. The patterns from Chapter 12 are the moves; the patterns from this chapter are what to avoid; Chapter 14 is the architectural frame that keeps the moves coherent at scale.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Run a one-day pattern-tax audit on a legacy subsystem.** Grep for `Factory`, `Provider`, `Manager`, `Handler` in class names; for abstract base classes with one concrete subclass; for interfaces with one implementation. Each is a candidate for collapse.
  2. **Migrate one Singleton to DI in your codebase this quarter.** Pick the most-accessed one. Constructor injection at the boundary; remove `getInstance`. Test isolation improvements arrive immediately.
  3. **Add a "Forbidden patterns" section to your CLAUDE.md.** List Singleton, Visitor, Factory Factory, Builder (where the language has named arguments). The agent will stop generating them after the file lands.

---

## Chapter 14: Hexagonal Architecture and Functional Core / Imperative Shell

A Helix subsystem in early 2024 had a `PricingService` that read customers from Postgres, evaluated rules from a Yaml config file, made HTTP calls to a tax-rate provider, charged via Stripe, and wrote results to a logging service, all inside a single 300-line method. Tests for the method required: a real Postgres test database, a real test S3 bucket for the Yaml config, a real test tax provider mock at a specific URL, a real test Stripe account, and the test logger. Each test took several seconds to set up and a few seconds to run; the suite was unbearably slow. Bugs that should have been unit-test-detectable instead required full integration tests; the team often skipped writing tests at all.

Aki refactored the subsystem in late 2024 to apply two related design principles: Hexagonal Architecture (Ports and Adapters) and Functional Core / Imperative Shell. The same business logic was extracted into pure functions; the I/O was pushed to the edges; the tests of the business logic became fast, deterministic, and numerous. The integration tests remained, but only for the I/O edges; the bulk of the test suite was now milliseconds per test.

This chapter is about the two related principles that organize a codebase to make this possible: Hexagonal Architecture as the high-level frame, and Functional Core / Imperative Shell as the testable-design pattern. Both are 2026-current; both compound with the test, refactoring, and pattern disciplines of the previous chapters.

### 14.1 Hexagonal Architecture

Hexagonal Architecture, also called Ports and Adapters, was named by Alistair Cockburn in 2005. The shape: the application's core business logic sits at the center; external systems (databases, message queues, HTTP APIs, user interfaces, file systems) are at the perimeter; the connections between core and perimeter go through _ports_ (abstract interfaces defined by the core) and _adapters_ (concrete implementations that bridge core and external system).

#### Definition: Hexagonal Architecture

Hexagonal Architecture places the application's business logic at the core, isolated from external systems. The core defines _ports_ (abstract interfaces) for the operations it needs to perform on external systems; _adapters_ implement the ports against specific external systems. The core depends only on its own ports; the adapters depend on both the ports and the external systems.

In plain language: write your business logic so it does not know how data gets in or out. Plug in the database, the HTTP transport, the queue, the UI as separate adapters. Swap any adapter without changing the core.

#### A worked example

The Helix pricing subsystem after refactoring:
    
    
    # Ports (defined by the core)
    class CustomerPort(Protocol):
        def find(self, cid: str) -> Optional[Customer]: ...
    
    class RuleConfigPort(Protocol):
