        def load_rules(self) -> list[Rule]: ...
    
    class TaxRatePort(Protocol):
        def rate_for(self, region: str) -> Decimal: ...
    
    class ChargePort(Protocol):
        def charge(self, customer: Customer, amount: Decimal, currency: str) -> ChargeResult: ...
    
    class AuditLogPort(Protocol):
        def log(self, event: str, data: dict) -> None: ...
    
    # Core (depends only on ports and on domain types)
    class PricingService:
        def __init__(self,
                     customers: CustomerPort,
                     rules: RuleConfigPort,
                     tax: TaxRatePort,
                     charges: ChargePort,
                     audit: AuditLogPort):
            self._customers = customers
            self._rules = rules
            self._tax = tax
            self._charges = charges
            self._audit = audit
    
        def price_and_charge(self, customer_id: str, product_id: str) -> ChargeResult:
            customer = self._customers.find(customer_id)
            rules = self._rules.load_rules()
            base_price = compute_price(customer, product_id, rules)  # pure function
            tax_rate = self._tax.rate_for(customer.region)
            total = base_price * (1 + tax_rate)
            result = self._charges.charge(customer, total, customer.currency)
            self._audit.log('charge_completed', {'customer_id': customer_id, 'amount': str(total)})
            return result
    
    # Adapters (one per external system)
    class SqlCustomerAdapter:
        def __init__(self, pool): self._pool = pool
        def find(self, cid): ...  # actual SQL
    
    class S3RuleConfigAdapter:
        def __init__(self, s3_client, bucket, key): ...
        def load_rules(self): ...  # actual S3 read and Yaml parse
    
    class HttpTaxRateAdapter:
        def __init__(self, http_client, base_url): ...
        def rate_for(self, region): ...  # actual HTTP call
    
    class StripeChargeAdapter:
        def __init__(self, stripe_key): ...
        def charge(self, customer, amount, currency): ...  # actual Stripe call
    
    class CloudWatchAuditLogAdapter:
        def log(self, event, data): ...  # actual CloudWatch call
    

The benefit: the core `PricingService` depends on five abstract ports. Tests pass in-memory fakes for each port. The bulk of the testing is on `PricingService`'s logic, fast and deterministic. The adapters are tested separately against the real external systems, slowly and integration-style.

#### The pure-function inset

Notice the `compute_price` function inside `price_and_charge`. It is a pure function: customer, product_id, rules in; price out. It can be tested without any ports at all. This is the _functional core_ idea (§14.3) at work inside the hexagonal frame.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "hexagonal architecture is enterprise overkill." The mistake is conflating the _frame_ with the ceremony. The frame is "define your ports, write your core against them, implement adapters separately." For a small service, this can mean five ports and five adapters; for a large service, fifty of each. The frame scales with the codebase; the ceremony only appears when it earns its keep.

A specific test: does the codebase have business logic that is tangled with I/O? If yes, hexagonal is justified. If no (a tiny script, a one-off tool), it is not. The Helix subsystem in 2024 had massive tangling; the refactor paid back within months.

### 14.2 Ports and adapters in detail

The vocabulary:

* **Port** : an abstract interface defined by the core. Named after the _capability_ the core needs (`CustomerPort`, `ChargePort`), not after the external system.
* **Adapter** : a concrete implementation of a port. Named after the _external system_ (`SqlCustomerAdapter`, `StripeChargeAdapter`).
* **Driver / Driven** : ports come in two flavors. _Driving ports_ are how the outside world enters the core (HTTP routes, CLI commands, event handlers triggering core actions). _Driven ports_ are how the core acts on the outside world (database access, vendor SDKs, message publishing). The terminology is from Cockburn's original paper.

#### Driver ports

Driver ports are the entry points to the core. A web service has driver ports for each HTTP route:
    
    
    class PricingDriverPort(Protocol):
        def price_and_charge(self, customer_id: str, product_id: str) -> ChargeResult: ...
    
    # Adapter: HTTP routing
    @app.post('/api/charge')
    async def http_charge(request: ChargeRequest, pricing: PricingDriverPort = Depends(get_pricing)):
        result = pricing.price_and_charge(request.customer_id, request.product_id)
        return {'status': result.status, 'amount': str(result.amount)}
    

The HTTP adapter knows about request/response shapes, status codes, JSON serialization. The driver port (which is satisfied by `PricingService`) knows only the domain operation.

#### Driven ports

Driven ports are how the core acts on the outside. The §14.1 examples were all driven ports.

The split matters: tests of the core run with fake driving inputs (call the methods directly, no HTTP) and fake driven outputs (in-memory adapters). The HTTP adapter is tested separately with an HTTP client; the database adapter is tested separately with a real database.

#### The dependency direction

The core depends on its own ports (abstract types it defined). The ports do not depend on the core's concrete classes. The adapters depend on both the ports (to implement them) and the external systems (to do the work). The external systems depend on neither.

Direction: external → adapter → port → core → port → adapter → external. The core sits in the middle; ports are abstractions on both sides.

This direction is what allows the core to be tested without external systems: substitute fake adapters; the core is unchanged.

### 14.3 Functional Core / Imperative Shell

Gary Bernhardt named _Functional Core / Imperative Shell_ (FC/IS) in a 2012 talk. The shape is a stricter version of Hexagonal: the core is not just isolated from I/O, it is _pure_. All side effects, all I/O, all mutation lives in a thin imperative shell at the edge.

#### Definition: Functional Core / Imperative Shell

In FC/IS, the application's core is a set of pure functions: they take inputs and return outputs with no side effects. The imperative shell handles I/O, side effects, and orchestration; it calls into the pure core with inputs and uses the outputs to drive side effects.

In plain language: do the thinking in pure functions; do the doing in a thin orchestration layer. The thinking is testable trivially; the doing is small enough to test directly.

#### A worked example

The pricing logic refactored further into FC/IS form:
    
    
    # Functional core (pure)
    def compute_price_decision(customer: Customer,
                               product_id: str,
                               rules: list[Rule],
                               tax_rate: Decimal) -> PriceDecision:
        """Pure: given inputs, return what should happen."""
        matching_rule = next((r for r in rules if r.matches(customer, product_id)), None)
        base_price = matching_rule.price if matching_rule else default_price(product_id)
        total = base_price * (1 + tax_rate)
        return PriceDecision(
            base_price=base_price,
            tax_amount=base_price * tax_rate,
            total=total,
            rule_id=matching_rule.id if matching_rule else None,
        )
    
    # Imperative shell
    class PricingOrchestrator:
        def __init__(self, customers, rules, tax, charges, audit):
            self._customers = customers
            self._rules = rules
            self._tax = tax
            self._charges = charges
            self._audit = audit
    
        def price_and_charge(self, customer_id: str, product_id: str) -> ChargeResult:
            # Gather inputs (side-effectful reads)
            customer = self._customers.find(customer_id)
            rules = self._rules.load_rules()
            tax_rate = self._tax.rate_for(customer.region)
    
            # Decide (pure)
            decision = compute_price_decision(customer, product_id, rules, tax_rate)
    
            # Act (side-effectful writes)
            result = self._charges.charge(customer, decision.total, customer.currency)
            self._audit.log('charge_completed', {
                'customer_id': customer_id,
                'amount': str(decision.total),
                'rule_id': decision.rule_id,
            })
            return result
    

The pure function `compute_price_decision` is the _decision logic_. It is testable with thousands of property-based test cases (Chapter 6) at microsecond speed. The orchestrator is a _coordinator_ : it does the reads, calls the pure function, does the writes. The coordinator is small (a few dozen lines), so it can be tested directly with fakes.

#### Why FC/IS is stricter than Hexagonal

Hexagonal allows the core to have any structure; the requirement is only that I/O is mediated by ports. FC/IS adds the constraint that the core's logic is _pure_ : no mutation, no I/O, no nondeterminism.

The benefit of the strictness: pure functions are the most testable code in any codebase. Property-based testing applies directly. Refactoring is safe by construction. Reasoning is easy because there is no hidden state.

The cost: not all logic fits naturally into pure functions. Some decisions are inherently sequential and stateful (a state machine with many transitions, a long-running computation that accumulates results). For these, the imperative shell can be larger, or the pure core can model the state explicitly (state-machine transitions as pure functions returning new states).

#### The split-the-decision pattern

A common FC/IS shape, used in the example above:

  1. **Gather** : read everything you need from external systems.
  2. **Decide** : call a pure function that returns a decision object.
  3. **Act** : write the consequences to external systems.

This three-phase structure makes the orchestration explicit. The pure decision can be tested exhaustively; the gather and act phases are thin enough to test with fakes; the whole orchestrator is easy to reason about.

### 14.4 Testing under hexagonal and FC/IS

The test pyramid (Chapter 5) takes on a clearer shape under these architectures:

* **Unit tests for the functional core** : thousands of fast tests, often property-based. The core is the bulk of the test count.
* **Component tests for the orchestrators** : dozens of tests with in-memory adapters. Verify orchestration logic without external systems.
* **Adapter tests** : a handful of tests per adapter, often integration-style with real or near-real external systems (Postgres in Docker, sqlite in memory, recorded Stripe responses).
* **End-to-end tests** : very few, only for the most critical user-visible flows.

The Helix subsystem after Aki's refactor had this distribution: 380 unit tests on the functional core, 25 component tests on the orchestrators, 12 adapter tests, 3 end-to-end tests. Total suite runtime came down by roughly an order of magnitude during the refactor, which is the typical shape when an I/O-tangled subsystem is refactored into FC/IS. The qualitative outcome is the reliable part: the fast suite ran on every commit; engineers used it freely; bugs surfaced at the unit-test level rather than in production.

#### Mutation testing in the core

The functional core's purity makes mutation testing (Chapter 6) particularly effective. Pure functions have no hidden state to confuse the mutation tool; every survival is a real gap in the test suite. Aki's pricing core had a mutation score of 89% after the refactor and the addition of property-based tests; the gap from 100% was mostly equivalent mutants.

### 14.5 The Repository pattern as FC/IS in practice

Chapter 7's Repository pattern is exactly the driven-port abstraction in hexagonal terms. Application code (the core) depends on the `CustomerRepository` interface; the SQL implementation is the adapter; tests use the in-memory implementation.

The chain of compounding disciplines:

* Test doubles (Chapter 7) → Repository pattern → driven ports → hexagonal architecture → functional core + imperative shell.

Each level builds on the previous. An engineer who has internalized the lower levels recognizes the higher levels as the natural conclusion. An engineer who skips levels finds the higher levels arbitrary or over-engineered.

This is why the chapter sequence matters: testing first (Chapters 5-8), then refactoring (9-11), then patterns sensibly applied (12-13), then architectural frame (this chapter). The order is the dependency order; each chapter builds on its predecessors.

### 14.6 The AI-Layer: agents and architectural shape

LLMs in 2026 know hexagonal architecture and FC/IS from training data. They can generate code that follows the shapes when asked. They are unreliable at:

* **Recognizing when the shape is appropriate.** The agent applies hexagonal to small scripts where it is overkill, or it generates flat code with tangled I/O for systems where hexagonal would help. The judgment of whether the codebase justifies the shape is human work.
* **Maintaining the shape over time.** The agent in agent-PR mode might add code that violates the shape (a driven port called from a pure function, side effects inside the functional core). The PR review (Chapter 15) is where this is caught.
* **Naming ports and adapters consistently.** The agent may generate port names like `IDatabase` or `DataAccessLayer` that do not follow the team's naming convention; the CLAUDE.md should specify the convention.

#### The CLAUDE.md for architectural shape

A CLAUDE.md entry that codifies the team's architectural preferences:
    
    
    ## Architectural shape
    
    We use Hexagonal Architecture for our backend services:
    - Business logic lives in `src/core/`.
    - Ports (Protocols) are defined in `src/core/ports/`.
    - Adapters live in `src/adapters/<system>/`.
    - Tests of the core use in-memory adapters from `tests/fakes/`.
    
    Within the core:
    - Decision logic should be pure functions in `src/core/decisions/`.
    - Orchestrators are in `src/core/orchestrators/` and use the gather/decide/act pattern.
    
    When adding a new external system, add a port first (in `src/core/ports/`),
    then an in-memory fake (in `tests/fakes/`), then the production adapter.
    

The instruction guides agent-generated code toward the shape. Agents follow the convention when it is specified; they generate inconsistent code when it is not.

#### Refactoring legacy code into the shape

The opening vignette (Helix's 300-line method) is a common starting point. The refactoring sequence:

  1. **Identify the I/O at the edges.** Find every database call, HTTP call, file read, queue operation. Each is a candidate for an adapter.
  2. **Define ports** for each I/O capability the core needs. Name them after the capability, not the system.
  3. **Inject the adapters** into the core class via the constructor.
  4. **Replace direct I/O calls with port calls.** Mechanical refactor; tests pass throughout if characterization tests (Chapter 10) are in place.
  5. **Find the decision logic and extract it as pure functions.** This is the FC/IS step, usually a separate PR.
  6. **Move tests** from integration-heavy to core-unit-heavy as the shape emerges.

Aki's refactor of the pricing subsystem took approximately three weeks distributed across two months. The agent did much of the mechanical work in step 4 (port introduction) and step 5 (pure function extraction); Aki directed and reviewed. The test suite came down by roughly an order of magnitude in runtime during the refactor (the §14.4 illustrative example puts numbers on it); the bug rate dropped in the following quarter. Before opening the agent for any of this work, Aki did what she always did: she wrote a small failing test for the next step's intended outcome and ran it first, so the agent had a target rather than a vague direction.

### 14.7 Working example: an order processing module under hexagonal + FC/IS

A complete real-shape example, sketched:
    
    
    # src/core/ports/customer_port.py
    class CustomerPort(Protocol):
        def find(self, cid: str) -> Optional[Customer]: ...
    
    # src/core/ports/payment_port.py
    class PaymentPort(Protocol):
        def charge(self, c: Customer, amount: Decimal) -> ChargeResult: ...
    
    # src/core/ports/inventory_port.py
    class InventoryPort(Protocol):
        def reserve(self, sku: str, qty: int) -> bool: ...
        def release(self, sku: str, qty: int) -> None: ...
    
    # src/core/ports/audit_port.py
    class AuditPort(Protocol):
        def log(self, event: str, data: dict) -> None: ...
    
    # src/core/decisions/order_decision.py (pure function)
    def decide_order(customer: Customer,
                     items: list[OrderItem],
                     current_date: date) -> OrderDecision:
        """Pure decision: given the customer, items, and date, what should happen?"""
        if customer.is_blocked:
            return OrderDecision(kind='reject', reason='customer_blocked')
        if not items:
            return OrderDecision(kind='reject', reason='empty_order')
        total = sum(item.price * item.quantity for item in items)
        discount = compute_discount(customer, total)
        final = total * (1 - discount)
        return OrderDecision(
            kind='accept',
            total=total,
            discount=discount,
            final_amount=final,
            items=items,
        )
    
    # src/core/orchestrators/order_orchestrator.py (imperative shell)
    class OrderOrchestrator:
        def __init__(self, customers: CustomerPort, payment: PaymentPort,
                     inventory: InventoryPort, audit: AuditPort):
            self._customers = customers
            self._payment = payment
            self._inventory = inventory
            self._audit = audit
    
        def place_order(self, customer_id: str, items: list[OrderItem]) -> OrderResult:
            # Gather
            customer = self._customers.find(customer_id)
            if not customer:
                return OrderResult(status='error', error='customer_not_found')
    
            # Decide (pure)
            decision = decide_order(customer, items, current_date=date.today())
    
            # Act
            if decision.kind == 'reject':
                self._audit.log('order_rejected', {'customer_id': customer_id, 'reason': decision.reason})
                return OrderResult(status='rejected', reason=decision.reason)
    
            for item in decision.items:
                if not self._inventory.reserve(item.sku, item.quantity):
                    # rollback and reject
                    for prev_item in decision.items[:decision.items.index(item)]:
                        self._inventory.release(prev_item.sku, prev_item.quantity)
                    return OrderResult(status='rejected', reason='inventory_unavailable')
    
            charge_result = self._payment.charge(customer, decision.final_amount)
            if charge_result.status != 'success':
                for item in decision.items:
                    self._inventory.release(item.sku, item.quantity)
                return OrderResult(status='rejected', reason='payment_failed')
    
            self._audit.log('order_placed', {
                'customer_id': customer_id,
                'amount': str(decision.final_amount),
                'items': len(decision.items),
            })
            return OrderResult(status='success', total=decision.final_amount)
    

The pure `decide_order` function is testable with hundreds of property-based test cases. The `OrderOrchestrator` is testable with in-memory adapters at component level. The HTTP route that invokes `place_order` is testable at the route level. Each layer has its own tests; the layers compose; the whole system is well-tested without an integration test for every behavior.

### 14.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A team applying hexagonal architecture for the first time draws their pricing service's port boundaries as follows. For each item, decide: is it a driver port, a driven port, or neither?

(a) `POST /api/charge` HTTP route handler. (b) `CustomerRepository.find(customer_id)`; the SQL-backed implementation. (c) `StripeChargeAdapter.charge(amount, currency, token)`. (d) A nightly scheduled job that runs `recompute_pricing_table()` on all customers. (e) An event-bus consumer that reacts to `customer.updated` events and refreshes a cache. (f) A pure function `compute_discount(customer, order, rules) -> Decimal`.

Hint

Driver ports = how the outside _enters_ the core. Driven ports = how the core _acts on_ the outside.

**Answer:**

(a) **Driver port** ; HTTP routes are how external callers enter the core. The handler translates the HTTP request into a call to a core method; the adapter is the routing/serialization layer.

(b) **Driven port** ; the core asks the Repository for data; the SQL adapter implements the request. The core does not know SQL; the Repository abstracts persistence.

(c) **Driven port** ; the core asks for a charge; the Stripe adapter translates the request into the vendor's API. The core does not know Stripe; the adapter abstracts the vendor.

(d) **Driver port** ; scheduled jobs are how time (an "external system") drives the core. The cron runner is the adapter; it knows about schedules and time, the core does not.

(e) **Driver port** ; event consumers are how external events drive the core. The bus consumer is the adapter that translates `customer.updated` events into core method calls.

(f) **Neither** ; a pure function inside the core is just the core. Ports are _boundaries_ between core and outside. A pure helper function used by the core is not at a boundary; it has no adapter; it is just code in the functional core (§14.3).

The general rule: ports are at boundaries. Driver ports answer "how does the outside get in?" Driven ports answer "how does the core reach out?" Pure code in the middle is not a port; it is the core. The distinction matters because tests of the core wire fake driver inputs (call the methods directly) and fake driven outputs (in-memory adapters); the core's internal pure functions need no test fakery at all.

**Q2.** A teammate has refactored a critical pricing function to take all its inputs as parameters (good!) but the function still calls `datetime.now()` once internally to check whether a promotional rule is currently active. They say: "It does not do I/O. The Hexagonal frame is satisfied; there are no port calls. Why are you asking me to inject the clock too?" Construct your argument for why injecting the clock (making the function fully pure) is worth the small extra parameter. Tie your argument to a _specific_ property-based test that you can write only after the change.

Hint

Property-based testing requires deterministic functions. `datetime.now()` makes the function nondeterministic.

**Answer:** The teammate is half right: no I/O happens. But "no I/O" is not the same as "pure." A function that reads from `datetime.now()` is _nondeterministic_ : given the same `customer` and `rules` arguments, it produces different results depending on when it is called. The result on Tuesday differs from the result on Wednesday; the result during a promotion differs from the result after.

This breaks the FC/IS contract in a specific way: **the function cannot be exhaustively tested with property-based testing because property tests cannot reproduce the failing time.** If Hypothesis finds a case where the function misbehaves at 23:59:59 UTC on the last day of a promotion, you cannot reliably re-run that test case; the next run is at a different time and the bug does not reproduce. The framework shrinks the input, but it cannot shrink "the time when the test ran." Your debugging is reduced to "this used to fail, can you check?"

Injecting the clock fixes this: `compute_price(customer, rules, current_time: datetime)`. Now:
    
    
    @given(customer_strategy, rules_strategy, st.datetimes(min_value=date(2020, 1, 1), max_value=date(2030, 12, 31)))
    def test_pricing_decision_is_deterministic_for_fixed_inputs(customer, rules, t):
        assert compute_price(customer, rules, t) == compute_price(customer, rules, t)
    

That property test runs thousands of generated `(customer, rules, time)` triples and catches the boundary case at 23:59:59 on the last promotion day. The framework records the failing triple in its example database; the next run replays the same triple; you can debug deterministically.

The deeper point: I/O isolation is a _step toward_ purity, but the FC/IS discipline is the full step. Nondeterministic dependencies (time, randomness, hidden caches, mutable globals) all break the same property; given the same inputs, the function must always produce the same output. The cost of the extra parameter is one line in the signature; the benefit is the entire surface of property-based testing being applicable. For pricing logic where edge-case bugs lose customer money, this is rarely a bad trade.

**Q3.** Defend or refute: "Hexagonal architecture is over-engineering for small services."

Hint

The frame versus the ceremony.

**Answer:** The frame ("define ports, write core against them, implement adapters separately") is appropriate for any service with business logic and external systems. The _ceremony_ (many layers of abstraction, separate packages, elaborate naming conventions) is the over-engineering.

For a small service with five operations and three external systems, hexagonal can mean five ports (one per external system or per capability), three adapters in production, three adapters in tests. The total code increase is modest; the testing improvement is significant. For a 100-line script with one I/O call, hexagonal is overkill; the script does not have business logic distinct from I/O.

The test: does the codebase have business logic tangled with I/O that you would like to test independently? If yes, the frame helps. If no, it does not. The size of the service is a poor proxy for the answer.

**Q4.** What is the "gather, decide, act" pattern, and why is it useful?

Hint

§14.3's three-phase structure.

**Answer:** Gather, decide, act splits an orchestration into three phases:

* **Gather** : read all needed inputs from external systems (side-effectful reads).
* **Decide** : call a pure function with the gathered inputs to produce a decision object.
* **Act** : write the consequences of the decision to external systems (side-effectful writes).

The pattern is useful because it isolates the decision (the interesting logic) from the I/O (the mechanical work). The decision is a pure function that can be tested exhaustively; the gather and act phases are thin orchestration that can be tested with fakes.

It also makes the orchestrator's structure explicit and predictable. Engineers reading the orchestrator know to look for the three phases; new orchestrators are easy to write because the shape is familiar.

The pattern fails when the orchestration is genuinely interleaved (decide-and-act repeatedly in a loop, with each decision depending on the previous action's result). For such cases, the orchestrator is larger and more imperative; the FC/IS discipline accommodates this with state-machine-style pure functions returning next states, but the shape is less clean.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Take a module in your codebase that has business logic tangled with I/O. Refactor it to hexagonal architecture: define ports, extract the core, implement adapters separately. Report the test-suite speed before and after.

Hint

The test-speed improvement is often dramatic (10× or more) for I/O-heavy modules.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common outcomes:

* The refactor takes 1-3 weeks for a substantive module.
* The test suite for the module shrinks by 5-20× in runtime (integration tests replaced by unit tests of pure functions).
* The test count _increases_ because the pure functions are easy to test in many cases.
* The bug rate in the next quarter drops because the unit tests catch issues earlier.

The compounding effect: refactoring the second module is faster than the first because the team has internalized the pattern and the supporting infrastructure (fake adapters, port conventions) is in place.

**Q6.** Identify the pure decision in an orchestration in your codebase. Extract it as a pure function. Test it with at least one property-based test (Chapter 6).

Hint

The decision is often a multi-line conditional in the middle of an orchestrator.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A typical finding: an orchestrator computes a value through several conditionals and arithmetic operations; the conditionals depend only on the inputs and a few constants. Extract the computation as a pure function `compute_<thing>(inputs) -> Decision`; the orchestrator calls it; the orchestrator is shorter and clearer.

A property-based test on the extracted function: typically a structural invariant ("the decision's `total` equals the sum of the items' prices times their quantities") or a domain rule ("a decision with a discount rule applied has `discount_amount > 0`"). Property tests on the pure function execute in microseconds and exercise hundreds of input combinations.

The lesson: pure functions are the most testable code in any codebase. Extracting them from orchestrators is high-leverage refactoring.

**Q7.** Use an AI agent to refactor a tangled module into hexagonal + FC/IS form. Specify the target shape in the prompt; review the agent's output. Did it apply the shape correctly? Did it leak I/O into the core?

Hint

The CLAUDE.md from §14.6 specifies the conventions; with it, the agent's output is more consistent.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* With a clear CLAUDE.md, the agent produces a passable hexagonal refactor with appropriate port and adapter names.
* Without it, the agent's ports and adapters have inconsistent names (`IDatabase` vs `DatabasePort` vs `CustomerRepository`) and the core may have small I/O leaks.
* The pure-function extraction is often incomplete; the agent extracts the obvious decision but leaves I/O-adjacent logic in the orchestrator. The human's review catches the remaining extractions.

The lesson: AI is a multiplier on architectural work, but it requires explicit guidance about the conventions. The CLAUDE.md is the lever; without it, the agent's defaults drift.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "Functional Core / Imperative Shell is the architectural shape best suited to AI-augmented engineering in 2026." Counter-argue: "FC/IS is too constraining for many real-world systems; hexagonal architecture without the purity constraint is the practical choice."

**Discussion:** Pro FC/IS: pure functions are AI-augmented engineering's sweet spot. AI generates and tests pure functions easily; mutation testing works best on them; property-based testing applies directly; refactoring is safe. As AI's role grows, the codebase that maximizes pure functions maximizes AI's productive surface. The codebase that is mostly imperative shell is mostly hard-to-test legacy.

Counter: FC/IS imposes a strict purity constraint that not all logic fits naturally into. Stateful workflows, distributed transactions, long-running computations with side effects: these are genuinely hard to express as pure functions. Hexagonal architecture (without the purity constraint) gives the I/O-isolation benefit while accommodating impure cores.

The synthesis: FC/IS where it fits; hexagonal everywhere else. The discipline is to _prefer_ purity in the core and to fall back to impurity when the domain genuinely requires it. The codebase ends up with a mix: a large pure core for decision logic, a smaller stateful core for workflows, a thin imperative shell for I/O. Each is testable in its own way; the integration is the orchestration; the shape stays clean.

**Q9.** Trace the dependency chain from Chapter 5 (test pyramid) through this chapter. How does each chapter's discipline depend on the previous ones?

**Discussion:**

The chain:

* **Chapter 5 (test pyramid)** : the shape that distributes testing across layers. Requires good unit tests to function.
* **Chapter 6 (property-based and mutation testing)** : the depth-of-testing discipline. Requires testable code to apply.
* **Chapter 7 (test doubles and Repository)** : the isolation mechanism. Requires the codebase to expose ports (or to be refactored to do so).
* **Chapter 8 (TDD vs test-after)** : the strategic choice. Requires the previous testing disciplines to be in place; the choice is moot if the tests would not be good either way.
* **Chapter 9 (the twelve refactorings)** : the mechanical moves. Requires tests to be safe.
* **Chapter 10 (refactoring under test)** : the safety net. Requires the testing disciplines from Chapters 5-7.
* **Chapter 11 (big refactors with feature flags)** : the strategic refactor. Requires the safety net and the team's commitment.
* **Chapter 12 (patterns that stayed useful)** : vocabulary for design moves. Requires familiarity with the moves themselves.
* **Chapter 13 (patterns that aged badly)** : the discipline of restraint. Requires knowing what to avoid.
* **Chapter 14 (hexagonal + FC/IS)** : the architectural frame. Requires all previous chapters: tests for isolation, refactorings for moves, patterns for vocabulary, restraint to avoid pattern-tax.

Each chapter is a prerequisite for the next; the order is the dependency order. A reader who has internalized Chapters 5-14 has the test-and-design foundation for the rest of the book.

### Chapter Summary

Hexagonal Architecture (§14.1, §14.2) places business logic at the core, isolates it from external systems via ports, and implements ports as adapters for specific external systems. The shape is the basis for testable design: tests of the core use in-memory adapters; tests of adapters run separately with real external systems.

Functional Core / Imperative Shell (§14.3) is the stricter variant: the core is pure, the shell handles all I/O and orchestration. Pure functions are the most testable code in any codebase; FC/IS maximizes the pure surface. The gather-decide-act pattern is the canonical orchestration shape under FC/IS.

Testing under hexagonal + FC/IS (§14.4) produces a clean test pyramid: thousands of pure-function unit tests, dozens of orchestrator component tests, a handful of adapter integration tests, very few end-to-end tests. The Helix subsystem refactor shrank the test suite's runtime by roughly an order of magnitude and dropped the bug rate.

The Repository pattern (§14.5) from Chapter 7 is the driven-port abstraction in hexagonal terms. The chapter sequence (testing → refactoring → patterns → architectural frame) is the dependency order; each chapter builds on its predecessors.

The AI-Layer (§14.6) is that LLMs know the shapes from training data but require explicit guidance to apply them consistently. The CLAUDE.md is the lever; with it, agent-generated code follows the shape; without it, defaults drift toward training-data norms.

Part VI (Chapters 15-16) covers code review and team practices. Code review, in 2026, is the central craft of reviewing AI-generated code; the shape of the codebase (Parts III, IV, V) is what makes review possible. With the testable and well-shaped codebase from Parts III-V in place, the team can review at scale; without it, review becomes overwhelming.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **For your next non-trivial backend module, separate the decision logic into a pure function.** Gather inputs (orchestrator). Call the pure decision. Act on the result. Three phases; the middle one is exhaustively testable.
  2. **Audit one tangled module against the gather-decide-act pattern.** Is the decision logic findable? If buried inside an orchestrator, extracting it as a pure function is one PR and a major test-suite improvement.
  3. **Spell out the architectural shape in CLAUDE.md.** Where ports live, where adapters live, where decisions live, where orchestrators live. The agent's code drifts toward whatever convention you specify; without specification, it drifts toward generic tutorial shapes.

---

## Chapter 15: Code Review as Discipline (Human and AI)

Carmen Ortiz watched the Currents engineering team's code review numbers in mid-2025 and noticed a pattern she could feel even before she could measure: senior engineers spent meaningfully longer on AI-generated PRs than junior engineers did, and the senior reviews caught a larger fraction of the bugs that later surfaced in production. The pattern was not subtle. It also was not a function of how _fast_ the senior engineers were; they were slower by stopwatch. They were thorough on the dimensions that mattered.

Carmen organized a workshop on code review discipline for the team. The first exercise: every engineer reviewed the same 800-line AI-generated PR and recorded their findings independently. When the lists were collated, a small set of issues appeared on every senior list and almost no junior list; these were the gaps that the senior reviewers had learned to look for and the junior ones had not. The workshop became a quarterly ritual; the discipline of code review became something the team could name, teach, and improve over time. Over the following quarters, the gap between newer and more experienced reviewers visibly narrowed.

(The numbers in this vignette are illustrative; the _pattern_ ; senior reviewers slower per PR but catching more durable bugs; is well-attested across the engineering organizations that have measured it.)

This chapter is about that discipline: what to look for in code review, what not to (style, taste, personal preference), and how the discipline changes when most PRs are AI-generated. The seniority-thoroughness pattern in Carmen's vignette is not anecdotal: Bacchelli and Bird's "Expectations, Outcomes, and Challenges of Modern Code Review" (ICSE 2013) measured Microsoft engineers and found the same gap, with more experienced reviewers catching meaningfully more defects per PR and spending meaningfully longer per review. Sadowski et al.'s 2018 Google paper confirmed the pattern at a different organization. The discipline is teachable, but the muscle memory takes time to build. Chapter 16 takes the team-level mechanics: PR conventions, async engineering, pair and mob programming with AI in the room. Together they form Part VI: review and team practices.

### 15.1 What code review is for

A code review serves several purposes simultaneously. Naming them helps prioritize what the reviewer's attention goes to.

#### Definition: Code Review

Code review is the practice of a second engineer examining a change before it is merged, with the goals of: (1) catching bugs that the author missed, (2) ensuring the change fits the codebase's conventions and architecture, (3) sharing knowledge across the team, and (4) maintaining the codebase's long-term health. Different reviews may emphasize different goals.

In plain language: another pair of eyes checks the work, with a structured set of things to check.

The goals are ranked roughly by per-review value:

  1. **Catching bugs** : highest immediate value. A bug caught in review costs minutes; the same bug shipped to production costs hours to days.
  2. **Architectural fit** : high long-term value. A PR that violates the codebase's shape pollutes the shape for everyone who reads it later.
  3. **Knowledge sharing** : medium value, accumulated over time. Reviewers learn from authors; authors learn from reviewers; the team's mental model converges.
  4. **Style and taste** : low value, often negative when over-applied. Style is usually a linter's job; taste disagreements consume review time without commensurate benefit.

The discipline is to focus on the high-value purposes; the failure mode is to spend most of the review on style and taste.

### 15.2 What to look for

The actionable checklist for a reviewer, in roughly the order of attention they should receive:

#### 1\. Does the change solve the problem it claims to solve?

Read the PR description. Read the linked ticket. Confirm that the code addresses the stated problem. A PR titled "Fix billing rounding bug for EU customers" that does not modify any rounding logic is a sign of confusion; ask before approving.

#### 2\. Are there bugs in the code?

The reviewer is looking for: untested edge cases (Thread C Lie #1), off-by-one errors (Lie #5), wrong error handling (Lie #4), hallucinated APIs (Lie #6 in AI-generated code, but also human-fabricated APIs). Read with an adversarial mindset: what input or state would break this?

Specific bug patterns to scan for:

* **Null and empty cases** : does the code handle `None`, empty list, empty string, zero?
* **Boundary cases** : what happens at the boundary of the conditional? At 0, at -1, at the maximum?
* **Error paths** : what does the function do when its dependency fails? Does it propagate, swallow, retry, or fall back?
* **Concurrency** : are there race conditions? Is shared state protected?
* **Resource leaks** : are files closed, connections released, transactions completed?

#### 3\. Are there tests that would catch regressions?

The tests are the second piece of code being reviewed. Apply Chapter 5's assertion-strength heuristic: for each test, what bug would it catch? If the answer is unclear, the test is coverage filler.

Specific test patterns to scan for:

* **Are there tests at all?** A PR with no tests is a yellow flag (sometimes appropriate, often not).
* **Are the tests testing the new code or the framework?** A test that asserts the function was called but not that it produced the right output is a Thread C lie.
* **Do the tests cover the edge cases the code claims to handle?** If the PR description mentions "handles EU customers," is there a test for an EU customer?
* **Are the tests at the right level?** A 200-line E2E test for what should be a unit test is a sign of pyramid inversion (Chapter 5).

#### 4\. Does the change fit the codebase's architecture?

Per Chapter 14: is the change in the right layer? Does it respect the ports-and-adapters boundary? Does it introduce I/O into the functional core? Does it bypass the Repository pattern (Chapter 7)?

Specific architectural smells:

* **A core function with a network call inline** : violates FC/IS.
* **A new dependency injected via Singleton** rather than constructor: violates Chapter 13.
* **An adapter calling another adapter** rather than going through the core: violates hexagonal.
* **A pattern from Chapter 13** (Singleton, Visitor, Factory Factory) introduced in new code: yellow flag.

#### 5\. Is the change consistent with surrounding code?

Naming, style, error handling conventions, file organization: does the new code look like the rest of the codebase? Inconsistencies are not bugs but they add cognitive load for future readers.

Specific consistency checks:

* **Naming** : do the names follow the codebase's conventions? Are domain concepts named in the codebase's vocabulary?
* **Error handling** : does the code raise the same exception types the rest of the codebase uses?
* **Import style** : are imports grouped and ordered as the codebase requires?
* **Test style** : are the tests structured like the codebase's other tests?

#### 6\. Is the change atomic?

Per Chapter 3: is the PR one atomic conceptual change, or is it three changes bundled together? A PR with unrelated changes should be split.

#### 7\. Does the change leave the code better or worse than it found it?

The Boy Scout Rule (Chapter 20 preview): a PR that touches a function should leave it slightly better (a small rename, a fix to a misleading comment, a removed dead branch). A PR that touches code and leaves it worse (adds a hack, leaves a TODO without ticket, introduces a workaround that the team has already agreed to remove) is a yellow flag.

### 15.3 What not to look for

The review's time is finite. Some categories of feedback consume time without commensurate benefit:

#### Style preferences that the linter handles

If the codebase has a configured linter, style is the linter's job. Reviewers should not spend time on indentation, line length, quote style, trailing commas, or other lint-handled details. The author should run the linter before opening the PR; the CI should enforce it; the reviewer should not duplicate the work.

#### Personal taste that the team has not agreed on

"I would have written this differently" is rarely useful feedback. If the team has agreed on a convention (in CLAUDE.md, in a style guide, in past code reviews), enforce the convention. If not, the author's choice is fine; do not block the PR on personal preference.

#### Speculative future flexibility

"What if we needed to support a third backend?" is the question that introduces pattern-tax (Chapter 13). The discipline is to evaluate the code against current requirements; if a second backend appears, the refactor can happen then. Reviewers who push for speculative flexibility produce over-engineered code.

#### Comprehensive refactoring of adjacent code

A PR that fixes a billing bug should not be expected to refactor the entire billing subsystem. The reviewer can note opportunities for follow-up work, but should not block the bug fix on unrelated refactoring.

#### Re-litigation of architectural decisions

If the team has agreed on hexagonal architecture (Chapter 14), a reviewer who pushes for a different architecture on each PR consumes time without benefit. Architectural decisions belong in ADRs (Chapter 17); the PR is not the venue for re-debating them.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "more feedback is better." The mistake is treating feedback volume as the metric. The metric is _bugs caught and architectural integrity preserved_ ; feedback that does not advance these goals is overhead. A review with two high-value comments is better than a review with thirty low-value ones.

### 15.4 The reviewer's vocabulary

A useful code review uses a small vocabulary that distinguishes the severity and intent of each comment. Carmen's Currents team adopted four labels. The four came from a postmortem in her previous role: an unlabeled "maybe consider" comment had been read by the author as advisory, merged unaddressed, and shipped the regression that defined her quarter. Since then, every comment she writes gets a label, and she has propagated the practice to every team she has led. The four labels:

* **Blocking** : the PR cannot merge without addressing this. Use for bugs, security issues, contract violations, architectural violations. Be sparing; if everything is blocking, nothing is.
* **Suggestion** : the reviewer suggests a change but does not require it. The author can take or leave it. Use for improvements that are not strictly necessary.
* **Nit** : a minor stylistic or organizational note that does not need to be addressed. Use for things the author can fix in a follow-up if they wish, or ignore.
* **Praise** : a positive note on something done well. Use for elegant solutions, thorough tests, good naming. Praise costs the reviewer nothing and reinforces the discipline; teams that never praise produce engineers who never know what they did right.

The labels speed up the author's response: they know what they must fix versus what they can choose to fix. They speed up the reviewer's writing: the label sets the tone.

Some teams use GitHub's "Approve / Request changes / Comment" buttons as a proxy. A "Request changes" review has at least one blocking comment; an "Approve" review has none. The label vocabulary maps onto the buttons naturally.

### 15.5 Reviewing AI-generated code: the Thread C checklist

A growing fraction of PRs in 2026 are AI-generated, fully or partially. Reviewing them requires applying the Thread C seven lies (introduced in Chapter 1 and developed throughout the book) as a checklist.

#### Lie #1: Plausible-but-untested edge cases

Scan for: code that handles the happy path well but does not explicitly address NaN, empty list, unicode, timezone, very large or very small numbers, special characters in strings, negative numbers, zero.

Question to ask: _what input would I try if I wanted to break this code?_ If the test suite does not include that input, the code is exposed.

#### Lie #2: Over-abstraction (pattern tax)

Scan for: layers of factories or providers without justification; abstract base classes with one concrete subclass; interfaces with one implementation; generic names (`Handler`, `Manager`, `Processor`); Strategy patterns for two-case conditionals.

Question to ask: _what does this abstraction add over the simpler alternative? Is there a real second case that justifies it?_

#### Lie #3: Inconsistent style with surrounding code

Scan for: different naming conventions, different error handling idioms, different test fixture style, different imports, different file organization compared to the surrounding code.

Question to ask: _does this PR look like the rest of our codebase?_ If not, the AI was not following the team's conventions; update CLAUDE.md to reduce the recurrence.

#### Lie #4: Missing or wrong-granularity error handling

Scan for: bare `except:` clauses, `try / except Exception` that swallows everything, `try / catch (e) { console.log(e); return null; }` patterns, retry logic without backoff, error messages without context.

Question to ask: _what does this code do when its dependency fails? Is that the right behavior?_

#### Lie #5: Subtle off-by-one or boundary errors

Scan for: comparisons using `<` versus `<=` near boundaries; loop bounds; array indexing; date math (especially with timezone or DST); rounding behavior; integer division.

Question to ask: _what happens at the boundary?_ Run the code mentally with inputs that are exactly at the boundary; verify the behavior matches the intent.

#### Lie #6: Hallucinated library APIs

Scan for: imports from library names you do not recognize; function calls with signatures that look unfamiliar; method names that sound plausible but might not exist; deprecated or outdated API usage.

Question to ask: _does this API actually exist?_ Verify by checking the library's current documentation or by running the code locally.

#### Lie #7: Plausible-looking insecure code

This lie is not in the original Thread C six but is worth treating with the same weight. AI-generated code routinely contains security mistakes that look fine to a reader who is not specifically scanning for them: SQL strings concatenated rather than parameterized, secrets embedded in code rather than read from a secret store, weak default crypto choices (MD5 for password hashing, ECB mode for block ciphers), uninstrumented authorization checks, package names that _look_ like the canonical library but resolve to a typosquatted package, and `eval` / `exec` on attacker-influenced input. The agent does not know which call sites are attacker-reachable; the agent does not know which packages have been hijacked since training; the agent does not know which crypto primitives your security team has banned.

Scan for: string concatenation into SQL or shell commands; hardcoded secrets, tokens, or credentials; suspicious package imports (verify the package name character-for-character against an authoritative source); `eval`, `exec`, `os.system`, `child_process.exec` with non-static input; missing authorization checks on new endpoints; weak crypto choices; unsigned/unauthenticated webhook handlers.

Question to ask: _if an attacker controls every variable in this function, what is the worst they can do?_ If you cannot answer or the answer is bad, fix or escalate to security review before merge.

#### Applying the checklist

For each AI-generated PR, run through the seven lies explicitly. The first few times this is slow (perhaps 10 minutes added to the review). With practice, it becomes reflexive (maybe 2 minutes added). The bug-catch rate is significantly higher than the unaided review; the time investment pays back within a few caught bugs. Appendix D provides the printable consolidated checklist.

### 15.6 AI code review tools

In 2026, AI code review tools provide a second pair of eyes that runs automatically on every PR. Three serious tools dominate:

* **Greptile** : deep codebase indexing; understands context across the repository; well-suited for architectural and consistency feedback.
* **CodeRabbit** : per-line feedback; faster turnaround; well-suited for tactical bug catching.
* **GitHub Copilot Review** : integrated with GitHub workflows; tight coupling to the platform; good for teams already invested in GitHub.

Each tool has strengths and weaknesses. Carmen's Currents adopted Greptile in 2025; the tool's contextual reviews catch consistency and architectural issues that per-line tools miss. Other teams prefer CodeRabbit's faster turnaround for quick PRs. The choice depends on the team's PR shape and the kinds of issues that recur.

#### How AI review tools complement human review

The tools are good at: scanning for common bug patterns at scale, catching consistency issues, surfacing potential edge cases, applying linting beyond what static tools do.

The tools are limited at: understanding the team's architectural decisions, weighing the importance of issues, distinguishing real concerns from false positives, communicating with the author with the right tone.

The combination: the AI tool runs first, surfaces a list of potential issues; the human reviewer reads the AI's findings, dismisses the false positives, adds the architectural and contextual feedback the AI missed. The total review time is sometimes shorter than human-alone (because the AI surfaces tactical issues quickly) and sometimes longer (because the human now has more issues to triage). The bug-catch rate is reliably higher.

#### Failure modes of AI review tools

* **Alarm fatigue** : a tool that surfaces 30 issues per PR, most of them low-value, trains the team to ignore the tool. The remedy is tuning the tool's strictness; some tools allow configuration.
* **Style-only feedback** : a tool that only catches lint-level issues duplicates the linter without adding value. The remedy is choosing a tool that goes deeper.
* **Architectural blindness** : a tool that does not understand the team's architectural conventions flags violations that the team accepts (or misses violations the team would block). The remedy is adding the team's conventions to the tool's configuration (CLAUDE.md-equivalent for the AI review tool).

### 15.7 The 2026 review workflow

A typical Currents PR in late 2025, with AI assistance:

  1. **Author opens PR** : includes description, links to ticket, optionally tags reviewers.
  2. **CI runs** : tests pass, linter passes, type checker passes. Required for the PR to be reviewable.
  3. **AI review tool runs** : Greptile (or equivalent) posts comments on the PR. The author can address some immediately; others remain for human consideration.
  4. **Human reviewer receives notification** : PR is now ready for human review (CI green, AI review complete).
  5. **Human reviewer reviews** : applies the Thread C checklist; engages with the AI tool's comments; adds architectural and contextual feedback.
  6. **Author addresses comments** : pushes additional commits; replies inline to specific comments.
  7. **Reviewer re-reviews** : usually faster than the initial review; confirms the changes address the comments.
  8. **Merge** : when blocking comments are resolved.

Total time: perhaps 2-6 hours of wall-clock time for a typical PR, with the human's time concentrated in steps 5 and 7. The SLA (Chapter 4) gives the author predictable turnaround.

### 15.8 Common review pitfalls

A list of patterns to avoid as a reviewer:

#### The rubber-stamp review

A reviewer who approves PRs in under two minutes without engaging is providing no value. Either the PR is trivially simple (rare) or the reviewer is not reviewing. The discipline is to engage; if you genuinely have no feedback after a careful read, say so explicitly ("I read this carefully and found nothing to flag; LGTM").

#### The block-on-personal-taste

A reviewer who blocks PRs on style preferences that are not team conventions consumes the author's time and trains the team to dread reviews. If you have a strong preference, propose it as a team convention; until it is a convention, do not block on it.

#### The two-week review

A reviewer who lets PRs sit for two weeks before reviewing breaks the SLA and the author's context. By the time the review arrives, the author has moved on; the feedback is harder to address. The SLA discipline (Chapter 4) is the fix; the personal discipline is to batch reviews twice per day rather than reactively.

#### The over-explainer

A reviewer who attaches a paragraph of explanation to every comment may be over-investing in each review. Comments should be terse; if the explanation requires a paragraph, link to an ADR or have a synchronous conversation. The PR is not the place for design education.

#### The architectural-debate-on-every-PR

A reviewer who re-debates the codebase's architecture on every PR is consuming the author's time without progress. If the architecture is wrong, write an ADR (Chapter 17) and propose the change explicitly; do not block PRs that follow the current architecture.

A senior reviewer's job, summarized: catch the bugs and architectural issues the team would otherwise miss, with the team's velocity preserved. Five high-value findings per PR is helping; fifty findings, half of them style-level, is hurting. Focus on the high-value ones; save the rest for follow-up PRs, for documentation, or for nothing at all.

### 15.9 A worked example: a Currents PR review

A 350-line PR at Currents in late 2025: adding a new endpoint to compute customer lifetime value (LTV) for use in the sales dashboard. The PR was generated mostly by Claude Code agent under tests-as-spec workflow. The author opened it and added a description.

The AI review tool (Greptile) posted three comments:

* "The new function `compute_ltv` is in `src/utils/`; similar revenue calculations are in `src/billing/`. Consider co-locating."
* "The test for `compute_ltv` covers the happy path but not the case of customers with zero orders. Suggest adding a test."
* "The function uses `float` for currency; the codebase convention is `Decimal`."

The human reviewer (Carmen) read the AI's comments and added her own. She wrote each comment the way she had trained herself to; label first, always, in bold, before the substance, so the author would know what was required of them before reading a single word of explanation:

* **Blocking** : "The function returns negative LTV for customers with refunds exceeding charges. This is mathematically correct but unusual; either guard against it or document the behavior."
* **Blocking** : "The new endpoint is registered in `routes/admin.py` but does not check the admin authorization; per ADR-019, admin endpoints require explicit authorization. Add the `@require_admin` decorator."
* **Suggestion** : "Consider extracting the rule about 12-month rolling window into a constant; it appears twice in the function body."
* **Nit** : "The variable name `data` in line 87 could be more specific."
* **Praise** : "Nice property-based test for the LTV invariant (LTV >= 0 when charges >= refunds)."

The author addressed the blocking comments by adding a guard and the decorator; chose to take the suggestion; ignored the nit. The PR merged within a day.

The total reviewer time: about 25 minutes (10 for the initial review, 10 for re-review, 5 for the back-and-forth). The Greptile comments saved perhaps 5 minutes of human work by surfacing the file-location and Decimal issues; the human's blocking comments were the high-value catches (the negative-LTV behavior and the authorization gap).

This is the 2026 review workflow at its best: AI for tactical issues, human for architectural and contextual ones, both feeding into a PR that ships within the SLA.

* * *

**Thread B;`calculateRefund`, stage 2: after first review**

The first review of `calculateRefund` was a 20-minute session. Three blocking comments emerged:

  1. **"NaN from the frontend"** : the `request.amount` field came from a user-submitted form. JavaScript floating-point can produce NaN; the original function had no guard and would propagate `NaN` silently into the database.

     * Reviewer: "What happens when `request.amount` is NaN? We need an explicit guard here."
  2. **"Double-refund protection missing"** : nothing prevented calling `calculate_refund` twice on the same order. The accounting system did not deduplicate; a network retry could produce a double refund.

     * Reviewer: "What prevents calling this twice? We need to check `refund_status` before computing."
  3. **"Currency hard-coded"** : the original used `'USD'` as a literal string instead of reading `order.currency`.

     * Reviewer: "Three of our customers pay in EUR. This silently produces wrong currency in the result."

Each comment was a blocking issue: a production deployment with the original code would have produced real accounting errors. The review took 20 minutes; the estimated cost of each bug in production would have been hours of investigation and a customer incident.

The post-review function added: a NaN guard on `request.amount`, a `refund_status` check at the top, and `order.currency` as the result currency. The reviewer approved on the first re-review. The lesson the team recorded: refund logic involving money is a domain where reviewers should apply the full six-point checklist, not skim.

* * *

### 15.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Two reviewers are reviewing the same 400-line PR. The reviews come back side by side:

**Reviewer A** posts 42 comments. Topics: indentation (8 comments), variable naming preferences (12), import order (6), 4 actual logic concerns, 8 style preferences contradicting other reviewers' historical comments, 4 questions.

**Reviewer B** posts 5 comments. Topics: 2 blocking logic concerns (one of which catches a subtle off-by-one), 1 architectural suggestion, 1 question about scope, 1 praise.

The team measures reviewers by "feedback count per PR." Reviewer A is on track for a strong quarterly rating; Reviewer B's count is low. What is wrong with this measurement, what is each reviewer doing right or wrong, and what should the team measure instead?

Hint

Feedback that catches bugs / improves architecture is the asset. Feedback that consumes author and reviewer time without that result is overhead.

**Answer:** The measurement is upside-down: "feedback count" treats noise as signal. Of Reviewer A's 42 comments, 14 (indentation + import-order) are the linter's job — if the linter is not catching them, fix the linter, not the PR. The 12 naming-preference and 8 style-preference comments are taste or bikeshedding; if the team has agreed on a convention, encode it in CLAUDE.md or a formatter. Only the 4 logic concerns and 4 questions are genuine review value — 8 of 42 comments. Reviewer A also consumes the author's response cycle (every comment needs at least an ack); the noise drowns the signal so the _real_ logic concern may be missed.

Reviewer B is concentrated on signal: 2 blocking logic concerns (including the real off-by-one), 1 architectural suggestion, 1 scope question, 1 praise. Five comments, all high-value, all worth the author's response cycle.

The team should measure: bugs caught in review, review SLA compliance, and re-review turnaround — not comment count. The metric a team chooses tells the team what to do. Comments-per-PR rewards Reviewer A; bugs-caught + SLA rewards Reviewer B; teams that ship well are teams where Reviewer B's approach is the norm.

**Q2.** Why are style preferences usually the wrong subject for code review feedback?

Hint

The linter usually exists.

**Answer:** Style decisions that the team has agreed on should be enforced by the linter, automatically and reliably. The reviewer should not duplicate the linter's work; that is wasted attention. Style decisions the team has _not_ agreed on should not be enforced by individual reviewers; either propose them as conventions (and add them to the linter) or accept the author's choice.

Reviewers who block on style produce two failure modes: (a) the author resents the bikeshedding and disengages from the review process; (b) the team's review capacity is consumed by trivia, leaving less for the high-value findings. Both are costly.

The narrow exception: style that the linter cannot catch and that the team has agreed on (architectural conventions, naming patterns, file organization). These belong in CLAUDE.md and in review checklists, and reviewers can flag them. But the bar is "team has agreed," not "I personally prefer."

**Q3.** Carmen reviews a PR and posts these five comments without labels:

  1. "The new endpoint doesn't check authorization; an attacker with the URL could read any user's data."
  2. "Consider extracting `calculate_rolling_window_12_months` into a constant; it appears three times."
  3. "There's an extra space at line 47 before the closing paren."
  4. "I noticed you handled the empty-list case with a guard at the top instead of through the loop's natural termination. Either works."
  5. "The property test for charge-minus-credits invariant is really nicely done."

The author replies "got it, will fix" and asks you (a peer reviewer) which of the five they actually have to fix to merge. Walk through each comment: which label (Blocking / Suggestion / Nit / Praise) would Carmen have used had she added labels, and what is the author's expected response to each?

Hint

The label is determined by the _consequence_ : must-fix-to-merge, optional-take-it-or-leave-it, take-or-ignore-trivially, or pure positive reinforcement.

**Answer:**

  1. **Blocking** ; security issue, contract violation. The author must fix before merge; Carmen will re-review the fix. No discretion.
  2. **Suggestion** ; improvement, not strictly required. The author can choose to extract the constant (5 minutes, modest gain) or leave it (the existing code is still correct). Either choice is defensible; Carmen will not block on this.
  3. **Nit** ; trivial, mostly cosmetic. The linter should be catching it; if not, the author can fix in 5 seconds or ignore. Carmen will not re-review for this; if the author leaves it, that is fine.
  4. **Suggestion** (leaning toward neither / observation); a stylistic preference where both approaches are reasonable. Sometimes this is "Nit" if the team has no preference; sometimes "Suggestion" if there is a slight team lean. The author can take it or leave it; either is fine.
  5. **Praise** ; pure positive. The author should not "fix" anything; the praise tells them _what they did right_ , which reinforces the discipline for future PRs.

So the author's mental sorting of the five: 1 → must do; 2 and 4 → take or leave; 3 → ignore or 5-second fix; 5 → smile and continue.

The reason the vocabulary matters: Carmen _should_ have added the labels. Without them, the author has to _infer_ the priority of each comment from context; which is what created the "got it, will fix" reply where the author was about to spend equal effort on the security bug and the extra space. Five seconds of label discipline from Carmen saves the author thirty minutes of misallocated effort. Multiplied across hundreds of PRs, the vocabulary is the highest-leverage change to make to a team's review culture.

**Q4.** Apply the Thread C seven lies to a hypothetical AI-generated function `compute_discount(customer, order)` that returns a discount fraction (0 to 1). For each lie, name one specific issue you would scan for.

Hint

The seven lies are listed in §15.5.

**Answer:**

* **Lie #1 (untested edge cases)** : does the function handle customer with no orders? Order with negative total? Customer with `tier=None`?
* **Lie #2 (over-abstraction)** : is the discount logic in a `DiscountCalculator` class with an interface and a factory, when a function would have worked?
* **Lie #3 (inconsistent style)** : does the function use the codebase's `Decimal` for the discount, or `float`? Does it follow the naming conventions?
* **Lie #4 (wrong error handling)** : what does the function do if the customer or order is None? Does it raise, return None, or return a default?
* **Lie #5 (off-by-one)** : are the threshold comparisons `>` versus `>=` correct? At the boundary (exactly 12 months), what happens?
* **Lie #6 (hallucinated APIs)** : does the function call any methods on customer or order that those classes do not actually have?
* **Lie #7 (insecure code)** : is the discount fraction value used to construct any SQL, shell command, or downstream input without sanitization? Is the function's authorization correct (can an attacker request arbitrarily large discounts for someone else)?

Running through the checklist takes a few minutes; the bugs it catches reliably justify the time.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Take a recent code review where you were the reviewer. Re-evaluate your comments using the §15.2 / §15.3 framework. Which comments would you classify as high-value? Which as low-value? Would you change anything?

Hint

The retrospective is more useful than the original review.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common finding: 30-50% of comments were high-value (catching bugs, flagging architectural issues, sharing useful knowledge), and the rest were lower-value (style preferences, personal taste, speculative future-proofing). The retrospective is the discipline that calibrates the reviewer's instincts.

A useful follow-up: track this for a month. Note the high-value comments per review; aim to keep the count steady while reducing the low-value comments. The total review time should drop; the bug-catch rate should not.

**Q6.** Use an AI code review tool (Greptile, CodeRabbit, or Copilot Review) on a recent PR in your codebase. Compare its findings to what you would have caught in human review. What did the AI catch that you missed? What did you catch that the AI missed?

Hint

The complementarity is the interesting part.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* The AI catches mechanical issues at scale (inconsistent imports across files, consistency with similar patterns elsewhere, common bug patterns).
* The human catches contextual issues the AI missed (architectural fit, alignment with recent team decisions, subtle domain-specific bugs).
* The AI sometimes flags false positives that the human can quickly dismiss; the false-positive rate varies by tool.
* The combined review catches more than either alone; the time investment is modest if the AI runs automatically.

The lesson: AI review tools are complements, not substitutes. The team that uses both productively gets the best of both; the team that uses either alone misses the other's contribution.

**Q7.** Conduct a workshop in your team modeled on Carmen's: have everyone review the same PR independently, then compare findings. What pattern emerges? What does it suggest about training opportunities?

Hint

§15's opening describes the exercise.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common patterns:

* A small number of "obvious" issues that everyone finds.
* A medium number of issues that experienced reviewers find but junior ones miss.
* A small number of issues that only one or two reviewers find (often the engineer with the most domain expertise).

The training opportunities are in the middle band: issues that experienced reviewers find consistently and junior reviewers miss. These are the patterns that can be taught explicitly (often by walking through the Thread C checklist with specific examples).

The exercise also surfaces _disagreements_ : cases where two senior reviewers disagree on whether something is a bug. These are opportunities for ADRs (Chapter 17) to formalize the team's decision and prevent recurring debate.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute: "In 2026, code review becomes more important than coding because most code is AI-generated. The team's review capacity is the bottleneck on shipping."

**Discussion:** For: AI generates code faster than humans can write it; the bottleneck moves to review; teams that cannot review well ship slower or ship worse. The discipline of review becomes the central craft skill (Chapter 1's argument extended).

Against: review can also be AI-assisted; the bottleneck moves _with_ AI's role; the relative cost of human review stays roughly proportional to the volume. Some review work (style, mechanical consistency) is automatable; the human's role concentrates on the genuinely hard parts (architecture, context). The total elapsed time of review per PR may not be the bottleneck if AI handles the easy parts.

The synthesis: review is _more important_ in 2026 because the volume is higher and the failure modes (AI-introduced bugs, Thread C lies) are characteristic; the human's role in review is _more concentrated_ (on the hard parts that AI cannot do) but does not necessarily grow proportional to the code volume. The team that invests in review discipline ships faster overall; the team that does not ships slower or ships worse. Either way, the discipline matters more, not less.

**Q9.** Map the connection between code review (this chapter) and the disciplines from Parts III-V (testing, refactoring, patterns, architecture). How do those disciplines make review easier?

**Discussion:** Each prior discipline contributes:

* **Testing (Chapters 5-8)** : a well-tested PR has a test suite that documents intent; the reviewer reads the tests as part of understanding what the change is supposed to do. Without tests, the reviewer must infer intent from the implementation alone, which is slower and less reliable.
* **Refactoring (Chapters 9-11)** : a PR that follows the twelve refactorings and the atomic-commit discipline is easier to read. The reviewer sees one conceptual change per commit; the cognitive load is manageable. A PR that bundles ten unrelated changes overwhelms the reviewer.
* **Patterns (Chapters 12-13)** : a PR that uses appropriate patterns is recognizable; the reviewer sees "Strategy on the tier" and immediately understands the shape. A PR that uses pattern-tax is harder to read because each layer must be navigated. A PR that uses anti-patterns (Singleton, Visitor) is a red flag for the reviewer.
* **Architecture (Chapter 14)** : a PR that fits the hexagonal frame is in the expected place; the reviewer knows where to find the core logic, where the adapters live, where the tests should be. A PR that violates the frame requires the reviewer to first understand the violation before evaluating the change.

The compounding: each discipline reduces the reviewer's cognitive load; together, they make review tractable at the scale of AI-augmented engineering. Without them, the reviewer drowns in volume. With them, the reviewer focuses on the genuinely hard parts.

This is why the chapter sequence in this book is what it is: testing, refactoring, patterns, architecture, then review. Each chapter is a prerequisite for the next; the review chapter could not be written without the prior work.

### Chapter Summary

Code review (§15.1) serves multiple purposes: catching bugs, ensuring architectural fit, sharing knowledge, maintaining long-term health. The reviewer's attention is finite; the discipline is to focus on the high-value purposes.

What to look for (§15.2): does the change solve the problem? Are there bugs? Are there tests? Does it fit the architecture? Is it consistent with surrounding code? Is it atomic? Does it leave the code better? The checklist is the reviewer's reflex.

What not to look for (§15.3): linter-handled style, personal taste, speculative future flexibility, unrelated refactoring, architectural re-litigation. Avoiding these preserves review capacity for the high-value findings.

The reviewer's vocabulary (§15.4): Blocking, Suggestion, Nit, Praise. The labels speed up communication and set the tone.

The Thread C checklist (§15.5) is the AI-generated-code review tool: untested edge cases, over-abstraction, inconsistent style, wrong error handling, off-by-one boundaries, hallucinated APIs, and (added in revision) plausible-looking insecure code. Running through the seven on every AI PR catches the characteristic 2026 failure modes; Appendix D has the printable consolidation.

AI code review tools (§15.6): Greptile, CodeRabbit, Copilot Review. Each catches tactical issues at scale; humans catch architectural and contextual issues. The combination is the 2026 review workflow.

Common pitfalls (§15.8): rubber-stamp reviews, block-on-personal-taste, two-week reviews, over-explainers, architectural-debate-on-every-PR. The discipline is to recognize the pitfalls and avoid them.

The 2026 review workflow (§15.7) is: CI passes, AI tool comments, human reviewer applies the checklist and adds context, author addresses, reviewer re-reviews, merge. The SLA from Chapter 4 keeps the workflow predictable.

Chapter 16 takes the team-level mechanics: PR conventions, async engineering, pair and mob programming with AI in the room. With the review discipline in place, the team-level workflows become the differentiator; a team with good review and good workflows ships faster than either alone.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Start every review comment with a bolded label.** `**Blocking:**` / `**Suggestion:**` / `**Nit:**` / `**Praise:**`. The author knows the required action before reading any prose. This is Carmen's habit; adopt it.
  2. **Keep Appendix D accessible (printed, bookmarked, or in your team's wiki) for the first month of AI-PR review.** Run through the seven Thread C lies deliberately at first. By month three the checklist becomes internalized; review gets faster, not slower, because you stop missing the recurring patterns.
  3. **Praise something specific on every PR you review.** Costs the reviewer nothing; tells the author what they did right; reinforces the discipline. Teams that never praise produce engineers who never know what they did well.

---

## Chapter 16: PR Conventions, Async Engineering, and Pair / Mob with AI in the Room

Carmen Ortiz's Currents engineering team operates in three timezones (San Francisco, Berlin, Bangalore). Synchronous communication is hard; meetings either exclude one timezone or run during someone's sleep. By 2025, the team had settled on a deeply async workflow: PRs as the unit of work, written communication as the default, synchronous time reserved for the cases where it earned its keep. The result was a team that shipped at a steady cadence despite the distributed shape.

In 2026, AI agents joined the workflow as third parties. The agents pair with engineers on hard problems; the agents implement features under tests-as-spec; the agents review PRs alongside humans. The team's async-first discipline accommodated this naturally: the AI is just another collaborator who communicates through written artifacts (PRs, comments, agent transcripts). But the boundaries between agent and human, between pair and mob, between async and synchronous, became important to articulate.
