This chapter is about the team-level workflows around PRs: how to structure a PR for review, how to default to async, when synchronous engineering earns its keep, and how pair and mob programming work in 2026 with AI in the room.

### 16.1 The PR as unit of work

Chapter 4 introduced the PR as the unit of work. This chapter develops the discipline at the team level.

#### The PR's lifecycle

A PR moves through several states:

  1. **Drafting** : the author is writing the PR; CI may not yet pass; reviewers are not expected.
  2. **Ready for review** : CI is green, the description is complete, reviewers are tagged.
  3. **Under review** : reviewers are reading and commenting.
  4. **Author responding** : the author is addressing comments.
  5. **Re-review** : reviewers are confirming the changes.
  6. **Approved** : ready to merge.
  7. **Merged**.

Each state has its own SLAs and signals. The author should mark drafts explicitly (GitHub's "Draft" feature, or a `WIP:` prefix); reviewers should respond to ready-for-review PRs within the team's SLA; the author should respond to comments within the team's SLA. Without these signals, PRs drift in ambiguous states; with them, the lifecycle is predictable.

#### PR sizing revisited

Chapter 4 named 200-400 lines as the substantive-change sweet spot. The data supporting this remains consistent in 2026: above 600-800 lines, review quality drops measurably; below 50-100 lines, the review overhead is disproportionate (every PR has a baseline cost of context-switching).

In practice, the right size depends on the change shape:

* **Bug fixes** : usually small (10-100 lines); reviewers can focus deeply on the specific change.
* **New features** : medium (200-400 lines); reviewers can hold the feature in their head.
* **Refactors** : medium to large (300-600 lines); reviewers can verify behavior preservation if tests are strong.
* **Documentation** : variable; small docs are easy, large docs require structural review.
* **AI-generated PRs** : should be sized like human PRs; the AI sometimes produces larger PRs by default, which the team should resize before submitting.

The discipline is to ask, before opening a PR: _can a reviewer review this carefully in one sitting?_ If not, split.

#### The atomic-commit discipline at the PR level

Chapter 3's atomic-commit discipline applies within the PR. A PR of 400 lines split into eight atomic commits is easier to review than the same 400 lines in one commit. The reviewer can read commit by commit, building understanding incrementally.

The convention at Currents: a PR with more than 200 lines should have between 3 and 10 commits, each atomic, with messages following Conventional Commits (Chapter 4). PRs with one giant commit or with twenty WIP commits are pushed back to the author for rebase.

### 16.2 PR descriptions that earn their keep

A PR description is the first thing the reviewer reads. A good description prepares the reviewer to do the rest of the work efficiently; a bad description sends them in circles.

#### The components of a good PR description

A useful PR description includes:

* **What** : one sentence summarizing the change.
* **Why** : the motivation; the problem being solved; a link to the ticket or ADR.
* **How** : the approach, briefly. Anything non-obvious about the implementation.
* **Testing** : what was tested, what was not. If manual testing was done, what scenarios.
* **Risk** : what could go wrong; rollback plan; whether it is behind a feature flag.
* **Reviewer note** : any specific area where the author wants reviewer attention.

A template enforces the components. Currents uses a GitHub PR template:
    
    
    ## What
    <One sentence>
    
    ## Why
    <Motivation; link to ticket>
    
    ## How
    <Approach; non-obvious decisions>
    
    ## Testing
    <What was tested>
    
    ## Risk
    <What could go wrong; rollback plan>
    
    ## Reviewer note
    <Specific areas of concern, if any>
    

The template is not bureaucracy. It is a forcing function for the author to think about the reviewer's needs. The discipline of filling in each section reveals gaps in the work itself.

#### What to put in "Why"

The "Why" section is the most important and the most often weak. A weak "Why" says "implementing TRELLIS-1234." A strong "Why" explains the underlying motivation: "Customers in the EU have been reporting that refunds are off by 0.05; this PR adjusts the rounding for accounts created before the 2021 schema migration. Reproduction: see TRELLIS-1234."

The reviewer needs the underlying motivation to evaluate whether the change addresses the right problem. The ticket number alone forces the reviewer to context-switch to the ticket; the explanation in the PR description keeps the reviewer in the PR.

#### AI-generated descriptions

In 2026, AI agents often generate the PR description from the commit messages and diff. The descriptions are usually structurally correct (they fill in each section) but sometimes shallow on the "Why" (the agent does not know the customer's underlying motivation). The author's job is to strengthen the "Why" section even when the AI generates the rest.

The CLAUDE.md (Chapter 22) can specify: "Always include a 'Why' section explaining the underlying motivation, not just the ticket reference. If the motivation is not clear from the conversation, prompt the engineer to provide it before opening the PR."

### 16.3 Async engineering as the default

A team with three time zones cannot be primarily synchronous; the math does not work. Even teams in a single time zone benefit from async-first because synchronous communication interrupts deep work.

#### Definition: Async-First Engineering

Async-first engineering treats written, asynchronous communication as the default for engineering work. Synchronous communication (meetings, calls, chat) is reserved for cases where it earns its keep: the discussion requires real-time back-and-forth, the topic is sensitive, or the participants need to align on a complex decision quickly.

In plain language: write things down by default; meet only when necessary.

#### The async-first practices

* **PRs as the primary communication** : design decisions show up in code, not in chat. The PR description and the ADR are the durable artifacts.
* **Written design proposals** : a major change starts with a document (an RFC or ADR in Chapter 17 sense) rather than a meeting. The document gets reviewed asynchronously; comments are addressed in writing; the decision is recorded.
* **Status updates in writing** : standups, if they happen, are async (written into a channel) rather than synchronous (verbal). The team that needs to know reads at their convenience.
* **Issues and tickets, not direct messages** : bugs and feature requests go into the tracker, not into someone's DM. The tracker is searchable; DMs are not.

The discipline is the _default_ : written first; synchronous when necessary. Some teams invert this (synchronous first, written when necessary) and find their work harder to compose, harder to onboard new engineers into, and harder to defend against context loss when someone leaves.

#### When synchronous earns its keep

Some situations are genuinely better synchronous:

* **Architecture debates** : a complex design question with multiple stakeholders may benefit from a one-hour meeting that produces an ADR. The meeting compresses what would otherwise be days of async back-and-forth.
* **Incident response** : when production is down, real-time coordination is faster than written. The post-incident write-up (Chapter 19) preserves the lessons.
* **Pair programming for hard problems** : two engineers (or one engineer plus an AI agent) working on the same code in real time can solve some problems faster than either alone. §16.5 develops this.
* **Mentoring conversations** : a junior engineer learning a complex system benefits from a real conversation more than a written exchange. Some learning is high-bandwidth.
* **Team-building** : relationships are built in low-stakes synchronous time; a fully async team can struggle to build trust without occasional voice or video.

The discipline is to _recognize_ when synchronous earns its keep, rather than defaulting to it.

Two objections recur from skeptics. The first: "async-first means we don't talk." It does not. Async-first teams talk plenty; they just default to writing first. The talking happens where it adds value (the cases above); the writing captures what is durable. The second: "async-first is slower because you wait for responses." This conflates latency for a single exchange with throughput across many. A team running twenty parallel async conversations ships more total work than a team blocked on serialized meetings, even when each individual exchange takes longer. Aggregate throughput favors async; per-exchange latency disfavors it. The right metric is the aggregate.

### 16.4 Pair programming in 2026

Pair programming has two engineers (or one engineer and one AI agent) working on the same code in real time. One drives (writes the code); the other navigates (thinks ahead, reviews the driving, suggests). The roles rotate.

#### When pair programming earns its keep

* **Complex problems** where two minds are genuinely better than one (subtle bugs, deep refactors, performance optimization, security-sensitive code).
* **Knowledge sharing** when one engineer has expertise the other lacks (onboarding, cross-team rotations).
* **AI collaboration** where the human and the AI are working together as a pair (next section).

#### When pair programming does not earn its keep

* **Routine work** that one engineer could do faster alone (small bug fixes, mechanical refactors, documentation).
* **Wide knowledge spread** where both engineers know the area and the work is straightforward; the second person is overhead.
* **Heads-down deep work** where flow matters more than collaboration.

The discipline is to _choose_ pair programming for the situations where it earns its keep, not to apply it universally. Teams that mandate pair programming for everything find that most work is slower; teams that never pair miss the cases where it would have been faster.

### 16.5 Pair programming with AI in the room

In 2026, an engineer working with Cursor agent, Claude Code, or Devin is in a pair-programming relationship with the AI. The dynamics differ from human-human pairing but the pattern is recognizable.

#### The roles

* **Human** : usually the navigator. Sets the direction, evaluates the AI's output, asks for revisions, makes the architectural decisions.
* **AI** : usually the driver. Writes the code, runs the tests, reports back. Sometimes the AI is the navigator (suggesting approaches the human had not considered).

The roles can flip: an experienced engineer may drive while the AI navigates (suggesting improvements as the engineer types). The discipline is to know which role you are in and to perform it well.

#### The cadence

A pair-with-AI session has a natural cadence:

  1. Human states the goal in clear terms (often a failing test, per tests-as-spec).
  2. AI proposes an implementation.
  3. Human reads and evaluates.
  4. If acceptable, the AI continues to the next piece. If not, human asks for revisions.
  5. Iterate; the cadence is fast (minutes per cycle).

The cadence is faster than human-human pairing because the AI does not need breaks, does not have ego, and types fast. The cadence is slower than solo work in some ways because the human must read and evaluate every AI output; the read time is the dominant cost.

#### What the human is doing

While the AI drives, the human is:

* **Reading** : scanning the AI's output for bugs, style issues, architectural fit.
* **Anticipating** : thinking about the next step before the AI proposes it.
* **Course-correcting** : catching the AI when it is heading in a wrong direction.
* **Holding context** : remembering what was discussed two iterations ago that the AI may have lost.

This is the _navigator_ role made explicit. An engineer who treats AI pair-programming as "let the AI cook while I check Slack" misses the navigator work and produces lower-quality output. The discipline is to engage fully during the session.

#### When AI pair-programming is wrong

* **The problem requires deep domain knowledge** the AI does not have. The AI's contributions are noise; the human is better solo.
* **The task is mechanical and the AI is unnecessary overhead**. Type the code yourself.
* **The session is dragging on without progress**. If you have been iterating for an hour without converging, the AI may not be the right partner for this problem; switch approach.

#### Common Misconception (revisited)

A common misconception is "AI pair-programming is just like human pair-programming." The mistake is overlooking the differences. The AI has no ego (so feedback can be more direct); the AI has limited context (so the human must hold the architectural picture); the AI does not fatigue (so the cadence can be faster); the AI's failure modes are characteristic (Thread C lies, Chapter 15) and different from human failure modes.

A reasonable mental model: AI pair-programming is its own discipline, sharing some features with human pair-programming but requiring its own techniques. Engineers who learn the discipline get the benefits; engineers who treat AI as either "a human teammate" or "just a tool" get neither.

### 16.6 Mob programming

Mob programming has three or more engineers working on the same code at the same time. One drives; the others navigate; everyone discusses; the role rotation is frequent (every 5-15 minutes).

Mob programming is rarer than pair programming and is appropriate for narrower contexts.

#### When mob programming earns its keep

* **Thorny problems** where many perspectives help (cross-cutting refactors, security-sensitive design, debugging a hard-to-reproduce issue).
* **Team-wide knowledge transfer** when the topic affects multiple roles (a new architectural pattern being introduced).
* **Resolving a stuck PR** that has been bouncing between reviewers; gathering everyone in real time can break the impasse.
* **Onboarding** where a new engineer learns by participating in real work alongside the team.

#### When mob programming is wrong

* **Routine work** : the cost (n engineers' time) exceeds the benefit.
* **Work that fits a single engineer** : the mob is overhead.
* **Teams with very different time zones** : the synchronous requirement is hard to satisfy.

A typical Currents pattern: mob programming once every two weeks, for a specific thorny problem identified in advance. The session is two hours, four engineers, one specific outcome (a tricky migration, a security review, a complex bug). The rest of the time is solo or pair.

#### Mob programming with AI

In 2026, mob programming can include an AI agent as one of the mob members. The dynamics shift: the AI's contributions are read in real time by all the humans; the rotation includes the AI as a possible driver; the AI sometimes navigates by reviewing the human driver's work.

This is novel enough that the team's conventions are still settling. A reasonable default: the AI is one voice in the mob, not the decision-maker; the humans evaluate the AI's contributions and decide; the rotation through the AI as driver is brief (10 minutes at most) before returning to a human driver.

### 16.7 The team's communication conventions

A team that has internalized async-first and pair/mob discipline benefits from explicit communication conventions. Currents' conventions:

* **PRs over chat for code feedback** : if you have feedback on code, put it in a PR comment, not in a DM. The PR comment is durable and visible to others.
* **ADRs over meetings for architectural decisions** : if a decision affects the team's code, write the ADR; review async; merge when consensus emerges.
* **Issues over emails for bugs and features** : if you find a bug or want a feature, file the issue; the tracker is the durable record.
* **Status in writing for daily updates** : standups are async via a daily channel update. The format is: yesterday, today, blockers.
* **Synchronous time for synchronous needs** : meetings are explicitly scheduled with a stated outcome; ad-hoc voice calls are for incidents and acute pair-programming.

The conventions reduce the question "where should this conversation happen?" to a quick lookup. New engineers learn the conventions in onboarding; the team uses them reflexively. Without the conventions, the team's communication scatters across many channels and no one knows where to find what.

Carmen's own additional convention, which she keeps mostly to herself but which the team has come to recognize: any written comment of hers that bears on a decision someone else has to make starts with a bolded label first. `**Decision needed:**`, `**Heads-up:**`, `**FYI:**`, in addition to the four review labels (`**Blocking:**`, `**Suggestion:**`, `**Nit:**`, `**Praise:**`) she uses in PR review. The reader knows what is required of them before they finish the first line.

### 16.8 A worked example: a Currents migration spanning multiple teams

A Currents migration in 2025 spanned three teams: Platform (Carmen's), Billing, and Data. The migration was the kind of work where async coordination across teams matters: each team has its own priorities, time zones, and pace.

The workflow:

  1. **ADR** : the Platform team drafted an ADR (Chapter 17) describing the migration's motivation, scope, and approach. Distributed via the team's documentation tool. Comments and questions came in async over a week.
  2. **Cross-team review** : the Billing and Data leads reviewed the ADR; raised concerns about specific subsystems; proposed adjustments. The discussion was async, threaded under the ADR.
  3. **One synchronous meeting** : a 90-minute video call with the three team leads to resolve the remaining open questions. The meeting produced specific commitments; the meeting notes were posted in writing.
  4. **Implementation** : each team owned its piece. PRs went into the relevant repositories; cross-team PRs (which touched multiple teams' code) had reviewers from each affected team.
  5. **Weekly written sync** : every Friday, Carmen posted a written update on the migration's status. The other teams responded async with their pieces.
  6. **One synchronous retrospective** : at the end of the migration, a 90-minute call to capture lessons. The retrospective notes were posted in writing.

Total synchronous time: about four hours across three months. Total written communication: dozens of PRs, comments, ADR threads, and updates. The migration completed on schedule despite the three teams' three time zones and three priorities.

The lesson: async-first scales to multi-team work in a way that synchronous-first does not. The synchronous time was used for the cases where it earned its keep (the kickoff meeting, the retrospective); the rest was async.

### 16.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A new engineering manager joins your team and proposes daily standup meetings (15 minutes, 9am sharp). The team spans three timezones: San Francisco, Berlin, Bangalore. 9am SF is 6pm Berlin and 9:30pm Bangalore. The manager argues: "Daily standups have been industry standard for 25 years; the team needs synchronous time for alignment." Construct your async-first counter-proposal, including the specific replacement for standup and the _measurable_ benefit you would claim within one quarter.

Hint

Throughput, time-zone reach, durability are the async-first arguments. What is the actual _outcome_ of a standup, and is there a cheaper way to achieve it async?

**Answer:**

**The async counter-proposal:** replace the synchronous daily standup with an async daily-update thread in the team's primary channel. Each engineer posts (in their own timezone, end of their working day) a short message: "Yesterday I X. Today I plan Y. Blocker: Z or none." Other engineers read at the start of their day. Total time per engineer: 2–3 minutes to write, 3–5 minutes to read everyone else's. Total team time per day: ~30 minutes spread across timezones, _not requiring overlap_.

**What the standup was for (and what we are not losing):**

* Status sharing; preserved, with a search-friendly written trail.
* Blocker surfacing; preserved; in fact often _better_ , because someone reading at 10am their time can immediately reach out to help, rather than waiting until the next morning's standup.
* "Did we see this?" alignment; preserved through the written record.

**What we are gaining:**

* Bangalore engineers stop logging in at 9:30pm or skipping standup; the implicit time-zone penalty is gone.
* The 15-minute synchronous slot × 3 timezones × 6 engineers = 18 engineer-minutes per day is freed for deep work.
* The written record is searchable; someone joining the team in week 3 can see what the team has been doing.

**Measurable claim within one quarter:** "Mean focused-work hours per engineer per week" goes up by some percentage (the standup-replacement frees the most-protected hours of the day for half the team). Alternatively: "Mean time to unblock" goes down (blockers surface in writing where anyone can act on them, not just the person physically in the standup room).

**The general framing for the manager:** async-first is not "we don't talk." It is "we default to writing first; we meet synchronously when synchronous earns its keep." Most standups do not earn their keep against the async alternative on distributed teams. Run the experiment for one sprint; compare. If the team feels disconnected, address the connection problem directly (a weekly synchronous 1:1 cadence, or a monthly all-hands video call), not by restoring a daily standup.

**Q2.** Below are two PR descriptions for the same actual change (a refactor of the auth module's session-handling). Critique each: which sections are present, weak, or missing, and which description prepares the reviewer to do their work efficiently? Identify the _single most-improving_ edit for the weaker one.

**Description A** :
    
    
    ## Summary
    Refactored session handling.
    
    ## Test plan
    Tests pass.
    

**Description B** :
    
    
    ## What
    Extract session storage from `AuthService` into `SessionRepository`, behind an interface.
    
    ## Why
    The team needs to support Redis-backed sessions for the multi-tenant migration (TRELLIS-2419).
    Inlining the storage layer into AuthService was blocking that work because tests would need
    real Redis to verify auth flows. With the Repository in place, tests use the in-memory fake.
    
    ## How
    SessionRepository protocol + SqlSessionRepository (existing behavior) + InMemorySessionRepository (tests).
    AuthService takes the repo via constructor injection. No production behavior change.
    
    ## Testing
    40 existing auth tests pass unchanged. 8 new tests use InMemorySessionRepository directly.
    Manually verified login/logout flow on staging.
    
    ## Risk
    Behavior should be identical. The injection wiring changes how AuthService is constructed in
    the application; verified all 3 call sites updated.
    
    ## Reviewer note
    The Protocol design in src/auth/session_repo.py; first time we are using Protocol over ABC
    in this codebase. Sanity-check that pattern.
    

Hint

The six components from §16.2: What / Why / How / Testing / Risk / Reviewer note. Reviewers prepare differently when each section is filled in.

**Answer:**

**Description A** has only What (one weak line) and Testing (one useless line). Missing: Why, How, Risk, Reviewer note. The reviewer arriving at this PR has to _reconstruct_ all the context themselves; read the diff, infer the motivation, guess the risks, decide where to focus. This consumes the reviewer's most expensive minutes on work the author could have done in five.

**Description B** has all six components and they are substantive. The Why section is the strongest part: it links to the underlying motivation (multi-tenant migration), names the ticket, and explains the upstream problem the refactor unblocks. The reviewer arrives knowing _why_ this refactor exists and _why now_ ; the rest of the review is structural.

**The single most-improving edit for A** : add the _Why_. If A could only add one section, the Why is the highest leverage. A reviewer who knows the underlying motivation can evaluate the rest of the PR efficiently; they know what trade-offs to weigh, what risks to look for, what alternatives to consider. A reviewer without the Why is debugging the PR's existence first, the PR's correctness second; that is twice the work.

Why "Why" matters more than the others in practice: most PR descriptions in the wild have some What ("Refactored X"), often a thin Testing ("tests pass"), and rarely Why. The Why is the most-skipped section _and_ the highest-value one. The book's PR-description discipline (§16.2) is mostly a discipline of taking the Why seriously.

**Q3.** When does pair programming earn its keep, and when is it overhead?

Hint

§16.4's categorization.

**Answer:**

Earns its keep:

* Complex problems benefiting from two minds (subtle bugs, deep refactors, performance, security).
* Knowledge sharing where one engineer has expertise the other lacks.
* AI collaboration where the human and AI are working together.

Is overhead:

* Routine work that one engineer could do faster alone.
* Cases where both engineers have the same knowledge and the work is straightforward.
* Heads-down deep work where flow matters more than collaboration.

The discipline is to choose when to pair, not to mandate it universally. A team that pairs everything wastes engineering capacity; a team that never pairs misses the cases where pairing would have been faster.

**Q4.** What is different about AI pair-programming compared to human pair-programming?

Hint

§16.5 lists several differences.

**Answer:**

* **No ego** : feedback to the AI can be direct; the AI does not take it personally.
* **Limited context** : the AI's context window is finite; the human must hold the broader architectural picture.
* **No fatigue** : the AI does not tire; the cadence can be faster.
* **Characteristic failure modes** : the AI's bugs cluster in specific patterns (Thread C lies); a human partner's bugs are more varied.
* **Speed asymmetry** : the AI types fast; the human reads and evaluates. The bottleneck moves to the human's reading speed.
* **Drift over long sessions** : the AI may forget what was discussed earlier in the session; the human must reanchor.

Recognizing these differences is the discipline. Engineers who treat AI pair-programming as identical to human pair-programming miss the specific techniques that make AI pair-programming productive.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Draft a PR template for your team, including the components from §16.2. Adapt to your team's specific needs (industry, scale, codebase shape). Test it on the next three PRs you open.

Hint

A template is a forcing function; adapt to make it useful, not bureaucratic.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A reasonable template starts with §16.2's six components, possibly adding:

* **Linked artifacts** : ticket, ADR, design doc.
* **Backward compatibility** : any breaking changes; migration notes.
* **Performance notes** : any expected performance impact.
* **Documentation** : what docs were updated.

Adapt to drop sections the team finds unnecessary; the goal is the right shape for the team, not a comprehensive checklist.

**Q6.** Plan an async-first migration of your team's communication patterns. Identify three patterns currently synchronous that could move to async, and three patterns currently async that should stay or become synchronous. Justify each.

Hint

§16.3 and §16.7's conventions are the framework.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common moves:

Synchronous → async:

* Standup → async daily updates in a channel.
* Initial architecture discussion → ADR draft with async comments.
* Code feedback in meetings → PR comments.

Stay or move to synchronous:

* Incident response → real-time coordination.
* Onboarding 1:1s → human conversation.
* High-stakes design debates with many stakeholders → one focused meeting after async preparation.

The pattern is _defaults_ : where do we go first by default? The exceptions are explicit and justified. The team adopts the new pattern for one or two cycles, then retrospects.

**Q7.** Pair-program with an AI agent on a non-trivial task (say, refactoring a 200-line function). Record what worked, what did not, and what specific habits you would adopt for future sessions.

Hint

The habits are the durable output of the exercise.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings and habits:

* **Worked** : the AI accelerated the mechanical parts (extracting helpers, renaming, applying refactorings). The total time was perhaps half of the solo version.
* **Did not work** : the AI sometimes proposed changes that did not fit the codebase's conventions (style drift); the human had to catch these.
* **Habit** : anchor the session at the start with the codebase's conventions (cite CLAUDE.md, name the patterns to follow). The anchoring saves re-explanation later.
* **Habit** : break long sessions into chunks with explicit recaps. The AI's context drifts; the recap re-anchors.
* **Habit** : after every iteration, read the diff line by line. The AI's work passes tests; the diff is where Thread C lies hide.

The habits compound across sessions; an engineer with months of AI pair-programming practice is meaningfully more productive than one starting.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "In 2026, the most important communication skill for engineers is writing well, because async-first work depends on it." Counter-argue: "Verbal communication remains essential because some problems can only be solved synchronously."

**Discussion:** For writing: async-first work is the dominant mode; written communication is the durable artifact; engineers who write clearly produce work that others can build on without constant clarification. Writing skill compounds across the career.

For verbal: certain problems (incident response, high-stakes debates, nuanced mentoring) genuinely require synchronous bandwidth. Engineers who cannot communicate verbally are limited in these contexts. Also, the team's social fabric is built in synchronous time; fully async teams struggle to maintain trust and culture.

The synthesis: both skills are essential, but the _balance_ has shifted toward writing. An engineer who is a brilliant verbal communicator but cannot write clearly will struggle in 2026 async-first teams; an engineer who writes well and is adequately verbal will thrive. The advice for engineers investing in skills: writing is the higher-leverage investment for 2026, but do not neglect verbal entirely.

**Q9.** Map the relationship between Chapter 15 (code review) and this chapter (PR conventions, async, pair/mob). How do they compound?

**Discussion:**

* **Async-first review (Chapter 15) is enabled by PR conventions (this chapter)** : a PR with a strong description (§16.2), appropriate size (§16.1), and atomic commits (Chapter 3) is reviewable in async. A PR without these requires synchronous follow-up.
* **The Thread C checklist (Chapter 15) is faster to apply in async** : the reviewer can take their time, scroll through the diff, run the code locally. Synchronous review is rushed; async review is thorough.
* **AI review tools (Chapter 15) feed into async workflows** : the tool's comments are async by nature; the human reviewer reads them at their convenience; the author addresses them at theirs.
* **Pair-programming reduces async review needs** : code written together is reviewed together; the PR review afterward is faster because two engineers already have context.
* **Mob programming substitutes for some reviews** : a feature built in a mob is reviewed by the mob during construction; the after-PR review can be lighter.

The compounding: the team that has both good review discipline and good PR/async/pair discipline ships faster than either alone. The disciplines are complementary; weakness in one limits the other.

The combined picture (Chapters 15-16): in 2026, the team's ability to review well in async, with AI assistance, on well-shaped PRs, with appropriate use of pair and mob, is the central competitive advantage in shipping. The disciplines are the team's craft.

### Chapter Summary

The PR as unit of work (§16.1) is the unit at which the team reviews and merges. Lifecycle states, sizing discipline, and atomic-commit hygiene make the PR reviewable. AI-generated PRs should be sized and shaped like human PRs.

PR descriptions (§16.2) prepare the reviewer; the six components (what, why, how, testing, risk, reviewer note) are the forcing function. The "Why" is the most often weak and the highest-leverage to strengthen.

Async-first engineering (§16.3) is the default for engineering work. Throughput, time-zone reach, and durability favor written communication; synchronous time is reserved for cases where it earns its keep.

Pair programming (§16.4) earns its keep for complex problems, knowledge sharing, and AI collaboration; it is overhead for routine work. The discipline is to choose, not to mandate.

AI pair-programming (§16.5) is its own discipline, sharing some features with human pair-programming but requiring its own techniques. The roles, cadence, and failure modes differ; the engineer who learns the discipline produces meaningfully more.

Mob programming (§16.6) is the multi-engineer extension; rare but valuable for thorny problems and team-wide knowledge transfer.

The team's communication conventions (§16.7) make the question "where does this conversation belong?" a quick lookup. Without conventions, communication scatters; with them, the team uses each channel for its purpose.

The Currents migration example (§16.8) illustrates how async-first scales to multi-team work that would be unmanageable synchronously.

Chapter 17 opens Part VII (Documentation and Naming) with documentation that actually helps. With the team-level disciplines from Part VI in place, the team's durable artifacts (ADRs, READMEs, RFCs, runbooks) become the foundation for long-term codebase health. Without those disciplines, the documentation rots in sync with the code.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Write a PR template for your team that includes "Why" before "What."** The "Why" is where reviewer cognitive load drops most. Six components, one paragraph each maximum; reject empty sections at review time.
  2. **Default to async; meet only when async fails.** Replace standups with written daily updates in a channel. The team running twenty parallel async conversations ships more than the team blocked on the daily 15-minute round-robin.
  3. **Treat AI pair-programming as its own discipline, distinct from human pair-programming.** No ego (give direct feedback); limited context (hold the architecture); fast cadence (read every diff); characteristic failure modes (Thread C). The engineer who learns it produces meaningfully more than the one who treats it as either a teammate or a tool.

---

## Chapter 17: Documentation That Actually Helps

A new engineer joined Helix in early 2026 and asked Aki for the team's documentation. Aki sent three links: the README at the repository root, a folder of Architecture Decision Records (ADRs), and a runbook for the on-call rotation. The new engineer was productive within a week. A new engineer who had joined the previous quarter at Trellis received a much larger documentation set: a wiki with hundreds of pages, half outdated, a folder of design proposals nobody had read in two years, and an FAQ written in 2020. The Trellis engineer took a month to find the documentation they actually needed.

The difference was not the volume. Trellis had more documentation than Helix. The difference was the _discipline_ : which documents were maintained, which were trusted, which served real needs. Documentation that ages well is documentation written for specific durable purposes; documentation that rots is documentation written for momentary purposes without a maintenance plan.

This chapter is about the documents that actually help: README, ADRs, RFCs, runbooks. Each has a purpose; each ages differently; each requires its own discipline. Chapter 18 takes naming as a parallel topic; the two together (Part VII) are the documentation craft.

### 17.1 The four documentation types

Most useful engineering documentation falls into four categories. The categories differ in scope, audience, lifecycle, and update cadence.

#### Definition: The Four Documentation Types

* **README** : orientation for someone arriving at the repository. Covers what the project is, how to set it up, how to run it, where to find more.
* **ADR (Architecture Decision Record)** : a record of a specific architectural decision, its context, and its consequences. Written once; rarely updated; referenced when the decision is questioned later.
* **RFC (Request for Comments)** : a proposal for a significant change, written before the change is committed. The discussion happens in comments on the RFC; the result is either adoption (in which case the RFC becomes an ADR) or rejection (in which case it is archived with the rejection note).
* **Runbook** : operational documentation for production scenarios. Covers how to debug, escalate, mitigate, or recover from specific situations.

Other documentation types exist (API references, tutorials, glossaries, FAQs) and have their place; the four above are the engineering core. A team with these four well-maintained has the bulk of what they need.

#### What each looks like in practice

A typical Helix repository's documentation:

* `README.md`: one page. What the project is, three commands to run it, links to the key folders.
* `docs/adrs/`: numbered files. `001-pricing-rule-engine.md`, `002-tokio-vs-async-std.md`, `003-trunk-based-development.md`. Each is a few pages.
* `docs/rfcs/`: similar shape but for proposals; each is moved to `adrs/` (with adoption note) or `archive/` (with rejection note) within a few weeks.
* `docs/runbooks/`: operational documents. `on-call.md`, `database-failover.md`, `deploy-rollback.md`. Each is focused on a specific scenario.

The structure is consistent across the company; engineers moving between teams find the documentation in the expected places.

### 17.2 README

The README is the first document anyone reads. Its job is to _orient_ the reader in ten minutes or less.

#### What belongs in a README

* **What the project is** : one paragraph. Not the marketing copy; what the code does.
* **How to set up** : prerequisites, install commands, configuration. The minimum to get the code running locally.
* **How to run** : the most common command(s) to start the application or run the tests.
* **Where to find more** : links to the docs folder, the ADRs, the runbooks, the deployment docs.
* **Status indicators** : build status, test coverage, license, version.

#### What does not belong

* **API reference** : lives in `docs/api/` or autogenerated.
* **Tutorial content** : lives in `docs/tutorials/`.
* **Detailed architectural explanation** : lives in ADRs.
* **Contribution guidelines** : lives in `CONTRIBUTING.md`.

The discipline is to keep the README short. A README that is twenty pages is no longer a README; it is a manual. A reader skimming it does not orient; they get lost. Helix's READMEs are typically one page; the rest goes in dedicated files linked from the README. The principle to internalize: do not conflate _important_ with _first-read_. Important documentation can live anywhere; the README's job is the _first-read_ job. Everything else gets a link from the README, not the content itself.

### 17.3 ADRs

An ADR records a specific architectural decision. The format, originated by Michael Nygard in 2011, has become standard in 2026:
    
    
    # ADR-N: Decision title
    
    Status: Proposed | Accepted | Superseded by ADR-M
    
    ## Context
    What is the problem? What are the constraints? What forces are at play?
    
    ## Decision
    What we decided.
    
    ## Consequences
    What this decision implies. Positive, negative, neutral.
    
    ## Alternatives considered
    What else we considered and why we did not choose it.
    

#### What ADRs are for

The deepest purpose of an ADR is to capture the _reasoning_ behind a decision, so that future engineers (or future-you) can re-evaluate the decision when the context shifts. The decision itself is in the code; the reasoning is not.

Without ADRs, decisions are made and then forgotten. A future engineer encounters the decision in the code, does not know why it was made, and either accepts it without understanding or undoes it without realizing the consequences. With ADRs, the engineer reads the ADR, understands the context, and either keeps the decision because the context still holds or proposes a replacement (a new ADR superseding the old).

#### When to write an ADR

An ADR is justified when the decision is:

* **Architectural** : affects how the codebase is organized, not just a local implementation choice.
* **Cross-cutting** : affects multiple subsystems, not isolated to one file.
* **Reversible at non-trivial cost** : a decision that can be changed in five minutes does not need an ADR; a decision that requires three days of refactoring to change does.
* **Likely to be revisited** : the context may change; the team should be able to re-evaluate without re-doing the original analysis.

Decisions that do not warrant an ADR: choosing a variable name, choosing a small library, following an existing pattern. These are local; their context is the code.

#### Example ADR
    
    
    # ADR-003: Trunk-Based Development at Trellis
    
    Status: Accepted (2025-02-15)
    
    ## Context
    Trellis has used GitHub Flow for six years. Branches have grown longer-lived as the team has grown to 200 engineers; conflicts on long-lived branches are increasing; CI is slow on PR branches; release coordination is friction.
    
    We considered: continuing GitHub Flow, moving to Git Flow, moving to trunk-based development.
    
    ## Decision
    Adopt trunk-based development. All work merges into main directly via short-lived branches (< 2 days). Feature flags (LaunchDarkly) hide incomplete work.
    
    ## Consequences
    - Faster integration; smaller conflicts.
    - Requires fast CI (target: < 15 minutes for the full suite).
    - Requires feature flags for any work that cannot ship immediately.
    - Requires culture shift toward small PRs (target: < 400 lines).
    - Initial cost: 6 weeks of migration work.
    
    ## Alternatives considered
    - Continue GitHub Flow: rejected because the existing pain is increasing as the team grows.
    - Git Flow: rejected because it is heavier ceremony for a continuously-deployed SaaS product.
    

The ADR can be read in three minutes. It records the context, the decision, the consequences, and the alternatives. A future engineer reading it understands not just _what_ but _why_. The "why" is what survives.

#### ADR lifecycle

An ADR is _immutable_ once accepted. If the team wants to change the decision, they write a new ADR that supersedes the old one; the old one is marked "Superseded by ADR-N." The history of decisions is the history of the codebase's reasoning.

This discipline is important. Mutable ADRs would lose the reasoning of past decisions; immutable ADRs preserve it. Future engineers can see the evolution: ADR-003 made trunk-based the decision; ADR-027 in 2028 might supersede it for some reason; ADR-027 references ADR-003 and explains what changed.

### 17.4 RFCs

An RFC is the proposal stage of an ADR. The format is similar; the status is "Proposed" rather than "Accepted." The RFC goes out for comment; the team discusses; the decision is made.

#### When RFC versus straight ADR

An RFC is appropriate when:

* The decision is _uncertain_ and benefits from input.
* The decision affects multiple teams or stakeholders.
* The decision will likely be questioned later; capturing the discussion is valuable.

A straight ADR (without RFC stage) is appropriate when:

* The decision is _clear_ and the team has internal consensus.
* The decision affects only the writer's team.
* The discussion is straightforward.

In practice, many teams use the RFC stage for major decisions and skip it for minor ones. The threshold is judgment; Helix uses RFCs for decisions affecting more than one team; smaller decisions go straight to ADR.

#### RFC discussion format

The RFC document is the anchor; comments happen inline (in the document, in GitHub PR comments on the RFC, in the document's comment thread). The discussion is async (Chapter 16); the threshold for moving forward is "no unresolved blockers from the participants."

After discussion converges, the RFC is either:

* Accepted: status changes to Accepted, document moves to `adrs/`, the implementation begins.
* Modified and re-circulated: substantive changes happen, followed by a new round of comments.
* Rejected: status changes to Rejected with a rejection note; archived for future reference.

### 17.5 Runbooks

A runbook is operational documentation for a production scenario. The audience is the engineer on call at 2am.

#### What belongs in a runbook

* **Trigger** : how the situation is detected. What alarm fires; what the dashboard looks like.
* **Diagnosis steps** : how to determine the specific cause. What to check, in what order.
* **Mitigation steps** : how to stop the impact. Often distinct from fixing the root cause.
* **Escalation** : when and how to wake up someone else.
* **Recovery verification** : how to confirm the impact is resolved.

#### Why runbooks are critical

A runbook is consulted _under stress_ at _off hours_ by someone who _may not have written it_. Every assumption about the reader's state of mind, available context, and patience must be conservative. A runbook that requires the reader to know domain knowledge they do not have, or to follow many steps without errors, will fail when needed.

A useful 2026 runbook example:
    
    
    # Runbook: Database Connection Pool Exhaustion
    
    ## Trigger
    - Alert: `db_pool_exhausted` firing in PagerDuty.
    - Symptom: API errors rising; latency P99 > 5s; logs show "no available connections" errors.
    
    ## Diagnosis (5 minutes)
    1. Check Grafana dashboard `db-pool-health`: confirm `active_connections` is near `max_connections` (default 100).
    2. Check recent deploys via `kubectl get deployments`: anything within the last 30 minutes that could have increased connection use?
    3. Check the slow-query log via `psql -c "SELECT query, calls, mean_exec_time FROM pg_stat_statements ORDER BY mean_exec_time DESC LIMIT 10"`: any query suddenly slow?
    4. Check application logs for new error patterns.
    
    ## Mitigation
    - Immediate (under 1 minute): scale the API pods up by 50% via `kubectl scale deployment api --replicas=<new>`. Each pod has its own pool, so more pods = more total capacity.
    - If recent deploy suspected: rollback via `./scripts/rollback.sh <deploy-id>`.
    - If slow query suspected: kill the query via `psql -c "SELECT pg_terminate_backend(<pid>);"` for the offending pid (from `pg_stat_activity`).
    
    ## Escalation
    If mitigation does not stabilize within 15 minutes, page the DBA on-call (via PagerDuty schedule "db-ops").
    
    ## Recovery verification
    - `active_connections` drops below 70% of `max_connections`.
    - Latency P99 returns to baseline (< 500ms).
    - Error rate returns to baseline (< 0.1%).
    - Wait 15 minutes after metrics normalize before closing the incident.
    

The runbook is specific, actionable, and tested under fire. Engineers on call follow it without needing to reason from first principles.

#### Runbook maintenance

A runbook becomes stale fast. The infrastructure changes; the alert names change; the kubectl commands change. The discipline is to update runbooks as part of the changes that affect them:

* Every incident triggers a runbook review; if the runbook missed something, update it.
* Every infrastructure change touching a runbook's scenario updates the runbook.
* Quarterly: an on-call review of all runbooks; outdated entries are flagged and fixed.

Without maintenance, runbooks are worse than no runbooks; they mislead the on-call engineer into wrong actions. The discipline is to either maintain or delete.

### 17.6 Documentation that ages well versus rots

The fundamental distinction in documentation discipline:

#### Documentation that ages well

* **Decisions and their reasoning** (ADRs): the reasoning is durable; the context shifts predictably.
* **Operational procedures** (runbooks): maintained in sync with the operations.
* **Project orientation** (READMEs): updated when the project's basic shape changes.
* **API contracts** (autogenerated): autogenerated from code; stays in sync by construction.

#### Documentation that rots

* **Implementation details written as prose** : the code is the documentation of itself; prose duplicates and rots.
* **Tutorials that depend on specific environments** : the environments change; the tutorials break.
* **FAQs that nobody maintains** : the questions change; the answers stale.
* **Design proposals that were never adopted but never archived** : the codebase moves; the proposals become misleading.

The discipline is to write documents in the first category and to avoid documents in the second. Engineers writing prose documentation of implementation details are doing two jobs (write the code, write the prose); the prose rots; the rot is harmful. And the corollary: _delete_ the documentation that has gone stale, do not leave it as a historical artifact. A repository with one well-maintained README, ten well-maintained ADRs, and three well-maintained runbooks is better-documented than a repository with five hundred stale wiki pages. The stale pages actively mislead readers; the focused documentation actively helps. Volume is not the metric.

### 17.7 The AI-Layer: AI as first-draft engine

In 2026, AI agents can generate first drafts of ADRs, RFCs, runbooks, and READMEs. The drafts are usually structurally correct (they follow the format) but require human attention to be useful.

#### What AI does well

* **Structural compliance** : the AI produces documents in the right format. Sections, headings, lists are correct.
* **Synthesis from context** : the AI can read PRs, commits, and conversation logs and produce a coherent ADR-shaped narrative.
* **Boilerplate** : the AI handles the routine parts (status lines, conventional opening paragraphs, links) quickly.

#### What AI does not do well

* **Identifying the _interesting_ alternatives considered**: the AI lists obvious alternatives but may miss the one that was the real contender. Human knowledge of the team's history is needed.
* **Capturing _why_ not just _what_** : the AI describes what was decided but is shallow on the underlying motivation. The "Why" section needs human strengthening.
* **Recognizing when _not_ to document**: the AI happily writes documentation for trivial decisions that did not warrant an ADR. The human's judgment is needed.
* **Updating documents as context shifts** : the AI can update on request but does not proactively notice when an ADR has been superseded by recent changes.

#### The workflow

A reasonable 2026 ADR workflow:

  1. The engineer drafts the ADR's first sentence in each section, capturing the key points.
  2. The agent expands the draft into a full ADR following the template.
  3. The engineer reads, strengthens the "Why," adjusts the "Alternatives considered" to reflect what was actually debated, removes any AI-generated platitudes.
  4. The team reviews async; the ADR is accepted, modified, or rejected.

The AI's role is acceleration; the human's role is depth and judgment. The CLAUDE.md (Chapter 22) can specify documentation conventions to anchor the AI's output:
    
    
    ## Documentation conventions
    
    - ADRs follow the Nygard format in `docs/adrs/`. Numbered sequentially.
    - The "Why" section is the most important; explain the underlying motivation,
      not just the immediate trigger.
    - The "Alternatives considered" section names the real contenders and why
      they were rejected, not exhaustive but informative.
    - ADRs are immutable once accepted; new decisions supersede via new ADRs.
    - Runbooks follow the template in `docs/runbooks/_template.md`.
    

With the convention specified, the AI's first drafts are closer to the team's house style; the human's polish is less invasive.

### 17.8 A worked example: an ADR for the Drizzle vs Prisma choice

Aki's team at Helix considered replacing Drizzle with Prisma in early 2025. The decision was significant (it would touch hundreds of files), so an RFC was opened, discussed for two weeks, and ultimately rejected. The RFC was archived; the existing decision (use Drizzle) was reaffirmed in a new ADR.
    
    
    # ADR-018: Stay on Drizzle ORM (Reaffirming ADR-002)
    
    Status: Accepted (2025-03-10). Reaffirms ADR-002.
    
    ## Context
    ADR-002 chose Drizzle as the ORM in 2023. In Q1 2025, three engineers proposed
    migrating to Prisma (RFC-014). The arguments for Prisma:
    - Larger community; more StackOverflow answers.
    - Better TypeScript inference in nested queries.
    - More mature migration tooling.
    
    The arguments against migration:
    - 200 hours of estimated work to migrate ~400 query sites.
    - Drizzle's bundle size is smaller; matters for our Cloudflare Workers deployment.
    - Drizzle's SQL-like API has been working well for the team.
    - Prisma's runtime requires a separate engine binary; complicates our build pipeline.
    
    After two weeks of RFC discussion, the team consensus was that the migration
    cost exceeds the benefit. Drizzle's pain points are real but tolerable; Prisma's
    benefits would be welcome but not transformative.
    
    ## Decision
    Stay on Drizzle. The 200 engineering hours that would have gone to migration
    will instead address the specific Drizzle pain points (RFC-014 lists three
    concrete improvements to the Drizzle wrappers in `src/db/`).
    
    ## Consequences
    - No migration work; engineering capacity preserved for product work.
    - Drizzle's pain points addressed via targeted wrapper improvements (issue #1842).
    - If Drizzle is abandoned by its maintainers or Prisma's advantages grow
      significantly, re-evaluate (suggest target date: Q1 2026).
    
    ## Alternatives considered
    - Migrate to Prisma (RFC-014): rejected due to cost.
    - Migrate to a third option (Kysely, TypeORM, raw SQL): not seriously considered;
      the choice was Drizzle vs Prisma.
    - Maintain Drizzle wrappers as proposed in RFC-014: accepted as part of this ADR.
    

The ADR is two pages. It captures the question, the decision, the consequences, and the alternatives. A future engineer reading it (in 2027, say) knows what happened in 2025 and why. If Drizzle's situation changes, the re-evaluation has a starting point.

The investment to write the ADR was about three hours including the RFC discussion synthesis. The payoff is durable: this decision will not need to be re-discussed from scratch.

### 17.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Your team is at three stages of decisions, and you need to advise which artifact to write for each:

(a) The Platform and Data teams are debating whether to standardize on Kafka or RabbitMQ for the next 5 years. Six engineers across two teams have opinions; nothing is decided.

(b) The Platform team has decided (in a 60-minute meeting last week) to drop support for the legacy XML API by Q3. Some engineers were not in the meeting and will ask "why?" repeatedly over the coming months.

(c) An engineer writes a 12-line shell helper that fetches a customer's data for support tickets. They name the variables clearly.

For each, decide: ADR, RFC, code comment, or no document. Justify.

Hint

ADR = accepted decision with durable reasoning. RFC = proposal under discussion. Some decisions are too small for either.

**Answer:**

(a) **RFC.** This is a proposal still in discussion; the team has not converged. Write the RFC (Context: current state, what's broken, why now; Proposal: Kafka or RabbitMQ; Alternatives: each one's case); circulate; collect async comments; the result is either (i) acceptance and the RFC becomes an ADR with status "Accepted," or (ii) revision and re-circulation, or (iii) rejection with an archived note. Without the RFC, the discussion happens in chat threads that no one finds in 18 months when someone questions the choice.

(b) **ADR.** A decision has been made; the meeting minutes have already evaporated; future engineers will ask "why did we drop XML support?" — multiple times over the next year. Write ADR-N (Context: who used XML, what fraction of traffic, what the migration cost would be; Decision: drop by Q3; Consequences: short-term customer pain offset by long-term maintenance savings; Alternatives: keep + deprecate + migrate, each rejected with reason). The ADR is immutable once written; future questions get answered by linking to it.

(c) **No document.** A 12-line shell helper with clear variable names _is_ the documentation. An ADR would be pattern-tax for documentation: ceremonial layering with no real reader benefit. If the helper is non-obvious, a code comment suffices. ADRs are for cross-cutting decisions with downstream consequences, not for "I wrote a small helper" judgments.

The general rule: ADR when the decision is architectural, cross-cutting, and likely to be re-questioned. RFC when the decision is still under team debate. Code comment for small local decisions; nothing for the trivial. Choosing the right artifact saves the team from documenting trivia _and_ from leaving important decisions undocumented.

**Q2.** A new engineering manager joins your team in 2028 and reviews the ADR repository. They find ADR-003 from 2025 documenting the decision to use Drizzle ORM, and notice the team is now actually using Prisma. The manager opens a PR titled "Update ADR-003 to reflect current state" that edits ADR-003 to say "We chose Prisma over Drizzle because..." Walk through _exactly_ why this PR is wrong, even though the manager's instinct (keep docs current) is normally a good one. Propose the right action instead.

Hint

ADRs are immutable not for ceremony but because they record _the reasoning at the time of the decision_. Mutation destroys that record.

**Answer:** The manager's instinct is wrong in this specific case because ADRs are not "current state documentation"; they are _chronological decisions with reasoning_. Editing ADR-003 to say "we chose Prisma" would:

  1. **Erase the actual 2025 decision** : in 2025 the team chose Drizzle (with reasons). That decision was _correct for its context_ in 2025. Erasing it leaves no record that the choice was ever made.
  2. **Hide the transition** : a future engineer reading the modified ADR-003 would think "we have always used Prisma," which is false. The story of _why_ we moved from Drizzle to Prisma is the actually-interesting thing, and the edit destroys it.
  3. **Conflate "current" with "correct"** : the manager is treating ADRs like code (must stay current). But ADRs are like _minutes of a meeting_ — correct for the time they were written.

**The right action** : write a new ADR (e.g., ADR-127 in 2028) titled "Migrate from Drizzle to Prisma" with status "Accepted (2028-04-15). Supersedes ADR-003." The Context section explains _what changed_ between 2025 and 2028 that made Drizzle no longer the right choice (e.g., Drizzle's typing improvements stalled while Prisma matured; team frustration with X feature; new requirement Y); the Decision says migrate; Consequences list the migration cost. Mark ADR-003 with a one-line note: "Superseded by ADR-127." Do not edit ADR-003's body.

What a future reader gets from this discipline:

* A clear history: in 2025 we chose Drizzle for reason X; in 2028 we moved to Prisma because Y changed.
* The ability to learn from the trajectory: under what conditions does our team change ORMs? What were our successful and unsuccessful migrations?
* Immunity to "we have always done it this way" thinking: every decision has a context and an expiration date; the ADR chain shows both.

The general rule: ADRs are append-only. Currency is achieved by writing new ADRs that supersede old ones, not by editing old ones. Treat them like Git commits: once landed, immutable; the way forward is more commits, not amending history.

**Q3.** What kinds of documentation tend to rot, and why?

Hint

§17.6's distinctions.

**Answer:** Implementation-detail prose rots because the code is the documentation of itself; the prose duplicates and drifts. Tutorials rot because they depend on specific environments that change. FAQs rot because the questions evolve. Stale design proposals rot because the codebase moves past them.

The common thread: documentation that is closely coupled to _details that change_ will rot at the rate of those details. The fix is either to remove the documentation (delete the FAQ if no one maintains it) or to autogenerate it (API references from code).

Documentation that does not rot at the same rate: decisions (the reasoning is durable), operations (maintained in sync with operations), orientation (updated when shapes change), contracts (autogenerated). The discipline is to write the durable kinds and to delete the rotting kinds.

**Q4.** Why is a runbook's audience more demanding than other documentation's audience?

Hint

When is the runbook read?

**Answer:** The runbook is read under stress, at off hours, by an engineer who may not have written it and may not have all the context. The reader may be tired, half-asleep, or panicking. The runbook must work for this reader; documentation that requires a fresh, knowledgeable, patient reader will fail.

This is why runbooks must be: specific (not "investigate the cause" but "run this exact command"), actionable (not "consider whether to escalate" but "page DBA on-call after 15 minutes without recovery"), and tested (the team simulates incidents periodically to verify the runbook works).

Other documentation has a more forgiving audience: README readers are fresh and curious; ADR readers are deliberate and have time. Runbook readers have neither luxury.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Audit your codebase's documentation. For each of the four types (README, ADRs, RFCs, runbooks), report: do you have them? Are they maintained? What is the gap?

Hint

The gap is the action item.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* README exists but is stale; setup steps no longer work.
* A few ADRs exist for major decisions but most architectural decisions are undocumented.
* RFCs are not a regular practice; design proposals happen in chat and disappear.
* Runbooks exist for the most critical scenarios but not for all alerts.

The gaps suggest action items: update the README in the next PR that touches the repo; write ADRs for the most significant undocumented decisions (one per sprint); adopt the RFC practice for the next major design; expand the runbook coverage one alert at a time. The work is incremental; the goal is steady progress over six months.

**Q6.** Write an ADR for a decision your team has made in the last quarter. Use the Nygard format. Include the "Why," the "Alternatives considered," and the "Consequences."

Hint

The "Alternatives considered" section is often the most informative for future readers.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. The exercise produces value in two ways: (a) the ADR itself preserves the decision's reasoning; (b) the act of writing it surfaces parts of the decision that were not explicit. Engineers often discover, while writing the ADR, that the "Alternatives considered" section is hard to fill in because the real alternatives were not articulated at the time.

The lesson: writing the ADR retrospectively reveals gaps in the original decision process. The next time, those gaps can be addressed before the decision is made.

**Q7.** Write a runbook for a real production scenario in your codebase. Use the template from §17.5. Have a teammate review it; have them imagine they are on call at 2am and follow the runbook. Where does it break down?

Hint

The breakdowns are the runbook's bugs.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common breakdowns:

* The runbook assumes the reader knows what tool to use; specify the exact command.
* The runbook's diagnostic step requires context the on-call engineer may not have; provide enough context inline.
* The runbook's mitigation step has a side effect the runbook does not mention; add the warning.
* The runbook's escalation path is unclear; specify the exact person or schedule to page.

The review-and-iterate cycle is part of the runbook's discipline. A runbook that has not been reviewed by someone other than the author is untested; testing it under simulated stress reveals the gaps.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute: "In 2026 with AI agents, code documentation is becoming less important because agents can explain code on demand."

**Discussion:** For: an engineer who can ask an AI to explain a function gets an explanation in seconds; written documentation may not be needed for code-level explanation. The AI's explanations are tailored to the asker's questions; they are more efficient than reading documentation that may not address the asker's specific need.

Against: code-level explanations are not the same as design documentation. The AI can explain _what_ a function does; it cannot reliably explain _why_ the function was designed that way (the AI does not have the historical context). ADRs capture the why; they remain irreplaceable.

Furthermore, the AI's explanations depend on the AI's continued availability. If the model is deprecated, the explanations evaporate. The written documentation persists. For long-term codebases (5+ years), the written documentation is the durable substrate; the AI is the runtime convenience.

The synthesis: low-level code documentation (function-level prose) is increasingly redundant with AI explanations. High-level documentation (ADRs, RFCs, runbooks, READMEs) is _more_ important in 2026, not less, because the AI cannot reliably reproduce the historical reasoning. The shift is in _which_ documentation matters: away from the implementation-level prose, toward the decision-level reasoning.

**Q9.** Map the relationship between ADRs (this chapter) and the disciplines from previous chapters (testing, refactoring, patterns, architecture, review). How do ADRs support those disciplines?

**Discussion:**

* **Testing** : an ADR can record a team-wide testing convention (e.g., "we use property-based testing for all pure functions in the core"). The ADR is the durable explanation; new engineers learn the convention from the ADR.
* **Refactoring** : an ADR for a major refactor (Chapter 11) records the target architecture, the rationale, the timeline. The ADR is the artifact that survives the migration.
* **Patterns** : an ADR can record the team's pattern preferences (e.g., "we prefer dependency injection over Singleton; we prefer pattern matching over Visitor"). Future engineers reading the ADR understand why these conventions exist.
* **Architecture (Chapter 14)** : the hexagonal architecture choice is exactly the kind of decision that warrants an ADR. The ADR explains why; the code shows how.
* **Review (Chapter 15)** : when a reviewer says "this PR violates ADR-018," the team has a shared reference. Without ADRs, the reviewer's argument is "I prefer it this way," which is weaker.

The compounding: ADRs are the team's _memory_ of why the codebase is the way it is. Without that memory, every decision must be re-debated; with it, the team builds on past decisions rather than re-deriving them. The investment in ADR discipline pays back over years; a team without it spends extra time re-litigating settled questions.

### Chapter Summary

The four documentation types (§17.1): README (orientation), ADRs (decisions), RFCs (proposals), runbooks (operations). Each has its purpose, its lifecycle, and its discipline. A team with these four well-maintained has the documentation core; everything else is supplementary.

READMEs (§17.2) orient first-time readers in ten minutes. Keep them short; link to dedicated documents for details. The discipline is _first-read_ , not _comprehensive_.

ADRs (§17.3) record decisions with their reasoning. The Nygard format (Context, Decision, Consequences, Alternatives) is the standard. ADRs are immutable; superseding decisions are new ADRs.

RFCs (§17.4) are proposals before they become ADRs. Used for decisions that benefit from discussion across teams or stakeholders. The discussion is async (Chapter 16).

Runbooks (§17.5) are operational documentation read under stress. Must be specific, actionable, and tested. Maintained in sync with operations; stale runbooks are worse than no runbooks.

The aging distinction (§17.6): decisions, operations, orientation, and contracts age well; implementation-detail prose, tutorials, FAQs, and unadopted proposals rot. Write the durable kinds; delete the rotting kinds.

The AI-Layer (§17.7) is that AI generates structurally correct first drafts but is shallow on the "Why" and unreliable at recognizing what should not be documented. The workflow is AI for drafts, humans for depth and judgment.

Chapter 18 takes naming as the parallel topic: naming as the act that crystallizes design. With documentation (this chapter) and naming (next) together, Part VII is the documentation craft.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Write one ADR this week for a decision your team made in the last quarter.** Use the Nygard format (Context / Decision / Consequences / Alternatives). Even retrospectively. The exercise reveals gaps in the original decision process and gives the team a template for future decisions.
  2. **Test your team's most-critical runbook by simulating the incident.** At 2am, half-asleep, can a teammate follow the runbook to recovery? If they get stuck at any step, the runbook has a bug. Fix it before the real 2am call.
  3. **Delete one piece of stale documentation this sprint.** A README section that no longer applies, an FAQ no one maintains, a tutorial that requires a deprecated environment. Stale docs are worse than no docs; deletion is a contribution.

---

## Chapter 18: Naming as Design — The Most Underrated Engineering Skill

A Trellis billing module in 2024 had a class called `OrderManager`. The class had thirty methods. The methods did three roughly distinct things: validating order inputs, applying business rules to compute the final order shape, and persisting orders to the database. Engineers reading the code could not predict which method did which thing from the names; every navigation required reading the body.

In late 2024, the team held a renaming workshop. Asher led it. The team listed all thirty methods, classified each by what it actually did, and proposed better names. They also reconsidered the class itself: `OrderManager` was too generic. They split it into three classes: `OrderValidator`, `OrderRuleEngine`, `OrderRepository`. Each method got a name that described its specific behavior. No production code changed semantically; only names changed.

The next sprint, a new engineer joined the team and was asked to add a feature in the billing module. The new engineer was productive in three days. The previous new engineer (who had joined before the renaming) had taken three weeks. The difference, the team agreed in retrospect, was the naming. The names told the new engineer what the code did; the previous version required them to read the bodies.

This chapter is about naming as design. The argument is that naming is the most underrated engineering skill because its cost is invisible (paid distributed across all readers) and its leverage is enormous (every reader of every file pays the naming tax forever). The 2026 angle is that AI assistants often produce passable but generic names; the engineer's renaming work is high-leverage.

### 18.1 Naming as the act that crystallizes design

A name encodes a _concept_. The concept exists in the code regardless of the name; the name is the externalization of the concept for the reader. Good names match the concept the code expresses; bad names mismatch, leaving the reader to discover the concept by reading the body.

#### Definition: Naming as Design

Naming is the act of choosing words that crystallize the _concept_ a piece of code expresses. The name is not the code; the name is the _contract_ the code makes with future readers. A good name allows the reader to understand the code's purpose without reading the body; a bad name forces them to read.

In plain language: naming is design because the act of naming forces you to decide what the code _is_. If you cannot name it, you do not yet understand it.

#### The "if you cannot name it" test

A useful diagnostic: try to name what a function does in a five-word phrase. If the phrase comes easily, the function has a clear purpose; the name should match the phrase. If the phrase is hard or requires "and" or "or" to capture multiple things, the function may be doing too much; consider splitting it (Chapter 9's Extract Function).

Examples:

* `calculate_late_fee_for_invoice`: clear. The function does one thing.
* `process_order_and_send_email`: ambiguous. The function does two things; split it.
* `handle_request`: generic. The function's purpose is hidden; rename or split.
* `manage_inventory`: generic. The function's purpose is hidden.

The discipline is to _not accept_ the generic names. Every time you encounter a "Manager," "Handler," "Processor," "Engine" in code, ask: what does it actually do? If the answer is "many things," that is itself a signal.

### 18.2 The renaming workshop

The Trellis renaming workshop is a specific practice the team adopted. The format:

  1. **Identify a module or class with naming problems.** Common targets: classes called `XxxManager`, `XxxHandler`, methods with generic verbs.
  2. **List every name** in the target. Methods, classes, variables, parameters.
  3. **For each name** , ask: what does this actually do? Write the answer in 5-10 words.
  4. **Propose a new name** based on the answer. The new name should be specific, accurate, and follow the codebase's conventions.
  5. **Apply the renames.** With IDE support or AI assistance, the mechanical work is fast.
  6. **Run the tests.** Renaming should preserve behavior; tests should pass.
  7. **Commit per logical group** (Chapter 3 atomic commits).

The workshop is typically 1-2 hours for a substantial module. It is done by 2-3 engineers together (one driver, others as reviewers). The output is a refactor PR with many renames.

#### Why a workshop format

Naming is inherently subjective; different engineers will propose different names. The workshop format produces _team consensus_ on the names; the agreed names are then enforced by the renaming PR. Without the workshop, individual engineers rename in different directions, and the codebase becomes inconsistent.

The workshop also surfaces _latent design issues_. A class that resists being renamed (no one can agree on what it does) is a class that has a design problem; the workshop reveals it. The team can then choose to split, redesign, or accept the ambiguity with a documented reason.

#### A worked example: the OrderManager rename

The opening vignette in concrete detail. The original `OrderManager` had methods:
    
    
    OrderManager:
      validateAddress
      validateItems
      validateCustomer
      validatePayment
      computeSubtotal
      computeDiscount
      computeTax
      computeTotal
      applyRule
      enrichWithCustomerData
      serialize
      save
      update
      delete
      loadById
      findByCustomer
      notifyCustomer
      scheduleReminder
      archiveOld
      ... (and 10 more)
    

The team's classification:

* **Validation** methods (4): `validateAddress`, `validateItems`, `validateCustomer`, `validatePayment`.
* **Rule application** methods (5): `computeSubtotal`, `computeDiscount`, `computeTax`, `computeTotal`, `applyRule`.
* **Persistence** methods (6): `serialize`, `save`, `update`, `delete`, `loadById`, `findByCustomer`.
* **Other** (15): a mix of notification, scheduling, archiving, and odd helpers.

The renaming workshop's output:
    
    
    OrderValidator:
      validateAddress, validateItems, validateCustomer, validatePayment
      -> retained names (already specific)
    
    OrderRuleEngine:
      computeSubtotal, computeDiscount, computeTax, computeTotal
      applyRule -> applyDiscountRule (specific)
      enrichWithCustomerData -> applyCustomerSpecificDiscounts
    
    OrderRepository:
      serialize -> serializeForStorage
      save -> insert
      update -> updateExisting
      delete -> deleteById
      loadById -> findById
      findByCustomer -> findActiveByCustomer
    
    OrderNotificationService:
      notifyCustomer -> sendOrderConfirmation
      scheduleReminder -> schedulePaymentReminder
    
    OrderArchiver:
      archiveOld -> archiveOlderThan90Days
    
    (remaining helpers moved to appropriate locations or extracted)
    

The renames are more specific. The class splits surface the implicit design (validation, rules, persistence, notification, archiving are different responsibilities). The codebase becomes navigable: an engineer looking for "where do we apply discounts" goes to `OrderRuleEngine.applyDiscountRule`; an engineer looking for "where do we save orders" goes to `OrderRepository.insert` or `.updateExisting`.

The total mechanical work: about three days of refactoring distributed across two sprints. The total cognitive payoff: every future read, every future modification, every future debugging session benefits. The investment is recovered within weeks.

### 18.3 Domain language alignment

Naming benefits enormously from using the domain's vocabulary. If the business calls something a "renewal," the code should say "renewal" not "recurring_purchase." If the team's product team uses "tenant" to mean a customer organization, the code should use "tenant" not "customer" for that concept.

#### Why domain language matters

Two reasons:

* **Conversation alignment** : when an engineer discusses code with a product manager, sales person, or customer-support engineer, the names in the code should match the names everyone uses. Cross-team conversation requires shared vocabulary; the code is part of the conversation.
* **Reading comprehension** : domain language carries connotation; technical generics do not. "Renewal" implies recurring, time-bounded, possibly auto-extending; "recurring_purchase" implies less. The code that uses "renewal" reads with the domain's meaning baked in.

#### The Eric Evans school of domain-driven design
