Eric Evans's 1990s-2000s work on domain-driven design (DDD) emphasized aligning code structure with domain language as a primary design tool. The Ubiquitous Language concept: every concept in the domain has one name, used consistently by the code, the team, and the product documentation.

In 2026, full DDD is heavy ceremony for many teams, but the Ubiquitous Language principle remains broadly useful. The discipline is: when a domain concept is named, use that name everywhere; do not invent technical synonyms.

#### A worked example: tenant vs customer

Helix's product team used "tenant" to refer to a customer organization (since Helix's product is multi-tenant). The early code (written when the team was smaller and less product-aligned) used "customer" for the same concept. The mismatch caused confusion: when a product manager filed a ticket about "tenant onboarding," engineers had to translate to "customer onboarding" in the code; new product managers were similarly confused in reverse.

The 2025 rename: every code reference to "customer" (in the tenant sense) was renamed to "tenant." The rename touched hundreds of files; the IDE handled the mechanical work; the PR was reviewed for cases where "customer" had been correctly used (an individual customer, not a tenant organization). The result: the code and the product conversation aligned. Onboarding conversations got shorter; the cognitive overhead of translation disappeared.

The lesson: a naming mismatch with the domain is a debt that pays interest every conversation. The fix is incremental rename PRs over time; the payoff is faster, clearer cross-team work.

### 18.4 Common naming antipatterns

A small catalog of antipatterns that should be recognized and refused.

#### The Generic Noun

Names like `data`, `info`, `value`, `item`, `result`, `output`, `manager`, `handler`, `processor`, `engine`. These do not crystallize a concept; they accept whatever the code does without comment. Diagnostic: the name does not change the reader's expectation. Replace with a domain-specific term.

#### The Implementation Detail in the Name

Names like `OrderDataAccessObjectImpl`, `CustomerServiceImpl`, `BillingControllerImpl`. The `Impl` suffix is a Java-era artifact; in modern languages, the impl is just the class. The `DataAccessObject` is a generic technical term; "Repository" is more specific to the role.

#### The Type Encoded in the Name

Names like `customer_list`, `order_array`, `tenant_dict`. The type is already in the static type; the name should encode the _purpose_ , not the type. `active_customers` is better than `customer_list`; `orders_by_status` is better than `order_dict`.

#### The Negative-Sense Boolean

Variables like `is_not_disabled`, `is_not_invalid`, `is_not_empty`. Double negatives confuse the reader. Use the positive sense: `is_enabled`, `is_valid`, `has_items`.

#### The Verbose Acronym Expansion

Names like `RequestForCommentsDocument` when the team's vocabulary is "RFC." The verbose form is harder to read and write; the acronym is more familiar in context. Use the acronym when the team uses it.

#### The Hungarian Notation Survival

Names like `strFirstName`, `iAge`, `arrCustomers`. Hungarian notation (prefix indicating type) was a 1990s convention that has aged badly with modern type systems. Use the static type; do not encode it in the name.

#### The Misleading Name

A name that suggests one thing while the code does another. `validateOrder` that also modifies the order; `getActiveCustomers` that returns inactive ones too; `is_admin` that is sometimes True for non-admins. Misleading names are the worst antipattern because readers trust them. Fix immediately.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "names should be short." The mistake is conflating _short_ with _clear_. A short name that is unclear is worse than a longer name that is clear. `cstm_lkup` is short and unclear; `find_customer_by_id` is longer and clear. The discipline is _clarity first, brevity second_.

A second common misconception is "names should describe the implementation." The mistake is naming the _how_ rather than the _what_. A function named `sort_orders_by_total_using_quicksort` exposes the implementation; if you change to mergesort, the name is wrong. Name the _what_ (`sort_orders_by_total`); the implementation is the body.

### 18.5 The AI-Layer: redirecting LLMs from generic names

LLMs in 2026 produce passable but generic names. The pattern is consistent: variables named `data`, `result`, `temp`, `value`; classes named `Manager`, `Handler`, `Service`, `Processor`; methods named `process`, `handle`, `execute`, `run`.

#### Why this happens

The training corpus includes many tutorial codebases that use generic names because the tutorials are language-agnostic; the tutorial's `User` is just any user, not a specific domain concept. The LLM has seen `data` as a parameter name millions of times; it defaults to `data`. The LLM has no domain knowledge of _your_ codebase; it cannot know that your domain calls the concept "renewal" rather than "subscription."

The fix is twofold: explicit CLAUDE.md guidance (Chapter 22) and active renaming during code review.

#### The CLAUDE.md discipline

Specify the team's naming conventions and vocabulary:
    
    
    ## Naming conventions
    
    - Use domain terms: tenant (not customer), renewal (not recurring_purchase),
      permission_set (not roles).
    - Do not use generic suffixes: Manager, Handler, Processor, Engine, Service.
      Use specific role nouns: Validator, RuleEngine, Repository, Adapter.
    - Function names are verb phrases: calculate_late_fee, find_active_subscribers.
    - Predicate names start with is_ or has_ and are in the positive sense:
      is_enabled (not is_not_disabled), has_items (not is_not_empty).
    - Type encoding in names is forbidden: active_customers, not customer_list.
    

With the convention specified, the AI's defaults shift. The remaining failure modes are caught in code review (Chapter 15).

#### Renaming AI output

A common 2026 workflow: the AI proposes code with generic names; the human reviews and renames before merging. The renames are often:

* `processItem` → `validateLineItem`
* `data` → `customer_record`
* `OrderManager` → `OrderRuleEngine`
* `handle` → `applyRefundRule`

Each rename is a one-line change; collectively, they transform the code's readability. The AI does the mechanical writing; the human does the naming. This is the same human-judgment / AI-mechanics split we have seen throughout the book; naming is the part where human judgment matters most.

### 18.6 Naming variables, parameters, and constants

Beyond classes and methods, names matter for the smaller scale: variables, parameters, constants.

#### Variables

A variable name should describe what the value _represents_ , not how it was computed. `customer_total_after_discount` is better than `t2` or `final`. The lifetime of the variable affects how much investment the name deserves: a one-line local variable can be short (`i` for a loop index); a variable used across thirty lines should be specific (`unprocessed_invoices`).

#### Parameters

Parameter names appear in function signatures and call sites; they should be clear at both. A parameter named `customer` is fine; a parameter named `c` is too short for most cases. The exception: when the type is obvious (a constructor with a single parameter of an obvious type), short names are acceptable.

#### Constants

Constants get the most investment: they appear in many places and may be the only documentation of what the constant means. `LATE_FEE_RATE = 0.05` is much better than `RATE = 0.05` or just an inline `0.05`. The constant's name should be the concept it represents.

#### Common Misconception (revisited)

A common misconception is "variable names in short functions can be short because the function is small." The mistake is forgetting that the function may grow. A variable named `t` in a 5-line function is fine; the same variable is bad in a 50-line function. Since you do not know when the function will grow, default to clear names; the cost is small, and the future-self benefits.

### 18.7 The renaming as ongoing discipline

Naming is not a one-time activity; it is an ongoing discipline applied at every PR. The team that names well never finishes naming; they incrementally improve.

#### The Boy Scout naming rule

The Boy Scout Rule (Chapter 20 preview) says "leave the code a little better than you found it." Applied to naming: every PR that touches a function with a generic or misleading name is an opportunity to improve. The PR includes the rename as part of the substantive change; the reviewer notes the improvement.

Carmen at Currents has tracked this metric over a year: the percentage of methods named with the generic patterns (Manager, Handler, etc.) versus specific names. The metric improved from 22% generic to 8% generic over the year, without any dedicated renaming sprint; the Boy Scout discipline applied per PR was enough.

#### Renaming as part of code review

A reviewer who notices a generic name in a PR can suggest the rename. The author either accepts (and renames in the PR) or declines (and explains). Either is fine; the conversation is the value. Over many PRs, the team's vocabulary aligns; the codebase converges on better names.

The discipline is to _care_ about names enough to bring them up in review. A team that reviews many PRs without mentioning a single name is missing the leverage of naming-as-design.

### 18.8 A worked example: a Currents renaming workshop

Carmen ran a renaming workshop at Currents in early 2025 on a module named `DataIngestionService`. The class had eighteen methods and four nested classes, all with generic names.

The workshop's output:
    
    
    Original:
      DataIngestionService
        process_record
        handle_batch
        manage_stream
        execute_pipeline
        DataProcessor
        BatchHandler
        StreamManager
        PipelineExecutor
    
    Renamed:
      EventIngestionService (because Currents ingests events, not generic data)
        parse_event
        deduplicate_batch
        multiplex_stream
        apply_transformations
        EventParser
        BatchDeduplicator
        StreamMultiplexer
        TransformationApplier
    

Each rename made the code's purpose visible. The "process_record" method became "parse_event" because it parses incoming events into Currents's internal Event type. The "handle_batch" became "deduplicate_batch" because that is what it does: removes duplicate events. The "manage_stream" became "multiplex_stream" because the class actually multiplexes multiple input streams.

The new engineer who joined the next month was productive in the ingestion subsystem within a week. The previous new engineer (before the rename) had taken three weeks. The investment in renaming: one day of workshop plus three days of PR work. The payoff: every engineer who reads the ingestion code, forever, has a faster understanding.

### 18.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Your team's quarterly review is approaching and your engineering manager asks each engineer to nominate one "high-leverage" investment they want the team to make next quarter. A peer pitches "automate one more part of CI"; another pitches "speed up the test suite"; you want to pitch "spend two days renaming the 30 worst-named classes in our codebase." The manager pushes back: "That doesn't ship features. Why would I invest two engineer-days in this versus the other proposals?" Quantify, as best you can, the actual return on the renaming investment. What metrics could the manager measure to verify the return six months from now?

Hint

A name is read many more times than written. Cost: bad name × every reader × every read. Multiply.

**Answer:**

**Cost-benefit calculation:** the team is 6 engineers; the codebase will exist for ~3 more years; assume each badly-named class is read on average 10 times per year per engineer (some are read more, some less; the average is conservative). So one bad name across 6 engineers × 10 reads × 3 years = 180 reads of a misleading or generic name. If each read costs 15–30 seconds of mental translation ("what does `OrderManager` actually do?"), that is 45–90 minutes of cumulative team time per badly-named class. Multiply by 30 classes: **22.5 to 45 hours of team time saved** , against a **16-hour investment** (two engineer-days). Return: 1.4× to 2.8× in the same period as the investment, growing thereafter.

The peer's CI proposal probably has a higher _visibility_ return (CI is what management sees); the speed-up-tests proposal has a higher _daily-felt_ return (engineers feel slow tests). But the rename has a return that compounds _every time someone reads the codebase_ ; which is most of the engineering work. The investment is silent; the payoff is silent; that is why it gets de-prioritized despite being high-leverage.

**Metrics the manager could measure six months later:**

  1. **Time-to-productivity for new engineers** : new hires onboarded _after_ the rename will reach feature-shipping faster than new hires onboarded before. Track first-PR-merged date and first-substantive-PR-merged date. A 1-day-shorter onboarding for one new engineer per quarter pays for the rename within the first hire.

  2. **Frequency of "what does this do?" questions in team chat or PR review** : a baseline metric (count of questions matching that pattern per week, pre-rename) versus the same metric post-rename. Reduction in this pattern is direct evidence of reduced translation tax.

  3. **Time-on-task in code review for unfamiliar modules** : if reviewers spend less time understanding _what code is supposed to do_ and more time evaluating _whether it does it correctly_ , the rename is paying back. Some teams track this; others can sample.

The honest pitch: "This investment is invisible to outside observers and silent in its returns. But it compounds for every engineer who reads the codebase, every day, for the codebase's lifetime. The CI and test-speed investments are also worth doing; rank-order them however you want. But do not let the rename slip just because it is silent; silent is what makes it under-invested industry-wide."

**Q2.** What is the "if you cannot name it" test, and what does it diagnose?

Hint

§18.1 introduces it.

**Answer:** The test: try to name what a function does in a five-word phrase. If the phrase comes easily and is clear, the function has a coherent purpose; the name should match the phrase. If the phrase is hard, requires "and," or is generic, the function may be doing too much or be poorly defined.

The test diagnoses _unclear function purpose_. A function that resists being named is a function that does not have a clear purpose; either it does multiple things (split it via Chapter 9's Extract Function), or its purpose is implicit and needs to be made explicit (rename to capture the purpose).

The deeper insight: naming and design are the same activity. If you cannot name it, you do not understand it; if you do not understand it, the code is unclear; the unclarity propagates to every reader.

**Q3.** A customer-support engineer files a ticket: "Tenant X reports that their renewal didn't roll over. Can you check what happened?" You open the codebase and search for "tenant"; no matches; search for "renewal"; no matches. After some digging you find: tenants are called `customers` in the code (despite the product calling them tenants); renewals are called `recurring_purchases`. Walk through _exactly_ what the cost of this mismatch is, and propose a one-quarter migration plan that captures the _full_ domain-language fix without disrupting feature work.

Hint

Costs accumulate at every cross-team conversation. Mitigation is incremental, not big-bang.

**Answer:**

**The cost of the mismatch:**

  1. **Every cross-team conversation incurs a translation tax.** Support says "tenant"; you mentally translate to "customer"; you say "customer" back; support translates again. The translation is small (~10 seconds per turn) but happens _every interaction_. Across a support team of 8 people × 5 engineer-customer conversations per day × ~250 working days = 10,000 translations per year. Even at 10 seconds each, that is ~28 hours of translation tax annually.
  2. **Onboarding new engineers takes longer.** A new engineer reads product docs (which say "tenant" / "renewal"), then reads code (which says "customer" / "recurring_purchase"), and has to build a private mental glossary to translate. Adds ~half a day to their effective onboarding.
  3. **Search and grep fail.** As the scenario above shows, you searched for "tenant" and found nothing. Searches across the codebase return false negatives because the engineer searches in domain terms (correctly) but the code uses technical synonyms. Time lost: variable, often hours per investigation.
  4. **Cross-team documents become inconsistent.** PRs talk about "customer renewal logic"; the ticket says "tenant renewal"; the ADR uses one term; the runbook uses the other. The codebase fragments.

**One-quarter migration plan (without disrupting feature work):**

* **Week 1** : Audit the mismatch. Grep all uses of `customer` and `recurring_purchase`; count call sites; identify the ones that are truly cross-cutting (the entity types and their primary methods) versus incidental (a local variable here, a comment there).
* **Week 2** : Write an ADR (Chapter 17). Decision: codebase term shifts from `customer` to `tenant`, and from `recurring_purchase` to `renewal`. Note the migration approach (rename in place); state the deletion of the old terms.
* **Weeks 3–4** : Apply IDE rename to the two entity types and their primary methods. Run the test suite; fix any callers that don't use the rename tool (e.g., string-based references in tests). Land in 2–3 PRs (atomic commits). This is mostly mechanical work; the IDE does the heavy lifting; the agent (Chapter 9) can help across hundreds of files.
* **Weeks 5–8** : Boy-Scout the remaining incidental uses (local variables, comments, log strings) as you touch each file for other reasons. Do not open a sweep PR; let it land naturally with the team's existing PR cadence.
* **Weeks 9–12** : Update cross-team artifacts (PR template, runbooks, internal product docs) to use the same terms. Add the terms to CLAUDE.md so future AI sessions use the new vocabulary.

**Outcome at end of quarter** : codebase, product, and team all speak the same domain language. Every future conversation is shorter; every future search succeeds; every future onboarding is faster. The investment was ~2 engineer-weeks distributed across the quarter; the payoff compounds across the codebase's remaining lifetime.

**Q4.** What are three antipatterns from §18.4, and how would you correct each?

Hint

The catalog includes generic nouns, type encoding, double negatives.

**Answer:**

* **Generic noun** (`OrderManager`): correct by identifying what it actually does and renaming to a specific role (`OrderValidator`, `OrderRuleEngine`, etc.). Often involves splitting the class.
* **Type encoded in name** (`customer_list`): correct by removing the type encoding and using a purpose-describing name (`active_customers`, `unprocessed_customers`). The static type system already captures the type.
* **Double negative boolean** (`is_not_disabled`): correct by inverting to the positive sense (`is_enabled`). Double negatives slow the reader; the positive sense reads naturally.

The general rule: name the _purpose_ (in domain terms), not the _implementation_ (in technical generics) or the _type_ (already in the type system).

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Identify five generic names in your codebase (Manager, Handler, Processor, etc.). For each, propose a specific name based on what the code actually does.

Hint

Read the body; the specific name often emerges from the dominant behavior.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* `OrderManager` → `OrderValidator` (after splitting; the manager did several things).
* `RequestHandler` → `LoginRequestHandler` (specific to login flow; other handlers had their own names).
* `DataProcessor` → `CsvImportProcessor` (the data was CSV; the processing was import).
* `Service` (just "Service") → `EmailNotificationService` (the service sent email notifications).
* `Engine` → `PricingRuleEngine` (the engine applied pricing rules).

Each rename is a small PR; collectively, they transform the codebase's navigability.

**Q6.** Hold a renaming workshop with your team on a module with naming problems. Document the renames, the discussion, and the outcome. What did the workshop reveal beyond the renames themselves?

Hint

Workshops often surface design issues; note them.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings beyond renames:

* The workshop reveals that the class does multiple things; the workshop concludes with a class split.
* The workshop reveals disagreement about what a concept _is_ ; the disagreement is the design issue. Resolving it (with input from product or domain experts) is the real value.
* The workshop reveals inconsistencies in the team's vocabulary (different engineers use different terms for the same concept). The workshop produces team consensus.
* The workshop reveals dead code that no one can name; the dead code is removed.

The renames themselves are the visible output; the design alignment and dead-code removal are the often-larger value.

**Q7.** Take a piece of AI-generated code in your codebase. Audit the names. How many are generic? How many are specific? Update CLAUDE.md to reduce the recurrence in future AI generations.

Hint

The CLAUDE.md update is the durable fix.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings: 30-60% of AI-generated names are generic (data, result, item, manager, handler, processor); the CLAUDE.md update specifies the team's naming conventions and forbidden generics.

After the update, the next batch of AI-generated code has noticeably more specific names. The remaining generics are caught in code review; over time, the rate drops.

The lesson: AI defaults can be shifted by explicit guidance. The team that does not write the guidance lives with the AI's defaults; the team that does shifts the defaults toward their own conventions.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "In 2026 with AI generating code, naming becomes more important because the AI's defaults are generic and the human must compensate." Counter-argue: "Naming becomes less important because the AI explains code on demand, regardless of the names."

**Discussion:** For naming-more-important: the AI defaults to generic names; the volume of generic names in a codebase compounds; the human's renaming work is high-leverage and irreplaceable. The codebase that uses the AI's defaults uncritically becomes harder to maintain over time; the codebase that renames as part of review compounds in clarity.

For naming-less-important: an engineer can ask the AI to explain code regardless of names; the AI can rename on demand; the human's investment in naming may be less leveraged than before. Furthermore, if the AI's explanations are good enough, the names need not be the primary documentation; the names are convenience, the AI is the substantive doc.

The synthesis: the AI helps with code-level understanding but does not eliminate the need for good names. Names are still the _first line of documentation_ ; they are what readers see at the speed of skimming, while AI explanations require asking. Names are still the _durable substrate_ ; AI models will be deprecated, and the names will outlast them. Naming becomes _more_ important because the AI multiplies code volume and the AI's defaults are weak; the human's compensating work is the differentiator.

The 2026-honest position: invest in naming discipline more, not less. The AI is a tool; the names are the codebase's identity.

**Q9.** Map the relationship between naming (this chapter) and the other compounding disciplines named in Chapter 1. How does naming support each?

**Discussion:**

* **Reading old code (Chapter 2)** : well-named code is faster to read. Reading time drops by perhaps 30-50% in a well-named codebase compared to a poorly-named one. The reading skill itself is multiplied by naming discipline.
* **Testing (Chapters 5-8)** : well-named functions are easier to test because the test name can match the function name (`test_calculate_late_fee_for_30_days`). The clarity of names propagates to the clarity of tests.
* **Refactoring (Chapters 9-11)** : well-named code is easier to refactor because the engineer can see what each function does without reading it. Refactoring under test is faster when the names guide the eye to the right places.
* **Code review (Chapter 15)** : well-named code is easier to review because the reviewer can scan the structure quickly. Time-to-understand drops; review SLAs are easier to meet.
* **ADRs (Chapter 17)** : well-named concepts make ADRs clearer; the ADR can reference the concept by name and the reader knows what is meant.
* **AI-collaboration (Chapter 22)** : well-named codebase guides the AI to better outputs; the AI imitates the names it sees. A codebase with consistent good names produces AI output with good names; a codebase with generic names produces AI output with generic names. The naming compounds the AI's effectiveness.

The general lesson: naming is a foundation skill that compounds every other discipline. Investing in naming pays back across all the disciplines simultaneously; neglecting it limits the leverage of every other skill.

### Chapter Summary

Naming is the act that crystallizes design (§18.1). The "if you cannot name it" test diagnoses unclear function purpose; the name is the contract the code makes with readers.

The renaming workshop (§18.2) is a team practice that produces consensus on names and often surfaces latent design issues. The Trellis OrderManager example illustrates the substantial productivity gain.

Domain language alignment (§18.3) makes the code a participant in cross-team conversation. The Ubiquitous Language principle from DDD is broadly applicable: use the domain's vocabulary; do not invent technical synonyms.

Antipatterns (§18.4): generic nouns, implementation details in names, type encoding, double negatives, verbose acronyms, Hungarian notation, misleading names. Each has a correction; recognizing them is the start.

The AI-Layer (§18.5): LLMs produce generic names by default; CLAUDE.md guidance and active renaming during review shift the defaults. The naming work remains the human's most distinctive contribution.

Naming at smaller scales (§18.6): variables, parameters, constants. The longer the lifetime, the more investment in clarity.

Renaming as ongoing discipline (§18.7): every PR is an opportunity. The Boy Scout naming rule produces gradual improvement without dedicated sprints.

Chapter 19 opens Part VIII (Technical Debt and Codebase Sustainability) with technical debt as a real concept. The naming, documentation, testing, and refactoring disciplines from Parts III-VII are the _prevention_ of debt; Chapter 19 onwards is about understanding and managing the debt that accumulates despite the prevention.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Apply the five-word phrase test to one of your team's generic-named classes this week.** `OrderManager` / `RequestHandler` / `DataProcessor`; what does it actually do, in five words? Use the answer as the new name. The rename is a 10-minute PR; the readability gain compounds across every future read.
  2. **Schedule a 60-minute renaming workshop with two teammates.** Pick a module nobody loves. List every name. For each: what does it actually do? Propose better. The output is one rename-only PR; the durable output is shared vocabulary the team carries into all future PRs.
  3. **Run a CLAUDE.md naming-conventions update after the next AI PR with generic names.** When the agent ships `data` / `result` / `Manager`, update the file to specify your domain's vocabulary. The next session's defaults shift; review burden drops within a week.

---

## Chapter 19: Technical Debt — A Real Concept, Not a Slogan

Aki at Helix tracked an interesting metric across 2025: the time her team's engineers spent on "feature work" versus "incidental work." Feature work was building new things the product needed; incidental work was fixing flaky tests, untangling deployment issues, debugging emergent behavior, working around legacy decisions. In Q1 2025, the split was 60/40 feature/incidental. By Q4 2025, the split was 40/60. The team's productive output had dropped without anyone working less hard.

The reason was technical debt. Helix had grown fast, made expedient decisions, and let some of them rot. The debt was now consuming engineering capacity. Aki ran a debt-quantification analysis (the techniques in §19.3) and produced a report: roughly one engineer-year of total team time per year was being consumed by debt. The team made a deliberate plan to address it; over Q1 2026, the incidental work dropped back to 35%; the team shipped more features in Q1 2026 than in Q4 2025 despite having the same headcount.

This chapter is about technical debt: what it actually is (Ward Cunningham's original framing, not the colloquial slogan), how to quantify it, and the new debt category that AI-generated code creates. The previous chapters were about preventing debt; this one is about understanding and managing the debt that accumulates anyway.

### 19.1 Cunningham's original framing

Ward Cunningham coined "technical debt" in 1992 in a specific, narrow sense. The framing has been widely flattened over the years; the original is more useful.

#### Definition: Technical Debt (Cunningham)

Technical debt is a _deliberate_ trade-off: an engineer chooses to ship code that does not yet fully match their understanding of the domain, with the explicit intention to refactor as understanding improves. The debt is the gap between current code and what the engineer now knows is correct. The interest payment is the friction of maintaining the gap.

In plain language: debt is when you knew better and shipped anyway, with a plan to fix later. The debt is the gap; the interest is the cost of the gap.

This is _not_ the colloquial sense of "bad code." Bad code written without understanding is not debt; it is just bad code. Debt requires the _deliberate_ trade-off and the _intention to refactor_. Cunningham's metaphor extends financial debt: you borrow knowingly to make a payment now, with a plan to pay back later.

#### Why the original framing matters

The flattened version ("debt is bad code") conflates two different things:

* **Deliberate debt** : a known trade-off, with a known cost, with a plan to fix. This is debt in Cunningham's sense. It is sometimes the right choice.
* **Inadvertent debt** : bad code from inexperience, missed deadlines, or shifting requirements. This is not debt; it is just unfinished work or technical mistakes.

The two categories require different responses:

* Deliberate debt is managed by the plan to fix. The team knows the debt exists; they monitor the interest; they pay back when the cost-benefit favors it.
* Inadvertent debt is addressed by improving the underlying causes (training, processes, deadlines, understanding). Refactoring the code is necessary but not sufficient.

Treating both as "debt" leads to muddled thinking. The team that calls everything debt cannot prioritize what to fix; the team that distinguishes can.

### 19.2 The four-quadrants model

Martin Fowler extended Cunningham's framing in 2009 with a four-quadrants model, splitting debt by _intentionality_ and _prudence_.

#### Definition: The Four Quadrants of Technical Debt

* **Deliberate-Prudent** : "We must ship now and deal with the consequences." The team knows the trade-off and accepts it for good reasons.
* **Deliberate-Reckless** : "We do not have time for design." The team knows better but skips the work anyway.
* **Inadvertent-Prudent** : "Now we know how we should have done it." The team did their best at the time; in hindsight, a different approach would have been better.
* **Inadvertent-Reckless** : "What's layering?" The team did not know better; the resulting code has problems that experienced engineers would have anticipated.

#### Why the quadrants matter

The quadrants prescribe different responses:

* **Deliberate-Prudent debt** : schedule the payback. The team made the right call; now they execute the plan.
* **Deliberate-Reckless debt** : change the team's culture. The team is choosing to skip design when they shouldn't; the fix is upstream of any specific code.
* **Inadvertent-Prudent debt** : refactor as you learn. The team's understanding has improved; the code can be improved with it.
* **Inadvertent-Reckless debt** : invest in the team's skill. The team needs to learn what they did not know; mentoring, training, or hiring is the answer.

Each quadrant has a different remediation. Pretending all debt is one thing leads to wrong responses; classifying the debt leads to right ones.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "all debt should be paid back." The mistake is treating debt as universally bad. Cunningham's framing explicitly includes _prudent_ debt: trade-offs that were the right call at the time and remain the right call. The team's job is not to eliminate all debt but to _manage_ it: pay back the costly debt, accept the cheap debt, prevent the reckless debt.

A second common misconception is "debt is just bad code." Cunningham's framing distinguishes deliberate trade-off from accidental mess. The two require different responses. Conflating them leads to either over-prescription (try to eliminate all imperfection) or under-prescription (accept all messes as just-how-things-are).

### 19.3 Quantifying technical debt

A standard objection to debt-talk is that it is impressionistic. "We have a lot of debt" is not actionable; it does not tell the team what to fix or how to measure progress. Quantification makes the conversation concrete.

#### Three quantitative signals

Three metrics that track debt cost without requiring agreement on what debt is. These are the same metrics the DORA team (Nicole Forsgren and colleagues) validated across thousands of teams in the _Accelerate_ research program and the annual _State of DevOps Report_ (2014 onward; the 2024 edition specifically called out AI's effect on each). DORA frames them as performance metrics; we use them here as debt diagnostics, but the metrics are the same:

* **Lead time per change** : how long does it take, from idea to production, to ship a typical change in this module? A module with debt has high lead time because every change is harder than it should be.
* **Defect rate** : how many bugs per month surface from this module? A module with debt has high defect rates because the structure obscures bugs.
* **On-call burden** : how many on-call incidents originate in this module per month? A module with debt is fragile in production. (DORA calls a related metric "Mean Time to Restore"; the on-call rate is the input that drives it.)

The three metrics together form a debt signature. A module with low scores on all three is healthy (or under-used). A module with high scores on all three is debt-laden and a payback candidate. A module with mixed signals (high lead time but low defects, say) is interesting and worth investigating.

#### The Helix Q1 2025 analysis (illustrative)

The numbers in this and the §19.6 follow-up tables are from a composite fictional scenario, not a published industry study; their purpose is to show what the _shape_ of a debt-quantification report looks like when a team takes the cycle seriously. Real teams will see their own distributions; the pattern (one outlier module dragging the rest) is the durable lesson.

Aki's analysis at Helix tracked these metrics across six modules:

Module | Lead time (median days) | Defects per month | On-call incidents per month  
---|---|---|---  
billing | 12 | 8 | 5  
pricing | 4 | 2 | 1  
ingest | 8 | 4 | 3  
auth | 3 | 1 | 0  
ui | 6 | 5 | 0  
reporting | 9 | 2 | 1  
  
The billing module was the clear outlier: high on all three. Aki's plan: address billing first; expected payback within two quarters; expected impact on team capacity is significant.

The investigation of billing revealed:

* Deliberate-prudent debt from 2023 (a known shortcut that was right then, and is still right; not the issue here).
* Inadvertent-prudent debt from 2024 (the team had learned more about the domain since; the code did not reflect the new understanding).
* Inadvertent-reckless debt from late 2024 (a few engineers had pushed code that experienced reviewers would have caught; the team's review discipline had been weak that quarter).
* Deliberate-reckless debt from Q1 2025 (the team had been skipping design under pressure; recent code reflected the skipping).

Each quadrant got a different response. The inadvertent-prudent debt was addressed by refactoring. The inadvertent-reckless debt was addressed by re-engaging the review discipline. The deliberate-reckless debt was addressed by team conversation about design pressure (the deadlines that had caused the skipping were less urgent than they had seemed). The deliberate-prudent debt was left alone.

#### Common Misconception (revisited)

A common misconception is "we cannot quantify debt." The mistake is conflating _imprecise_ with _not measurable_. Lead time, defect rate, and on-call burden are all measurable; the team that does not measure them is choosing not to. The measurements are imperfect; they are still vastly better than the impression-based alternative.

A second common misconception is "the metrics will be gamed." The mistake assumes the metrics are used punitively. If lead time is the team's evaluation metric, engineers will cut corners to ship faster, gaming the metric. If lead time is the team's _diagnostic_ metric, used to identify debt-laden modules for investment, no one games it. The framing matters: metrics-as-diagnostic are useful; metrics-as-evaluation are dangerous.

### 19.4 The AI-Layer: unreviewed plausibility as a new debt category

The 2026-specific debt category that did not exist before AI assistants: _unreviewed plausibility_.

#### Definition: Unreviewed Plausibility

Unreviewed plausibility is code that appears correct, passes tests, and ships, but was not understood deeply enough by any human reviewer to confirm correctness. The code may genuinely be correct; the team has no way to know. Future maintainers will treat it as authoritative because it has been in production; the apparent stability is not the same as verified correctness.

In plain language: AI generates code; the team approves it because the tests pass; nobody really understands the code; the code is debt because the team is now dependent on something they did not validate.

#### Why this is debt

The cost is paid when something goes wrong. A bug surfaces in the code; the engineer investigating it has no mental model (the code was generated, never deeply understood, and the original author may not remember why it was structured that way); investigation takes much longer than it would for human-authored code with the same complexity.

The cost is also paid when the code needs to change. An engineer who needs to extend or modify the AI-generated code starts from scratch in understanding it; the implicit assumptions in the code are not documented; the changes risk breaking what the original tests did not cover.

#### How to limit the debt

* **Review discipline** : apply the Thread C checklist (Chapter 15) rigorously to AI-generated code. The reviewer's understanding becomes the team's understanding.
* **ADRs for non-obvious AI decisions** : when the AI makes a decision (which library, which structure, which trade-off), the human writes an ADR (Chapter 17) capturing the reasoning. The ADR survives the model deprecation.
* **Test the human's understanding, not just the AI's output** : a review that ends with "the tests pass" without the reviewer understanding why they pass is incomplete. The reviewer should be able to predict what would break if specific lines were changed; if they cannot, they have not understood.
* **Mutation testing on AI-generated code** (Chapter 6): mutation testing reveals whether the AI's tests genuinely test or merely cover. Mutation testing is the quantitative check; the human's review is the qualitative check.

#### The debt accumulates fastest where review is weakest

A pattern observed across teams in 2025-2026: the modules with the highest unreviewed-plausibility debt are the ones where the team's review discipline is weakest. The AI generates code at speed; if review keeps up, the debt is bounded; if review does not keep up, the debt accumulates per-PR and compounds.

The fix is upstream of any specific module: invest in review discipline (Chapter 15), keep PR sizes manageable (Chapter 16), maintain CLAUDE.md (Chapter 22). With these in place, AI-generated code is reviewed at the depth that prevents the debt; without these, debt accrues faster than ever before.

### 19.5 The strategic debt-management cycle

A team that takes debt seriously has a cycle:

  1. **Measure** : track lead time, defect rate, on-call burden per module. Quarterly or continuously.
  2. **Identify** : find the modules where the metrics are degrading or are highest. These are the debt-laden modules.
  3. **Investigate** : classify the debt by the four-quadrants model. Determine which quadrant dominates.
  4. **Plan** : schedule remediation appropriate to the quadrant. Refactoring for inadvertent-prudent; review discipline for unreviewed-plausibility; team conversation for deliberate-reckless; training for inadvertent-reckless.
  5. **Execute** : do the remediation work. Track progress.
  6. **Re-measure** : confirm the metrics improved. If not, the remediation was wrong or insufficient.

The cycle repeats. Teams that cycle quarterly tend to keep debt under control; teams that wait until debt is overwhelming spend much more effort to recover.

#### When to pay back debt

Three signals suggest debt should be paid back now:

* **The interest is consuming engineering capacity.** When the team's incidental-work percentage exceeds 40-50%, debt is the dominant constraint on velocity. Pay back the biggest sources.
* **The debt is blocking a needed change.** When a feature cannot be built because the underlying code makes it impractical, the debt must be addressed before the feature.
* **The debt has known compounding cost.** When a debt is known to grow worse over time (a deprecated dependency that will be unsupported, a security issue that will eventually be exploited), paying back early is cheaper than paying back later.

#### When to leave debt alone

* **The debt is in a rarely-touched module.** Refactoring code that no one touches has poor return on investment.
* **The debt is deliberate-prudent and the trade-off still holds.** The team made the right call; the call is still right.
* **The remediation cost exceeds the interest cost.** Some debt is cheaper to live with than to pay back.

The discipline is to _choose_. Refactoring everything is wasted effort; refactoring nothing accumulates problems. The cycle is the choice mechanism.

### 19.6 Working example: the Helix billing refactor decision

Aki's Q1 2025 debt analysis identified billing as the debt hotspot. Before starting on remediation, she wrote a single failing test that encoded what success would look like: a billing-module change of typical size, opened on a Monday, merged before Friday with no rollback. Not a unit test of code, but an executable expectation about the team's behavior. She would not consider the remediation done until that test (run against the team's PR history) passed. This is the same discipline she applies to agent sessions; _write the test that defines success first_ ; extended to organizational work.

The remediation:

* **Q1 2025** : deeper analysis. Confirmed the four quadrants. Identified the specific code areas that contributed most to the metrics.
* **Q2 2025** : review-discipline reset. The team committed to applying the Thread C checklist to billing-related PRs; PR sizes were enforced; AI-generated code was reviewed at depth. This addressed the unreviewed-plausibility component.
* **Q2-Q3 2025** : a big refactor (Chapter 11). The parallel-paths strategy migrated billing from the legacy structure to a cleaner one. This addressed the inadvertent-prudent debt.
* **Q3 2025** : training for the engineers who had produced the inadvertent-reckless debt. Mentoring on the patterns they had missed; pair-programming with senior engineers.
* **Q4 2025** : team conversation about deadline pressure. The product team and engineering team agreed on a slightly slower cadence in exchange for less deliberate-reckless work.

By Q4 2025, the billing metrics had improved:

Metric | Q1 2025 | Q4 2025  
---|---|---  
Lead time (days) | 12 | 4  
Defects/month | 8 | 2  
On-call/month | 5 | 1  
  
In this illustrative scenario, the investment is roughly two engineer-quarters distributed across the year, and the payoff is a noticeable expansion of the team's effective capacity as the worst hotspot stops consuming it. The specific percentages are not the point; the _direction_ (the metrics move together, in the same direction, on a quarter timescale) is. Real teams seeing their own metrics move this way after similar interventions can read this as confirmation; teams seeing nothing move should investigate whether the remediation matched the debt quadrant.

The lesson: debt remediation is a multi-front program, not a single refactor. Code refactoring addresses inadvertent-prudent debt; review discipline addresses unreviewed-plausibility; team conversation addresses deliberate-reckless; training addresses inadvertent-reckless. A program that does only one of these and ignores the others will leave debt sources active and the metrics will not improve.

### 19.7 The Trellis Asher case: managing debt in an 8-year codebase

Asher's role at Trellis is in part a debt-management role. The 8-year codebase has accumulated debt of all four kinds; Asher's job (and the staff engineering function generally) is to keep the debt under control without halting feature work.

Asher's pattern, observed across 2025:

* **Continuous monitoring** : Asher checks the team's metrics monthly. Modules trending worse get attention before they become emergencies.
* **Targeted refactors** : every quarter, Asher leads one focused refactor on a debt-laden module. The refactors are small enough to fit in a quarter (Chapter 11 sized).
* **Review of design proposals** : Asher reviews ADRs and RFCs to catch deliberate-reckless trade-offs before they become code.
* **Mentoring** : Asher pairs with newer engineers on the modules he understands; the pairing transfers knowledge and prevents inadvertent-reckless debt. The first thing he teaches in any pairing session is the question he asks of every surprising line: _what was the author worried about?_ New engineers initially treat surprising code as wrong; Asher's framing turns the surprise into a question.
* **Acceptance of some debt** : Asher does not try to fix everything. Some debt is left alone because the cost of remediation exceeds the cost of living with it.

The discipline is _ongoing_ , not episodic. Trellis has not had a "big debt-cleanup sprint" in years; the debt is managed continuously. The codebase remains workable despite its age because the management is constant.

* * *

**Thread B;`calculateRefund`, stage 3: after first bug**

Eight months after the first review, a production incident arrived. A support ticket described "double-charged refunds": some orders showed a refund processed, with the order still marked as `PARTIAL` rather than `FULL`, causing the accounting system to believe further refunds were pending. Two customers had received incorrect confirmation emails as a result.

The post-mortem traced the bug to a status-determination logic error. The function computed:
    
    
    status=RefundStatus.FULL if remaining_after == 0 else RefundStatus.PARTIAL
    

But `remaining_after` was computed using `order.total_refunded` from the database at request time. For orders with a prior partial refund, the current request's amount could be exactly the remaining balance, but if the database read and the refund write were not atomic, a race condition left `total_refunded` stale. The status check passed; the accounting write used the stale value; the order stayed `PARTIAL`.

The four-quadrants classification: **inadvertent-prudent**. The original author knew refund status was important (they had added the status field deliberately), but did not anticipate the concurrency edge case, which required production-scale load that staging never saw to manifest. Not reckless. Not deliberate. Inadvertent and, in hindsight, avoidable with a row-level lock or an optimistic-concurrency check.

The remediation: a database-level constraint that prevented a `PARTIAL` status when `total_refunded + amount == total`, plus a test that simulated the concurrent request in the test suite. The fix was four lines; the investigation was three hours. The ADR recorded: refund finalization requires a serializable transaction, not optimistic reads.

* * *

### 19.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Two engineers describe the same legacy module differently in your team's chat:

* Engineer X: "The payment module is full of technical debt. We should rewrite it."
* Engineer Y: "The payment module has tech debt from 2022 when we shortcut the refund logic for a deadline, and we said we'd come back. We never did. Also there's some code in there from an engineer who left that nobody understands. But that's just bad code, not debt."

Whose framing is closer to Cunningham's original definition, what is the practical difference, and how does the difference change what the team should _do_ about each issue?

Hint

Cunningham's debt is a _deliberate_ trade-off with an _intention to fix later_. Bad code from inexperience or accident is not debt in his sense.

**Answer:** Engineer Y is closer to Cunningham's original definition; Engineer X is using the colloquial flattening that treats "any bad code" as "debt."

**The practical difference matters because the remediation is different:**

The 2022 shortcut Engineer Y describes is _deliberate-prudent_ debt (§19.2's four quadrants): the team consciously made a trade-off, knew the cost, intended to pay back. The fix is to **schedule the payback** when the cost makes sense. This is what "debt" actually means in Cunningham's framework, and what the metaphor is useful for.

The "code from an engineer who left that nobody understands" is _not Cunningham's debt_. It is one of:

* **Inadvertent-prudent debt** : the team did their best at the time; in hindsight, better choices exist. Fix: refactor as understanding improves; the four-quadrants model applies.
* **Inadvertent-reckless debt** : the original engineer did not know better. Fix: not refactoring per se, but _training the current team_ in the patterns the original missed.
* **Unreviewed-plausibility debt** (§19.4): if the code was AI-generated and never deeply reviewed. Fix: review discipline; mutation testing.

Engineer X's flat "it's all debt, rewrite it" misses these distinctions and would lead the team to a big rewrite (Chapter 21 explains why this is usually wrong) when the right answer is a _multi-front program_ : schedule the 2022 payback this sprint; pair-program through the orphan code to understand it before deciding; introduce review discipline so new orphan code does not accumulate.

The general lesson: words matter. "Technical debt" is overloaded in industry chat to mean "code I don't like." Cunningham's original is narrower (deliberate + intention-to-fix) and more useful, because it tells you _what to do_. When your team uses the word "debt," ask: is this Cunningham debt (deliberate trade-off, plan to fix), or is it bad code (which has its own different remediations)? Different categories, different responses.

**Q2.** Apply the four-quadrants model to a real piece of code in your codebase. Classify it; what response does the classification suggest?

Hint

The two axes are intentional/inadvertent and prudent/reckless.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* A "shortcut" introduced under a deadline that has stayed for two years: deliberate-prudent (the shortcut was right then) or deliberate-reckless (the team should have come back; they did not). Either way, the response is to schedule the payback if the cost still justifies it.
* A "weird" structure introduced by an engineer who has since left: probably inadvertent-prudent (the engineer did their best; we have learned more since). Refactor when the code is touched anyway.
* A "messy" subsystem that nobody really understands: possibly inadvertent-reckless (the original engineers did not know better) or unreviewed plausibility (it was AI-generated; nobody reviewed deeply). The response depends on classification; training or review-discipline reset, respectively.

The exercise produces value in the classification itself, regardless of what response is chosen. Naming the kind of debt clarifies the conversation.

**Q3.** Six months ago, your team merged 47 agent-generated PRs into the `payments` module. The PRs all passed CI, were "approved" by reviewers within an average of 4 minutes each, and shipped without incident. Today a production bug surfaces: refunds for a specific currency combination produce wrong amounts. You investigate; the bug is in a file that was created in those 47 PRs. You ask three of the engineers who reviewed those PRs to explain the function; none of them can. The tests pass; the bug is real. Diagnose what kind of debt this is, why it is uniquely a 2026 phenomenon, and prescribe a specific remediation for _this_ module that does not require a moratorium on AI-generated PRs.

Hint

The tests pass, the reviewers approved, and nobody actually understood. That gap is what §19.4 names.

**Answer:** This is **unreviewed-plausibility debt** (§19.4) at scale. The code looked plausible enough that the 4-minute reviews approved it; the tests covered the structure that existed (because the agent wrote both); nobody on the team built a real mental model of what the function does. The team has accepted a maintenance obligation (the code is in production) without having paid the understanding cost. The bug is what that gap looks like when it bites.

**Why 2026 specifically?** Pre-AI, code was written _by_ engineers who at minimum had to mentally compile what they typed. Even sloppy 2018 code had a primary author who could explain it. AI-generated code at volume breaks that assumption: the human reviewer is the only person on the team who saw the code with intent, and if their review was 4 minutes long, that "seeing" was shallow. Multiply by 47 PRs and the team is now maintaining code that nobody actually understands — not the author (an agent that has been deprecated), not any reviewer.

**Specific remediation for this module (not a moratorium):**

  1. **Triage the remaining un-understood code** : identify the files in the module that came from those 47 PRs. Estimate the percentage of code-by-line that no current team member can explain.
  2. **Mutation testing on the module** (Chapter 6): the mutation score directly measures "do the tests actually test, or do they just execute?" Low mutation score = the tests were also generated by the agent and pass without testing. Strengthen tests where the surviving mutants point.
  3. **Pair-debug session on the bug** : two engineers, one bug, write the failing test first, then read the function line-by-line until both can explain it. Document the function's behavior in a short ADR; that ADR is now durable institutional memory for code that previously had none.
  4. **Update CLAUDE.md** with what you learned about _this codebase's failure modes_ : the currency combination edge case. Future AI sessions on this module will at least be warned.
  5. **Reset review discipline** for new AI-generated PRs in this module: Thread C checklist mandatory; reviewer must be able to predict what would break if specific lines changed (the "test the human's understanding" rule); 4-minute reviews are no longer acceptable for `payments`.

This is multi-front remediation (§19.5's cycle). Notice that none of it requires banning AI PRs; what it requires is the _supporting discipline_ the team skipped in the original 47 PRs. The lesson is sequencing (also in ch-22 Veridian case study): the AI workflow earns its keep only when the review discipline that supports it earns its keep too.

**Q4.** When is debt remediation justified, and when should debt be left alone?

Hint

§19.5's signals.

**Answer:** Remediation justified when:

* The interest is consuming engineering capacity (incidental work exceeds 40-50%).
* The debt is blocking a needed change.
* The debt has compounding cost (a deprecated dependency, a known security issue).

Left alone when:

* The debt is in a rarely-touched module (low return on investment).
* The debt is deliberate-prudent and the trade-off still holds.
* The remediation cost exceeds the interest cost.

The choice is a quantitative trade-off. The team that quantifies (lead time, defects, on-call) has the data; the team that does not is making the decision impressionistically and often gets it wrong.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Track the three debt metrics (lead time, defects, on-call) for your team's modules over a month. Identify the worst-performing module. Apply the four-quadrants analysis to its debt; propose a remediation plan.

Hint

The analysis is the half; the plan is the other half.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A representative outcome:

* Worst module: payments. Lead time 11 days; defects 6/month; on-call 3/month.
* Quadrant analysis: 60% inadvertent-prudent (the codebase has learned); 25% unreviewed-plausibility (AI-generated, weakly reviewed); 10% deliberate-prudent (a known shortcut still right); 5% deliberate-reckless (recent under-pressure work).
* Plan: a Q2 refactor for the inadvertent-prudent component (Chapter 11 sized); a review-discipline reset for the unreviewed-plausibility component (apply Thread C checklist); team conversation about Q1 pressure for the deliberate-reckless.

The plan is multi-front; no single intervention addresses all the debt. Track the metrics over the next quarter to verify improvement.

**Q6.** Identify a piece of code in your codebase that is unreviewed plausibility (AI-generated, weakly reviewed). Apply the Thread C checklist now. What did the original review miss?

Hint

Retrospective review often surfaces gaps.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings: the original review missed untested edge cases (Lie #1), inconsistent style (Lie #3), or over-mocking (Chapter 7). The retrospective review catches these now; the team can update the original PR or open a follow-up to address them.

The deeper lesson: retrospective reviews are a useful debt-management tool. The team that periodically reviews old AI-generated code (sampling rather than exhaustively) catches gaps before they become incidents.

**Q7.** Conduct a debt classification exercise with your team. Identify five debt items; for each, classify by quadrant; agree on a response. Compare the classifications across team members; where you disagree, discuss why.

Hint

Disagreements often reveal different assumptions about what was known when.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common patterns:

* Team members agree on the most-obvious cases (clear deliberate-prudent, clear inadvertent-reckless).
* Team members disagree on the middle cases (was this deliberate or inadvertent? was this prudent or reckless?).
* Disagreements reveal different assumptions about team history (was the team's understanding here in 2023?).
* Discussion of the disagreements produces shared understanding and better future classifications.

The exercise is valuable for the discussion more than for the specific classifications. The team's calibration on debt becomes a shared skill.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute: "In 2026, technical debt is a more dangerous problem than in 2016 because AI generates plausible-looking code faster than humans can validate it."

**Discussion:** For: AI accelerates code volume; review discipline must keep up; teams that have not adapted are accumulating unreviewed-plausibility debt at rates that did not exist before; the cost surfaces over time but the accumulation is happening now.

Against: AI also accelerates review (Chapter 15) and refactoring (Chapter 10); the same speedup that produces volume can address it. Teams with good discipline are using AI to both produce and validate at higher rates; the net effect is faster shipping with controlled debt. The "more dangerous" framing assumes static review discipline; in practice, the discipline is itself evolving.

The synthesis: AI is a multiplier in both directions. With strong review discipline, AI accelerates both code production and debt prevention; net debt may not grow faster. Without strong review discipline, AI accelerates production while leaving debt prevention behind; net debt grows faster than ever. The variance between teams in 2026 is larger than in 2016; the well-disciplined teams are pulling ahead, the poorly-disciplined teams are accumulating debt faster than they can recover.

The actionable conclusion: the team's review discipline is the variable. Investing in it is the highest-leverage 2026 craft investment for managing debt.

**Q9.** Map the relationship between debt management (this chapter) and the disciplines from Parts III-VII. How do those disciplines prevent debt? Which kinds?

**Discussion:**

* **Testing (Chapters 5-8)** prevents inadvertent-reckless debt: tests catch the bugs that less-experienced engineers would have missed. Mutation testing prevents unreviewed-plausibility debt: it quantifies whether the tests genuinely test, which catches the AI's weak assertions.
* **Refactoring (Chapters 9-11)** prevents inadvertent-prudent debt: the codebase keeps improving as understanding grows; the gap between current code and current understanding stays small.
* **Patterns (Chapters 12-13)** prevent over-engineering: avoiding pattern-tax prevents one form of inadvertent-reckless debt; recognizing when patterns apply prevents under-engineering.
* **Architecture (Chapter 14)** prevents tangled code: the hexagonal frame catches I/O leaks into the core early; the structure prevents one kind of inadvertent-prudent debt.
* **Code review (Chapter 15)** prevents unreviewed-plausibility debt directly: rigorous review is the antidote.
* **Documentation (Chapter 17)** prevents the _recurrence_ of decisions: ADRs preserve the reasoning so future engineers do not re-make the same trade-off without understanding it.
* **Naming (Chapter 18)** prevents the slowdown that comes with poor names: better names mean faster reading, less debt-by-confusion.

The disciplines together form a _prevention_ layer. Debt still accumulates despite them (the world changes; team understanding improves; deliberate trade-offs are made). But the rate of accumulation is bounded by the disciplines; teams with strong discipline pay less interest and have more capacity for the deliberate-prudent debt they choose to take.

### Chapter Summary

Cunningham's debt (§19.1) is a deliberate trade-off, not a colloquial label for bad code. The original framing distinguishes deliberate from inadvertent and prudent from reckless; the four-quadrants model (§19.2) operationalizes the framing.

Quantification (§19.3) makes debt actionable: lead time, defect rate, on-call burden are measurable signals. The metrics inform which debt to address and when.

The AI-Layer debt category (§19.4) is unreviewed plausibility: code that appears correct, passes tests, and ships, but was not understood deeply enough by humans. The mitigation is rigorous review discipline (Chapter 15), ADRs for AI decisions (Chapter 17), and mutation testing (Chapter 6).

The strategic cycle (§19.5) is measure, identify, investigate, plan, execute, re-measure. Teams that cycle quarterly keep debt under control; teams that wait until overwhelmed spend much more effort recovering.

The Helix billing example (§19.6) illustrates a multi-front remediation program: code refactoring for inadvertent-prudent; review-discipline reset for unreviewed-plausibility; training for inadvertent-reckless; team conversation for deliberate-reckless. The metrics improved dramatically over the year.

The Trellis ongoing pattern (§19.7) is continuous debt management as a staff-engineering function: monitoring, targeted refactors, design review, mentoring, acceptance of some debt. The codebase remains workable despite its age because the management is constant.

Chapter 20 takes the practical patterns for paying down debt: the Boy Scout Rule, the Strangler Fig pattern, and Branch-by-Abstraction. The patterns are the _how_ ; this chapter has covered the _what_ and _why_.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Measure the three metrics (lead time, defect rate, on-call burden) for your team's top three modules this month.** Whatever you find is the baseline. The trend over the next quarter is the diagnostic; the worst module is the candidate for investment.
  2. **Classify every "debt" the team complains about into a quadrant before discussing remediation.** Deliberate-prudent gets payback when the cost makes sense; inadvertent-prudent gets refactoring; deliberate-reckless gets culture change; inadvertent-reckless gets training. Different responses; muddled classifications produce muddled fixes.
  3. **For every AI-generated PR merged in the last month, ask whether anyone could explain it line-by-line.** If not, that is unreviewed-plausibility debt accumulating. Schedule a retrospective review pass on the riskiest modules before the debt compounds.

---

## Chapter 20: Boy Scout Rule, Strangler Fig, and the Decomposition Story

A Trellis engineer was assigned to fix a bug in a customer-data module that had not been touched in eight months. The bug was a small off-by-one in date arithmetic; the fix was three lines. The engineer's PR contained the three-line fix and seven additional small changes: a renamed variable that had been misleading, a deleted dead conditional, an extracted helper function, an updated docstring, a removed `// TODO` from 2022 that the original author had since left the company. The reviewer asked: why all the extras? The engineer's answer: "Boy Scout Rule."

This chapter is about the three patterns for incremental debt remediation: the Boy Scout Rule for small continuous improvement, the Strangler Fig pattern for incremental migration, and Branch by Abstraction as the alternative. Each pattern produces gradual improvement without requiring the big-refactor commitment of Chapter 11. Each is more sustainable than scheduled debt-cleanup sprints.

### 20.1 The Boy Scout Rule

The Boy Scout Rule, originally from scouting and popularized in software by Robert C. Martin, is the discipline of leaving the code a little better than you found it on every PR that touches it.

#### Definition: The Boy Scout Rule

The Boy Scout Rule states: every time you touch a piece of code (for any reason), leave it a little cleaner than you found it. The improvements are small (a rename, a deleted dead branch, an extracted helper); they are bundled with the substantive work; they accumulate across the team's PRs.

In plain language: do not just fix the bug; also fix one small adjacent thing while you are there.

#### Why the discipline works

Three operational reasons:

* **The cost is bounded per PR** : a Boy Scout improvement is a few minutes; the substantive work is hours; the proportional overhead is small.
* **The improvements accumulate** : across hundreds of PRs across years, the codebase gets meaningfully cleaner without anyone scheduling a refactor sprint.
* **The discipline travels with the engineer** : an engineer who has internalized the rule applies it in every codebase they touch; the habit compounds across their career.

#### What counts as a Boy Scout improvement

Appropriate Boy Scout work:

* **Rename a misleading variable** to match what it represents.
* **Delete a dead conditional** whose branch can no longer be taken.
* **Extract a helper function** from a section of inline logic.
* **Update a stale comment** that no longer matches the code.
* **Delete a stale`// TODO`** whose author has left and whose target is no longer relevant.
* **Add a missing test** for a behavior the bug fix touched.
* **Remove an unused import** or unused variable.

Each is a small change; each takes a few minutes; each leaves the code measurably better.

#### What does not count

The discipline is _small_ improvements. Larger work belongs in dedicated PRs:

* **Refactoring an entire class** : too big for Boy Scout; open a separate PR.
* **Rewriting a subsystem** : definitely too big.
* **Renaming a public API across many files** : too big.
* **Restructuring a folder** : too big.

The rule of thumb: a Boy Scout improvement should not require its own PR description section. If you need to explain it, it is too big; open a separate PR.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "the Boy Scout Rule justifies scope creep." The mistake is conflating _small improvement_ with _unbounded refactoring_. A PR that arrived to fix a three-line bug and now contains 300 lines of refactoring is not Boy Scout; it is scope creep. The reviewer will (rightly) push back; the engineer should split.

The discipline is _small and aligned_ : the improvement is small enough to fit within the PR's scope and aligned with what the PR is doing. A PR fixing a bug in date arithmetic can fix a related misleading comment; it should not refactor the entire date module.

### 20.2 The Boy Scout Rule with AI

In 2026, AI agents can identify and apply small improvements at speed. The Boy Scout discipline scales with AI: the agent can scan the file you are editing for improvement opportunities, apply them mechanically, and present them for review.

#### The workflow

  1. The human opens the file to fix a bug or add a feature.
  2. The human asks the AI: "while you are in this file, identify three small Boy Scout improvements that align with what we are doing."
  3. The AI proposes the improvements with rationale.
  4. The human reviews and accepts the ones that fit (typically two of three; one is often overreach).
  5. The AI applies them as separate commits within the PR.
  6. The reviewer sees the substantive change and the Boy Scout improvements as distinct commits.

The cost to the human is minimal; the cost to the codebase is negative (it gets cleaner); the cost to the reviewer is small (the improvements are visible per commit and easy to evaluate).

#### The CLAUDE.md discipline

A CLAUDE.md entry that captures the Boy Scout norm:
    
    
    ## Boy Scout improvements
    
    When working on a file, identify small adjacent improvements:
    - Renaming misleading variables.
    - Deleting dead conditionals.
    - Extracting small helpers.
    - Updating stale comments.
    - Deleting stale TODOs.
    
    Apply these as separate commits within the PR. Do not include
    improvements unrelated to the substantive change; those belong
    in separate PRs.
    

The instruction sets the norm; the AI follows it; the team's Boy Scout rate increases without each engineer remembering to apply the discipline.

### 20.3 The Strangler Fig pattern

The Strangler Fig pattern, named by Martin Fowler in 2004, is the technique of migrating from one system to another by gradually replacing the old with the new, in place, while both coexist.

#### Definition: Strangler Fig

The Strangler Fig pattern migrates a system by growing the new system around the old, redirecting functionality piece by piece from old to new, until the old is fully replaced and can be removed. The transition is incremental; both systems coexist during the migration; the migration completes when no functionality remains in the old system.

In plain language: do not rewrite; gradually replace, one piece at a time, with the old as the safety net throughout.

#### The mechanics

The Strangler Fig has three phases per piece of functionality:

  1. **New implementation alongside old** : the new code is written; the old code remains active. Requests still go to the old.
  2. **Redirection** : requests for the migrated functionality go to the new code; the old code is no longer the active path but remains as a fallback.
  3. **Deletion** : after the new code has been stable for some period (weeks or months), the old code is deleted.

Repeat for each piece of functionality until the old system is empty. Then delete the empty shell.

#### A worked example

Trellis migrating a legacy reporting service to a modern one:
    
    
    Initial state:
      All reports served by LegacyReportingService.
    
    Phase 1 (Month 1-2):
      ModernReportingService is built. Two reports (Customer Activity and
      Revenue Summary) are implemented in it. The Modern service is deployed
      but no traffic routes to it yet.
    
    Phase 2 (Month 3):
      Traffic for Customer Activity and Revenue Summary is routed to Modern.
      All other reports still served by Legacy.
    
    Phase 3 (Month 4-6):
      Additional reports migrated to Modern, one at a time, with monitoring
      between each.
    
    Phase 4 (Month 7):
      All reports served by Modern. Legacy code path is dead.
    
    Phase 5 (Month 8):
      Legacy code is deleted. The migration is complete.
    

At each phase, the old system is the safety net. If the new system has bugs, the team rolls back to the old. Only after the new is proven over weeks of production does the old get deleted.

#### How Strangler Fig differs from a big rewrite

A big rewrite (Chapter 21) builds a complete new system, then switches over in one event. The risks: the new system is unproven at switchover; the old is irretrievable after switchover; the project commits to months or years of no shipping.

Strangler Fig is the opposite: each piece is small (one report's worth of work); the old is irretrievable only after each piece's stability is proven; the project ships continuously throughout the migration.

The cost of Strangler Fig is the _coexistence overhead_ : for the duration of the migration, both systems exist and must be maintained. The cost is real but bounded; the alternative (big rewrite) has different and usually larger risks.

### 20.4 Branch by Abstraction

Branch by Abstraction is an alternative migration pattern by Paul Hammant. The difference from Strangler Fig is the placement of the abstraction.

#### Definition: Branch by Abstraction

Branch by Abstraction introduces an abstraction layer between the application and the implementation it depends on; the old implementation satisfies the abstraction; the new implementation is built behind the same abstraction; traffic is gradually routed to the new implementation; when complete, the old implementation is deleted (the abstraction itself may also be removed).

In plain language: extract an interface; the old code implements it; build the new code behind the same interface; swap implementations one piece at a time.

#### Branch by Abstraction versus Strangler Fig

The two patterns have similar shapes but different emphases:

* **Strangler Fig** : routes _requests_ between old and new. The decision happens at the request boundary.
* **Branch by Abstraction** : routes _operations_ between old and new implementations. The decision happens at the abstraction boundary inside the system.

For a stateless service (like a reporting API), Strangler Fig is natural: route at the HTTP layer. For a stateful component (like a database access layer), Branch by Abstraction is natural: route at the interface inside the application.

The two patterns can combine. A migration might use Branch by Abstraction for the database layer (the application calls `IDataAccess`; old and new implement it) and Strangler Fig for the HTTP layer (some endpoints route to the new, some to the old).

#### A worked example: Aki migrates Helix from Diesel to Drizzle

Helix originally used Diesel (Rust ORM) for database access. In 2024, the team decided to migrate to a TypeScript service using Drizzle. Branch by Abstraction was the right pattern:

  1. The Rust service exposed a typed interface for database operations (the existing Diesel calls).
  2. The new TypeScript service implemented the same interface using Drizzle.
  3. The Rust service's database calls were routed through a gateway that could dispatch to either the local Diesel implementation or the remote TypeScript service.
  4. One operation at a time, the gateway was reconfigured to route to the new service. Each migration was a small flag flip.
  5. After all operations were migrated, the Rust service's Diesel code was deleted; the gateway became a simple HTTP client to the new service.

Total migration time: about four months. The risk profile: each operation's migration was independent; problems with one did not affect others; rollback was a flag flip.

### 20.5 When to refactor opportunistically versus when to schedule

A team has two modes for paying down debt:

* **Opportunistic** : the Boy Scout Rule applied per PR. Small improvements bundled with substantive work. No dedicated time.
* **Scheduled** : a planned refactor (Chapter 11 size) with allocated team time. Strangler Fig or Branch by Abstraction may be the vehicle.

Both modes have their place. The discipline is to know which fits the situation.

#### When opportunistic is enough

* The debt is distributed across many files; no single hotspot dominates.
* The codebase is small enough that opportunistic improvements compound visibly.
* The team's PR throughput is high; small improvements add up fast.
* The metrics (Chapter 19) are stable or improving.

#### When scheduled is necessary

* The debt is concentrated in one or two large subsystems; opportunistic improvements would not converge.
* The codebase has a structural problem (architectural debt; Chapter 14) that requires coordinated change.
* The metrics are degrading and the team's capacity is being consumed.
* A specific upcoming change requires the debt to be addressed first.

#### The mixed mode

Most teams operate in a mixed mode: opportunistic improvements continuously, scheduled refactors for the larger items. The mix's exact balance depends on the team's situation; the discipline is to use both rather than defaulting to one.

Asher's pattern at Trellis (Chapter 19) is mixed: continuous Boy Scout via the team's PRs, one scheduled refactor per quarter led by Asher or another staff engineer. The continuous work keeps the codebase from rotting; the scheduled work addresses the concentrated debt.

### 20.6 Common pitfalls

A small inventory of patterns to avoid:

#### The Boy Scout Rule as scope creep

A PR that arrived to fix a three-line bug and contains 300 lines of refactoring is not Boy Scout; it is scope creep. The improvements should be _small_ and _aligned_ with the substantive change. Anything else belongs in separate PRs.

#### The "while we are here" refactor
