An engineer touches a function and decides to refactor it from scratch. The result: a PR that mixes a small fix with a large refactor, neither well-scoped. The reviewer cannot evaluate the changes independently; the bug fix is delayed by the refactor's review; the refactor is rushed by the bug fix's urgency. Split.

#### The neverending Strangler Fig

A Strangler Fig migration that started in 2023 and is still partial in 2026 is a problem: the team is permanently maintaining two systems, the migration's promised payoff is deferred forever. The discipline is to _commit_ to the migration's completion within a planned timeline; if the team cannot commit, do not start.

#### The abstraction without a second implementation

Branch by Abstraction with one implementation is just an extra layer of indirection. The pattern justifies itself when the second implementation exists; until then, the abstraction is pattern-tax (Chapter 13). The discipline is to introduce the abstraction _as part of_ the migration, not in anticipation of a hypothetical second case.

#### The migration that destabilizes the existing system

A poorly-executed migration touches the old system in ways that break it. The team is now fighting fires in the old system while building the new; the migration's cost is much higher than planned. The mitigation: the migration should _minimize_ changes to the old system; the new system grows alongside, not by modifying the old.

#### Common Misconception (revisited)

A common misconception is "the team should pay down debt aggressively whenever possible." The mistake is treating debt remediation as universally good. Refactoring rarely-touched code has poor return; over-investing in debt-cleanup at the expense of features hurts the business; the right _amount_ of debt-work is what matches the actual cost of the debt. A team paying back deliberate-prudent debt that is not actually consuming capacity is wasting effort; a team that ignores genuinely costly debt is letting it compound.

The discipline is _calibrated effort_ : measure (Chapter 19), prioritize, execute appropriately. The team that calibrates ships features and manages debt simultaneously; the team that does not calibrate either over- or under-invests.

### 20.7 The AI-Layer: agent-assisted incremental migration

AI agents are highly useful for the mechanical work of Strangler Fig and Branch by Abstraction migrations.

#### What AI does well

* **Implement the new path against the old as spec** : the agent reads the old implementation, the tests, and the interface; produces a new implementation that satisfies them.
* **Apply renames and moves consistently across many files** : a migration that touches dozens of call sites is mechanical work the agent handles in minutes.
* **Generate shadow-comparison code** : per Chapter 11, the comparison logic between old and new is itself code; the agent can generate it from the interface.
* **Update CLAUDE.md as the migration progresses** : documenting which subsystems have moved to the new path is the kind of work the agent maintains.

#### What AI does not do well

* **Decide which piece to migrate next** : prioritization is human work; the agent can analyze but the decision is judgment.
* **Investigate shadow mismatches** : when the comparison reveals differences between old and new, the human investigates whether the difference is a bug or a benign formatting variation.
* **Decide when to delete the old** : after the new is stable, the human decides when to delete the old. The agent can do the mechanical deletion; the decision is human.
* **Maintain two implementations under behavioral drift** : if the old system gets bug fixes during the migration, the agent does not automatically mirror them into the new system; the human catches the drift in review.

#### The combined workflow

A 2026 Strangler Fig migration with AI:

  1. Human plans the migration's pieces and order.
  2. For each piece: 
     * Agent implements the new path.
     * Agent generates shadow-comparison logic.
     * Human reviews the implementation and the comparison.
     * Production runs both paths; comparison surfaces any differences.
     * Human investigates differences.
     * When stable, human flips the flag to route to the new path.
  3. After all pieces migrate, agent deletes the old code.
  4. Human verifies no callers remain.

The human's time is concentrated in planning, review, and decision; the agent's time handles the mechanical bulk. The total elapsed time is significantly shorter than human-alone; the risk profile is the same as without AI because the human still owns the decisions.

### 20.8 A worked example: Aki's continuous improvement at Helix

A representative month of Aki's debt management at Helix:

* **Week 1** : Aki opens four PRs (substantive feature work). Each includes 2-3 Boy Scout improvements aligned with the work.
* **Week 2** : One PR's review surfaces a discussion about a generic name in adjacent code. Aki opens a small renaming PR to address the naming; the discussion is closed.
* **Week 3** : Aki notices the metrics for the `ingest` module are degrading. She runs the debt classification analysis (Chapter 19); identifies inadvertent-prudent debt accumulating from the team's recent learning. Plans a small refactor for the next sprint.
* **Week 4** : The refactor lands (Chapter 9 style, applied to several files in the `ingest` module). The metrics improve over the following weeks.

No big refactor sprint; no debt-cleanup event; just continuous incremental improvement bundled with the substantive work. The codebase improves; features continue to ship; the team's capacity is preserved.

This is the discipline at scale. A team where every engineer practices it produces a codebase that ages well; a team where no one practices it produces a codebase that rots.

### 20.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Two engineers are working on the same bug-fix PR (a 3-line fix to a date-parsing function). They both interpret "Boy Scout Rule" differently and propose adding adjacent improvements:

**Engineer A's PR** : the 3-line bug fix, plus a renamed variable in the same function (was `d`, now `parsed_date`), a deleted stale `// TODO 2022` comment in the same file, and an extracted helper from a 25-line block. Three commits, ~30 lines beyond the fix.

**Engineer B's PR** : the 3-line bug fix, plus a refactor of the entire date-parsing module (split into three files), plus renaming an unrelated function in another file, plus 14 new tests for unrelated functions, plus updating the README. Twelve commits, ~600 lines beyond the fix.

Which one is doing Boy Scout Rule correctly? What is the _diagnostic_ the reviewer should apply to decide on similar future PRs?

Hint

Size and alignment, both. The diagnostic: would the improvement need its own description in the PR summary?

**Answer:**

**Engineer A is doing it right.** Three improvements, each small (a few minutes of work each), each _aligned_ with the substantive change (touching the same function or file). The reviewer can evaluate the bug fix without being distracted; the improvements compound across PRs without slowing this one.

**Engineer B is committing scope creep** , not Boy Scout. Six hundred lines of "while I'm here" work in a bug-fix PR has three problems:

  1. The reviewer cannot evaluate the bug fix without first evaluating the 600 lines of refactor. Review time inflates 10×.
  2. The refactor itself deserves its own review, its own PR description, its own ADR if structural. Burying it inside a bug fix means none of those happen.
  3. If the refactor introduces a regression, reverting "the bug fix PR" reverts both. The atomic-commit principle (Chapter 3) breaks down because the PR is no longer about one conceptual change.

**The diagnostic to apply** (the line between Boy Scout and scope creep): _would the improvement need its own description in the PR summary?_ If yes, it is too big for Boy Scout — open a separate PR. If no (the rename, the dead comment, the small helper), it belongs in this PR.

A second diagnostic: a Boy Scout improvement is _aligned_ with the substantive change (same function, same file, same conceptual area). An improvement in an unrelated file or unrelated function is scope creep by definition; bundle it elsewhere.

**The reviewer's correct response** to Engineer B's PR: "Please split this. Land the 3-line bug fix as PR 1 (today); open PR 2 for the date-parsing refactor (this week, with its own description); open PR 3 for the unrelated rename and tests (whenever)." Engineer B may resist (it feels like "extra work"); the discipline is to insist. The team's review capacity is the bottleneck (Chapter 15); splitting preserves that capacity.

**Q2.** Compare the Strangler Fig pattern with the big rewrite alternative. What makes Strangler Fig the safer choice?

Hint

The risk profile of each.

**Answer:** Strangler Fig migrates piece by piece; the old system remains as the safety net throughout; the team ships features continuously during the migration; each piece is reversible. The big rewrite builds a complete new system, then switches over in one event; the new system is unproven at switchover; the project commits to a long period of no shipping; the old is irretrievable after switchover.

Strangler Fig's safety comes from: (a) the small step size (each piece is bounded), (b) the safety net (the old works if the new breaks), (c) the continuous shipping (the team gets feedback throughout), (d) the reversibility (each step can be rolled back).

The big rewrite's risk comes from the opposites: large step, no safety net, no shipping, one-shot. Joel Spolsky's "Things You Should Never Do, Part I" essay (Chapter 21) names this as the canonical failure mode of software engineering. Most big rewrites take much longer than estimated and end either in abandonment or in a hybrid worse than either original.

**Q3.** Three migration scenarios. For each, decide whether Strangler Fig or Branch by Abstraction is the right pattern. Briefly justify.

(a) Your team's monolith exposes a `/api/v1/reports/*` HTTP API to external customers. You are building a new reporting service from scratch with a cleaner API. Customers cannot be asked to migrate URLs immediately; the old API must continue working for at least 12 months.

(b) Your internal authentication module currently uses bcrypt for password hashing. The security team wants to migrate to Argon2id, but the application code that calls `auth.verify_password(...)` should not change. The migration must handle existing users (bcrypt hashes in the database) and new users (Argon2id hashes going forward).

(c) Your team is replacing the existing in-process job queue (custom Python code) with a hosted queue service (e.g., Amazon SQS). Application code that publishes jobs should keep its existing API; consumers may change.

Hint

Strangler Fig: route requests at the _external boundary_. Branch by Abstraction: route operations at an _internal interface_.

**Answer:**

(a) **Strangler Fig.** The migration is at an _external boundary_ (HTTP API consumed by customers). Each `/api/v1/reports/<name>` endpoint migrates independently: the old monolith continues to serve traffic for endpoints not yet migrated; the new reporting service serves the migrated ones; an HTTP gateway (or DNS routing) dispatches per-endpoint. After 12 months when all endpoints are migrated and customers have moved, the monolith's reporting code is deleted.

(b) **Branch by Abstraction.** The migration is at an _internal interface_ (`auth.verify_password`). The application code that calls this function should not change; only the implementation behind it does. Define a `PasswordHasher` protocol; the bcrypt impl handles legacy hashes; the Argon2id impl handles new ones; the orchestration logic ("which hasher for this user's stored hash format?") sits behind the protocol. The application calls `auth.verify_password` the same way before, during, and after the migration.

(c) **Branch by Abstraction** (with a small Strangler Fig component). The publishing side is internal — application code that calls `queue.publish(job)` should not change; that is Branch by Abstraction. The _consumer_ side may be external (separate processes), which makes the consumer-side migration look more like Strangler Fig (each consumer migrated independently). Both patterns combine: BBA inside the publisher; Strangler Fig at the consumer-process boundary.

**The general rule** : ask _where the migration's decision point is_. External HTTP boundary, message-queue boundary, anything that crosses a network or process? Strangler Fig — route requests. Internal interface within one application? Branch by Abstraction — route operations behind the interface. The two patterns can combine in larger migrations; do not feel obligated to pick exactly one if the migration crosses multiple boundary types.

**Q4.** When is opportunistic refactoring (Boy Scout) sufficient, and when must the team schedule a dedicated refactor?

Hint

§20.5's signals.

**Answer:** Opportunistic is sufficient when:

* Debt is distributed across many files; no hotspot.
* PR throughput is high enough that small improvements compound.
* Metrics are stable or improving.

Scheduled is necessary when:

* Debt is concentrated in one or two large subsystems.
* The codebase has structural debt requiring coordinated change.
* Metrics are degrading.
* An upcoming change requires the debt to be addressed first.

Most teams operate in a mixed mode: continuous Boy Scout, one or two scheduled refactors per quarter for the larger items. The mix's balance depends on the team's situation; the discipline is to use both.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Audit your last 10 PRs. Identify which contained Boy Scout improvements and which did not. For the ones that did not, would there have been an opportunity? Track this for a month and try to increase the rate.

Hint

The improvement rate is the metric.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings: 30-50% of PRs contain Boy Scout improvements unprompted; another 20-30% had clear opportunities that the engineer missed. The deliberate practice over a month often raises the rate to 70-80%.

The improvements often align with what the engineer is reading anyway: while reading a file, they notice the misleading name or the dead branch. The investment is minimal; the cumulative effect is substantial.

**Q6.** Plan a small Strangler Fig migration for a module in your codebase that is due for replacement. Identify the pieces to migrate; sequence them; estimate the timeline. What is the smallest first piece?

Hint

The first piece sets the pattern; pick something small with clear interface.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A reasonable plan:

* The smallest first piece is usually a stateless operation with few callers (a single endpoint, a single utility function).
* Migrate that piece end-to-end first; verify the migration pattern works for the team; resolve any infrastructure issues (flag service, comparison logic).
* Subsequent pieces follow the pattern; each is similar in shape.
* The timeline depends on the team's capacity; a 10-piece migration might be one piece per sprint, so 10 sprints (5 months) at a moderate pace.

The first-piece selection matters because it tests the team's infrastructure. A small, isolated piece succeeds quickly; a complex piece can stall the whole migration on infrastructure issues.

**Q7.** Use an AI agent to apply Boy Scout improvements to a file you are editing. Have the agent identify three improvements; evaluate which are appropriate; commit them as separate commits. Report how the workflow went.

Hint

The CLAUDE.md instruction in §20.2 is the lever.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* The agent identifies three improvements with reasonable rationale.
* One or two are clearly good; you accept.
* One is overreach (the agent proposes refactoring an adjacent function that does not need it); you decline.
* The accepted improvements land as separate commits within the PR.
* Total time investment: a few minutes; cumulative codebase improvement over a quarter is significant.

The lesson: AI is a multiplier on Boy Scout discipline. The engineer who uses it well produces a measurably cleaner codebase than the engineer who does not.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "The Boy Scout Rule scales to large codebases because the improvements compound; the alternative (scheduled refactors only) does not scale because the codebase grows faster than scheduled time."

**Discussion:** For: a codebase touched by many engineers across many PRs accumulates improvements faster than any scheduled refactor can. The compound effect is enormous: 1000 PRs per month, each with two small improvements, produces 2000 improvements per month, far more than a scheduled refactor's output. Boy Scout is _parallel_ ; scheduled refactor is _serial_.

The opposite framing (only scheduled refactors): an engineer who never improves while passing through accumulates no cleanup; the scheduled refactor must address everything; the scheduled refactor's scope is overwhelming and never completes. The team either accepts the rot or commits to an unrealistic refactor.

The synthesis: both are needed. Boy Scout handles the distributed improvements; scheduled refactors handle the concentrated and structural ones. Either alone is insufficient; together, they keep the codebase clean at scale.

The 2026 angle: AI makes Boy Scout cheaper and more effective; the discipline scales further than it did in 2016. Teams that adopt AI-assisted Boy Scout pull ahead in codebase quality.

**Q9.** Map the relationship between this chapter's patterns (Boy Scout, Strangler Fig, Branch by Abstraction) and Chapter 19's debt categories. Which patterns address which kinds of debt?

**Discussion:**

* **Boy Scout** addresses _distributed inadvertent-prudent debt_. The team has learned; the codebase incorporates the learning incrementally. Each small improvement reflects current understanding.
* **Boy Scout** also addresses _unreviewed-plausibility debt_ : the engineer reading the code as part of touching it deepens their understanding; the code becomes less unreviewed.
* **Strangler Fig** addresses _concentrated inadvertent-prudent debt_ : a subsystem the team would design differently today is migrated to the new shape, piece by piece. The migration is the structured version of Boy Scout at module scale.
* **Strangler Fig** also addresses _deliberate-prudent debt that has matured_ : the original trade-off was right; it is no longer right; the migration replaces the trade-off with the current best understanding.
* **Branch by Abstraction** addresses the same debt as Strangler Fig but is appropriate for internal components rather than external boundaries.
* **None of these patterns directly address _deliberate-reckless_ or _inadvertent-reckless_ debt**: those are team-culture and training issues, addressed by the disciplines from earlier chapters (review, mentoring, documentation).

The general pattern: structural debt is addressed by structural patterns (Strangler Fig, Branch by Abstraction). Distributed debt is addressed by distributed practice (Boy Scout). Behavioral debt is addressed by behavioral change (review discipline, training). Treating all debt as the same kind leads to wrong responses; matching the pattern to the debt category produces results.

### Chapter Summary

The Boy Scout Rule (§20.1) is the discipline of small continuous improvement on every PR. Improvements are bounded and aligned; scope creep is the failure mode. The discipline accumulates across hundreds of PRs and produces gradual codebase improvement without dedicated refactor sprints.

Boy Scout with AI (§20.2) scales the discipline: the agent identifies and applies small improvements at speed; the human reviews and accepts. The CLAUDE.md anchors the convention.

The Strangler Fig pattern (§20.3) migrates systems incrementally: new alongside old, gradual redirection, deletion when stable. The pattern is the safer alternative to the big rewrite; the cost is the coexistence overhead during the migration.

Branch by Abstraction (§20.4) is the internal-component variant: route through an abstraction layer that has old and new implementations behind it. Suitable for stateful components inside an application; Strangler Fig is suitable for external service boundaries; the two patterns can combine.

When to opportunistic versus scheduled (§20.5): opportunistic for distributed debt; scheduled for concentrated and structural debt. Most teams operate in a mixed mode.

Common pitfalls (§20.6): Boy Scout as scope creep; the neverending migration; the unjustified abstraction; the destabilizing migration. Each has a discipline that prevents it.

The AI-Layer (§20.7) accelerates the mechanical work of all three patterns; the human's role is concentrated in planning, decision, and review.

Chapter 21 closes Part VIII with the migration alternative the chapter has avoided: the big rewrite. The chapter examines when (rarely) it works, when (mostly) it fails, and why the Strangler Fig is the better default. With Chapters 19, 20, and 21 in place, the debt-management discipline is complete.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Apply the Boy Scout Rule on your next PR.** Identify one small adjacent improvement (rename, dead-branch removal, extracted helper, stale TODO cleanup); commit it separately within the PR. Three Boy Scout improvements per PR, across hundreds of PRs, compounds without ever scheduling a "refactor sprint."
  2. **For the next migration your team considers, draft the Strangler Fig version on paper _before_ the rewrite version.** Identify the pieces, sequence them, name the first piece. If the Strangler Fig is feasible, it is almost always the right call; only if it is infeasible does the rewrite earn consideration.
  3. **Commit to a deletion deadline before starting the migration.** "All functions migrated by Q4; old code deleted in Q1 next year." Open-ended migrations become permanent dual-system tax; deadlines are the only discipline that prevents this.

---

## Chapter 21: Migrations and the Big Rewrite Question (and Why It Usually Fails)

In 2002, Joel Spolsky wrote an essay titled "Things You Should Never Do, Part I" arguing that big rewrites of working software are almost always a mistake. The essay's primary example was Netscape, which spent 1998-2000 rewriting its browser from scratch and effectively lost the browser market to Internet Explorer during the rewrite. Spolsky's claim was strong: rewriting working software discards the most valuable thing in the codebase, which is the _bug fixes accumulated over years_. Even ugly code embodies lessons learned the hard way; throwing it away to start fresh loses those lessons.

The essay has been re-read and re-debated for two decades. Many engineers have shared the "rewrite failed" experience: spending months or years rebuilding a system, accumulating new bugs the original system had already fixed, and either abandoning the rewrite or shipping a worse version of the original.

A smaller number of engineers have shared the "rewrite succeeded" experience: the rare cases where the rewrite was the right call and produced a system meaningfully better than the original. Netflix's migration from Oracle to Cassandra in 2010-2012 is one. Apple's silicon migration is another. These cases share specific properties; identifying them is part of the discipline.

This chapter is about that question: when does the big rewrite work, when does it fail, and why is Strangler Fig the safer default. With Chapters 19 (understanding debt) and 20 (incremental patterns) in place, this chapter completes Part VIII.

### 21.1 The Spolsky argument

Spolsky's argument has three main parts:

#### Part 1: Code embodies bug fixes

A codebase that has been in production for years has been hit by many bugs. Each bug was found, debugged, and fixed; the fix is now in the code. The fix is often invisible (a special case, a defensive check, a careful ordering) but load-bearing (without it, the bug returns).

A rewrite starts from the _concept_ of what the system should do, not from the _accumulated learning_ of how it was actually used. The rewrite produces a codebase that is conceptually clean but has not been hit by the bugs the original had. The bugs will be hit, one by one, in the new codebase; each will be debugged from scratch; the cumulative debugging cost is significant.

#### Part 2: The rewrite consumes the team's capacity

A rewrite that takes 18 months is 18 months of no shipping. The product does not get new features; competitors continue to ship; users either wait or migrate to alternatives. The opportunity cost is real.

In Spolsky's Netscape example, the rewrite consumed 1998-2000 while Internet Explorer continued to ship. The result was IE's dominance for nearly a decade. The rewrite's technical merits did not matter because the market had moved by the time the rewrite shipped.

#### Part 3: The rewrite is usually overconfident

Engineers proposing rewrites often underestimate the original system's complexity. The original has been touched by many engineers, contains many edge cases, and reflects many decisions whose context is not obvious. The rewrite team plans for the _visible_ complexity (the features the system has, the API surface) and is surprised by the _invisible_ complexity (the edge cases, the historical decisions).

The result is the canonical "we are 90% done; the last 10% will take 90% of the time" pattern. The visible work was completed on schedule; the invisible work consumed the remaining time, multiplied by some unpredictable factor.

### 21.2 When the rewrite was right

Spolsky's argument is strong but not universal. A small number of rewrites have succeeded. Their common properties:

#### The rewrite was driven by a structural impossibility

The original system's architecture fundamentally could not accommodate a required new behavior. The rewrite was not a preference for cleaner code; it was a necessity for new functionality.

Netflix's Oracle-to-Cassandra migration was driven by the fundamental incompatibility of Oracle's licensing and scaling characteristics with Netflix's growth trajectory. The migration was not optional; the rewrite was the only path forward.

#### The team had explicit, sustained organizational commitment

The rewrite was approved at the executive level; the timeline was multi-year; the team was protected from feature-work demands during the rewrite; the risk was acknowledged and accepted.

Apple's silicon migration in 2020-2022 had this property: the company committed to the migration, accepted that some products would have transition costs, and protected the engineering team to complete the work. The migration shipped because the organization was aligned.

#### The new system was provably better in concrete ways

The rewrite was justified by specific, measurable improvements: cost reduction, scale, performance, regulatory compliance. The improvements were not aesthetic; they were quantifiable.

Cassandra versus Oracle for Netflix: significantly lower per-query cost, better horizontal scaling, no vendor lock-in. The improvements were quantifiable; the team could project the payback period; the executive sponsor could approve based on numbers.

#### The rewrite was done by the same team

The team that wrote the new system was largely the same as the team that maintained the old. They knew the old system's edge cases; they had been on call for its bugs; they could anticipate the invisible complexity that the rewrite would expose.

Twitter's 2011 transition from Ruby on Rails to Scala for the core timeline service was led by engineers who had maintained the Ruby system; their knowledge of its quirks informed the Scala design. The transition succeeded; subsequent transitions led by less-knowledgeable teams have not been as successful.

### 21.3 When the rewrite usually fails

Many more rewrites have failed. Their common properties (often inverse to §21.2):

#### The rewrite was driven by aesthetic dissatisfaction

The original system was "messy" or "old" or "not how we would write it today." The rewrite was proposed to clean up; the cleanup was the goal, not the means.

Aesthetic rewrites usually fail because the original's "mess" turns out to encode learnings the rewrite re-derives. The rewrite team is repeatedly surprised; the timeline slips; the rewrite never ships.

#### The team had limited or unsustained commitment

The rewrite was approved tentatively; the team also had feature commitments; the timeline was assumed to be shorter than realistic. When the rewrite ran into difficulty, the team's capacity was divided between rewrite and features; both suffered.

The 1998-2000 Netscape rewrite had this pattern in part: the team was not fully insulated from competitive pressure; the rewrite slowed as feature pressure mounted.

#### The "improvements" were vague

The rewrite was justified by general improvements (better performance, easier maintenance, modern stack) without specific, measurable targets. When the rewrite shipped, the improvements were marginal at best; the team's investment did not pay back.

#### The new system was built by a different team

A new team built the rewrite; the original team maintained the old. The new team did not know the old system's edge cases; the rewrite was repeatedly surprised by them; the timeline slipped.

This is the most common failure mode in 2026. A startup hires a "rewrite team" specifically to replace a legacy system; the rewrite team is unfamiliar with the legacy; the rewrite takes 3× the planned time and produces a system that has bugs the original had already fixed.

### 21.4 The Strangler Fig as the alternative

For most cases where a rewrite is being considered, the Strangler Fig pattern (Chapter 20) is the safer alternative. The mapping:

* **Concern** : the original system has accumulated bug fixes that a rewrite would lose.

* **Strangler Fig response** : the original system remains active during the migration; each piece is migrated only when the new piece is proven; the bug fixes are not lost because the original is the safety net throughout.

* **Concern** : the rewrite consumes 18 months of no shipping.

* **Strangler Fig response** : the migration ships continuously; each piece's migration is a small, shippable change.

* **Concern** : the rewrite is overconfident about complexity.

* **Strangler Fig response** : each piece is small enough to estimate accurately; the team's accuracy improves as they migrate; misestimates affect one piece, not the whole.

The Strangler Fig substitutes incremental risk for catastrophic risk. The total cost of the migration may be similar; the variance is much lower. For most teams in most situations, the lower variance is worth the (marginal) higher mean cost.

### 21.5 The conditions for considering a rewrite

If you are tempted to propose a rewrite, the disciplined checklist:

  1. **Is a Strangler Fig migration feasible?** If yes, prefer it. The threshold for "feasible" is low: even messy legacy systems are usually amenable to Strangler Fig if you are willing to design the abstraction layer.
  2. **Is the motivation a structural impossibility?** If the new requirement cannot be implemented in the old system at any reasonable cost, rewrite is justified. If the motivation is aesthetic, it is not.
  3. **Does the organization have sustained commitment?** A multi-year rewrite needs executive sponsorship; without it, the rewrite will be undermined by feature pressure.
  4. **Is the improvement measurable and substantial?** "Cleaner code" is not a justification; "30% lower hosting cost" or "5× throughput" is.
  5. **Is the rewrite team the same team that maintains the legacy?** If yes, they bring the knowledge. If no, plan for the rewrite team's discovery cost of the legacy's complexity.

If all five answers favor rewrite, the rewrite is defensible. If any one does not, reconsider.

This book takes a stronger position than "reconsider": **if any one of the five does not hold, do not rewrite.** The failed rewrites in §21.3 each violated one or two criteria, not all five; the threshold of "all five must hold" is what would have caught them. Teams that allow "we have four out of five" through the door usually discover during execution that the missing one was the load-bearing one. Five-of-five is not over-restrictive; it is the historical bar that distinguishes the rewrites that shipped from the rewrites that did not. Hold the bar.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "the codebase is so bad that rewrite is the only option." The mistake is treating "bad" as a property of the code rather than the team's relationship to it. A codebase that no one understands is bad _for this team_ ; the same codebase might be fine for engineers who knew it. The fix is often training or hiring, not rewriting. Spolsky's "things you should never do" is partly about resisting the impulse to fix a team-knowledge problem with a code rewrite.

A second common misconception is "the new technology is so much better that we must rewrite to use it." The mistake is overvaluing the new technology and undervaluing the old code's accumulated learnings. New technology is rarely as transformative as it appears; old code is rarely as bad. A modest gradient of improvement does not justify a multi-year rewrite.

### 21.6 The migration-via-Strangler-Fig alternative in detail

When a rewrite is tempting but a Strangler Fig is feasible, the Strangler Fig version of the same migration:

#### Step 1: identify the migration's pieces

Decompose the system into pieces that can be migrated independently. The pieces should be:

* **Bounded** : a piece is one specific functionality (one endpoint, one subsystem, one operation).
* **Independent** : pieces can be migrated in any order without dependencies.
* **Shippable** : each piece's migration produces a stable system.

For a system that is hard to decompose this way, the Strangler Fig is harder; the rewrite is more tempting. But "hard" is rarely "impossible"; the team's design work is to find the decomposition.

#### Step 2: build the new path for one piece

Implement the new system for the first piece. Use the old as a specification (the new should behave equivalently). Add comparison logic (per Chapter 11) to verify equivalence.

#### Step 3: migrate traffic to the new path

For the first piece, route traffic to the new path. Monitor for differences. Roll back if necessary.

#### Step 4: iterate

For each remaining piece, repeat steps 2 and 3. Each piece is a complete migration cycle.

#### Step 5: delete the old

After all pieces are migrated and stable, delete the old system.

The total work is comparable to a rewrite; the risk profile is dramatically lower. Each piece's risk is bounded; the whole migration's risk is the sum of bounded risks, not a single catastrophic risk.

### 21.7 A worked example: the Trellis billing decision

Trellis considered a rewrite of its billing subsystem in late 2024. The proposal: replace the legacy 2018-era code with a modern implementation, in a multi-month project.

Asher led the analysis. He applied the §21.5 checklist:

  1. **Is Strangler Fig feasible?** Yes. The billing subsystem had clear boundaries (charge, refund, subscription change, webhook). Each could be migrated independently.
  2. **Is the motivation structural?** Mostly aesthetic. The legacy code worked; it was unpleasant to work in but not blocking new features.
  3. **Sustained commitment?** Unclear. Leadership was open but had not committed to protecting a multi-month rewrite from feature pressure.
  4. **Measurable improvement?** Vague. The team expected "cleaner code"; no specific metrics.
  5. **Same team?** Mostly. The proposed rewrite team was largely the same as the maintenance team.

The conclusion: Strangler Fig was feasible; the rewrite's motivation was aesthetic; commitment was uncertain; improvements were vague. The disciplined answer was the Strangler Fig version (which Chapter 11 described). The team adopted that approach.

The Strangler Fig migration took six months and shipped continuously. The legacy code was deleted at the end. The team's velocity in the billing subsystem improved significantly. A full rewrite would have taken longer, would have lost the legacy's accumulated bug fixes, and would have consumed the team's capacity during a competitive period.

The lesson: rewrite is rarely the right call. When it is tempting, the discipline is to apply the checklist; the answer is almost always Strangler Fig.

### 21.8 The AI-Layer: AI and migration scope

AI changes the migration calculation in specific ways.

#### AI lowers the implementation cost of either approach

For both rewrite and Strangler Fig, AI accelerates the mechanical implementation. A rewrite's implementation cost is lower than it was in 2020; a Strangler Fig's per-piece implementation is also lower. The relative ratio is similar; both options are faster than before.

#### AI does not lower the discovery cost of the legacy's complexity

The legacy's edge cases must still be discovered. AI can read the legacy code and produce a re-implementation, but it does not know which edge cases are load-bearing and which are vestigial. The human's investigation work (Chapter 2 reading discipline) remains.

#### AI changes the verification cost

For both approaches, AI-assisted shadow comparison (per Chapter 11) makes verification more thorough. The team can compare old and new across millions of inputs; differences surface that would have been invisible in human-only verification. Both rewrite and Strangler Fig benefit; Strangler Fig benefits more because the verification per piece is more local.

#### AI does not change the organizational calculus

Sustained commitment, measurable improvements, and team continuity remain the determining factors. AI is a tool; the team and the organization are the system. A rewrite that would have failed in 2020 due to organizational issues would still fail in 2026 with AI assistance; the technical work was not the bottleneck.

#### The 2026 recommendation

For most migrations, Strangler Fig remains the safer choice. AI's speedup applies to both approaches; the Strangler Fig's risk profile is unchanged but its execution speed is faster. The rewrite's risk profile is also unchanged; the speedup does not compensate for the higher variance.

The rare cases where rewrite is justified (§21.2's properties) are largely independent of AI. Some teams will use AI to make a 24-month rewrite into an 18-month rewrite, but the rewrite's success probability is the same; the variance is the same. The disciplined choice is the same.

### 21.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A team lead at your company sends this proposal in Slack: "The `inventory` module is 8 years old, the code is messy, and a new engineer joining the team takes a month to understand it. I propose we spend Q3 rewriting it in TypeScript. Modern stack, clean architecture, faster onboarding." You have read Spolsky's "Things You Should Never Do, Part I." Construct a one-paragraph reply that surfaces _each_ of Spolsky's three concerns specifically against this proposal, and propose the next conversation the team should have.

Hint

Spolsky's three: (1) code embodies bug fixes, (2) rewrites consume capacity, (3) rewrites are usually overconfident. Each applies here.

**Answer:**

Suggested reply:

> "I think there's a real problem with new-engineer onboarding, but I want to push back on rewrite as the answer because of three patterns I have seen go wrong before. **First** , 8 years of inventory-module code includes 8 years of bug fixes; every special case for a customer SKU edge case, every workaround for a vendor quirk, every defensive check after the 2020 incident. A rewrite re-encounters all of those bugs in production from scratch; the 'cleaner' code we ship will re-discover them over months. **Second** , Q3 of rewrite is a quarter of no shipping in `inventory`, while competitors and customer requests keep coming. The opportunity cost is real, and our experience with prior rewrites (the 2023 auth-service rewrite stretched from 'one quarter' to two and shipped with regressions) suggests we may be over-confident about the timeline. **Third** , the onboarding-difficulty problem may actually be a _naming_ and _documentation_ problem more than a _code-shape_ problem. Renaming 30 misleading classes (Chapter 18) and writing a few ADRs (Chapter 17) would also reduce onboarding time; for two engineer-weeks of work, not a quarter." "Next conversation: let's apply the §21.5 five-criteria checklist to the rewrite proposal honestly. If all five hold, I will support it; if any do not, I propose we evaluate the Strangler Fig alternative or the lighter naming/documentation intervention first."

The reply hits all three Spolsky concerns (bug fixes, capacity, overconfidence), references team-specific evidence (the 2023 auth rewrite), and proposes a constructive next step (apply the checklist) rather than just blocking. This is the disciplined response: not "no" but "let's verify the case before we commit."

**Q2.** Three rewrite proposals come up in industry discussion. For each, apply the §21.2 success-properties test (structural impossibility / sustained commitment / measurable improvement / same team continuity) and decide: is this case more likely to succeed or fail? What is the riskiest property in each?

(a) A 12-engineer startup proposes to rewrite their Ruby-on-Rails monolith into Go because "RoR doesn't scale and Go is the future." The CEO is excited; the existing team will lead it; the migration is estimated at 4 months. No specific scaling problem has hit them yet.

(b) Netflix in ~2010 announced they would migrate from Oracle databases to Cassandra over 18 months. The migration was driven by Oracle's licensing costs being projected to exceed Netflix's affordable infrastructure spend within 2 years; the same database team led the migration; executive commitment was explicit; specific metrics (cost per query, scaling characteristics) defined success.

(c) A 200-engineer enterprise SaaS company hires a new VP of Engineering who proposes rewriting their 6-year-old Java backend in Rust to "modernize the stack." A new team is hired to do the rewrite; the existing team continues to maintain the Java codebase during the migration; timeline is "as long as it takes."

Hint

Strong proposals have all four properties; weak ones miss one or more. Apple silicon and Netflix Oracle-to-Cassandra had all four; most failed rewrites had one or two.

**Answer:**

**(a) Likely to fail.** Only same-team holds. Structural impossibility (no, RoR scales fine for most companies; the rewrite is hype-driven), measurable improvement (no, "doesn't scale" is not a metric), and sustained commitment (weak, 12-engineer startup will let feature pressure interfere) all fail. Riskiest: _measurable improvement_ — without a target the team will not know when they have succeeded.

**(b) Strong proposal, succeeded historically.** All four hold: Oracle licensing was structurally incompatible with Netflix's growth; cost-per-query and scaling were measurable; executive backing was explicit; the database team migrated their own work.

**(c) Likely to fail.** Three of four are weak. Structural impossibility (no, aesthetic); measurable improvement (vague); sustained commitment (split between rewrite team and maintenance team); same team (**no** — new team hired). The same-team violation is the most directly fatal: the new team does not know the Java codebase's edge cases and will discover them in production, late and expensively.

**The general lesson** : all four properties must hold (per §21.5). Most failed rewrites had two or three. The same-team property is the most commonly violated and the most directly fatal; if you have to bring in a "rewrite team" separate from the maintenance team, the rewrite has already started losing.

**Q3.** When is a rewrite a worse choice than a Strangler Fig migration that would accomplish the same goal?

Hint

Compare the risk profiles.

**Answer:** Almost always. The rewrite has higher variance: the project either succeeds spectacularly or fails catastrophically. The Strangler Fig has lower variance: each piece's risk is bounded; the team ships throughout; the legacy is the safety net.

For the rewrite to be justified, the Strangler Fig must be _infeasible_. The threshold for infeasibility is high: even messy legacy systems are usually amenable if the team is willing to design the abstraction.

The narrow cases where rewrite is right (§21.2) involve genuine structural impossibility (the legacy cannot be extended at all) or specific organizational support that makes the variance acceptable. Most cases that _feel_ like they justify rewrite actually do not; the disciplined answer is Strangler Fig.

**Q4.** Why does the chapter argue that AI does not change the rewrite-vs-Strangler-Fig calculation?

Hint

§21.8's reasoning.

**Answer:** AI lowers the implementation cost of both approaches; the relative ratio is similar. AI does not lower the discovery cost of legacy complexity, the verification cost of large changes, or the organizational cost of multi-month projects. The factors that determine which approach succeeds are largely AI-independent.

A rewrite that would have failed in 2020 due to organizational issues (lack of commitment, aesthetic motivation, vague improvements) would still fail in 2026 with AI. The 2026-honest position is that AI accelerates execution but does not change the strategic choice; the discipline of preferring Strangler Fig is the same.

The single caveat: AI makes large-scale shadow comparison much cheaper, which helps both approaches but is more impactful for Strangler Fig (where the comparison is local) than for rewrite (where the comparison is end-to-end). This shifts the relative attractiveness slightly toward Strangler Fig, not toward rewrite.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Apply the §21.5 checklist to a hypothetical rewrite proposal for a subsystem in your codebase. Score each question; based on the scores, recommend rewrite, Strangler Fig, or "do nothing."

Hint

The checklist is binary per question; the recommendation depends on the count.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common pattern:

* Strangler Fig feasible? Usually yes.
* Structural motivation? Often no; the motivation is aesthetic or generic.
* Sustained commitment? Uncertain; rarely fully committed.
* Measurable improvement? Often vague.
* Same team? Often yes for small companies, no for larger ones.

The recommendation is usually Strangler Fig. If all five answers favor rewrite, rewrite is defensible; otherwise, Strangler Fig. The checklist's discipline is to slow down the "let's rewrite" impulse and force explicit answers.

**Q6.** Analyze a past rewrite (in your team's history or a public case) using the §21.2 and §21.3 frameworks. Did it succeed or fail? Which properties of the success or failure cases apply?

Hint

Public cases (Netscape, Twitter, etc.) have written analyses to reference.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common pattern for public cases: most public rewrites that succeeded match §21.2's properties; most that failed match §21.3's. The retrospective analysis confirms the framework; the framework is itself confirmed by the retrospectives.

For team-internal rewrites: the analysis often reveals that the team's prior rewrites failed for the reasons §21.3 names; the team is better positioned to avoid those reasons in future migrations.

**Q7.** Plan a Strangler Fig migration for the same subsystem you considered in Q5. Identify the pieces; sequence them; estimate the timeline. Compare to what a rewrite of the same scope would entail.

Hint

The Strangler Fig should be shorter or comparable, with much lower variance.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A common pattern:

* The Strangler Fig timeline is comparable to the rewrite's optimistic estimate (e.g., both estimated 6 months).
* The Strangler Fig's actual timeline tracks the estimate closely (variance bounded per piece).
* The rewrite's actual timeline is often 1.5-3× the estimate (variance is the full project).
* The Strangler Fig ships continuously; the rewrite has no shipping until completion.

The lesson: the rewrite's _expected value_ is similar to the Strangler Fig's; the rewrite's _variance_ is much higher. For most teams, the lower variance is worth the marginal additional cost of the Strangler Fig.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Defend or refute Joel Spolsky's "Things You Should Never Do, Part I" position in 2026 light. Have AI assistants and modern tooling changed the calculation?

**Discussion:** For Spolsky's position: the fundamental arguments (code embodies bug fixes, rewrites consume capacity, rewrites are overconfident) are about code and organizations, not about tooling. AI does not change them. The rewrites that fail in 2026 fail for the same reasons rewrites failed in 2002.

Against: AI lowers the cost of re-implementation, which makes some rewrites feasible that were not in 2002. AI's shadow comparison capability lowers the verification risk. AI's code-generation speed compresses the rewrite's "no shipping" period. The calculation shifts marginally toward rewrites being more feasible.

The synthesis: Spolsky's argument was correct in 2002 and remains largely correct in 2026. AI shifts the calculation by maybe 10-20% in favor of rewrites; the rare cases where rewrites are right become slightly less rare. For the vast majority of cases, Strangler Fig remains the correct call. The discipline of preferring Strangler Fig is durable; the marginal cases where rewrite is justified are still narrow.

The 2026-honest position: Spolsky's essay remains required reading; AI does not make it obsolete; the temptation to rewrite is still mostly wrong; the discipline of preferring incremental migration is more important than ever as the volume of legacy code grows.

**Q9.** Trace the relationship between Chapters 19, 20, and 21. How do they together form a complete debt-management discipline?

**Discussion:**

* **Chapter 19** (debt as concept) provides the _vocabulary and measurement_ : what debt is, how to classify it, how to quantify it. Without this, the team cannot talk about debt or measure its cost.
* **Chapter 20** (Boy Scout, Strangler Fig, Branch by Abstraction) provides the _incremental remediation patterns_ : how to pay back debt without big disruptions. Most debt should be addressed this way.
* **Chapter 21** (the rewrite question) provides the _escape valve and its limits_ : when (rarely) the incremental approach is wrong; the discipline for evaluating the rare cases.

Together: the team measures debt (Chapter 19), addresses it incrementally (Chapter 20), and reserves the rewrite option for the rare cases where the incremental approach is genuinely infeasible (Chapter 21).

The completion: a team with this full discipline keeps debt under control without overinvesting in cleanup. The codebase ages well; features ship continuously; the variance is bounded. The team that lacks any one of the three runs into trouble: without Chapter 19, they cannot prioritize; without Chapter 20, they cannot execute incrementally; without Chapter 21, they over-rewrite. With all three, the discipline is complete.

This is the durable craft of 2026 codebase stewardship. Part IX (Chapters 22-23) takes the next step: the AI-collaborative workflow itself and the working engineer's synthesis. With Part VIII in place, the codebase is sustainable; with Part IX, the engineer working in it is sustainable too.

### Chapter Summary

Spolsky's argument (§21.1) against rewrites: code embodies bug fixes; rewrites consume capacity; rewrites are usually overconfident. The argument survives 2026 scrutiny.

The successful rewrites (§21.2) share specific properties: structural impossibility motivation, sustained organizational commitment, measurable substantial improvement, same team continuity. Netflix Oracle-to-Cassandra and Apple silicon are canonical examples.

The failed rewrites (§21.3) share inverse properties: aesthetic motivation, limited commitment, vague improvements, different team. Netscape 1998-2000 is canonical.

The Strangler Fig (§21.4) is the safer alternative for most cases. It substitutes incremental risk for catastrophic risk; the variance is bounded; the team ships throughout.

The §21.5 checklist disciplines the rewrite-vs-Strangler decision: feasibility, motivation, commitment, measurability, team continuity. Five strong yeses justify rewrite; fewer favor Strangler Fig.

The Trellis billing example (§21.7) illustrates the discipline applied: rewrite was tempting but the checklist favored Strangler Fig; the team chose Strangler Fig; the migration succeeded.

The AI-Layer (§21.8): AI lowers implementation cost for both approaches but does not change the strategic calculation. The factors that determine success are largely AI-independent.

Chapter 22 opens Part IX (AI-Collaborative Workflow + Synthesis) with the 2026 AI workflow itself: agent mode, the spec-driven workflow, the prompting patterns that work, the CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md convention, and the AI code-review tools. With Parts III-VIII covering the craft disciplines, Part IX integrates them with the AI collaborator who is now part of the team.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Apply the §21.5 five-criteria checklist the next time someone proposes a rewrite.** All five must hold; if any one is weak, the answer is Strangler Fig. The checklist is the historical bar that distinguishes the rewrites that shipped from the ones that did not.
  2. **Re-read Spolsky's "Things You Should Never Do, Part I" essay every time you are tempted by a rewrite.** It is short. It is old. It is right more often than not. Twenty-three years after publication, the failure mode it described is still the dominant failure mode of proposed rewrites in 2026.
  3. **When you do greenlight a rewrite, demand the same engineers who maintained the legacy lead it.** The other engineers in the room will discover the legacy's complexity _during_ the rewrite; at the worst possible time. The team-continuity criterion is the most overlooked, and the one most likely to sink an otherwise-justified rewrite.

---

## Chapter 22: Coding with AI — The 2026 Workflow

In April 2026, Aki shipped a 1,200-line feature in one afternoon. The feature was a new pricing rule type for Helix's customers: a usage-based tier with rollover credits. Aki wrote no code herself; she did what she now did at the start of every non-trivial agent session: wrote the failing tests first, refused to open Claude Code until they were red. The habit had formed the hard way the previous autumn, when an agent had produced six hundred plausible-looking lines that passed her shallow tests and still managed to corrupt webhook state in staging; the recovery took two days and a Sunday. Eight tests this time. Only then did she open the agent in agent mode and let it work. Two hours later, the implementation passed all eight tests. Aki reviewed every commit (the agent had produced eleven), strengthened two tests with property-based variants, asked the agent to refactor one section that had drifted from the codebase's style, and merged.

The same feature in 2022 would have taken Aki a week of writing, reviewing, debugging, and iterating. The compression was not 5× because Aki was faster; she was actually slower at the coding-keyboard step. The compression was 5× because the agent did the coding-keyboard step, and Aki's time was concentrated in the parts that compound: writing the spec, reviewing the output, strengthening the tests.

This chapter is about that workflow: agent mode, the spec-driven workflow, the prompting patterns that work, the CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md convention, the AI code-review tools, and the broader pattern of human-as-reviewer / agent-as-implementer. With Chapters 1-21 covering the craft disciplines, this chapter integrates them with the AI collaborator who has become part of the team.

### 22.1 Agent mode

The defining 2026 capability is _agent mode_ : an AI tool that can read your codebase, run commands, write code, run tests, and iterate, all without per-step human approval.

#### Definition: Agent Mode

Agent mode is an AI tool configuration where the agent operates autonomously across multiple steps: it can read files, modify files, execute shell commands, observe results, and decide the next action. The human sets the goal; the agent executes; the human reviews the results.

In plain language: the agent gets to drive for a while; the human watches and steers.

#### Key agent-mode tools in 2026

* **Cursor agent** : integrated into the Cursor editor; specialized for refactoring, feature work, and codebase navigation.
* **Claude Code** : Anthropic's CLI-based agent; well-suited for terminal-driven workflows, complex multi-step tasks, and integration with existing toolchains.
* **Devin** : agentic in a more sandboxed environment; specialized for ticket-to-PR workflows where the agent does the full cycle.
* **v0** : Vercel's UI-focused agent; specialized for frontend work.

Each tool has different strengths. A team might use Cursor agent for IDE-bound work, Claude Code for terminal-bound work, Devin for autonomous ticket execution. The choice depends on the work shape.

> **A note on tool names** : this book names specific tools (here and elsewhere: Cursor, Claude Code, Devin, v0, Greptile, CodeRabbit, GitHub Copilot Review, LaunchDarkly, Statsig, Unleash, Flipt) because they are the ones working engineers actually use in 2026. By 2027–2028, this list will look dated: tools get renamed, acquired, retired; new ones appear. The _shape_ of agent mode (autonomous multi-step execution under human direction) and the _shape_ of an AI review tool (deep codebase indexing, per-line feedback, platform-integrated review) are durable. The specific names are not. When you read this book in 2028, mentally substitute whatever your team is using; the disciplines transfer.

#### What agent mode does well

* **Mechanical multi-step work** : refactoring across many files, applying renames consistently, generating tests from specifications.
* **Self-verification** : running tests, observing failures, fixing the failures, iterating until tests pass.
* **Codebase navigation** : reading many files, understanding patterns, applying the team's conventions (when conventions are specified).
* **Pattern-based implementation** : implementing features that follow a known template the agent has seen.

#### What agent mode does not do well

* **Novel architectural decisions** : the agent does not know your domain, your team's history, or your strategic constraints. The agent picks defaults; humans choose strategy.
* **Investigating non-deterministic bugs** : an agent given "this is flaky" tries the obvious approaches; the genuinely hard bugs require human investigation.
* **Negotiating trade-offs** : the agent does what the spec says; the human decides what the spec should be.
* **Maintaining institutional memory** : the agent forgets between sessions; ADRs and CLAUDE.md are the durable record.

### 22.2 The spec-driven workflow

The dominant 2026 workflow combines tests-as-spec (Chapter 8) with agent mode:

  1. **Human writes the spec** : failing tests that describe the desired behavior.
  2. **Human invokes the agent** : with the spec, the codebase, and any relevant context.
  3. **Agent implements** : writes code, runs tests, iterates.
  4. **Human reviews** : when the agent reports completion, the human reviews the diff line by line.
  5. **Human iterates** : requests changes (rename this, strengthen this test, refactor that section).
  6. **Human merges** : when satisfied.

The workflow's key property is that the human's role is _spec-writing and review_ , not _coding_. The agent does the typing; the human does the thinking.

#### Why the workflow works

Three mechanisms compound:

* **The spec is precise** : failing tests are unambiguous; the agent's success criterion is clear. Vague prompts produce vague code; tests produce specific code.
* **The cycle is fast** : the agent's iteration is seconds; the human's review is minutes; the total feedback loop is much faster than human-only coding.
* **The human's attention is where it should be** : on the spec (what the code should do) and the review (whether the code does it correctly). The mechanics of writing are delegated; the judgment stays with the human.

#### Common Misconception

A common misconception is "spec-driven means the human just writes prompts." The mistake conflates _spec_ with _prompt_. A prompt is natural language; the agent interprets and may misinterpret. A spec is a test that the agent's output must pass; there is no interpretation slack. Spec-driven specifically means failing tests; prompt-driven is a weaker version that produces vaguer code.

A second common misconception is "tests-as-spec works only for pure functions." The mistake is overgeneralizing from the easy cases. Tests-as-spec works for any code with verifiable behavior: HTTP endpoints, database operations, queue consumers. The tests describe inputs and expected outputs; the spec is whatever those tests check.

### 22.3 Prompting patterns that work

The agent's quality depends on the prompt. Some patterns produce better results than others; the patterns are learnable.

#### Pattern 1: Provide context

A prompt with relevant context produces better results than a prompt without. Context includes: the failing test, the relevant existing code, the team's conventions (cite CLAUDE.md), the constraints (do not modify file X, use library Y).

Weak prompt: "Add a function to compute late fees."

Stronger prompt: "Add a function `calculate_late_fee(invoice, current_date)` that returns a Decimal. Specs: see the failing tests in `tests/test_late_fee.py`. The function should follow the conventions in CLAUDE.md (use Decimal not float; raise InvalidInvoiceError for bad inputs; conform to the pure-function style in `core/decisions/`). Do not modify the InvoiceRepository class."

The stronger prompt is more text but produces better output; the agent has the constraints it needs.

#### Pattern 2: Ask for tests

A prompt that includes "and add tests" produces tests; a prompt that does not may not. The test request should be specific: "add property-based tests for invariants" or "add tests for the edge cases (negative amount, zero amount, currency mismatch)."

#### Pattern 3: Iterate explicitly

When the agent's first output is partially right, do not start over. Iterate: "the implementation is correct but the test for currency mismatch is too weak; strengthen it to verify the specific error message."

The agent handles iteration well. The human's role is to identify what needs improvement and to ask specifically.

#### Pattern 4: Constrain scope

A prompt with broad scope produces broad output, often beyond what was intended. A prompt with narrow scope produces narrow output.

Broad: "Refactor the billing module to be cleaner."

Narrow: "In `billing/refund.py`, apply Extract Function to lines 45-72; name the new function `compute_proration_for_partial_refund`. Do not modify other files."

The narrow version is reliable; the broad version is risky.

#### Pattern 5: Ask for the plan first

For non-trivial work, ask the agent to propose a plan before implementing. "What steps would you take to add the rollover-credit feature? Do not implement yet; propose the steps."

The plan reveals the agent's understanding. If the plan is wrong, refine it before implementation. If the plan is right, "implement the plan."

#### Patterns that do not work

* **Vague prompts** : "Make this better" produces unpredictable results.
* **No context** : prompts that do not reference the codebase produce generic code that does not fit.
* **No success criterion** : prompts without failing tests or measurable outcomes produce code the human cannot evaluate easily.
* **One-shot over multi-step** : trying to fit everything in one prompt for a multi-day project is fragile; break into steps with reviews between.

### 22.4 The CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md convention

A pattern that emerged in 2025 and consolidated in 2026: a file at the project root (typically named `CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md`, or both) that the agent reads at the start of every session.

#### Definition: The CLAUDE.md Convention

CLAUDE.md (or AGENTS.md) is a project-root file that captures the team's conventions, anti-patterns, and context for AI agents. The agent reads it before any session; the conventions become the agent's defaults for the project.

In plain language: write down what you want the agent to know; the agent reads it; the agent's outputs match the team's expectations.

#### What belongs in CLAUDE.md

A useful CLAUDE.md typically includes:

* **Codebase orientation** : what the project is, where the key folders are, how to run tests.
* **Naming conventions** : §18.5's discipline made explicit.
* **Test conventions** : which framework, where fixtures live, which style of doubles to use (§7.6).
* **Pattern preferences** : §13.5's discipline made explicit.
* **Anti-patterns** : things never to do (use Singleton, use Visitor, etc.).
* **Architectural shape** : hexagonal or other; where ports live, where adapters live (§14.6).
* **Git conventions** : commit message format, branching, PR template (§4.6).
* **Specific gotchas** : areas of the codebase that the team has learned the hard way.

#### Why it works

Without CLAUDE.md, the agent's defaults reflect training data: generic names, common patterns, popular libraries. With CLAUDE.md, the agent's defaults shift toward the team's specific conventions. The output is more consistent and requires less correction in review.

The file is also a _durable artifact_ (Chapter 17): a new engineer reading it learns the conventions; an old engineer updating it captures recent decisions. The file serves both the agent and the humans; it is the team's living onboarding document.

#### Maintaining CLAUDE.md

A few practices for keeping it useful:

* **Update when conventions change** : a team decision that affects the codebase should land in CLAUDE.md as part of the same PR.
* **Update when the agent produces wrong output** : if the agent does X when the team wants Y, the gap is a CLAUDE.md gap; update.
* **Review periodically** : quarterly or per-release, read the file end-to-end; remove stale entries; consolidate redundancy.
* **Keep it short** : aim for under 300 lines. Longer files are skipped by the agent (and by humans); shorter files are read.

### 22.5 AI code review tools (revisited)

Chapter 15 introduced AI code review tools (Greptile, CodeRabbit, Copilot Review). In the 2026 workflow, these tools are part of the spec-driven cycle: the agent produces the PR; the AI review tool checks it; the human reviews after the AI has surfaced tactical issues.

#### How the tools fit the workflow

* **Greptile** : deep codebase indexing; understands cross-file context; well-suited for catching consistency issues across the codebase.
* **CodeRabbit** : per-line feedback; faster turnaround; well-suited for tactical issues like null checks, error handling, common bug patterns.
* **Copilot Review** : integrated with GitHub Workflows; tight coupling to the platform.

Each tool has strengths and weaknesses; teams typically adopt one based on integration and PR shape. The choice is mostly path-dependent (the team's existing tooling) and somewhat workflow-dependent (the PR's typical content).

#### The combined review workflow

A 2026 PR with both an implementing agent and a reviewing AI tool:

  1. Human writes spec.
  2. Agent implements; PR is opened.
  3. CI runs; tests pass.
  4. AI review tool posts comments on the PR.
  5. Human reads the AI review's comments; addresses easy ones immediately (often by asking the agent to revise).
  6. Human reviews the PR substantively, applying the Thread C checklist (Chapter 15).
  7. Human merges when satisfied.

The total time is significantly less than human-only review of a human-implemented PR. The bottleneck is the human's review; everything else is fast.

### 22.6 The agent-commits-the-PR workflow

A 2025-2026 emerging pattern: the agent opens the PR, not just implements the code.

#### The workflow

  1. Human writes a ticket or spec.
  2. Human invokes the agent in PR mode with the spec.
  3. Agent works on a branch, makes commits, runs tests, writes a PR description, opens the PR.
  4. Human reviews the PR like any other.
  5. Human and agent iterate via PR comments.
  6. Human merges.

The agent acts as a junior engineer who takes a ticket from the queue and produces a PR. The human acts as the senior engineer who reviews and shepherds the PR to merge.

#### What this enables

A team running this workflow can have many parallel PRs in flight, each driven by an agent, each reviewed by a human. The team's throughput is multiplied; the human's role concentrates on review.

#### What this requires

The team must have:

* A clear ticket-writing discipline (the agent's input is the ticket; vague tickets produce bad PRs).
* A CLAUDE.md with strong conventions (the agent's output reflects the conventions).
* Strong review discipline (the human's review is the quality gate; weak review produces a flood of pattern-tax PRs).
* Infrastructure for many parallel branches (CI capacity, merge queue, conflict management).

Without these, the workflow produces volume without quality. With them, it produces throughput.

#### When the workflow does not fit

* Critical changes that need a human author: security fixes, complex architectural changes, business-critical features.
* Novel work where the spec is uncertain: the agent does what it is told; uncertain specs produce uncertain output.
* Small teams that cannot scale review: more PRs need more reviewers; a team of three engineers cannot review 30 agent PRs per day.

For these, the human-as-implementer workflow remains. The agent-commits-PR is one pattern, not a universal default.

#### A position worth taking: do not adopt agent-commits-PR on teams under ten engineers

The workflow's payoff is throughput; many agent-PRs in flight, each reviewed in parallel. Below roughly ten engineers, the team does not have the reviewer capacity to consume the throughput; the agent's PRs queue, the reviews degrade under volume pressure, and the unreviewed-plausibility debt (Chapter 19) accumulates faster than the team can pay it back. The workflow that _looks_ like a force-multiplier for a small team is, in practice, a debt-multiplier.

The recommendation: small teams (under ten engineers) should use the agent in pair-programming mode (Chapter 16 §16.5), where the human is the author of each PR and the agent is the assistant. The agent-commits-PR pattern earns its keep when there are enough humans to keep up with what it produces. Set the threshold explicitly; do not drift into it.

### 22.7 Anti-patterns of AI-assisted engineering

A few patterns to avoid:

#### Trusting the agent's "tests pass"

The agent reports tests pass. The reviewer skims; merges. The tests were AI-generated; they pass because they happen to test what the implementation does, not what the requirements specify. The implementation is wrong; the tests are wrong; the PR is wrong; the production is wrong.

The discipline: read the diff, including the tests. The reviewer's understanding is the quality gate, not the agent's.

#### Accepting first drafts as final

The agent produces a first draft in seconds. The human accepts it because it works. But the first draft is rarely the best version; iteration improves it. Skipping iteration produces lower-quality code at the same speed.

The discipline: iterate at least once. Ask the agent to improve specific aspects; the second draft is reliably better.

#### Letting the agent design

The agent is asked to "decide what architecture to use." The agent picks defaults that may not fit the team. The codebase drifts toward generic patterns; the team's distinctive design is lost.

The discipline: the human designs; the agent implements. Architectural decisions belong in ADRs (Chapter 17); the agent reads the ADR and applies the decision.

#### CLAUDE.md neglect

The team writes a CLAUDE.md and never updates it. The agent's outputs reflect outdated conventions; the team's reviewers correct each PR individually instead of updating the file.

The discipline: update CLAUDE.md as part of the PRs that exercise new conventions. The file is a living artifact, not a one-time setup.

#### Ignoring the cost of agent invocations

A team that adopts the agent-commits-PR workflow (§22.6) at scale is making a recurring API-cost commitment that is easy to underestimate. At 2026 LLM pricing, a single agent session for a moderate-sized feature is not free; a team running dozens of such sessions per engineer per week sees a real line item on their cloud bill. The trap is that the cost lands on a different budget than the one the engineering manager controls, so it stays invisible until finance asks.

The discipline: at minimum, track agent-session cost per engineer per week and surface it on the same dashboard as the productivity gains. Make the trade-off explicit. If the cost-per-shipped-feature dwarfs the savings-in-engineer-time, the workflow is not paying back for your team's cost basis; reconsider.

#### Sending sensitive code to third-party AI providers

A subtler anti-pattern: the team uses an external AI provider (cloud-hosted Claude, GPT, Cursor) without considering what the provider sees. Source code is sent; the provider may log; the logs may be retained; the retention may exceed your contract with your own customers about data residency. Regulated environments (healthcare, finance, EU customers under GDPR) and codebases containing trade secrets are particularly exposed. "We use the enterprise tier with logging disabled" is the right starting point, not the end of the conversation.

The discipline: confirm with your security and legal team which provider, which tier, and which regions are approved before adopting agent mode broadly. For sensitive codebases, evaluate self-hosted or in-region inference; for codebases with no special sensitivity, document the choice in an ADR (Chapter 17) so the next engineer does not re-derive the question.

#### Treating the disciplines as temporary scaffolding

The deepest anti-pattern of all: assuming the disciplines this chapter describes are workarounds for current limitations and will become unnecessary as agents improve. They are not. They are the workflow that maximizes the team's value regardless of the agent's capabilities. A future agent that is twice as good still benefits from clear specs, good CLAUDE.md, and disciplined review; more so, because the agent will produce more output that needs reviewing. The disciplines do not become obsolete as the model improves; they become more leveraged. Teams that wait for "the agent to get good enough" before adopting the disciplines arrive at the future without the muscle memory; teams that adopt the disciplines now compound advantages as model capability grows.

* * *

**Failure case study: when agent-commits-PR went wrong at a fictional fintech**

The disciplines in this chapter prevent specific failure modes. To make those concrete: a fictional fintech (call them "Veridian"), 8-engineer team in Q4 2025, adopted the agent-commits-PR workflow (§22.6) without first adopting the supporting disciplines. The story is composite (every element is a thing that has actually happened to teams that have shared their post-mortems publicly in 2025), and the result is the textbook failure case.

**What happened.** Veridian's CTO read about the workflow, was excited, told the team to use it. Within four weeks the team was opening 40+ agent-generated PRs per day across the eight engineers. The reviewer rotation could not keep up; PRs queued; engineers approved at a glance to clear the queue; the merge-then-revert rate climbed to 27%. In week 6, an agent-generated PR introduced a payment-processing change that passed a thin set of agent-written tests but had a subtle off-by-one in interest accrual (Thread C Lie #5). The bug shipped, accrued over a weekend, and required a five-figure customer reimbursement. The post-mortem revealed: no CLAUDE.md (the agent was inventing conventions from scratch each session), no mutation testing on the changed module, the reviewer who approved the PR spent under three minutes on it because their queue had eleven other waiting PRs.

**What the team did next.** They paused agent-commits-PR for a quarter. Wrote a CLAUDE.md. Added mutation testing on the payments module. Reinstated the Thread C checklist (Chapter 15) as a mandatory review step. Switched, for the moment, to the pair-programming-with-AI mode (Chapter 16 §16.5) where the human is the PR author and the agent is the assistant. By Q2 2026 they tentatively returned to agent-commits-PR, but only on modules with mutation score above 75%, with a documented CLAUDE.md, with PR-size limits enforced (no agent PR over 600 lines without an explicit waiver). Merge-then-revert rate dropped to 4%.

**The lesson.** Agent-commits-PR is a force multiplier when the supporting disciplines are in place. Without them it is a debt accelerator. The §22.6 recommendation ("not under ten engineers") is not arbitrary; it is the threshold below which the supporting disciplines cannot keep up with the throughput. Veridian's mistake was sequencing: adopting the workflow before adopting the disciplines. Teams reading this should sequence correctly: disciplines first, workflow second, throughput third.

This failure case is included because the book otherwise reads as a sequence of success stories. The disciplines work; teams that skip them produce stories like Veridian's. Both halves are true; both halves belong in the book.

* * *

### 22.8 Working example: a Helix feature shipped in one afternoon

The opening vignette in detail. Aki's task: add a new pricing rule type (usage-based with rollover credits) to Helix.

#### Step 1: Write the tests (30 minutes)

Aki opens `tests/test_usage_based_pricing.py` and writes eight failing tests:
    
    
    def test_usage_below_tier_charges_at_unit_price(): ...
    def test_usage_at_tier_boundary_uses_lower_price(): ...
    def test_rollover_credit_consumed_before_new_charges(): ...
    def test_rollover_credit_expires_after_12_months(): ...
    def test_negative_usage_raises_error(): ...
    def test_zero_usage_returns_zero_charge(): ...
    def test_currency_consistency_with_customer(): ...
    def test_property_based_total_equals_charge_minus_credits(): ...
    

The tests describe the rule's behavior precisely. Aki runs them; all eight fail because the implementation does not exist.

#### Step 2: Invoke the agent (5 minutes)

Aki opens Claude Code in agent mode. The prompt:

"Implement the usage-based pricing rule type with rollover credits. Specs: failing tests in `tests/test_usage_based_pricing.py`. Conventions: CLAUDE.md. Code lives in `core/pricing/rules/`. Use Decimal not float. Use property-based testing where applicable. Make all tests pass."

#### Step 3: Agent works (90 minutes of agent time, 0 minutes of Aki time)
