The agent reads CLAUDE.md, the failing tests, and the existing rules in `core/pricing/rules/`. It implements:

* A `UsageBasedRule` class in `core/pricing/rules/usage_based.py`.
* A helper `apply_rollover_credits(usage, credits, current_date)` as a pure function.
* A registration of the new rule type in the rules registry.
* 12 commits, each atomic, with Conventional Commits messages.

The agent runs the tests; all eight pass. The agent reports completion.

#### Step 4: Aki reviews (45 minutes)

Aki reads each commit. The implementation looks reasonable; the tests pass; the style matches the codebase. Aki applies the Thread C checklist:

* Untested edge cases: the agent did not test what happens when rollover credits exceed usage (negative effective usage). Aki adds a test; the test passes (the implementation handles it correctly, but the test was missing).
* Over-abstraction: none observed.
* Inconsistent style: one variable named `c` for clarity in a comprehension; Aki leaves it (acceptable for short scope).
* Wrong error handling: the agent raises `ValueError` for negative usage; the codebase convention is `InvalidUsageError`. Aki asks the agent to fix; the agent fixes in one commit.
* Off-by-one: the rollover-credit-expires-after-12-months test uses `<` and the implementation uses `>=`. Aki investigates: the implementation is correct, the test was using `<` to test the boundary at exactly 12 months. The semantics align.
* Hallucinated APIs: none observed.

Aki strengthens the property-based test with one additional invariant. Asks the agent to add the new test; the agent does. The total work in this step: 45 minutes.

#### Step 5: Merge (5 minutes)

Aki runs the tests one more time; opens the PR; the AI review tool posts comments; Aki addresses the easy ones; merges.

#### Total time: about two and a half hours of Aki's time

For a 1,200-line feature with full tests, property-based coverage, conventional commits, and a clean PR. In 2022, the same feature would have taken Aki perhaps a week of focused work. The compression is not magical; it is the result of the workflow:

* Aki's time is concentrated where it has the most leverage (spec, review).
* The agent's time handles the mechanical bulk.
* The codebase's preparation (CLAUDE.md, conventions, hexagonal shape) allows the agent to produce code that fits.

The lesson: the 2026 workflow is not "AI replaces engineers"; it is "AI multiplies engineers who have invested in the supporting disciplines." Engineers without CLAUDE.md, without review discipline, without test discipline cannot get this multiplier. Engineers who have invested in those disciplines see the multiplier directly.

### 22.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A teammate has been using AI for 3 years. They describe their workflow: "I write a function header, then I write `# what this should do` as a comment, and Copilot autocompletes the body line by line. I review each suggestion before pressing Tab. Sometimes the suggestion is wrong and I fix it manually." They ask whether this is "agent mode." Walk through the distinction between their workflow and agent mode in 2026: what is each one good at, what risks each carries, and when each is the right choice.

Hint

Per-step approval (autocomplete) vs autonomous multi-step execution (agent mode). Both have their place.

**Answer:** The teammate's workflow is **autocomplete-with-human-approval-per-step** , not agent mode. Both are useful; they fit different work.

**Their workflow's properties:**

* The human reviews and accepts/rejects each suggestion before it lands.
* Failure mode: a wrong suggestion gets rejected immediately; no downstream effect.
* Cycle time: per-line (seconds to a minute per suggestion).
* Best for: small, well-understood code where the human's mental model is complete; the AI is producing the next line, not the next _decision_.

**Agent mode's properties:**

* The human sets a goal (often a failing test); the agent autonomously reads files, writes code, runs the test suite, iterates, and reports back when done.
* Failure mode: a wrong implementation can land along with the right ones; the human's review is at the _end_ , not at each step.
* Cycle time: minutes to hours per task.
* Best for: well-scoped multi-step work where the goal can be precisely stated (tests-as-spec, mechanical refactors across many files, applying a known pattern).

**The trade-off is _speed-to-completion versus per-step-control_.** Autocomplete is slower per task but the human catches errors immediately; agent mode is faster but the human's catching is concentrated at review time, and bad code can ship if the review is shallow.

**When each is the right choice:**

* **Autocomplete** wins when:

    * The work is novel or in unfamiliar code (the human is building their own mental model as they go).
    * The work touches sensitive code (security, payments, data) where each line matters.
    * The agent's training data is weak in this area (rare languages, internal frameworks).
* **Agent mode** wins when:

    * The work is mechanical and the goal is precisely specifiable (failing tests, named refactoring move).
    * The work spans many files in a uniform way (rename across 87 call sites; apply pattern X across module Y).
    * The human is the reviewer; the spec is sharp; the cycle of "test → agent → review" is faster than typing the implementation.

Most working engineers use both. The teammate is doing autocomplete well; they may benefit from adding agent mode for the specific kind of work where the spec is precise enough. Neither replaces the other; the discipline is choosing per task.

**Q2.** What is the spec-driven workflow, and why does it produce better results than prompt-driven workflows?

Hint

The spec is verifiable; the prompt is interpretable.

**Answer:** Spec-driven means the human writes failing tests describing the desired behavior; the agent's job is to make the tests pass. Prompt-driven means the human writes natural-language instructions; the agent interprets them and produces code.

The spec is verifiable: the agent's output either passes the tests or does not. The prompt is interpretable: the agent may misinterpret, produce code that does not match the human's intent, and the human must compare the result against their own mental model.

Spec-driven produces better results because the verification is automatic; the agent's success criterion is unambiguous; iteration converges quickly. Prompt-driven is fuzzier; iteration is harder because each attempt may be interpreting the prompt differently.

The spec-driven workflow is the operationalization of Chapter 8's tests-as-spec applied to AI implementation.

**Q3.** Your team has been using Claude Code agent mode for a month. Each engineer types similar context preludes at the start of every session: "We use pytest. Use Decimal not float. No Singletons. Tests live in tests/. Prefer fakes to mocks." The team has not yet written a CLAUDE.md. What specific problems does this prompt-prelude-per-session pattern create? Construct the case for moving the same content into CLAUDE.md, including what the team _gains_ beyond just "saving keystrokes."

Hint

Prompt preludes are session-local and reinvented each time. CLAUDE.md is durable and team-shared.

**Answer:**

**Problems the prompt-prelude pattern creates:**

  1. **Drift between engineers.** Engineer A types "Use Decimal not float; no Singletons"; Engineer B types "Money values use Decimal; avoid Singletons unless..." The same intent expressed slightly differently produces slightly different agent behavior across the team. The codebase ends up with subtly different conventions in code authored via Engineer A's sessions vs Engineer B's.

  2. **No team review.** Prompt content is invisible to the rest of the team. If Engineer A is missing a key convention ("we always use ULID for IDs, not UUID"), nobody on the team can review their prompt and catch the omission. Each engineer's prompt is their own.

  3. **No version history.** When a convention changes ("we are migrating from float to Decimal for money values; new code should use Decimal"), there is no place where this update lands. Each engineer eventually hears about the change in different ways; some prompts get updated, others don't.

  4. **Length pressure.** Each prompt session has limited context. As preludes grow ("here's the framework, here's the conventions, here's the anti-patterns, here's where things live..."), prompt length eats into the actual task-context budget. The longer the prelude, the less room for the actual task.

  5. **Lost institutional memory.** New engineers must re-derive the prelude by asking around or by trial-and-error with AI sessions. The team's hard-won "do not use library X, it has a known bug" knowledge lives in nobody's prompt and gets re-learned.

**What CLAUDE.md gains beyond keystroke-savings:**

* **Team-reviewable, version-controlled, durable.** The file is a normal git artifact: reviewed in PRs, tracked over time, updated as the team learns. The transition from "Engineer A's lore" to "team's standing context" is the durability lift.

* **The agent reads it once per session.** No prompt-length pressure; the task-context budget is fully available for the actual task.

* **New engineer onboarding.** New engineers read CLAUDE.md and start with the team's full context; same as a senior engineer. The "trial and error" learning curve compresses.

* **Failure-mode learning.** When a Thread C lie sneaks through review and ships a bug, the team updates CLAUDE.md ("we do not import this package; it has been hijacked"). Future AI sessions across the team avoid the same lie. The team's bugs become the team's collective immunity.

* **The file serves humans too.** A CLAUDE.md is also a one-page codebase orientation. Engineers reading it learn the team's conventions; the agent reading it learns the team's conventions; both audiences benefit from the same investment.

The general rule: prompts are tactical; CLAUDE.md is strategic. Tactical context (this specific task's constraints) belongs in the prompt. Strategic context (team conventions, anti-patterns, where things live) belongs in CLAUDE.md. Engineers using only prompts re-invent the strategic context every session; teams that codify it in CLAUDE.md compound the investment.

**Q4.** Name three prompting patterns that work and three that do not.

Hint

§22.3 lists both.

**Answer:**

Work:

* **Provide context** : cite CLAUDE.md, include relevant existing code, name constraints.
* **Ask for tests explicitly** : specify the kind of tests (property-based, edge cases) and the framework.
* **Iterate explicitly** : when partial output is wrong, refine specifically rather than starting over.

Do not work:

* **Vague prompts** : "make this better" produces unpredictable results.
* **No success criterion** : prompts without failing tests or measurable outcomes are hard to evaluate.
* **One-shot for multi-day projects** : fitting too much into one prompt produces fragile execution.

The discipline is to write prompts that maximize the agent's chance of producing the right output. Vague prompts are not the agent's failure; they are the human's failure to specify.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Write or update a CLAUDE.md for your codebase. Include the seven categories from §22.4. Run an AI session before and after; compare the output quality.

Hint

The before-and-after comparison is the value.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* The "before" CLAUDE.md (if any) is generic; the "after" is specific to the codebase's conventions.
* The agent's output before-CLAUDE.md uses generic patterns and names; the output after-CLAUDE.md matches the codebase's conventions.
* The review time per AI-generated PR drops after CLAUDE.md is in place, because there are fewer corrections to make.

The cost of writing CLAUDE.md is modest (a half-day for the first version); the payoff is on every subsequent AI session.

**Q6.** Implement a small feature using the spec-driven workflow. Write the tests first, invoke the agent, review the output. Record the time spent on each step and any issues you encountered.

Hint

The breakdown reveals where the human's time is concentrated.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A typical breakdown for a moderate-sized feature:

* Test-writing: 30-60 minutes.
* Agent invocation and execution: 5 minutes of human time (the agent runs while the human does other work).
* Review: 30-60 minutes.
* Iteration: 15-30 minutes.
* Merge: 5 minutes.

Total human time: 1.5-3 hours for a feature that would take a full day or more without the workflow. The human's time is concentrated in test-writing and review; coding-time is delegated.

**Q7.** Use the agent-commits-PR workflow (per §22.6) for a small ticket. The agent opens the PR; you review. Did the workflow work? Where did it break down?

Hint

The breakdowns are the learning.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* The agent opens a PR with reasonable description, atomic commits, and passing tests.
* The PR may include scope creep (the agent decides to "improve" adjacent code); the human pushes back.
* The PR's tests may have weak assertions that mutation testing would reveal; the human strengthens.
* The agent does not know the team's architectural decisions; the human catches violations of conventions not yet in CLAUDE.md.

Each breakdown is a CLAUDE.md update opportunity. After a few iterations, the workflow's break-down rate drops; the team's CLAUDE.md captures more of the team's tacit knowledge.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "In 2026, the engineer's value is measured by the quality of their specs and reviews, not by the code they write." Counter-argue: "The engineer's value is still measured by understanding the system deeply; that understanding requires writing code, not just reviewing it."

**Discussion:** For specs-and-reviews: the agent handles writing; the human's distinctive contribution is the specification and the review; the value moves up the abstraction stack. The engineer who is best at writing precise specs and rigorous reviews is the most productive in the 2026 workflow.

Against: writing code is how engineers learn the system. An engineer who only specs and reviews never builds the deep mental model that comes from typing through the implementation. The agent does the work; the human's expertise atrophies; the team becomes dependent on the agent in a way that is fragile.

The synthesis: both are partially right. The engineer of 2026 writes less code than the engineer of 2020, but does not write zero code. Writing code remains the way to understand a system deeply; reviewing code is necessary but not sufficient. The right balance is perhaps 30% writing (for understanding, for the parts AI cannot handle), 70% specifying and reviewing (for leverage). The engineer who writes nothing loses the understanding; the engineer who reviews nothing loses the leverage.

The discipline is to _write deliberately_ : when learning a new area, when debugging a hard problem, when designing a novel system. Otherwise, specify and review.

**Q9.** Map the dependency between this chapter and the previous 21. Which earlier chapters does the 2026 workflow most depend on?

**Discussion:**

The workflow's prerequisites:

* **Chapter 1** (the 2026 frame): the workflow makes sense only if you understand why AI changed the equation.
* **Chapter 2** (reading old code): the human's review is reading; the discipline transfers.
* **Chapter 3** (atomic commits): the agent's commits should be atomic; the convention is enforced via CLAUDE.md.
* **Chapter 5-8** (testing): the spec is tests; without test discipline, the workflow cannot work.
* **Chapters 9-11** (refactoring): the agent does mechanical refactors at speed; the human directs and reviews.
* **Chapters 12-13** (patterns): the agent applies patterns; the human catches over-application.
* **Chapter 14** (hexagonal): the agent generates code that fits the shape only if the shape is specified.
* **Chapter 15-16** (review): the human's role is review; the discipline is the chapter's content.
* **Chapter 17** (documentation): ADRs and CLAUDE.md are the durable substrate.
* **Chapter 18** (naming): the agent's names are guided by CLAUDE.md naming conventions.
* **Chapters 19-21** (debt): the workflow accelerates production; without debt discipline, production outruns review.

Every chapter is a prerequisite. The 2026 workflow does not stand alone; it is the integration of every craft skill in the book. Engineers who skip earlier chapters and try to adopt the workflow find that they cannot direct or review the agent well; the workflow produces volume without quality.

The lesson: the AI multiplier is real but requires the multiplied skills. The book's structure (craft first, AI workflow last) reflects this; the book's reader who internalized the early chapters is positioned to maximize the 2026 workflow's leverage.

### Chapter Summary

Agent mode (§22.1) is the defining 2026 capability: AI tools operate autonomously across multiple steps under human direction. Cursor agent, Claude Code, Devin, v0 are the main tools.

The spec-driven workflow (§22.2) combines tests-as-spec with agent mode: human writes failing tests, agent implements, human reviews. The workflow concentrates the human's time on spec-writing and review; the agent handles the mechanics.

Prompting patterns (§22.3) that work: provide context, ask for tests, iterate explicitly, constrain scope, ask for a plan first. Patterns that do not: vague prompts, no context, no success criterion, one-shot for multi-day work.

CLAUDE.md (§22.4) is the project-root convention: a file the agent reads at session start. It captures codebase orientation, conventions, anti-patterns, and gotchas. The file shifts the agent's defaults toward the team's specific norms.

AI code review tools (§22.5) complement the human review: tactical issues at scale, freeing the human to focus on architecture and context.

The agent-commits-PR workflow (§22.6) lets the agent take a ticket and produce a PR; the human reviews. The workflow requires strong ticket discipline, CLAUDE.md, and review capacity; not all teams or tasks fit.

Anti-patterns (§22.7): trusting "tests pass" blindly, accepting first drafts, letting the agent design, neglecting CLAUDE.md. Each has a discipline that prevents it.

The Helix worked example (§22.8): a 1,200-line feature shipped in one afternoon. The human's time is concentrated in spec and review; the agent's time handles the mechanics. The workflow is the integration of every prior chapter's discipline.

Chapter 23 closes the book with the working engineer's craft mental model: the synthesis across all 22 chapters, the three protagonists converging, and the trajectory of craft from 2026 forward.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Write a CLAUDE.md for your codebase this week.** Seven sections: orientation, naming conventions, test conventions, pattern preferences, anti-patterns, architectural shape, Git conventions. Aim for under 300 lines. Every AI session afterward gets your defaults instead of training-data defaults.
  2. **Adopt the spec-driven workflow on your next non-trivial feature.** Failing tests first, agent second, review third. Even if you normally test-after. The cycle is faster than describing the work in prose; the resulting tests are real tests, not coverage filler.
  3. **Sequence correctly: disciplines first, workflow second, throughput third.** Veridian's failure case study is in this chapter for a reason. Teams that adopt agent-commits-PR before adopting Chapter 15's review discipline accelerate debt accumulation; teams that sequence in order compound advantages.

---

## Chapter 23: The Working Engineer's Craft Mental Model + Where Craft Is Going

In March 2026, Trellis, Helix, and Currents collaborated on an unusual project: a joint customer-data migration. A regulatory change required all three companies (which served overlapping customer bases through different products) to update how customer consent was recorded, propagated, and audited. The change was deep: it touched billing systems, data pipelines, audit logs, customer-facing interfaces, and every downstream consumer.

The three companies' staff engineers led the cross-organization effort: Asher Goldman from Trellis, Aki Tanaka from Helix, Carmen Ortiz from Currents. Each brought a different organizational context, a different stack, a different set of constraints. They were also working on different aspects of the same broader problem: Asher coordinated the legacy-codebase implications at Trellis; Aki led the AI-collaborative implementation at Helix; Carmen managed the team-level coordination at Currents. The project completed in five months, on schedule, with no customer-visible incidents.

This chapter is the synthesis. It traces the disciplines from Parts I-IX through that joint project, articulates the working engineer's craft mental model, and considers where craft is going in 2026-2028. With Chapter 22's AI workflow in place and the prior chapters covering the supporting disciplines, this chapter is the book's closing argument: craft in 2026 is not what it was in 2016; the disciplines that compound have shifted; the working engineer who internalizes the new compounding skills compounds over a career.

### 23.1 The seven disciplines that compound

The book has covered many disciplines. The ones that _compound_ over a career, named for the seven core skills the book has developed:

  1. **Tests as spec** (Chapters 5-8): the discipline of writing tests that document and verify intent. Compounds because every well-tested function is a foundation for future change.
  2. **Refactoring under test** (Chapters 9-11): the discipline of restructuring code with a safety net. Compounds because every refactor leaves the codebase a little better than it was, and the safety net keeps the work safe.
  3. **Naming as design** (Chapter 18): the discipline of crystallizing concepts through names. Compounds because every reader of every file benefits forever from a good name.
  4. **ADRs** (Chapter 17): the discipline of recording decisions with reasoning. Compounds because every future engineer who reads an ADR understands the past without re-deriving it.
  5. **Code review as discipline** (Chapter 15): the discipline of catching bugs and architectural drift at the PR level. Compounds because every PR reviewed well is a piece of the codebase that does not need to be reviewed badly later.
  6. **Version-control craft** (Chapters 3-4): the discipline of atomic commits, clean history, conventional commits, feature flags. Compounds because every well-shaped commit is a piece of history that future engineers can navigate.
  7. **AI as collaborator** (Chapter 22): the discipline of directing AI agents through specs and reviews, with CLAUDE.md and disciplined workflows. Compounds because every AI session is more productive when the team's preparation is better.

These seven are the chapter's argument for what to invest in. They are not the only valuable skills (debugging, performance work, security, observability, etc., are also valuable), but they are the ones with the highest compound rate over a 2026-2030 career.

#### Why these seven compound

Each shares two properties:

* **The investment is per-PR or per-decision, not per-codebase** : the discipline is exercised continuously rather than in big projects.
* **The benefit is durable beyond the model that wrote the code** : AI models will be deprecated; the disciplines persist; the disciplines are what makes the codebase survive model transitions.

The seven are _codebase-permanent_ skills. They produce artifacts (tests, refactors, names, ADRs, reviews, commits, AI-collaboration habits) that compound across the codebase's life. Other skills (language proficiency, framework expertise, vendor SDK knowledge) are codebase-temporary; they depreciate as the technology evolves. The seven compound; the depreciating skills do not.

### 23.2 The three protagonists converge

The book has used Asher, Aki, and Carmen as illustrative figures for three different engineering contexts. The joint migration project is where they converged.

#### Asher's role (Trellis context)

Asher's daily reality is the 8-year codebase. For the joint migration, Asher's job was the _legacy implications_ : which parts of Trellis's existing billing and audit systems would need to change; what the migration cost in his codebase would be; what could be done with Strangler Fig (Chapter 20) and what required deeper rework.

Asher applied the disciplines from earlier chapters:

* **Reading old code** (Chapter 2): two weeks of reading the relevant Trellis subsystems before proposing any changes. He asked his standard question (_what was the author worried about?_) at every defensive check, every magic constant, every load-bearing comment in the legacy consent code. The question saved the team an estimated week of investigation that would otherwise have gone into "fixing" code that was deliberate compatibility shimming.
* **ADR** (Chapter 17): the migration's effect on Trellis was documented in ADR-241, with the alternatives considered (rewrite the audit system; Strangler Fig migration; modify in place).
* **Strangler Fig** (Chapter 20): the chosen approach for the audit system, allowing continuous shipping during the migration.
* **Refactoring under test** (Chapter 10): characterization tests for the existing audit behavior before any changes.
* **Code review** (Chapter 15): every cross-team PR reviewed by Asher and at least one other staff engineer; the Thread C checklist applied rigorously.

#### Aki's role (Helix context)

Aki's daily reality is the AI-collaborative workflow. For the joint migration, Aki's job was the _implementation pace_ : leveraging AI to ship Helix's portion fast, while maintaining quality through review.

Aki applied:

* **CLAUDE.md** (Chapter 22): updated for the migration's conventions (specific consent-record formats, audit-event schemas).
* **Spec-driven workflow** (Chapter 22): each Helix-side change was tested first; the agent implemented; Aki reviewed. Aki kept her habit even for the cross-organization work; no agent session opened until a failing test for that session's intended outcome was already red on her screen. The discipline scaled from solo work to a multi-team migration without modification.
* **Property-based testing** (Chapter 6): the consent-propagation logic was inherently combinatorial; property tests caught edge cases that example tests would have missed.
* **Hexagonal architecture** (Chapter 14): the new consent module was a hexagonal addition to Helix's core, with ports for the data pipeline, the audit service, and the customer-facing API.
* **Naming as design** (Chapter 18): the team's vocabulary for consent-related concepts was agreed and codified before any code was written.

#### Carmen's role (Currents context)

Carmen's daily reality is the team-level workflow at 50-person scale. For the joint migration, Carmen's job was the _coordination_ : making the three-company collaboration possible across teams, time zones, and organizational priorities.

Carmen applied:

* **Async-first engineering** (Chapter 16): the cross-organization communication was almost entirely async; written documents and PR comments were the primary medium; the few synchronous meetings were focused and productive. Her label-first convention spread to the other two companies during the migration: by the third week, Asher's Trellis team and Aki's Helix team had quietly adopted the same `**Blocking:**` / `**Suggestion:**` / `**Decision needed:**` shape for cross-team comments, because Carmen's were so much easier to act on than the alternatives. A small habit, copied; the convergence Carmen most valued.
* **Trunk-based development** (Chapter 4): Currents shipped its portion via small PRs through the migration; trunk-based was the model that scaled to the migration's parallel work.
* **Feature flags** (Chapter 4): the migration's rollout used flags; each company could enable the new consent behavior independently of the others.
* **PR conventions** (Chapter 16): a cross-organization PR template was agreed and used by all three teams; the consistency reduced review friction.
* **AI code review tools** (Chapter 15): all three companies used AI review tools; the tools caught tactical issues across team boundaries.

#### What the convergence shows

The same disciplines applied to three different contexts produced three different but compatible workflows. Asher's heavy reliance on Chapter 2 (reading old code) and Chapter 20 (Strangler Fig) reflects his codebase's age; Aki's heavy reliance on Chapter 22 (AI workflow) reflects her team's AI-native culture; Carmen's heavy reliance on Chapters 4 and 16 (team practices) reflects her team's scale. None of them used all of the book's chapters equally, but each used the chapters that fit their context.

The lesson: the book's disciplines are a _toolkit_. Different engineering contexts emphasize different tools. The 2026 working engineer knows the toolkit and chooses what to apply; the toolkit is not a recipe to follow universally.

* * *

**Thread B;`calculateRefund`, stage 5: five years later (2031)**

The `calculateRefund` function whose evolution we followed through chapters 2, 10, 15, and 19 still exists in 2031. It does not, however, exist as a single function. The Helix billing subsystem grew over five years; pressure accumulated; three distinct concerns inside the function diverged enough that keeping them together became friction. The 2031 shape:
    
    
    # core/refund/validator.py
    class RefundValidator:
        def validate(self, order: Order, request: RefundRequest) -> ValidatedRefundRequest: ...
            # NaN guard, refund-status check, currency consistency,
            # tenant-policy enforcement (added 2028 for the multi-tenant migration).
    
    # core/refund/calculator.py  (pure)
    def compute_refund_amount(order: Order, validated: ValidatedRefundRequest,
                              rules: list[RefundRule]) -> RefundAmount: ...
        # Pure decision: amount + proration + rule attribution.
        # Property-based tested with thousands of generated orders.
    
    # core/refund/persister.py
    class RefundPersister:
        def persist(self, order: Order, amount: RefundAmount) -> RefundResult: ...
            # Serializable transaction (the fix from the 2026 incident).
            # Idempotency key (added 2027 after the retry-storm incident).
            # Event publication to the audit bus (added 2029).
    

The split happened in three ADRs across the five years, each justifying one extraction:

* **ADR-094 (2027)** : extract `RefundValidator`. Rationale: validation logic grew when the tenant-policy concept arrived; combining it with calculation made both harder to test in isolation.
* **ADR-121 (2028)** : extract `compute_refund_amount` as a pure function. Rationale: property-based testing of refund math kept hitting the orchestrator's I/O dependencies; the pure-function extraction was the right move under Chapter 14's FC/IS shape.
* **ADR-156 (2029)** : extract `RefundPersister`. Rationale: the persistence step accumulated three concerns (the 2026 race-condition fix, idempotency, audit events) that did not belong with computation.

What the five-year story shows: a function that started as a 55-line monolith in 2025 is not "still the same function, refactored prettier" in 2031. It has become _three_ functions with three ADRs explaining each split, and the splits map onto the book's compounding disciplines; characterization tests (Chapter 10) made the first refactor safe; the post-bug debt classification (Chapter 19) drove the persister extraction; the property-based testing pressure (Chapter 6) drove the pure-function extraction; the ADR discipline (Chapter 17) made all three reversible later.

Future maintainers reading the 2031 code do not need to re-derive any of this. The ADRs explain it. The model that wrote the 2025 first draft has been deprecated for years; the model that wrote the 2027 extraction has been deprecated for years; the code is maintainable by humans reading code, which was the point.

This is the long-arc payoff of the disciplines this book has taught. Each chapter's discipline contributed one slice of the five-year journey; the journey is what produces a codebase that does not rot.

* * *

### 23.3 The working engineer's craft mental model

Synthesizing the seven disciplines and the three convergences, the working engineer's mental model in 2026:

#### The three-year question

From Chapter 1: _in three years, when someone debugs this code at 2am, will they thank you or curse you?_ The single most useful framing for any technical decision. Every chapter of the book has applied this question to its discipline.

#### Code as liability, asset as user value

Also from Chapter 1: every line of code is something you must maintain; the asset is what the code does for users. Producing more lines per hour without producing more user value per hour is a net loss. Craft is what keeps the asset-to-liability ratio sane.

#### The bottleneck moved, not disappeared

The 2026 engineering bottleneck is not typing code; it is reviewing, integrating, and maintaining code. AI handles the typing; humans handle the rest. The skills that compound are the ones that operate on the second category.

#### The codebase outlives the model

The AI model that wrote the code in 2024 has been deprecated by 2026. The code remains. The codebase that is maintainable by humans reading code (not by AI continuing to assist) is the codebase that survives model transitions. Craft is what makes code maintainable by humans.

#### Discipline over dogma

Every rule has its reasoning and its when-to-break-it. The engineer who knows when to skip TDD is more valuable than the engineer who applies it religiously; the engineer who knows when to apply TDD is more valuable than the engineer who refuses it on principle. Both extremes are easy; the middle is craft.

#### Long-term thinking

Every chapter has asked "what happens to this in three years?" The compounding disciplines are the ones that pay back over years, not weeks. The working engineer in 2026 invests in long-term compounding skills even when shorter-term skills feel more immediately rewarding.

#### Vocabulary as multiplier

Shared vocabulary (patterns, anti-patterns, principles) makes design conversations fast and precise. The team that has shared vocabulary discusses designs at speeds that teams without it cannot match. The investment in learning the vocabulary pays back over every subsequent conversation.

### 23.4 Where craft is going (2026-2028)

The book has emphasized the 2026 frame. A working engineer should also think about where the disciplines are going.

#### AI-pair-programming as default

By 2027-2028, AI pair-programming will likely be the default for most engineering tasks. Engineers who work without AI assistance will be the exception rather than the rule. The discipline of effective AI collaboration (Chapter 22) will be foundational; engineers without it will be at a measurable disadvantage.

#### Tests-as-spec as the canonical workflow

The spec-driven workflow (Chapter 22) is consolidating. By 2027-2028, writing tests first and asking AI to implement may be the dominant pattern for most backend work. Engineers who can write strong specs will be the most productive.

#### Code review focused on intent, not mechanics

As AI handles more of the mechanical correctness (and AI review tools catch more tactical issues), human review will concentrate on _intent_ : does the code solve the right problem? Does it fit the architecture? Are the trade-offs deliberate? The review skill becomes more strategic, less tactical.

#### The 10x engineer myth dies

The "10x engineer" was an artifact of an era when typing speed and code-mechanics expertise produced large productivity gaps. With AI multiplying everyone's mechanics, the gap shifts to skills that are harder to multiply: strategic judgment, architectural taste, domain expertise. Variance still exists but the shape is different; the "10x" framing becomes obsolete.

#### Sustained codebases as the differentiator

Companies that can sustain a codebase for 5-10 years (Trellis-style) will outperform companies that cannot. The reason: AI-augmented teams ship features faster, so codebases grow faster, so the cost of poor sustainability is paid sooner. The disciplines of this book (testing, refactoring, naming, ADRs, review, debt management) are exactly what enables long-term sustainability.

#### The craft skills compound; the depreciating ones depreciate faster

The trend from Chapter 1's compounding/depreciating table accelerates. Memorizing API signatures, filling in CRUD templates, writing happy-path tests: these become less valuable faster. Reading old code, naming as design, refactoring under test, AI direction: these become more valuable faster. The barbell deepens.

### 23.5 The book's argument in one paragraph

The argument across the book: craft in 2026 is not what it was in 2016. AI generates plausible code at near-zero per-line cost; the cost of reviewing, integrating, and maintaining that code is unchanged. The bottleneck moved from typing to reviewing. The compounding disciplines (tests as spec, refactoring under test, naming as design, ADRs, code review, version-control craft, AI direction) are the ones that pay back over years and that the working engineer should invest in. The depreciating skills (API memorization, CRUD-filling, lint enforcement) are not worth career investment. Every PR is an opportunity to practice the compounding disciplines; every PR is an opportunity to neglect them. Over a five-year career, the difference between the engineer who practiced and the engineer who neglected is enormous. The book has given the disciplines and the case for each; the rest is the reader's practice.

### 23.6 A closing exhortation

The reader who has finished this book has read about a lot of disciplines. The temptation is to try to adopt them all at once. This produces overwhelm and abandonment.

The discipline this book most wants to leave you with: _adopt one at a time_. Pick the discipline you find most compelling; practice it on the next month of work; integrate it into your reflexes. Then pick another. Over a year, you can deeply internalize five or six disciplines; over a career, you internalize all of them and continuously refine.

The working engineer's craft mental model is not built in a week. It is built in years of deliberate practice, one discipline at a time, with feedback from real codebases and real teammates. The book is the map; the practice is the journey.

Carmen Ortiz at Currents said something at the end of the joint migration that the team adopted as a slogan: "Craft is what you do when no one is watching." The agent is not watching; the metrics are not watching; the manager is not watching. You are watching. The discipline you apply when no one is watching is the craft you bring to the work; that craft is what compounds over years and what makes you the kind of engineer the rest of the team wants to work with.

### 23.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A friend three years into their engineering career asks for your honest advice: "I want to invest in becoming a better engineer over the next 5 years. I have limited time; maybe 4 focused hours per week beyond my actual job. Which of the seven compounding disciplines (§23.1) should I invest in first, second, third? Why that order specifically for someone at year three of their career?"

Hint

Some disciplines are prerequisites for others. Some pay back faster. Your friend has limited bandwidth; sequence matters.

**Answer:**

Recommended order for a year-3 engineer with 4 hours/week:

**First: Reading old code + code review as discipline (~3 months).** These are the foundation skills (Chapters 2 + 15). A year-3 engineer is reading more code than they are writing; investing in reading skill compounds on every PR they review and every new module they explore. Practice: pick one open-source codebase to read for 30 minutes each week; in their day job, apply the Thread C checklist (Appendix D) on every AI-generated PR. Returns are immediate: better reviews, better mental models, less time lost to "what does this do?"

**Second: Tests as spec + refactoring under test (~3 months).** Once reading is sharper, the next skill is the prerequisite for safe change. Practice: on every new function, write the failing test first (even if the team does test-after); for legacy refactor work, write characterization tests before changing anything. These two disciplines compound on each other; better testing makes safer refactoring; safer refactoring teaches sharper testing.

**Third: ADRs + naming as design (~3 months).** Now the engineer has read enough code and changed enough code to know which decisions matter. Practice: write one ADR per quarter for a decision the team made; in every PR, rename one misleading name. These two are slower-payoff than the first six months — the leverage is on future readers, not on the engineer doing the work today — but they are what separates a year-5 engineer from a year-3 engineer with the same hours logged.

**Fourth: AI as collaborator + version-control craft (~3 months and ongoing).** These are skills that compound _with_ the other five; doing them first without the foundation produces volume without quality. By month 10, the engineer's review and refactoring instincts can keep up with the speed an AI workflow produces, and their commit history can support the disciplined Git work that makes the rest of the team's life easier.

The order is **reading → testing → reasoning about decisions → AI collaboration**. Each builds on the prior. The mistake to avoid: investing in AI collaboration first (it is shiniest, it feels modern, it shows the fastest "look, I shipped a thing"). Without the foundation, AI collaboration produces unreviewed-plausibility debt (Chapter 19); fast output the engineer cannot maintain. With the foundation, AI collaboration is a force multiplier on durable skills.

At year 8 your friend will look back and notice that the disciplines they invested in early are the ones now feeling like reflexes; the disciplines they put off are the ones still feeling like work. That observation is the compounding.

**Q2.** Open your last merged PR. Apply the three-year question to each substantive change in it: in 2029, when an engineer (possibly not you) debugs this code at 2am during an incident, will they thank you or curse you? For each _thank_ or _curse_ , identify the specific feature of your code that produced the answer. Then propose one concrete change you could make to a future similar PR; _not_ this one, going forward; that would shift at least one curse into a thank-you.

Hint

The three-year question is a personal audit, not a theoretical exercise. Pick the actual PR.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise; the answer is yours. A common pattern from working engineers who do this honestly:

**Likely thank-yous in a well-written PR:**

* A test that names a specific edge case (e.g., `test_refund_with_currency_mismatch`); at 2am the test name tells the engineer what the function is supposed to do.
* A clear name on a function (`validate_refund_eligibility` rather than `check`); the engineer reads the call site and knows the intent.
* An ADR linked from the PR description; when the 2am engineer wonders "why is this so complicated?", the ADR has the answer.

**Likely curses:**

* A magic number with no constant name (`if days_late > 30` rather than `if days_late > LATE_FEE_THRESHOLD_DAYS`); the 2am engineer has to guess whether 30 is a business rule or just a default.
* A function called `process` or `handle` or `data`; semantic generic, no signal about purpose.
* A bug fix with no test; the 2am engineer is debugging a regression; they cannot tell if the regression hit _this_ fix or something else.
* An "ad hoc" workaround with no comment explaining what was being worked around; the 2am engineer is tempted to "clean it up" and re-introduce the original bug.

**One concrete change for future PRs** : pick one of your common curses and commit to fixing it on the next PR. For most engineers, "name one generic variable" or "add one explanatory comment near a non-obvious workaround" is the highest-leverage change to start with. You can practice all the disciplines this book describes by improving one habit at a time across PRs. The three-year question is the personal audit that surfaces which habit to improve next.

The book's argument compressed into one practice: every PR is a letter to a 2029 engineer who may be you. Write accordingly.

**Q3.** Why does the chapter argue that the "10x engineer" framing becomes obsolete in 2026-2028?

Hint

What multiplies what?

**Answer:** The 10x engineer framing assumed that some engineers were 10× more productive than others, primarily through typing speed and code-mechanics expertise. AI multiplies everyone's mechanics; the gap that came from mechanics shrinks.

The gap that remains is in skills that AI cannot multiply: strategic judgment, architectural taste, domain expertise, review discipline, communication. These are real but less amenable to the "10x" framing because their gains are harder to attribute to one person.

The future engineer who is genuinely outstanding is one whose specs are sharper, whose reviews catch more issues, whose architectural decisions are better, whose teams ship more sustainably. The variance still exists but the shape changes from "fast typist" to "wise director." "10x" is not the right vocabulary for the latter.

**Q4.** What is "discipline over dogma," and how does the book apply it?

Hint

Every rule has its reasoning and its when-to-break-it.

**Answer:** Discipline over dogma is the principle that every rule comes with its reasoning and its when-to-break-it clause. The book applies it by treating every discipline as context-dependent: TDD when appropriate (Chapter 8), feature flags when justified (Chapter 4), patterns when applicable (Chapters 12-13), rewrites when rarely (Chapter 21).

The opposite (dogma over discipline) is the failure mode: applying every rule universally, treating exceptions as moral failings, and producing engineers who follow recipes rather than think.

The deeper purpose: engineering is judgment-bound. Rules are heuristics that guide judgment; they are not substitutes for it. The engineer who knows the rules deeply enough to know when to break them is the engineer the book aims to produce.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Take your last quarter of work and audit it against the seven disciplines. For each discipline, rate your practice on a scale of 1-5. What is the lowest-rated discipline? What is one habit you could adopt to raise it?

Hint

The lowest score is the highest leverage.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* High in coding mechanics (refactoring, version-control); lower in the more strategic disciplines (ADRs, naming as design, AI direction).
* The lowest-rated is often the one the engineer has not consciously practiced.
* A specific habit: for whichever discipline is lowest, commit to applying it once per PR for the next month. The habit becomes reflexive after a few weeks of intentional practice.

The deeper lesson: improvement in the lowest-rated discipline produces the most leverage because the other disciplines are already mostly in place; raising the floor is more valuable than raising the ceiling.

**Q6.** Choose one discipline from §23.1 to invest in deeply over the next quarter. Write a plan: what specific habits will you adopt, how will you measure progress, who will give you feedback?

Hint

A specific plan is more durable than a vague intent.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. A reasonable plan structure:

* **Discipline chosen** : e.g., naming as design.
* **Habits to adopt** : in every PR I open, identify at least one name that could be improved and rename it; in every PR I review, comment on at least one naming decision; once a month, lead a small renaming workshop with a teammate.
* **Progress measurement** : track the renames per PR; track the team's general code-clarity perception (survey monthly).
* **Feedback source** : a senior engineer who reviews my PRs and gives feedback on naming quality.

The plan is specific enough to execute; the measurement is concrete enough to evaluate. Adjust at the end of the quarter based on what worked.

**Q7.** Lead a synthesis discussion with your team. Identify the disciplines your team practices well and the ones it neglects. What is the team's plan for the disciplines it neglects?

Hint

Team-level discussion produces team-level commitment.

**Answer:** This is an open exercise. Common findings:

* Teams typically practice 3-5 disciplines well and 2-4 poorly.
* The neglected ones often correlate (a team that does not do ADRs also does not do strong reviews because both require deliberate process).
* The team's plan should address the disciplines that block other improvements: a team that does not do ADRs is making decisions that will be re-debated; addressing ADRs unblocks better debt management, better onboarding, better review.

The team-level discussion produces commitment that individual-level commitment cannot. The disciplines compound at the team level; the practice is collective.

#### Think Deeper

**Q8.** Argue: "By 2030, the craft disciplines this book describes will be largely automated; humans will direct AI at a higher abstraction level." Counter-argue: "The craft disciplines are not automatable because they require human judgment about what good means; they will remain human work."

**Discussion:** Pro-automation: AI has improved rapidly; many tasks that seemed irreducibly human in 2020 are now automated; by 2030, more will be. The craft disciplines (review, naming, ADR-writing) involve judgment but the judgment may be learnable from training data; future AI may handle them well.

Pro-human: judgment is grounded in _context_ that the AI does not have: the team's history, the customer's needs, the company's strategy, the engineer's experience. Even a future AI with broader context will not have the human's lived understanding of the team's decisions. The craft disciplines remain human because the judgment is human.

The synthesis: some craft work will automate over time; some will remain human. The automated portion expands the human's leverage; the human concentrates on the part that cannot be automated. The trajectory is one of _concentration_ , not _replacement_. Engineers who internalize the craft disciplines are positioned to ride the wave: as more of the lower-level work automates, their leverage on the higher-level work increases.

The 2030-honest position: AI will be more capable; the craft disciplines will be more leveraged, not less; the working engineer's value continues to compound. Investing in the disciplines now is investing in a long career.

**Q9.** Trace the book's argument from start to finish. How does each chapter contribute to the synthesis?

**Discussion:**

* **Chapter 1** establishes the frame: AI changed the equation; the disciplines that compound have shifted.
* **Chapter 2** opens the practice: reading old code is the foundation skill.
* **Chapters 3-4** are version-control craft: local and team.
* **Chapters 5-8** are testing: the foundation of all later disciplines.
* **Chapters 9-11** are refactoring: how to improve code safely under test.
* **Chapters 12-14** are patterns and architecture: the vocabulary and frame.
* **Chapters 15-16** are review and team workflow: how the code becomes maintainable across the team.
* **Chapters 17-18** are documentation and naming: how decisions and concepts persist.
* **Chapters 19-21** are debt and migrations: how to manage the inevitable accumulation.
* **Chapter 22** is the AI workflow: how to integrate the AI collaborator.
* **Chapter 23** is the synthesis: the seven compounding disciplines and where craft is going.

Each chapter builds on its predecessors. The synthesis is what emerges when all the chapters are internalized: a working engineer who has the seven compounding disciplines as reflexes, who applies them across diverse codebases and team contexts, who continues to compound their value over years.

The book's structure is the dependency order. The reader who reaches this chapter has the foundation; the practice over the next career years builds on it.

### Chapter Summary

The seven compounding disciplines (§23.1): tests as spec, refactoring under test, naming as design, ADRs, code review, version-control craft, AI as collaborator. These compound over a career; the depreciating skills (API memorization, mechanics) do not.

The three protagonists converge (§23.2) in the joint migration project. Asher (legacy, Strangler Fig), Aki (AI workflow, hexagonal), Carmen (team, async): different emphases, same disciplines.

The working engineer's craft mental model (§23.3): the three-year question, code as liability, the bottleneck moved, the codebase outlives the model, discipline over dogma, long-term thinking, vocabulary as multiplier.

Where craft is going (§23.4): AI-pair-programming as default, tests-as-spec as canonical, review focused on intent, the 10x engineer myth's obsolescence, sustained codebases as the differentiator. The compounding/depreciating barbell deepens.

The book's argument in one paragraph (§23.5): craft in 2026 is the set of disciplines that minimize the cost of reviewing, integrating, and maintaining code, which is the cost that did not collapse when AI lowered the cost of writing.

The closing exhortation (§23.6): adopt one discipline at a time; practice deliberately; integrate over years. Craft is what you do when no one is watching.

The book closes here. The disciplines are the map; the practice is the journey. The reader who internalizes the disciplines over the next five years will be the kind of engineer the next decade rewards; the reader who does not will see their value erode as AI eats the depreciating skills. The choice is yours; the time is now; the disciplines are the way.

#### Three Takeaways

  1. **Pick one of the seven compounding disciplines and practice it deliberately for the next month.** Not all seven; one. Whichever felt most undeveloped during your read. After a month, pick the next. Over a year you internalize five or six; over a career you internalize all seven and refine continuously.
  2. **Schedule a quarterly self-audit against the disciplines.** Twenty minutes per quarter. Where am I strong? Where am I weak? What is one habit I will add next quarter? The audit is the discipline that prevents drift.
  3. **Read this book again in 2028.** The tools will be different; the disciplines will not. Mentally substitute whatever your team is using; the framework holds. If a section reads stale, it is mine, not yours; the disciplines are what compound.

---

## Appendices

The book ends with four appendices. Each is designed to be useful after you have finished the book, not while reading it. Keep them somewhere you can find them when you need them.

* * *

### Appendix A — Reading List

Fifteen books and essays that compound with this book's disciplines. Read whichever fits the discipline you are deepening.

#### On testing and refactoring

  1. **Kent Beck — _Test-Driven Development by Example_ (2002).** The canonical TDD text. Beck himself frames TDD as a thinking tool, not a religion; this is the book Chapter 8 cites.
  2. **Martin Fowler — _Refactoring: Improving the Design of Existing Code_ (2nd ed., 2018).** The catalog of refactorings; far longer than the twelve in Chapter 9, but the same disciplines extended.
  3. **Michael Feathers — _Working Effectively with Legacy Code_ (2004).** The bridge between Chapters 9–11 and the worst real codebases. Characterization tests (Chapter 10) and "seams" are from this book.
  4. **Kent Beck — _Tidy First?_ (2023).** Beck's late-career reframing of refactoring as small, continuous tidying. Pairs with Chapter 20's Boy Scout Rule.

#### On craft and pragmatism

  5. **Andrew Hunt and David Thomas — _The Pragmatic Programmer_ (2nd ed., 2019).** The 1999 first edition is the classic; the 2019 anniversary edition is current. Many disciplines in this book are descendants of Hunt and Thomas's "pragmatic" framing.
  6. **John Ousterhout — _A Philosophy of Software Design_ (2nd ed., 2021).** Ousterhout's "deep modules" and "tactical tornado" concepts complement Chapters 13, 14, and 19.
  7. **Steve McConnell — _Code Complete_ (2nd ed., 2004).** Older but durable; the granular mechanics of writing code well, beneath the architectural concerns of this book.

#### On engineering organizations

  8. **Will Larson — _An Elegant Puzzle_ (2019)** and **Will Larson — _The Engineering Executive's Primer_ (2024).** The team-level and org-level patterns behind Chapters 4, 15, and 16. Carmen's playbook at Currents reads like one of Larson's case studies.
  9. **Camille Fournier — _The Manager's Path_ (2017).** The other essential engineering-management text. Code review culture, mentoring, and the staff-engineer role appear throughout the book.
  10. **Frederick Brooks — _The Mythical Man-Month_ (1975, anniversary ed. 1995).** Still right about most things. The "no silver bullet" essay applies to AI in 2026 as well as it did to tooling in 1986.
  11. **Nicole Forsgren, Jez Humble, and Gene Kim — _Accelerate_ (2018).** The DORA metrics that Chapter 19 leans on for debt quantification.

#### On architecture and design

  12. **Eric Evans — _Domain-Driven Design_ (2003).** The Ubiquitous Language concept from Chapter 18 is from this book. Evans's broader framework is heavier than most teams adopt; the naming-as-design lesson is universal.
  13. **Martin Kleppmann — _Designing Data-Intensive Applications_ (2017).** Architecture-shaped questions this book deliberately did not cover. Pairs with Chapter 14 when the codebase grows past a single service.
  14. **Erich Gamma, Richard Helm, Ralph Johnson, John Vlissides — _Design Patterns_ (1994).** The "Gang of Four" book. Read for vocabulary; treat the implementations as 1994-Java idioms, not 2026 prescriptions. Chapter 12 covers the six patterns from this book that survived.

#### On the moment

  15. **Hillel Wayne — essays at hillelwayne.com.** Not a book but a body of work; Wayne's pieces on property-based testing (Chapter 6) and software engineering as a discipline (Chapter 8) are the modern complement to the older texts above. Particularly relevant: "Are We Really Engineers?" and the property-based-testing series.

* * *

### Appendix B — The 12 Refactorings Pocket Reference

Quick-reference card for the twelve refactorings from Chapter 9. Use it during code review; tape it to the wall.

| # | Name | Signal | Mechanical move  
---|---|---|---  
1 | Extract Function | Function over 30–50 lines; sections separated by comments that read like function names | Lift the section into a named function; pass the inputs it uses; return what the caller needs  
2 | Inline Function | Function adds no semantic value over its body; called once; name does not clarify the call site | Replace the call with the body; delete the function  
3 | Rename | Name is misleading, generic, or out of date (`data`, `info`, `Manager`, `handle`) | Use the IDE's rename tool (or the agent's) — verify the change is purely mechanical, not cross-system  
4 | Move | Function is in the wrong file; uses more of another module's data than of its current module's | Move the function; update imports; run tests  
5 | Replace Conditional with Polymorphism | Long if/elif/switch on a type tag, repeated in many places | Define a protocol; one class per case implementing it; dispatch via a registry  
6 | Replace Magic Number with Constant | Literal value appears with no obvious meaning | Hoist to a named constant near the place of use; not every literal needs naming — only the unexplained ones  
7 | Extract Class | Class has methods that divide into clear sub-groups, each using a different subset of the class's fields | Split the class into one per responsibility; keep the original as a thin facade if callers depend on its public API  
8 | Replace Inheritance with Composition | Class inherits from another to _share implementation_ , not because it _is-a_ the parent | Make the parent a member rather than a base; or use a trait/mixin/dataclass approach  
9 | Introduce Parameter Object | Function takes 5+ parameters, some conceptually grouped | Group the cohesive parameters into a dataclass; pass the object  
10 | Replace Loop with Pipeline | Loop builds a list by filtering and transforming with intermediate accumulators | Convert to a comprehension, `filter`/`map`, or the language's fluent collection API  
11 | Combine Functions into Class | Multiple functions take the same data and operate on it in related ways | Construct a class around the data; the functions become methods; the data lives in one place  
12 | Decompose Conditional | Condition logic and branch action are tangled; the reader must understand both at once | Extract the condition into a named predicate; extract each branch into a named action  
  
**The deliberate-practice rule** : keep this card visible for the first two-to-three months. Look for the signals during every PR; apply the matching refactoring; commit it as a separate atomic commit (Chapter 3). After a quarter, the list is in your head and the card becomes unnecessary.

**The AI-Layer rule** : agents excel at the mechanical execution of all twelve. They are unreliable at recognizing _when_ a refactoring applies and at the _granularity_ (the agent tends to over-extract small helpers and over-apply polymorphism). The 2026 workflow is _human identifies, agent executes, human reviews_ (Chapter 9 §9.13).

* * *

### Appendix C — Minimum Glossary

A focused, alphabetical glossary of ~55 terms the book uses repeatedly. Not the originally-planned 300-entry exhaustive reference; that scope was deferred. This minimum covers the core vocabulary a reader needs to navigate later parts of the book and to use the disciplines in conversation. The chapter reference points to the primary discussion; cross-references are noted where the term appears in multiple chapters.

**ADR (Architecture Decision Record).** A record of an architectural decision, its context, and its consequences, written once in the Nygard format (Status / Context / Decision / Consequences / Alternatives). ADRs are immutable once accepted; superseding decisions are new ADRs that reference the old one. Chapter 17 §17.3.

**Adapter (pattern).** A wrapper that translates an external system's interface into the application's expected interface. Distinct from Strategy: Adapter is about _external translation_ ; Strategy is about _alternative algorithms within the application_. Chapters 12 §12.2, 14 §14.2.

**Agent mode.** An AI tool configuration where the agent autonomously reads files, modifies files, runs commands, and iterates across multiple steps without per-step human approval. The human sets the goal and reviews the result. Chapter 22 §22.1.

**Atomic commit.** A commit satisfying three properties: (1) one conceptual change; (2) the tree at the commit builds and passes tests; (3) a message that states what changed and why in imperative mood. Chapter 3 §3.1.

**Boy Scout Rule.** The discipline of leaving the code a little cleaner than you found it on every PR. Improvements are small (a rename, a deleted dead branch, an extracted helper) and aligned with the substantive change. The line between Boy Scout and scope creep is _small + aligned_. Chapter 20 §20.1.

**Branch by Abstraction.** A migration pattern where an abstraction layer sits between the application and its dependency; old and new implementations satisfy the same abstraction; traffic is gradually routed to the new. Suitable for migrations at internal interfaces. Chapter 20 §20.4.

**Characterization test.** A test written after the code, with no opinion about correctness, whose purpose is to lock in current behavior so refactoring preserves it. Distinct from a regression test (which assumes the prior behavior was wrong) and a unit test (which asserts intended behavior). Chapter 10 §10.2.

**CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md.** A file at the project root that AI agents read at the start of each session. Captures the team's conventions, anti-patterns, architectural shape, and codebase-specific gotchas. Shifts the agent's defaults toward the team's specific norms. Chapter 22 §22.4.

**Code review labels.** The four-label vocabulary (`**Blocking:**`, `**Suggestion:**`, `**Nit:**`, `**Praise:**`) for review comments, used by Carmen at Currents. The label tells the author what action is required before reading any prose. Chapter 15 §15.4.

**Code review SLA.** A documented turnaround time the team commits to for each step of the PR review cycle (first response, author response, re-review). At Currents: 4 business hours / 1 business day / 4 business hours, with escalation after 5 days. The SLA is an engineering culture artifact, not a manager metric — engineers who review promptly are enabling team velocity. Chapter 4 §4.5.

**Compounding discipline.** A skill whose value grows over a career because the world rewards more of it each year. The book's seven: tests as spec, refactoring under test, naming as design, ADRs, code review, version-control craft, AI as collaborator. Contrast: _depreciating skills_ (API memorization, CRUD-filling). Chapter 1 §1.3; Chapter 23 §23.1.

**Cunningham's debt (original framing).** Ward Cunningham's 1992 definition of technical debt: a _deliberate_ trade-off in which an engineer ships code that does not yet match their understanding of the domain, with the explicit intention to refactor as understanding improves. Distinct from the colloquial flattening that calls any bad code "debt." Each form of debt requires a different remediation (Chapter 19 §19.1).

**Conventional Commits.** A commit-message format with a fixed type-and-scope prefix (`feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `chore`, etc.). Enables automatic changelog generation, automatic semver bumps, and queryable history. Chapter 4 §4.2.

**DORA metrics.** Four key delivery performance metrics from the _Accelerate_ / _State of DevOps Report_ research: lead time for changes, deployment frequency, change failure rate, mean time to restore. Used in this book as debt diagnostics. Chapter 19 §19.3.

**Driver port / Driven port.** In Hexagonal Architecture, _driver ports_ are how the outside enters the core (HTTP routes, CLI commands, event consumers). _Driven ports_ are how the core acts on the outside (database access, vendor SDKs, message publishing). Chapter 14 §14.2.

**Dummy / Stub / Fake / Spy / Mock.** The five Meszaros test doubles, in order of sophistication. Dummies satisfy signatures; stubs return canned values; fakes have working logic with shortcuts; spies record interactions; mocks add expectations to stubs. Chapter 7 §7.1.

**FC/IS (Functional Core / Imperative Shell).** Gary Bernhardt's pattern: the application's core is pure functions; an imperative shell handles I/O, side effects, and orchestration. A stricter form of Hexagonal Architecture. Chapter 14 §14.3.

**Feature flag.** A runtime conditional under external control that allows code to be shipped in a deactivated state and activated gradually after deployment. Decouples _deploying code_ from _releasing functionality_. Chapter 4 §4.4.

**Four-quadrants model (of debt).** Martin Fowler's extension of Cunningham's debt framing along two axes: _deliberate_ vs _inadvertent_ (intentionality) and _prudent_ vs _reckless_ (judgment). Each of the four quadrants has a different remediation. Chapter 19 §19.2.

**Hexagonal Architecture (Ports and Adapters).** Alistair Cockburn's pattern: application's business logic at the core, external systems at the perimeter, connections via abstract _ports_ (defined by the core) and concrete _adapters_ (implementations). Chapter 14 §14.1.

**Mutation score.** The fraction of mechanically-generated code alterations (mutants) that the test suite detects (kills). Measures whether tests genuinely test or merely cover. High coverage + low mutation score = tests execute the lines but do not catch changes. Chapter 6 §6.4.

**Mutation testing.** A technique that mechanically alters production code in small ways (changing `<` to `<=`, removing a return, replacing a constant) to produce _mutants_ , then runs the test suite against each. The mutation _score_ is the fraction killed by the suite. Reveals whether tests genuinely test or merely cover. Chapter 6 §6.4.

**Nygard format.** The standard ADR format introduced by Michael Nygard: Title, Status, Context, Decision, Consequences, Alternatives. Used throughout the book. Chapter 17 §17.3.

**Pair programming / Mob programming.** Two engineers (or one engineer + an AI agent) working on the same code in real time, one driving and the other navigating (pair); or three+ engineers with frequent role rotation (mob). Earns its keep for complex problems, knowledge sharing, AI collaboration; overhead for routine work. Chapter 16 §16.4, §16.5, §16.6.

**Parallel paths.** The technique used in big refactors where the new and old implementations run simultaneously, with traffic routed by feature flag and outputs compared in shadow mode. Substitutes incremental risk for catastrophic risk. Chapter 11 §11.2.

**Pattern-tax.** The overhead of design patterns applied speculatively for hypothetical flexibility that never arrives. Common forms: multi-layer factories, abstract base classes with one concrete subclass, interfaces with one implementation, generic class names. Thread C lie #2. Chapter 13 §13.3.

**Port.** See _Driver port / Driven port_. The abstract interface defined by the core in Hexagonal Architecture.

**Property-based testing.** Testing by stating a property that must hold for all inputs in some class, then having the framework generate many inputs to try to falsify the property. Tools: Hypothesis (Python), fast-check (JS/TS), Proptest (Rust). Chapter 6 §6.1.

**PR template.** A standard structure for pull request descriptions: What / Why / How / Testing / Risk / Reviewer note. A forcing function for the author to think about reviewer needs. The "Why" is the most often weak and the highest-leverage to strengthen. Chapter 16 §16.2.

**Pure function.** A function with no hidden inputs or outputs beyond its named arguments and return value; given the same inputs, it produces the same output, with no side effects. The unit on which property-based testing and FC/IS depend. Stricter than "no I/O"; nondeterminism (clock reads, randomness, hidden state) also breaks purity. Chapter 14 §14.3.

**Reading with intent.** The practice of approaching unfamiliar code with a specific question in mind, with a defined exit condition (you know what counts as an answer; you stop when you have it). Distinct from reading-by-scrolling. Chapter 2 §2.1.

**Refactoring under test.** The discipline of restructuring code while a test suite continuously verifies that behavior is preserved. Tests are the oracle; a refactor that breaks tests is by definition not behavior-preserving and must be revisited. The combination of Chapter 9's twelve refactorings with Chapter 10's characterization-test pattern. Chapter 10 §10.1.

**Reflog (`git reflog`).** Git's local history of every state HEAD and branches have been in. Two retention windows: 90 days for reachable refs (`gc.reflogExpire`), 30 days for unreachable / orphaned commits (`gc.reflogExpireUnreachable`). The safety net for destructive operations like `reset --hard`. Chapter 3 §3.4.

**Repository pattern.** A pattern where each domain entity has a small typed interface (typically `find`, `save`, `delete`, and a few queries); application code depends on the interface, not on the storage technology. Makes fakes ergonomic for testing. Chapter 7 §7.3.

**Runbook.** Operational documentation for a production scenario, intended for an engineer on call at 2am. Must be specific, actionable, and tested. Components: trigger, diagnosis steps, mitigation, escalation, recovery verification. Chapter 17 §17.5.

**Semantic versioning (semver).** A versioning contract: MAJOR for incompatible API changes, MINOR for backward-compatible new features, PATCH for backward-compatible bug fixes. The right contract for libraries and CLIs; usually the wrong contract for continuously-deployed SaaS. Chapter 4 §4.3.

**Shadow read.** During a parallel-paths refactor, running both implementations in production and comparing outputs without affecting the user-facing response. Surfaces differences between old and new before traffic is routed to new. Chapter 11 §11.2.

**Snapshot testing.** A testing technique that captures the output of a function on a given input and stores it; subsequent runs compare against the stored output. Appropriate for large structured deterministic outputs where the team reads the diff. Becomes ritual (and worse than no testing) when the team approves snapshot updates without reading them. Chapter 5 §5.3.

**Spike.** A deliberate exploratory implementation, often thrown away. Used to confirm a design is viable before committing to a production-quality version. Tests come _after_ the spike has revealed the right shape; tests during the spike are usually wasted. Chapter 8 §8.4.

**Spec-driven workflow.** A 2026 workflow combining tests-as-spec with agent mode: humans write failing tests as a precise spec; AI implements; humans review and iterate. The book's position: tests-as-spec is TDD with a different driver, not a new workflow. Chapter 22 §22.2; Chapter 8 §8.5.

**Strangler Fig.** Martin Fowler's pattern for migrating from one system to another: grow the new system around the old, redirect functionality piece by piece, delete the old when fully replaced. The safer alternative to the big rewrite. Chapter 20 §20.3.

**Strategy (pattern).** A pattern defining a family of interchangeable algorithms, each in its own class implementing a common interface. Distinct from Adapter: Strategy organizes a family of internal algorithms; Adapter wraps an external system. Chapter 12 §12.1.

**TDD (Test-Driven Development).** Kent Beck's discipline: write a failing test, write the minimum code to make it pass, refactor with the tests as the safety net. The cycle (red-green-refactor) is the discipline, not the order. Chapter 8 §8.1.

**Test pyramid.** The heuristic test-suite shape: ~60-70% unit tests, ~20-30% integration tests, ~5-10% end-to-end tests. Preserves CI feedback speed, localizes failures, minimizes brittleness. Variants: hourglass, diamond. Chapter 5 §5.1.

**Tests-as-spec.** A workflow where failing tests describe the desired behavior precisely; the human writes the tests; the AI implements the code that makes them pass; the human reviews. The book's position: this is TDD with the agent as the driver of the green step. Chapter 22 §22.2; Chapter 8 §8.5.

**Thread B.** A recurring sidebar in the book following the `calculate_refund` function across five maturity stages: first draft (ch-2), after first review (ch-15), after first bug (ch-19), after first refactor (ch-10), five years later (ch-23). Demonstrates how disciplines compound on the same lump of code.

**Thread C (the seven lies).** The seven characteristic patterns of bugs in AI-generated code: (1) untested edge cases; (2) over-abstraction / pattern-tax; (3) inconsistent style; (4) wrong error handling; (5) off-by-one / boundary errors; (6) hallucinated APIs; (7) plausible-looking insecure code. Appendix D consolidates the checklist. Chapter 15 §15.5.

**Three-pass technique.** A reading discipline for new codebases: first a _breadth pass_ (every directory, every top-level file; output: one-page sketch); then a _depth pass_ (read three or four core modules in full); finally a _query-driven pass_ (extend the model per question). Most engineers skip directly to pass 3; the first two passes are what produce a model that holds across tasks. Chapter 2 §2.5.

**Three-year question.** The book's central framing: _in three years, when someone debugs this code at 2am, will they thank you or curse you?_ Applied throughout to test, refactor, name, ADR, review, commit, AI session. Chapter 1 §1.6.

**Trunk-based development.** A branching model where all engineers commit to a single long-lived branch (usually `main`) multiple times per day, with feature flags hiding incomplete work. Requires fast CI, feature flags, and small PRs. Chapter 4 §4.1.

**Ubiquitous Language.** Eric Evans's principle from _Domain-Driven Design_ : every concept in the domain has one name, used consistently by the code, the team, and the product documentation. Mismatches between code names and product names create translation tax. Chapter 18 §18.3.

**Unreviewed-plausibility debt.** The 2026 debt category Cunningham did not predict: code that appears correct, passes tests, and ships, but was not deeply understood by any human reviewer. Forms when AI-generated code outruns review capacity. Chapter 19 §19.4.

**Visitor (pattern).** A 1995 pattern for adding new operations to a class hierarchy without modifying the classes (double-dispatch via accept methods). Largely obsolete in 2026 languages with pattern matching over sum types; remains appropriate where the language lacks pattern matching. Chapter 13 §13.2.

* * *

### Appendix D — AI Code Review Checklist (Thread C, Consolidated)

The seven characteristic failure modes of AI-generated code in 2026. Run through them on every AI-generated PR; the first few times this is slow, after a month it is reflexive. Bug-catch rate beats unaided review by a meaningful margin (Chapter 15 §15.5).

#### The seven lies

#### 1\. Plausible-but-untested edge cases

Happy-path bias. The function works for the input the agent imagined; it does not work for NaN, empty list, empty string, unicode, timezone, very large or very small numbers, negative numbers, zero.

**Ask:** _What input would I try if I wanted to break this?_ If no test covers that input, the code is exposed.

#### 2\. Over-abstraction (pattern tax)

`UserRepositoryFactoryProvider` when `get_user(id)` would have worked. Abstract base classes with one concrete subclass. Interfaces with one implementation. Strategy applied to a two-case conditional. Generic names: `Handler`, `Manager`, `Processor`, `Engine`.

**Ask:** _What does this abstraction add over the simpler alternative? Is there a real second case that justifies it?_ If not, simplify.

#### 3\. Inconsistent style with surrounding code

Different naming convention. Different error-handling idiom. Different test fixture style. Different imports. Different file organization than the rest of the codebase.

**Ask:** _Does this PR look like the rest of our codebase?_ If not, the agent missed conventions — fix this PR and update CLAUDE.md so the next session does not repeat the miss.

#### 4\. Missing or wrong-granularity error handling

Bare `except:` clauses. `try / except Exception` that swallows everything. `try { ... } catch (e) { console.log(e); return null; }`. Retry without backoff. Error messages without context.

**Ask:** _What does this code do when its dependency fails? Is that the right behavior?_
