#### 5\. Subtle off-by-one or boundary errors

Comparisons with `<` versus `<=` near boundaries. Loop bounds. Array indexing. Date math (especially timezone or DST). Rounding behavior. Integer division. The worst kind of bug: almost-right code.

**Ask:** _What happens at the boundary?_ Mentally trace the code with inputs that are exactly at the boundary; verify the behavior matches the intent. Then mutation-test (Chapter 6).

#### 6\. Hallucinated library APIs

Imports from library names you do not recognize. Function calls with signatures that look unfamiliar. Method names that sound plausible but might not exist. Deprecated or outdated API usage.

**Ask:** _Does this API actually exist?_ Verify against current library documentation or by running the code locally. Special caution: typosquatted package names that look like the real library but resolve to malicious or abandoned packages — verify character-for-character.

#### 7\. Plausible-looking insecure code

SQL strings concatenated rather than parameterized. Hardcoded secrets, tokens, or credentials. Weak default crypto (MD5 for passwords, ECB mode). Missing authorization checks on new endpoints. `eval` / `exec` / `os.system` / `child_process.exec` on attacker-influenced input. Unsigned webhook handlers.

**Ask:** _If an attacker controls every variable in this function, what is the worst they can do?_ If you cannot answer or the answer is bad, fix or escalate to security review before merge.

#### The two-minute checklist (printable)

For each AI-generated PR, run through these seven prompts in order. Each is fast; the discipline is that you actually do all seven, every time, until it is reflex:

  1. **Edge cases:** What input would break this? Tested?
  2. **Abstraction:** Does any layer here exist without justification?
  3. **Style:** Does it look like the codebase?
  4. **Errors:** What happens when the dependency fails?
  5. **Boundaries:** What happens at exactly the boundary?
  6. **APIs:** Are all the imports and signatures real?
  7. **Security:** What does an attacker controlling the inputs achieve?

If any answer concerns you, comment **Blocking** (Chapter 15 §15.4). If you are unsure, comment **Suggestion** and ask. Do not merge until the seven prompts come back clean.

#### Why this works

The seven prompts compress what an experienced reviewer does silently. The novice reviewer asks none of them; the senior reviewer asks all seven without thinking; the engineer with this checklist asks all seven deliberately and ramps to senior-level review thoroughness in a few months. The checklist is the externalized form of an internalized discipline.

* * *

### Appendix E — Templates

The book gives you the disciplines and the reasoning behind them. This appendix gives you the _starting artifacts_ — four templates you can copy into your repo today and adapt to your team's specifics. Each template is intentionally short. The longest single template fits on one screen. Adapt rather than abandon: a too-long template that no one fills in is worse than a short one that the team uses reflexively.

#### Template 1: `~/.gitmessage` — commit message template
    
    
    # Subject: imperative mood, ≤ 50 chars, no trailing period
    #
    # Why this change is needed (not what it does — the diff shows that).
    # Wrap at 72 chars.
    #
    # Refs: TICKET-123
    

Enable with `git config --global commit.template ~/.gitmessage`. From Chapter 3 §3.8 Q7.

#### Template 2: `.github/pull_request_template.md` — PR description template
    
    
    ## What
    <One sentence summary of the change.>
    
    ## Why
    <The motivation. What problem does this solve? Link to ticket, ADR, or incident.>
    
    ## How
    <Brief overview of the approach. Anything non-obvious about the implementation.>
    
    ## Testing
    <What was tested. What was not. If manual testing was done, what scenarios.>
    
    ## Risk
    <What could go wrong. Rollback plan. Behind a feature flag? Which one?>
    
    ## Reviewer note
    <Specific areas the author wants reviewer attention, if any.>
    

The "Why" is the highest-leverage section and the one most often weak; treat it as required. From Chapter 16 §16.2.

#### Template 3: `CLAUDE.md` (or `AGENTS.md`) — codebase conventions for AI agents
    
    
    # Project conventions for AI agents
    
    ## Codebase orientation
    <What this project is. Where the key folders are. How to run tests.>
    
    ## Commit conventions
    Use Conventional Commits.
    Allowed types: feat, fix, refactor, test, docs, chore, perf, ci.
    Allowed scopes: <enumerate>.
    Subject line ≤ 50 chars. Body wrapped at 72.
    
    ## PR conventions
    One atomic change per PR. Target `main`. Use the template at `.github/pull_request_template.md`.
    Behind a feature flag if user-visible. Flag name follows `<scope>_<feature>_<verb>`.
    
    ## Naming conventions
    Use domain terms (list a few critical ones: e.g. `tenant` not `customer`, `renewal` not `recurring_purchase`).
    Function names are verb phrases. Predicates start with `is_` or `has_` in the positive sense.
    Generic suffixes are forbidden: Manager, Handler, Processor, Engine, Service (use specific role nouns instead).
    
    ## Test conventions
    Framework: <pytest / vitest / jest / etc>.
    Fixtures live in <fixtures path>; do not introduce new test frameworks.
    Prefer fakes over mocks for stateful dependencies. Use in-memory implementations in `tests/fakes/`.
    Cover happy path, at least one error path, and at least one boundary case.
    
    ## Pattern preferences
    Prefer plain functions to classes when a class has no state and one method.
    Prefer concrete types to interfaces when there is only one implementation.
    Prefer constructor injection (DI) to Singletons.
    Prefer pattern matching to Visitor where the language supports it.
    Introduce abstractions when a second concrete case exists, not before.
    
    ## Anti-patterns to avoid
    - Do not introduce a new HTTP client; the codebase uses <existing client>.
    - Do not introduce a Singleton; use the existing DI container.
    - Do not catch the base Exception type; catch specific exceptions only.
    - Pattern-tax (Factory Factory, Provider Factory, single-impl interfaces) is forbidden.
    
    ## Architectural shape
    We use Hexagonal Architecture (or whichever applies):
    - Business logic lives in `src/core/`.
    - Ports (Protocols) are defined in `src/core/ports/`.
    - Adapters live in `src/adapters/<system>/`.
    - Decision logic should be pure functions in `src/core/decisions/`.
    
    ## Where to look first
    - Tests are in <tests path>.
    - Domain models are in <models path>.
    - Entry points are in <entry points path>.
    
    ## Codebase-specific gotchas
    <List specific historical decisions or risks. E.g.:
    - The `legacyRoundScore` function in `src/scoring/` exists for backward compatibility with pre-2021 accounts; do not "simplify" it without reading ADR-018.
    - Package `pii-redactor` looks similar to `pii-redact` (typosquatted on npm in 2024); use only the former.>
    

Adapt to your codebase. Aim for under 300 lines total. From Chapter 22 §22.4.

#### Template 4: ADR — Architecture Decision Record (Nygard format)

Filename convention: `docs/adrs/NNN-short-title.md`, e.g. `docs/adrs/018-drizzle-vs-prisma.md`.
    
    
    # ADR-NNN: <Short decision title>
    
    Status: Proposed | Accepted (YYYY-MM-DD) | Superseded by ADR-MMM
    
    ## Context
    <What is the problem? What are the constraints? What forces are at play?
    This is where the *Why* lives. Future readers need to understand what made the
    decision necessary, what the existing state was, and what would happen if no
    decision were made.>
    
    ## Decision
    <What we decided. State it once, clearly, in a few sentences.>
    
    ## Consequences
    <What this decision implies — positive, negative, and neutral.
    Be honest about the costs. The decision still passes the cost-benefit test;
    the costs are what the team committed to live with.>
    
    ## Alternatives considered
    <What else we considered. For each: one paragraph on why we did not choose it.
    The point is to record the *real* contenders, not exhaustive completeness.
    A reader in 2028 should be able to see what we weighed.>
    

ADRs are immutable once accepted. Superseding decisions are new ADRs that reference the old one. From Chapter 17 §17.3.

#### Template 5: Runbook — operational documentation

Filename convention: `docs/runbooks/<scenario>.md`, e.g. `docs/runbooks/database-failover.md`.
    
    
    # Runbook: <Scenario name>
    
    ## Trigger
    <How is this situation detected? What alert fires? What does the dashboard look like?>
    
    ## Diagnosis (target: 5 minutes)
    <Specific steps to determine the cause. Each step should be one exact command
    or one exact dashboard check. No abstractions; no "investigate the issue.">
    
    1. <Step 1: specific command or check.>
    2. <Step 2: specific command or check.>
    3. <Step 3: specific command or check.>
    
    ## Mitigation
    <How to stop the impact. Often distinct from fixing the root cause.
    Each option should have a specific command. State the expected outcome
    of each option so the on-call engineer knows when to escalate.>
    
    - Immediate (under 1 minute): <exact command>
    - If <cause X> suspected: <exact command>
    - If <cause Y> suspected: <exact command>
    
    ## Escalation
    <When to wake up someone else. Be specific about timing and who.>
    
    If mitigation does not stabilize within <N> minutes, page <role> on-call via <pager system>.
    
    ## Recovery verification
    <How to confirm the impact is resolved. Specific metrics and thresholds.>
    
    - <Metric 1 returns to <threshold>.>
    - <Metric 2 returns to <threshold>.>
    - Wait <N> minutes after metrics normalize before closing the incident.
    

Runbooks are read under stress at 2am. Specific, actionable, tested. From Chapter 17 §17.5.

* * *

The five templates together cover most of a team's standing artifacts. Once they are in place, the disciplines in this book have the substrate they need. The templates are starting points; teams customize and add over time. The most valuable customizations are the _codebase-specific gotchas_ in CLAUDE.md and the _real production scenarios_ in runbooks — both of which only your team can write.

* * *
